L7: This Clinical Review Addendum clarifies the data presentation in the original NDA Primary Clinical Review by Dr. Brian Porter (submitted July 25, 2011) with regard to efficacy outcomes for laryngeal HAE attacks observed in the Phase 3 icatibant development program. In Section 6.1.5 Analysis of Secondary Endpoint(s) of the original NDA Clinical Review, the data discussed under the heading Detailed Analysis of Laryngeal HAE Attacks (pages 72-76) reflect the intent-to-treat (ITT) analysis for both the active treatment (icatibant) and placebo groups for FAST-3. Therefore, while the ITT analyses presented in this section (including Tables 19 and 20) are accurate, this Addendum clarifies that these subjects do not represent a true placebo comparator group.
L8: Only two subjects were originally randomized to placebo in FAST-3 for the purposes of the ITT analysis. However, of these, one subject (054-320-011) received icatibant as rescue medication 3.4 hours after receiving placebo, due to worsening laryngeal symptoms. The second subject (054-356-004) never received blinded placebo as originally assigned, due to worsening laryngeal symptoms that led the Investigator to administer open-label icatibant to this patient, rather than blinded study treatment. This was considered a protocol violation, as discussed in the original Clinical Review under Section 3.2 Compliance with Good Clinical Practices. Thus, although both of these subjects are classified as placebo-recipients for the purposes of the ITT analysis, the laryngeal attack population lacks a true placebo comparator group. Therefore, as shown in Table 19 under the heading Patient-based Symptom Severity Scores (pages 72-73), although there was a greater decrease in self-reported symptoms at Hour 4 for subjects originally randomized to placebo versus icatibant in FAST-3, both these placebo subjects had actually received icatibant by Hour 4 post-dosing. In turn, the utility of this randomized ITT comparison is questionable. Moreover, with regard to rescue medication use, as discussed in the original Clinical Review under the heading Use of Rescue Medications (page 76), these data are difficult to interpret without a randomized placebo comparator group, although it is noteworthy that both subjects originally randomized to placebo in FAST-3 received icatibant either as a rescue medication or as open-label treatment, due to worsening laryngeal symptoms.
L9: This Addendum is provided only for clarification of the data presentation in Section 6.1.5, as no conclusions discussed in the original Primary Clinical Review are affected.

L32: 1 Recommendations/Risk-Benefit Assessment
L33: 1.1 Recommendation on Regulatory Action
L34: According to a review of the clinical data, the recommended action for this Complete Response is Approval. In brief, Jerini US, Inc. originally submitted NDA 22-150 via the 505(b)(1) pathway on October 22, 2007, to seek approval for icatibant for the treatment of HAE, which was later revised to the treatment of acute attacks of HAE. The Agency issued a Not Approvable action letter for the original NDA on April 23, 2008, which cited multiple CMC deficiencies, a lack of long-term nonclinical toxicity studies, incomplete characterization of age and gender effects on pharmacokinetics, and several clinical deficiencies in the icatibant development program: 1) the failure to demonstrate the efficacy and safety of icatibant for the proposed indication, based on the results of the original pivotal Phase 3 program (e.g., lack of a statistically significant treatment effect versus placebo, the choice of a questionable active control comparator agent, the lack of established validity of the primary patient reported outcomes measure); 2) the lack of data demonstrating the safe use of Firazyr in non-healthcare settings, such as with self-injection by patients; 3) the need to further define dose selection in a sufficient number of patients based on the clinical endpoint or other validated and related biomarkers. In turn, on February 25, 2011, Shire HGT for Jerini US, Inc. submitted a Complete Response to this Not Approvable action letter, with additional information submitted to address the nonclinical and CMC deficiencies, as well as each of the clinical deficiencies, as discussed in this Clinical Review.
L35: This review concludes that the Complete Response for NDA 22-150 supports the approval of icatibant (Firazyr) injectable solution in a pre-filled syringe for the treatment of acute attacks of hereditary angioedema (HAE) in adults 18 years of age and older. The Applicant has conducted an additional Phase 3 placebo-controlled efficacy trial (FAST-3) to establish the efficacy of icatibant for the proposed indication and has submitted a Complete Study Report for this trial, as well as an Integrated Summary of Efficacy in the context of results of two prior pivotal Phase 3 trials submitted with the original NDA (FAST-1 and FAST-2). In addition, another Phase 3 trial (FAST-4) was conducted to establish the safety and efficacy of self-administered icatibant by non-healthcare workers in non-healthcare settings for the treatment of acute HAE attacks. When considered in conjunction with pooled safety data from the icatibant Phase 3 development program, the efficacy data presented in this Complete Response supports approval of icatibant for the treatment of acute HAE attacks in adults.
L36: 1.2 Risk-Benefit Assessment
L37: HAE is a rare, autosomal dominant, inheritable disease caused by a quantitative or qualitative functional deficiency in C1 esterase inhibitor (C1-INH), which leads to dysregulated accumulation of the potent vasodilator bradykinin. This results in recurrent, intermittent attacks of potentially life-threatening mucosal edema at various bodily sites, most commonly the skin, abdomen, and upper respiratory tract. In addition to causing physical disfigurement and severe pain, acute HAE attacks may compromise the upper airway and cause significant hypotension, both of which can be fatal. While two products are currently approved and available in the U.S. for the prophylaxis of HAE attacks (Cinryze, a highly purified human plasma-derived C1-INH replacement product, and danazol, a synthetic androgenic steroid), only two products are currently approved in the U.S. to treat acute HAE attacks: Berinert (a human plasma-derived C1-INH replacement product administered intravenously) and Kalbitor (ecallantide: a plasma kallikrein inhibitor injected subcutaneously). Both Berinert and ecallantide have been associated with potentially severe hypersensitivity reactions, including anaphylaxis.
L38: Icatibant is a new molecular entity and is the first drug in its class of bradykinin type 2 receptor antagonists. Unlike the other agents approved for the treatment of acute HAE attacks, icatibant has been developed specifically to target the receptor-ligand interactions of the bradykinin-bradykinin receptor pathway, as a novel treatment for acute HAE attacks in adults. The safety and efficacy assessments of icatibant are based primarily on results generated from three pivotal Phase 3 safety and efficacy trials (FAST-1, FAST-2, and FAST-3) in adults with Type I or Type II HAE, as well as an additional Phase 3 trial, FAST-4, which evaluated the safety and efficacy of self-administered icatibant by non-healthcare workers for acute HAE attacks. FAST-1 and FAST-3 were both randomized, placebo-controlled, parallel group trials, whereas FAST-2 was similarly designed, but utilized tranexamic acid, an agent unapproved for the treatment of acute HAE attacks in the U.S., as a control treatment. In contrast, FAST-4 was an open-label, uncontrolled trial, focused primarily on evaluating the safety of the proposed mode of delivery (i.e., self-injection using a pre-filled syringe). Both FAST-3 and FAST-4 were conducted in response to clinical deficiencies cited in a Not Approvable action letter that was issued following review of the original icatibant NDA submission, which included FAST-1 and FAST-2. In turn, FAST-3 and FAST-4 are reviewed in detail in this Complete Response review, within the context of the original icatibant Phase 3 clinical program (FAST-1 and FAST-2) that was reviewed in the initial NDA submission.
L39: The original Phase 3 icatibant program did not establish the safety and effectiveness of icatibant for the proposed indication. Using time to onset of primary symptom relief based on VAS assessments as the primary efficacy endpoint, FAST-1 failed to demonstrate a statistical difference between treatment groups, although a non-significant numerical trend was noted in favor of the icatibant group. While the results of
L40: FAST-2 demonstrated a statistically significant decrease in time to symptom relief onset in the icatibant group versus control subjects, the use of tranexamic acid as a control treatment was questioned by the Division, as this agent is unapproved for this indication in the U.S., and its effects on HAE symptomatology have not been fully characterized. Thus, the use of tranexamic acid as a comparator could potentially inflate a positive treatment effect attributed to icatibant, if tranexamic acid were to have a negative treatment effect on HAE symptoms. In turn, the results of an additional randomized, placebo-controlled trial, FAST-3, were key to characterizing the risk-benefit profile of icatibant for the proposed indication. These Phase 3 trials all utilized a PRO measure, the Visual Analog Scale (VAS), to quantify self-reported (patient-assessed) symptomatology. Validation studies of this outcome measure were reviewed with the original NDA submission and ultimately found to be consistent with the Agency's guidance on PRO development. However, given the novelty of VAS-based outcome measures in HAE development programs and the Division's lack of regulatory experience with such efficacy endpoints, the Division concluded during the original NDA review that the validity of the VAS as used in the icatibant development program had not been fully established.
L41: The additional Phase 3 trials submitted in the icatibant Complete Response (FAST-3 and FAST-4) addressed the clinical deficiencies of the original development program. As recommended by the Division, FAST-3 was similar in design and scope to FAST-1 and FAST-2 and was noted to have an increased proportion of subjects enrolled from domestic (U.S.) study sites. Although the primary efficacy endpoint was designated as a composite VAS-based symptom score encompassing both cutaneous and abdominal HAE symptoms (VAS-3: abdominal pain, skin pain, skin swelling), the same endpoint designated as the primary efficacy endpoint in FAST-1 and FAST-2 was retained as the key secondary efficacy endpoint in FAST-3. The results of FAST-3 demonstrated a highly significant statistical difference in time to onset of symptom relief based on both primary VAS symptom rating and composite VAS-3 score, in favor of the icatibant group over placebo. This significant difference favoring icatibant was maintained when efficacy data were examined separately for cutaneous versus abdominal attacks. In addition, a post hoc analysis of time to symptom onset based on the composite VAS-3 score also demonstrated statistically significant group differences in FAST-1 and FAST-2, which favored icatibant-recipients. Secondary efficacy endpoint analyses, subgroup analyses, and sensitivity analyses generally support the results of the primary analysis for FAST-3. Thus, if the questionable efficacy results of FAST-1 and FAST-2 are taken as supportive data, the definitive efficacy results of FAST-3 support the approval of icatibant for the proposed indication of the treatment of acute HAE attacks in adults. Efficacy data generated from the icatibant self-administration trial (FAST-4) demonstrated a consistent treatment effect of icatibant, with regard to time to onset of symptom relief, confirming the effectiveness of icatibant when self-injected by non-healthcare workers in non-healthcare settings.
L42: The safety profile of icatibant as determined from an individual review of each of the Phase 3 efficacy trials, as well as a pooled safety analysis of all subjects receiving blinded treatment for an initial HAE attack during the randomized treatment phase of these trials, demonstrates adequate safety and tolerability, thereby supporting the approval of this agent for this rare but potentially life-threatening indication. Although the safety database is relative small, given the rarity of HAE, which is a designated orphan disease, the feasibility of conducting larger controlled trials is questionable, particularly with regard to laryngeal HAE attacks, which are rarer than cutaneous and abdominal HAE attacks. Despite its limited scope, however, the icatibant safety database did not reveal any significant safety signals, which are uniquely attributable to icatibant, other than the development of local injection site reactions in nearly all icatibant recipients that far exceeded rates seen with placebo treatment. These included injection site erythema, swelling, pruritus, burning sensation, and warmth. However, these reactions were rarely severe and generally improved without medical intervention within several hours of icatibant injection. Given the potentially life-threatening effects of HAE, these adverse events are considered acceptable as a consequence of effective treatment. Icatibant use was also shown to minimize rescue medication use (i.e., C1-INH replacement therapy, opioids, anti-emetics, NSAIDs, corticosteroids) during acute HAE attacks.
L43: Moreover, in contrast to adverse drug reactions that have been reported for both Berinert (C1-INH replacement) and Kalbitor (ecallantide), no hypersensitivity reactions were observed following icatibant treatment in the Phase 3 clinical program. In addition, studies across all three pivotal efficacy trials revealed no significant risk of immunogenicity, which was not unexpected for this decapeptide drug. Postmarketing data from the European Union submitted in this Complete Response, which the Applicant estimates encompasses 5,379 patient exposures to Firazyr since the time of E.U. approval on July 11, 2008, through July 11, 2010, have also not revealed any safety signals related to drug hypersensitivity. Thus, this decreased risk of drug-specific antibody formation may represent a significant benefit of icatibant over currently approved treatments for acute HAE attacks.
L44: A review of safety data from FAST-4 suggests that icatibant pre-filled syringes are a safe and effective mode of drug delivery outside of a clinical setting by patients who are non-healthcare workers. In contrast, ecallantide, which is also delivered subcutaneously, is labeled for delivery exclusively by qualified healthcare workers only in clinical settings equipped to diagnose and treat anaphylaxis. Similarly, C1-INH replacement therapies must be administered intravenously by trained persons (whether in clinical or nonclinical settings) and, thus, carry the risks and challenges associated with all intravenous formulations, including the need for adequate vascular access. Thus, self-administration of subcutaneous icatibant may be a significant improvement over currently available treatments, from both a logistical and safety perspective.
L45: Of note, the potential role of icatibant as a prophylactic treatment for HAE was not evaluated in this development program, and it is not clear whether icatibant treatment will lead to fewer HAE attacks over time. During the open-label extension phases of the three pivotal Phase 3 efficacy trials, patients received repeated long-term intermittent icatibant dosing (e.g., up to 142 total doses of icatibant delivered over several years in one patient). Thus, this safety database is less informative with regard to larger overall icatibant doses for a single HAE attack, as it is for recurrent, intermittent dosing for multiple attacks over time. Nonetheless, the available safety data are adequate to support a regulatory decision for drug approval.
L46: 1.3 Recommendations for Postmarket Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies
L47: No postmarketing risk evaluation and mitigation strategies are recommended.
L48: 1.4 Recommendations for Postmarket Requirements and Commitments
L49: No clinical trial postmarketing requirements or commitments are recommended.
L50: 2 Introduction and Regulatory Background
L51: 2.1 Product Information
L52: Icatibant (Firazyr) is a new molecular entity developed for the treatment of acute attacks of HAE in adults. HAE is a rare (prevalence of 1 in 10,000 to 1 in 50,000), inheritable, autosomal dominant disease characterized by recurrent episodes of angioedema of the skin and mucosal surfaces due to uncontrolled bradykinin-mediated vasodilation, which commonly involve the head and neck, bowel, and respiratory tract. Acute HAE attacks may be life-threatening, particularly if they involve upper airway edema and/or severe hypotension due to vasodilation. Icatibant is a decapeptide antagonist of the bradykinin type 2 receptor that is designed for subcutaneous administration from a pre-filled glass syringe (b) (4) which contains 30 mg of icatibant acetate in a 3 mL solution. The Applicant proposes to co-package the pre-filled syringe with a single (b) (4) 25G needle (b) (4) packed separately in the same carton.
L53: In addition to the active ingredient, the sterile, preservative-free acetate buffer solution also contains 7.45 mg sodium chloride, 1.32 mg acetic acid, 0.64 mg sodium hydroxide/ml, and water for injection adjusted to pH 5.5 \pm 0.3. By directly inhibiting receptor-ligand interaction and antagonizing bradykinin receptor function, icatibant counters the predominant symptoms of acute HAE attacks that are mediated by dysregulated bradykinin accumulation, including bradykinin-mediated vasodilation, skin
L54: swelling, mucosal swelling, laryngeal edema, skin pain, abdominal pain, and hypotension. The proposed dose of icatibant is 30 mg delivered by subcutaneous injection, with repeat 30 mg doses as needed up to every six hours, not to exceed three doses (90 mg total) within a 24-hour period.
L55: 2.2 Table of Currently Available Treatments for Proposed Indications
L56: As summarized in the follow table, there are currently two other approved products indicated for the treatment of acute attacks of HAE (Berinert, a human C1 esterase inhibitor replacement product, and Kalbitor, a plasma kallikrein inhibitor), as well as two products approved for the prophylaxis of HAE attacks (Cinryze, a highly purified plasma-derived C1 esterase inhibitor replacement product, and danazol, a synthetic androgenic steroid). Not listed in this table are stanozolol and oxymetholone, two synthetic androgenic steroids that were approved for HAE prophylaxis but are no longer marketed in the U.S. for this indication (stanozolol is no longer manufactured in the U.S., although oxymetholone is still marketed for other non-HAE-related indications). Also not included in this table are epsilon aminocaproic acid and tranexamic acid, which are used as chronic treatments for HAE in some foreign countries but are not approved for this indication in the U.S. Fresh frozen plasma (FFP) has also been used off-label in the U.S. as acute treatment or short-term prophylaxis for HAE attacks, although its efficacy is controversial.

L60: *Labeling states safety and efficacy in children 0 to 12 years of age have not been established;

L62: Of note, severe hypersensitivity reactions, including anaphylaxis, are listed in the Warnings and Precautions section of the Berinert Prescribing Information (PI), while the PI for Kalbitor contains a boxed warning for anaphylaxis, indicating Kalbitor should only be administered by a healthcare professional capable of managing anaphylaxis and HAE. Similarly, severe hypersensitivity reactions, including anaphylaxis, are also listed in the Warnings and Precautions section of the Cinryze PI, which further indicates that epinephrine should be available for the immediate treatment of acute severe hypersensitivity reactions. Allergic reactions (urticaria, pruritus, nasal congestion) have also been reported for danazol, although a causal relationship to the drug has not been established. Of note, danazol carries a boxed warning emphasizing its contraindication in pregnancy, as well as reports of thromboembolic events, peliosis hepatis, benign hepatic adenomas, and benign intracranial hypertension.
L63: 2.3 Availability of Proposed Active Ingredient in the United States
L64: Icatibant is a new molecular entity and is not approved in the U.S. for any indication. However, icatibant was approved in the European Union on July 11, 2008, for the symptomatic treatment of acute attacks of HAE in adults. According to the Summary of Product Characteristics labeling, “Firazyr may be self-administered or administered by a caregiver only after training in subcutaneous injection technique by a healthcare professional,” as “Firazyr is intended for use under the guidance of a healthcare professional.”
L65: 2.4 Important Safety Issues with Consideration to Related Drugs
L66: There are no other members of this pharmacologic class (bradykinin type 2 receptor antagonist) currently approved in the U.S. or any other country for HAE treatment or any other indication. Although the risk of severe hypersensitivity reactions has been associated with other agents approved for the treatment of acute HAE attacks (Berinert and Kalbitor), these products are of a different pharmacologic class.
L67: 2.5 Summary of Presubmission Regulatory Activity Related to Submission
L68: Jerini US, Inc. originally submitted NDA 22-150 via the 505(b)(1) pathway on October 22, 2007, to seek approval for icatibant for the treatment of HAE, later revising this indication to the treatment of acute attacks of HAE. As summarized in Table 2, the original NDA submission consisted of data from seven clinical trials: three Phase 1 trials evaluating safety, tolerability, pharmacokinetics, and QTc effects in healthy volunteers (JE049-1101, JE049-1102, JE049-1103), a single open-label proof-of-concept Phase 2 safety, tolerability, and pharmacokinetic trial in adult HAE patients (JE049-2101), an observational PRO validation study in adult HAE patients (JE049-4102) that characterized the minimum clinically significant difference in Visual Analog Scale (VAS), and two Phase 3 safety and efficacy trials: JE049-2103 (FAST-1), which compared the effectiveness of icatibant versus placebo in the treatment of acute HAE attacks, and JE049-2102 (FAST-2), which compared the effectiveness of icatibant versus a control agent with unclear activity in HAE (tranexamic acid, which is unapproved in the U.S. for the treatment of acute HAE attacks). In addition to the randomized blinded treatment of initial cutaneous and/or abdominal HAE attacks, FAST-1 and FAST-2 both allowed for open-label icatibant treatment of initial laryngeal HAE attacks, as well as recurrent HAE attacks of any type that occurred during an open-label extension period, into which all subjects from the blinded treatment phase were automatically enrolled (regardless of initial treatment assignment). A smaller number of patients who met trial participation criteria but did not experience an initial HAE attack prior to the close of enrollment were also allowed to enter the extension phase and receive open-label icatibant as needed for any subsequent HAE attack. For completeness sake, Table 2 also lists in italics the third similarly designed pivotal Phase 3 efficacy trial (FAST-3), as well as a fourth Phase 3 trial (FAST-4) that evaluated icatibant self-administration, neither of which were included in the original NDA submission.

L78: Following a review of the trials included in the original NDA submission, the Division issued a Not Approvable action letter on April 23, 2008, which cited multiple CMC deficiencies, the lack of long-term nonclinical toxicity studies, incomplete characterization of age and gender effects on icatibant pharmacokinetics (as discussed in the Clinical Pharmacology NDA Review), as well as the following clinical deficiencies in the icatibant development program:
L79: 1. The submitted data from your clinical program do not provide substantial evidence that icatibant is sufficiently safe and effective for the proposed indication of the treatment of acute attacks of hereditary angioedema (HAE). The uncertain efficacy of the comparator drug, tranexamic acid, in the treatment of acute attacks of HAE complicates interpretation of the results of Study JE049-2102 (FAST-2). Study JE049-2103 (FAST-1) failed to demonstrate a statistically significant treatment difference between placebo and icatibant. In addition, there are concerns regarding the validity of the primary endpoint used in both studies (time to onset of symptom relief using the Visual Analog Scale). Without substantial evidence of the efficacy of the proposed dose of icatibant, we cannot evaluate if there is appropriate safety. Before icatibant may be approved, you must submit sufficient evidence of the efficacy of icatibant for the treatment of patients with acute attacks of HAE. This evidence must be generated by using a reliable instrument to assess efficacy and an appropriate control arm. You will need to demonstrate appropriate safety for the dose shown to be efficacious.
2. Firazyr injection is likely to be used in settings outside the usual healthcare delivery environment, such as self-injection by patients. Submit data to show that Firazyr can be safely used in such settings.
3. Dose-selection should be further defined in sufficient patients based on the clinical endpoint or other biomarkers that are validated to be related to the clinical endpoint.
L80: On February 25, 2011, Shire HGT for Jerini US, Inc. submitted a Complete Response to this Not Approvable action letter. In addition to information submitted to address the nonclinical and CMC deficiencies, the Applicant submitted the following information to address each of the clinical deficiencies:
L81: Demonstration of Clinical Efficacy
L82: Two pivotal Phase 3 efficacy trials were included in the original NDA submission, which produced inconsistent efficacy results that did not support drug approval. FAST-1 consisted of 56 patients randomized to icatibant (30 mg SC) versus placebo for an initial moderate to severe cutaneous or abdominal HAE attack, eight patients treated with open-label icatibant for an initial laryngeal HAE attack, and 42 subjects treated during the open-label extension phase with a total of 128 icatibant doses for a total of 109 HAE attacks. FAST-2 consisted of 74 patients similarly randomized to icatibant versus tranexamic acid (1000 mg taken orally every 6-8 hours up to 6 doses over 2 days) for an initial cutaneous or abdominal HAE attack, three patients treated with open-label icatibant for an initial laryngeal HAE attack, and 35 subjects treated during the open-label extension phase with a total of 131 icatibant doses for a total of 122 HAE attacks. A statistically significant difference was not observed in FAST-1 for the primary efficacy endpoint of time to onset of primary symptom relief, as determined by self-reported Visual Analogue Scale (VAS) ratings of the predominant symptom manifestation based on HAE attack location (e.g., skin, abdomen). In contrast, FAST-2 demonstrated a statistically significant decrease in time to onset of primary symptom relief in the icatibant arm versus the control tranexamic acid arm, which the Applicant felt supported the proposed indication. However, the Division questioned the validity of this interpretation, given that tranexamic acid is not approved in the U.S. to treat acute HAE attacks. Therefore, it was unclear whether tranexamic acid could potentially have lengthened time to symptom relief onset in the control arm, if it conferred a negative effect on HAE manifestations, thereby inflating any positive treatment effect seen with icatibant. For these reasons, the original Phase 3 program was considered insufficient to establish the efficacy of icatibant for the proposed indication.
L83: As noted in the Not Approvable action letter, the Division recommended the Applicant conduct an additional placebo-controlled trial of icatibant, similar in scope to FAST-1 and FAST-2, in order to establish replicate efficacy results in support of the proposed indication. The Applicant responded by completing HGT-FIR-054 (FAST-3), which was a placebo-controlled trial conducted in 98 subjects and similar in design to FAST-1, with the main difference being the designated primary efficacy endpoint of time to symptom relief onset based on a mean composite VAS symptom rating (VAS-3), comprised of skin pain, skin swelling, and abdominal pain symptom components. Given the Division's recommendation at an End of Review Meeting on December 15, 2008, to use the same primary efficacy endpoint in FAST-3 as was used in FAST-1 and FAST-2, the Applicant designated time to onset of primary symptom relief as the key secondary
L84: endpoint in FAST-3. A Special Protocol Assessment (SPA) for this trial was submitted by the Applicant on February 13, 2009.
L85: In reviewing the SPA, one of the Division's major considerations was the validity of the VAS rating scale used to define the main efficacy endpoints in the pivotal Phase 3 trials, as previously noted in the Not Approvable action letter issued for the original NDA. The VAS is defined as a 100 mm horizontal line, with the right-most extreme value equal to 100 mm connoting the worst possible symptoms, whereas the left-most extreme (0 mm) indicates no symptoms. Subjects are asked to rate their symptoms by physically marking this line somewhere along this continuum, with reference to the two extremes. While the VAS has been widely used as a pain assessment tool in clinical research, its proposed use in the icatibant development program to assess individual symptom domains in acute HAE attacks was novel. In the original NDA submission, the Applicant had addressed the validity of the VAS through a series of PRO validation studies evaluating the face, content, and clinical validity of the VAS, including post hoc comparative analyses from the Phase 3 database of patient symptom diary data and physician-based clinical assessments during acute HAE attacks. In addition, the Applicant conducted Study JE049-4102 to characterize the minimum clinically significant difference in the VAS by correlating self-assessed VAS ratings with ratings on the Verbal Descriptor Scale (VDS), a 5-point scale that the Applicant considered to be a comparative gold standard for evaluating self-reported symptom change over time. By determining the relationship between incremental changes in the VAS and clinically relevant changes in the VDS, the Applicant proposed that the minimum clinically significant difference in VAS rating was 9 mm on the 100 mm scale.
L86: While collectively, these validation studies were consistent with Agency guidance on PRO development programs (Guidance for Industry: Patient-Reported Outcome Measures: Use in Medical Product Development to Support Labeling Claims -- December 2009), the Division was cognizant of the lack of prior regulatory experience with VAS-based efficacy endpoints in HAE clinical programs, as well as the novel nature of the VAS when used in this manner. In turn, the Division was unable to make a final determination as to the validity of the VAS in assessing HAE symptomatology, during review of the original NDA submission. Likewise, in response to the proposed SPA, although the Division was generally supportive of the overall trial design of FAST-3, given the novelty of the proposed primary endpoint and the lack of regulatory experience with VAS-based outcome measures in HAE clinical programs, the Agency issued a No Agreement SPA letter on April 2, 2009, indicating that the design and planned analysis of FAST-3 did not adequately address the objectives required to support a marketing submission. Ultimately, the Applicant conducted FAST-3 as originally proposed, enrolling the first subject in July of 2009.
L87: Safety and Efficacy of Icatibant Self-administration
L88: With regard to demonstrating the safety of icatibant for self-administration in emergency settings, the Applicant conducted an additional Phase 3 clinical trial (JE049-3101 or FAST-4) in 56 subjects to assess the safety, tolerability, and perceived convenience of self-administered icatibant outside of a medical setting. This multicenter, open-label, uncontrolled trial of single-dose icatibant 30 mg SC also assessed efficacy as a secondary endpoint. At the outset of the trial, patients with documented Type I or Type II HAE (eight icatibant-naïve subjects and 48 subjects previously treated with icatibant) were trained in the self-administration of icatibant using a placebo-filled syringe. Immediately following training, icatibant-experienced subjects were given a single icatibant syringe for self-administration and a symptom diary and were instructed to self-administer this dose at the outset of their next HAE attack of qualifying severity. In contrast, icatibant-naïve subjects were treated at the investigator's medical institution at the time of their first post-enrollment HAE attack, where icatibant was administered by a healthcare professional. Following resolution of this initial attack, these subjects were similarly given a single icatibant syringe for self-administration and a new symptom diary and were then allowed to self-administer icatibant at the onset of a second HAE attack, followed by evaluation at the clinical site within 48 hours of treatment (where up to two more injections of icatibant could be administered every 6 hours, as needed). One subject was lost to follow-up after completing icatibant self-treatment, and another 95 subjects were enrolled in the trial, trained in the use of icatibant, but never experienced an HAE attack during the course of the trial and, therefore, did not self-administer icatibant. The Applicant suggests that the nature and severity of adverse events (AEs) documented in this trial following icatibant self-administration were similar to what was observed in prior clinical trials of icatibant administered in medical settings by healthcare workers and identified no new safety signals.
L89: Dose-selection based on Clinical Endpoint or Biomarkers
L90: The Applicant states that icatibant dose-selection was originally based on several Phase 1 clinical pharmacology studies, a Phase 2 repeat-dose proof-of-concept trial that included intravenous and subcutaneous dose-ranging (JE049-2101), data generated from a human intravenous bradykinin challenge model, and additional pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic modeling. An integrated analysis of these data (JE049-5108) was submitted in this Complete Response, in which the Applicant argues that the 30 mg SC dose of icatibant carried forward into the three pivotal Phase 3 efficacy trials is safe and effective for the proposed indication and, therefore, no further dose definition is necessary. The Applicant also submitted a Complete Study Report for an additional pharmacokinetic trial of repeat icatibant dosing in healthy adults (HGT-FIR-065) that was conducted since the original NDA was denied approval, which the Applicant feels further supports the selected dose.
L91: 2.6 Other Relevant Background Information
L92: Icatibant is approved and marketed in the European Union (37 countries), and the Applicant has indicated that an estimated 5,379 patient exposures to Firazyr have occurred cumulatively in the postmarketing setting between July 11, 2008 (time of E.U. approval) and July 11, 2010. Safety data from this postmarketing exposure is discussed in Section 8 Postmarket Experience.
L93: 3 Ethics and Good Clinical Practices
L94: 3.1 Submission Quality and Integrity
L95: A review of the ethical and clinical research practices utilized for the clinical trials newly submitted with this Complete Response submission (FAST-3 and FAST-4) revealed no deficiencies which compromised the validity of the data collected. In particular, data from FAST-3 were obtained from 67 study sites in the United States and 10 foreign countries (Australia, Canada, Hungary, Israel, Mexico, Romania, Russia, South Africa, Turkey, Ukraine), with the majority of subjects recruited domestically (219 out of 369 screened subjects for a total of 65 enrollees). Of the foreign study sites, the greatest number of subjects were enrolled in Israel (n = 10), whereas 0 to 5 subjects were enrolled in each of the other countries. As icatibant is a new molecular entity, an audit by the Division of Scientific Investigations (DSI) was already conducted for the original NDA submission in which those study sites that enrolled the highest number of subjects for FAST-1 and FAST-2 were evaluated for scientific integrity and adherence to Good Clinical Practices. In contrast to the original application, however, the majority of study sites for FAST-3 were located domestically. In addition, a statistical review of efficacy data from FAST-3 by the Biometrics Review Team did not detect any outliers in performance by individual study site, which would require an additional DSI audit. Moreover, the Applicant states that no debarred investigators participated in these trials. Thus, no additional DSI audit for this Complete Response was requested.
L96: 3.2 Compliance with Good Clinical Practices
L97: The Applicant has certified that all clinical trials were conducted under Good Clinical Practices (GCP). However, the original NDA review noted that several GCP violations were identified for FAST-1 (two subjects with missing source data from one study site) and FAST-2 (one subject with retrospectively entered data and two subjects with lost source documents and informed consent forms signed after the screening visit). In addition, two protocol violations were noted in the randomized treatment phase of FAST-3: one subject who was randomly assigned to placebo treatment subsequently developed laryngeal symptoms that were treated with open-label icatibant and was assigned to the laryngeal population; one placebo-recipient was enrolled in the trial,
L98: despite not meeting the inclusion criterion of having at least one VAS score \geq 30 mm. These violations do not appear to reflect systemic bias in the underlying samples, however, and are not suspected to have markedly affect data analysis or interpretation.
L99: 3.3 Financial Disclosures
L100: No investigators associated with FAST-1, FAST-2, or FAST-3 were listed as having any financial conflicts of interest. One financial disclosure was made for a German-based investigator who involved in FAST-4, who had received research funding (37,000 euros in 2010) from the Applicant for a separate single investigator-initiated study regarding HAE classification in clinical practice. This investigator certified that she had no other financial interests related to the Applicant prior to the initiation of FAST-4. The Applicant certifies that the data obtained from this clinical site was unbiased, with protocol adherence and source documentation monitored by a contract research organization on an ongoing basis during the trial.
L101: 4 Significant Efficacy/Safety Issues Related to Other Review Disciplines
L102: 4.1 Chemistry Manufacturing and Controls
L103: With regard to the drug substance, all related substance impurities have limits (b) (4) and the total impurities are (b) (4). A total of 17 impurity peaks were not identified, with a specification limit of (b) (4) each, but this was deemed acceptable for NDA approval, following discussion with the Pharmacology/Toxicology Review Team. Specifically, the CMC Review Team has recommended approval of this drug product, with a post-approval commitment to provide chemical structures for impurity peaks (b) (4) that were not fully identified with structures and impurity peaks (b) (4) that were not identified. With regard to the drug product, the degradation product (b) (4) in the injection has a specification limit of (b) (4) at batch release and (b) (4) during stability testing, which was deemed acceptable for NDA approval, following consultation with the Pharmacology/Toxicology Review Team. The specification limits for heavy metals (b) (4) were determined to be (b) (4) based on USP standards. Batch analysis of 17 batches indicated the following heavy metal levels: (b) (4). In turn, the Applicant committed to set alert and action limits for manufacturing to complement these specifications and to reevaluate these limits upon obtaining a sufficient number of production batches (b) (4). This approach was deemed acceptable by the CMC
L104: Review Team, following consultation with the Pharmacology/Toxicology Review Team. In addition, structural alerts were identified for three leachables: (b) (4). Although these leachables are not detected in the drug product, given that their detection limits range from (b) (4) to (b) (4).
L105: However, upon consultation with the Pharmacology/Toxicology Review Team, none of these leachables were considered genotoxic, based on AMES testing or quantitative structure-activity relationships. Thus, overall, the CMC Review Team recommends approval of the drug product.
L106: Given that icatibant is supplied in pre-filled syringes, a Quality Microbiology Review to assess the adequacy of the sterilization and (b) (4) manufacturing processes for the drug-device product was completed (see Quality Microbiology Review by Dr. Vinayak Pawar, dated June 3, 2011). This review indicated that these procedures were acceptable and recommended approval of the drug product. In addition, the Office of Compliance determined that all the pertinent facilities in the Establishment Evaluation System are acceptable, although a final determination regarding the drug product manufacturing facility is pending at the time of this Clinical Review.
L107: A consultation from the Center for Devices and Radiologic Health (CDRH) raised a concern with the proposed pre-filled syringe device, regarding the potential for inappropriate disconnection of the (b) (4) needle hub from the (b) (4) pre-filled syringe after or during assembly by the user, due to potential incompatibility between the needle hub and syringe tip. This potential problem was identified in several postmarketing reports of device failures with the same (b) (4) syringe used for the delivery of other drugs (adenosine, risperidone) via both needle-containing and needleless (IV connector) delivery systems that utilized luer lock devices. In turn, CDRH has recommended that the Applicant address any potential design flaws by conducting device performance testing (per ISO (b) (4)).
L108: (b) (4) prior to NDA approval, as well as completing a formal simulated use/human factors study to assess the functionality and usability of the proposed Firazyr syringe-needle configuration. Of note, limit syringe/needle leak test data were submitted with the NDA, which will be reviewed for ISO (b) (4) compliance. The CMC Review Team has indicated with it is acceptable to provide ISO (b) (4) testing as a post-approval commitment, given that the Clinical Review Team did not identify immediate concerns regarding syringe-needle device failures from the available clinical trial and postmarketing data.
L109: In turn, in response to a request for information from the Division dated July 8, 2011, the Applicant communicated to the Division on July 13, 2011, that no premarketing or postmarketing adverse event reports of device failures had been submitted for icatibant. The Applicant estimated that inclusive of clinical trials and postmarket use, the number
L110: of syringe-needle units used to date was 1,189 in the U.S. and 14,788 outside of the U.S. These totals included both icatibant injections in clinical trials and commercial use, as well as placebo injections delivered in clinical trials, which utilized the same syringe-needle devices and were delivered in identical fashion as icatibant, either by healthcare providers or through self-injection by patients. There have been two postmarketing reports associated with syringe complaints: one report of icatibant leakage from the capped syringe when pressure was applied to the pre-filled syringe and a second report of syringe leakage in its original packaging, which the investigator felt was due to the luer lock breaking off from the syringe during transport. Neither of these syringe complaints related to detachment of the needle hub from the syringe tip during or after assembly by the user. With regard specifically to self-administration, the Applicant reported 92 self-injections in the single clinical trial that evaluated icatibant self-administration (FAST-4), along with 96 placebo self-injections with the same syringe-needle configuration, which were done for training purposes in this same trial. Although there were five AE reports in a total of four icatibant-recipients that were categorized under the MedDRA System Organ Class of “General disorders and administration site conditions,” these AEs all related to local injection site reactions and were not associated with device failures. The Applicant cannot quantify the precise number of icatibant self-injections in the postmarketing setting from the E.U., but an ongoing Icatibant Outcome Survey involving eight E.U. countries indicated that as of April 21, 2011, out of 378 icatibant-treated attacks, 47.6% were reported as treated with self-injected icatibant. Thus, overall, from the safety data reported (and in the absence of formal human factor studies), syringe-needle device failures do not appear to be a significant safety issue for the icatibant device configuration.
L111: 4.2 Clinical Microbiology
L112: Icatibant is not an antimicrobial agent and is not intended to impact microbial growth. Thus, a Clinical Microbiology Review will not be completed.
L113: 4.3 Preclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L114: The Pharmacology/Toxicology Review Team has completed review of the pivotal 39-week toxicology study in dogs and an impurity qualification study that directly responded to nonclinical requirements listed in the Not Approvable action letter for the original NDA submission. A 26-week pivotal rat study that was submitted to IND 68,214 was reviewed prior to this Complete Response submission, for which a NOAEL was not identified due to reproductive tract toxicities. In the pivotal 39-week dog study, end-organ toxicity was noted in the reproductive tract of male and female animals, including testicular, ovarian, prostatic, and uterine atrophy and decreased sperm counts. Systemic injection reactions were noted in high-dose animals, and decreases in heart rate were noted following icatibant treatment. The histopathologic effects of icatibant on
L115: both the reproductive tract and heart appear related to the drug's primary mechanism of action of bradykinin type 2 receptor inhibition. In the 26-week pivotal rat study, end-organ toxicity was noted in the reproductive tract of male and female animals, including testicular, ovarian, prostatic, and uterine atrophy (as in the 39-week dog study), delayed parturition leading to fetal death, as well as additional toxicities, including skin ulceration, focal fibrosis at injection sites, macrovesicular vacuolation of brown fat (in the aorta and connective tissue), adrenal gland hypertrophy, decreased hematopoiesis in the bone marrow, lymphocyte depletion in the thymus, and agonal congestion in the lungs. Of note, reproductive toxicities were observed with daily administration of icatibant, which does not reflect the intended clinical dosing of icatibant for human use. In the pivotal dog toxicology study, adverse histopathologic findings were not observed in dogs that were dosed with icatibant biweekly for nine months.
L116: The Pharmacology/Toxicology Review Team also evaluated the proposed impurity specifications, which the Applicant plans to control at the level of the drug substance, rather than the drug product, in order to ensure that these are process impurities, rather than drug degradants. Proposed limit specifications for metals (b) (4) were also evaluated. In addition, the Review Team requested from the Applicant additional historical control data and histopathology results for low and mid-dose rats in the (b) (4) impurity qualification study, given findings of myocarditis and kidney sclerosis seen in high-dose rats; a signed final report of these data had not yet been received from the Applicant at the time of this Clinical Review. Discussions between the Pharmacology/Toxicology Review Team and the CMC Review Team regarding impurities, degradants, leachables, and heavy metal content are all discussed in Section 4.1 Chemistry Manufacturing and Controls. Overall, given the relatively low dosing frequency expected with icatibant in the clinical setting and its dose-dependent nonclinical toxicity profile, as well as the favorable benefit-risk ratio demonstrated in the icatibant clinical program, the Pharmacology/Toxicology Review Team has recommended approval of the drug product with a nonclinical postmarketing requirement for the submission of long-term carcinogenicity studies in rat and mouse.
L117: 4.4 Clinical Pharmacology
L118: Icatibant is rapidly absorbed after subcutaneous injection, with peak plasma concentrations noted at 30 minutes post-dosing and rapid elimination (t_{1/2} = 0.6 – 1.5 hours) that is predominantly non-renal. Icatibant exhibits low serum protein binding (~44%) and is extensively metabolized into two main inactive metabolites, M1 and M2. In this Complete Response submission, the Applicant has submitted two new Phase 1 clinical pharmacology trials (HGT-FIR-061, a thorough QT prolongation study that was reviewed by the Agency's QT-Interdisciplinary Review Team for safety purposes, and HGT-FIR-065, a pharmacokinetic trial in healthy adults), a population pharmacokinetic analysis encompassing all pharmacokinetic data, and a pooled data analysis for HGT-FIR-061 and HGT-FIR-065 evaluating the effects of age and gender on icatibant
L119: pharmacokinetics. Both age and gender effects were noted with respect to rates of icatibant clearance in clinical pharmacology trials of healthy subjects. While elderly subjects (&gt; 65 years) had similar Cmax values as younger adults (18-45 years), AUC values were increased by approximately 66-116%. This resulted predominantly from reductions in icatibant clearance by 40-60% in elderly subjects compared to younger adults, which correlated to a longer half-lives in elderly subjects (t_{1/2} in males = 1.5 hours; t_{1/2} in females = 1.1 hours). Decreased rates of icatibant clearance were also noted in females compared to males, with 2.3-fold increases in both Cmax and AUC noted for young females. In elderly females, Cmax was similarly increased 2.3-fold compared to elderly males, whereas AUC was increased 1.8-fold. Thus, this interaction between age and gender effects resulted in a 2.5-fold greater Cmax value and a 4-fold greater AUC value in elderly females compared to younger males. Increases in clearance rates were also noted as body weight increased. Thus, when clearance data for age-matched females and males were corrected for body weight (which is generally lower in females compared to males), clearance rates by gender were more comparable. Whether these subgroup effects on icatibant clearance had any demonstrable impact on clinical efficacy is discussed in Section 6.1.7 Subpopulations, while potential effects on safety are discussed in Section 7.5.3 Drug-Demographic Interactions.
L120: Overall, the Clinical Pharmacology Review Team has recommended approval of this drug product, as the Applicant has provided adequate explanation of the age and gender-related differences in icatibant pharmacokinetics, as well as justification for proposing the same dose of icatibant, regardless of age and gender. This determination was based on post hoc PK analyses from the two newly conducted PK trials (HGT-FIR-061 and HGT-FIR-065) and a population PK analysis of all available PK data focusing on the effects of age and gender. In addition, the Clinical Pharmacology Review Team feels that although a formal dose-ranging trial was not completed, dose selection was adequately characterized by the Applicant, based on several clinical pharmacology trials, pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic modeling data, a human intravenous bradykinin challenge model, and the Phase 3 clinical evaluation of the selected 30 mg SC dose of icatibant.
L121: 4.4.1 Mechanism of Action
L122: HAE is a rare inheritable autosomal dominant disease resulting from a quantitative (Type I HAE) or qualitative functional (Type II HAE) deficiency in complement 1 (C1) esterase inhibitor (C1-INH) due to mutations in the C1-INH gene. A third form of HAE (Type III) with an indistinguishable clinical phenotype from Type I and Type II HAE also exists due to an unknown genetic defect that is unrelated to C1-INH deficiency. C1-INH is a serine protease inhibitor that irreversibly complexes with its target serine proteases, preventing C1 complement autoactivation and inactivating several coagulation factors, including XIIa (activated Hageman factor), XIa, XIIa, as well as plasma kallikrein. In HAE, the increased activity of plasma kallikrein results in dysregulated release of
L123: bradykinin from high molecular weight kininogen. It is this accumulation of bradykinin during acute HAE attacks that is thought to result in the predominant clinical signs and symptoms of HAE, resulting from increased vascular permeability, vasodilation, and visceral smooth muscle contraction. In turn, the clinical presentation of HAE is characterized by recurrent spontaneous attacks of varying frequency, involving cutaneous, abdominal, and/or laryngeal edema of variable duration, which may be disfiguring, extremely painful, and life-threatening (particularly related to airway compromise and hypotension). Icatibant is a synthetic decapeptide, which is similar in structure to bradykinin and acts as a direct antagonist of the bradykinin type 2 receptor, thereby counteracting the pathophysiologic effects of uncontrolled bradykinin production that result from C1-INH deficiency during acute HAE attacks.
L124: 4.4.2 Pharmacodynamics
L125: Multiple trials were conducted to assess pharmacodynamic biomarkers in the original NDA submission in support of dose-selection. These characterized the dose-dependent inhibition of bradykinin effects by icatibant on blood pressure and heart rate in healthy volunteers (JE049-1001, JE049-1102, JE049-1103). In brief, an exogenous intravenous bradykinin challenge model was used as a pharmacological tool in healthy subjects to investigate the dose range and regimen for later clinical trials of icatibant. Intravenous dosing was utilized in these studies in order to minimize variability in the exposure-time profile of the drug. These studies were designed to determine the dose of icatibant that would provide near complete inhibition of exogenously administered bradykinin in healthy adults, given the hypothesis that this would correlate with improvement in bradykinin-mediated symptoms of acute HAE attacks. Thus, the dose of icatibant selected for exploration in Phase 3 trials was largely determined mechanistically, based on the plasma levels of bradykinin expected to occur in acute HAE attacks and the plasma levels of icatibant needed to completely antagonize these bradykinin effects, as assessed in the human bradykinin challenge model.
L126: A Phase 2 proof-of-concept trial (JE049-2101), which was reviewed with the original NDA submission (see the Clinical Review by Dr. Susan Limb, dated February 29, 2008, and the Clinical Pharmacology Review by Dr. Partha Roy, dated March 21, 2008), suggested the comparability of the pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic profile of icatibant in HAE patients versus healthy volunteers at equivalent doses (assessed in separate clinical pharmacology trials). In addition, this trial generated limited dose-ranging information based on clinical efficacy and safety data for single doses of icatibant at a limited number of intravenous and subcutaneous dose levels, as acute treatment for moderate to severe cutaneous or abdominal HAE attacks, including the 30 mg subcutaneous dose, which was the dose ultimately studied in Phase 3 clinical trials. Based on the results of this trial, the time to self-reported symptom relief appeared relatively rapid with subcutaneous dosing of icatibant--approximately 30 minutes to the onset of symptom relief. In addition, efficacy results were similar for the 30 mg subcutaneous dose and the highest intravenous dose tested of 0.8 mg/kg, while the
L127: higher 45 mg subcutaneous dose did not appear to offer any added benefit over the 30 mg dose. In turn, the 30 mg subcutaneous dose was carried forward into Phase 3 clinical trials.
L128: Newly presented in this Complete Response submission are pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic modeling data (JE049-5108) in healthy subjects and HAE patients, which the Applicant feels support Phase 3 dose-selection. A review of these data is provided in the Clinical Pharmacology NDA Review. In addition, a Clinical Study Report for an additional Phase 1 pharmacodynamic trial (HGT-FIR-061) examining the effects of icatibant on QT prolongation that was conducted after the original NDA submission was also included with this Complete Response. This trial was conducted voluntarily by the Applicant, after concerns over the potential for icatibant to affect QT interval prolongation were raised in Study JE049-1103, a Phase 1 pharmacokinetic and safety trial conducted in healthy adults. A review of the 12-lead ECG and Holter monitoring data from this trial by the Agency's QT-Interdisciplinary Review Team identified several instances of ST/T wave changes and QT prolongation (report submitted to IND 68,214 and dated January 30, 2007). Given this evidence of QT prolongation by icatibant, the Agency concluded that although a thorough QT study was not warranted, these findings would have to be addressed in the product labeling. However, the Applicant chose to evaluate this effect further in this thorough QT prolongation study, which is briefly summarized below.
L129: Protocol Title: HGT-FIR-061—The Effect of Icatibant on QT and QTc Intervals: A Randomized, Placebo Controlled, Active Comparator, Crossover Study in Healthy Adult Volunteers
L130: Enrollment Initiation Date: February 16, 2010
L131: Enrollment Completion Date: August 12, 2010
L132: Design and Methods: HGT-FIR-061 was a Phase 1 randomized, placebo- and active-controlled, open-label crossover trial whose primary objective was to assess whether a single 30 mg or 90 mg SC dose of icatibant caused QT interval prolongation in healthy male and female adults. Subjects were randomized to variable sequences of four treatment periods, each consisting of a 24-hour ECG assessment prior to a 24-hour treatment period in which subjects received either placebo, a single 3 mL SC injection of 30 mg icatibant, three subcutaneous injections of 30 mg icatibant for a total dose of 90 mg, or a single oral dose of moxifloxacin 400 mg. This was then followed by a minimum 2-day wash-out period, prior to advancement to the next subsequent treatment period. A total of 82 subjects were enrolled in the trial and received at least one dose of study drug, with 71 subjects completing all four treatment periods. ECG acquisition and interpretation was done by blinded investigators who were unaware of subjects' treatment assignment and treatment period sequence. The primary analysis consisted of determining the largest upper 1-sided 95% confidence limit for QTcI for icatibant 30
L133: mg and 90 mg relative to placebo at the same nominal time, using a linear mixed model with fixed effects for baseline QTc, treatment, period, sequence, schedule time, and treatment by time interaction as a repeated measure within subject by treatment. A determination of no effect on QT prolongation was concluded if the maximum of this upper confidence limit fell below 10 msec.
L134: Summary Results
L135: No subject had a maximum QTcI, QTcF, or QTcB value of greater than 480 msec or a change from baseline of greater than 60 msec following receipt of icatibant at either dose. All placebo-corrected change from baseline mean values for QTcI were below 1.7 msec for both icatibant 30 mg and icatibant 90 mg, with the upper limit of all 2-sided 90% confidence intervals below 10 msec at all time points. Results based on QTcF and QTcB were similar to those for the primary QTcI analysis. In addition, results for the moxifloxacin positive control indicated that the lower limit of the 2-sided 99% confidence interval for placebo-corrected change from baseline mean values for QTcI was greater than 5 msec from 1-3 hours post-dose. The Applicant states that these control findings were consistent with typical values established for moxifloxacin when used as a positive control agent in QT prolongation studies, thereby establishing assay sensitivity in this study. The Applicant also states that there was no evidence of icatibant-mediated QT prolongation in either males or females at the proposed treatment doses (30-90 mg SC). A review of this trial by the Agency's QT-Interdisciplinary Review Team (QT-IRT) concluded that the effects of icatibant on the length of the QT interval were below the threshold level of regulatory concern (see QT-IRT Consult Review by Moh-Jee Ng, dated May 26, 2011). A description of the trial's findings was recommended for inclusion in the proposed icatibant product label. No deaths, SAEs, or severe AEs reported during the study, and most AEs were mild in severity and resolved without specific intervention. No clinically significant changes in laboratory tests, vital sign assessments, or ECG findings were reported in icatibant-recipients.
L136: 4.4.3 Pharmacokinetics
L137: A review of data establishing the pharmacokinetic profile of icatibant is discussed in the Clinical Pharmacology NDA Review. These data were generated from Phase 1 clinical pharmacology trials and a Phase 2 proof-of-concept trial with intravenous and subcutaneous dose-ranging information that were submitted with the original NDA. In this Complete Response submission, both population pharmacokinetic data and pharmacokinetic/ pharmacodynamic modeling data have been included, as discussed above in Section 4.4 Clinical Pharmacology and Section 4.4.2 Pharmacodynamics. In addition, the Applicant has submitted a Clinical Study Report for a Phase 1 pharmacokinetic trial (HGT-FIR-065) in healthy adults that was conducted since the original NDA submission, which is summarized briefly below. Further details may be found in the Clinical Pharmacology NDA Review.
L138: Protocol Title: HGT-FIR-065—An Open-label, Phase I, Single-center Study to Determine the Pharmacokinetics and Safety of Multiple Doses of Subcutaneously Administered Icatibant in Healthy Adult Male and Female Subjects
L139: Enrollment Initiation Date: March 30, 2010
L140: Enrollment Completion Date: May 16, 2010
L141: Design and Methods: HGT-FIR-065 was a Phase 1 open-label, single-center trial to assess the pharmacokinetics, tolerability, and safety of multiple doses of icatibant 30 mg SC in healthy male and female adults (18-45 years, BMI = 18-30 kg/m2 inclusive, weight &gt; 50 kg). Subjects received three individual doses of icatibant 30 mg/3 mL SC, each separated six hours apart, with pharmacokinetic (plasma concentrations of icatibant, M1 metabolite, and M2 metabolite, in order to determine Cmax, tmax, t1/2, AUClast, AUC∞) and safety parameters (AEs, injection site reactions, clinical laboratory tests [hematology, serum chemistry, urinalysis], vital signs, physical examination, 12-lead ECG) assessed throughout the 2-day post-dosing inpatient observation period. A total of 21 subjects received all planned doses of study drug and completed all planned assessments with no major protocol violations.
L142: Summary Results
L143: The overall mean values for Cmax and AUC0-6hr were comparable for all three doses of icatibant, suggesting no drug accumulation. Mean t1/2 was estimated to range from 1.06-1.16 hrs following subcutaneous administration and did not vary across doses. Of note, both the M1 and M2 metabolites showed approximately a 25% increase between the first two icatibant doses but were comparable between the second and third, with an estimated t1/2 of 3.19-3.53 hrs for M1 and 3.47-3.67 hrs for M2, achieving steady state by 12 hours post-dosing. Thus, although icatibant was not shown to accumulate, levels of M1 and M2 accumulated between the first and second doses, as shown in Figure 1, which was constructed by the Applicant.
L144: Figure 1: Mean plasma concentrations of icatibant, M1, and M2 after icatibant 30 mg SC every 6 hours for 3 doses in healthy adults
L145: The figure is a line graph showing the mean plasma concentrations of icatibant and its metabolites M1 and M2 over an 18-hour period. The y-axis represents concentration in ng/mL, ranging from 0 to 1200. The x-axis represents time in hours, ranging from 0 to 18. Icatibant (blue line with circles) shows three distinct peaks corresponding to the three doses administered every 6 hours. The first peak occurs at approximately 1 hour with a concentration of about 900 ng/mL. The second peak occurs at approximately 7 hours with a concentration of about 1100 ng/mL. The third peak occurs at approximately 13 hours with a concentration of about 1000 ng/mL. Metabolite M1 (red line with triangles) and Metabolite M2 (green line with squares) show lower, broader peaks. Their concentrations generally peak around 2 hours and 8 hours, with values around 300-350 ng/mL. L147: No new safety signals emerged from this study, with no deaths, SAEs, or AE-related discontinuations.
L148: 5 Sources of Clinical Data
L149: 5.1 Tables of Studies/Clinical Trials
L150: In addition to the two Phase 3 clinical efficacy trials (FAST-1 and FAST-2) that were submitted and reviewed with the original NDA submission, this Complete Response includes one additional Phase 3 placebo-controlled clinical efficacy trial (HGT-FIR-054 or FAST-3), which is reviewed herein within the context of FAST-1 and FAST-2. Both clinical efficacy and safety data were obtained from all three of these pivotal Phase 3 trials. Comparative efficacy analyses from these trials are reviewed in Section 6 Review of Efficacy, while pooled safety data are discussed in Section 7 Review of Safety. The Applicant also conducted an additional Phase 3 trial to evaluate the safety of icatibant for self-administration in non-healthcare settings by non-healthcare workers, as shown in Table 3 (the first two pivotal Phase 3 efficacy trials included in the original NDA submission are listed in italics).
