L669: Despite nearly universal occurrence following icatibant-administration, these local injection site reactions had largely abated by 48 hours post-dosing, as determined by a review of individual patient line listing data. Thus, rates of investigator-assessed injection site reactions made at the 48-hour post-dosing follow-up clinic visit were far lower than self-reported assessments recorded during the self-administration phase (as determined from a review of individual patient line data in Listing 12.2.7.2.3 and Listing 12.2.7.2.4 from the Clinical Study Report of FAST-4: erythema = 8 subjects (14.3%), swelling = 4 subjects (7.1%), burning sensation = 4 subjects (7.1%), itching/pruritus = 4 subjects (7.1%), warm sensation = 5 subjects (8.9%), skin pain = 2 subjects (4.2%). During the naïve-treatment phase, investigators determined that injection site reactions were resolved by the 48-hour follow-up clinic visit in all subjects except for erythema, burning sensation, and warm sensation, which were each observed in a single subject (12.5%). These data were all consistent with local tolerability data generated from the pivotal Phase 3 efficacy trials and did not indicate an increase rate of local injection site reactions associated with icatibant self-administration.
L670: Adverse Events by Subgroups: Similar numbers of male (5 of 18 = 33.3%) and female (12 of 38 = 31.6%) subjects experienced AEs during the self-administration phase of FAST-4, whereas one male (5.6%) and three female (7.9%) subjects experienced a severe AE. When stratified by rescue medication use, all of the 11 subjects who used rescue medications experienced an AE (nine with worsening or recurrence of HAE, two with abdominal pain, and one each with gastrointestinal pain, headache, and dizziness). In contrast, only 7 of 45 subjects (15.6%) who did not take rescue medications experienced an AE (4 subjects with worsening or recurrence of HAE, and one each with headache, migraine, rhinitis, back pain, and skin swelling).
L671: These differences in AE rates were not surprising, as subjects who used rescue medications could be expected to have HAE symptoms of greater severity than those who did not require additional medications.
L672: Secondary Efficacy Results
L673: The efficacy of self-administered icatibant was evaluated as a secondary study endpoint in FAST-4. Efficacy analyses from this trial were based primarily on the self-administration phase involving all 56 subjects in the trial. Data from the subset of eight icatibant-naïve patients in this trial who participated in the naïve-treatment phase in which icatibant was initially administered by a healthcare worker were also analyzed. Of note, given that FAST-4 was an uncontrolled trial, a statistical comparison of self-administered icatibant to healthcare worker-administered icatibant cannot be made, although data for the eight subjects involved in the naïve-treatment phase of this trial are provided for trend comparisons.
L674: Time to Onset of Symptom Relief: Based on symptom ratings during the self-administration phase of FAST-4, the median time to symptom relief based on a 50% reduction in composite VAS-3 score was 2.6 hrs for the entire sample (95% CI: 2.0-4.0 hrs), 2.0 hrs (95% CI: 1.8-4.0 hrs) for the 44 evaluable icatibant-experienced subjects (two icatibant-experienced subjects without documented symptom relief were censored from the analysis), and 4.0 hrs (95% CI: 2.0-6.2 hrs) for the eight evaluable icatibant-naïve subjects. Median time to primary symptom relief (defined as in FAST-1, FAST-2, and FAST-3) was noted to be even shorter: 2.0 hrs for the entire sample, 2.0 hrs (95% CI: 1.3-2.0 hrs) for 43 evaluable icatibant-experienced subjects, and 2.1 hrs (95% CI: 2.0-4.0 hrs) for seven evaluable icatibant-naïve patients. Consistent with the efficacy results generated during the self-administration phase, results from the naïve-treatment phase in which eight icatibant-naïve patients were administered icatibant by healthcare workers, the median time to onset of symptom relief based on VAS-3 was 2.0 hrs (95% CI: 1.9-5.9 hrs) in seven evaluable subjects, and the median time to onset of primary symptom relief was 4.2 hrs (95% CI: 2.0-24.0 hrs) in five evaluable subjects. These findings were generally consistent with response times observed in the pivotal Phase 3 efficacy trials.
L675: Change in Mean VAS Symptom Scores: An examination of mean VAS scores over time for the composite symptom measure (VAS-3) and individual symptom domains during both the self-administration and naïve treatment phases of FAST-4 revealed similar patterns for both icatibant-naïve and icatibant-experienced subjects with regard to the timing of symptom relief, consisting of a rapid fall in symptom scores over the first 4 hours post-dosing, followed by a slower but persistent decline over the next 48 hours. A representative graph of these data generated by the Applicant is shown in Figure 4.

L680: Self-described Clinical Status at 48 hours Post-dosing: The majority of HAE attacks were considered resolved by 48 hours post-dosing, as reported in 41 of 48 (85.4%) icatibant-experienced subjects and 7 of 8 (87.5%) icatibant-naïve subjects. Of note, a slightly lower percentage of icatibant-experienced subjects considered their HAE attack to be satisfactorily resolved by 48 hours (37 of 48 subjects = 77.1%). During the naïve-treatment phase, only 5 of 8 subjects (62.5%) reported their attack as resolved at this time point, although a higher percentage (7 of 8 subjects = 87.5%) considered their attack to be satisfactorily resolved. Of note, although subjects were instructed to return to the clinic site for persistent symptoms within 6-48 hours of icatibant self-administration for additional healthcare worker-administered doses if needed, only one subject returned to receive an additional dose of icatibant. Other subjects preferred to self-manage any persistent symptoms independently.
L681: Convenience of Icatibant Self-Administration: An 8-item questionnaire designed to assess the convenience of icatibant self-administration was completed by all 56 subjects in the study; of note, the psychometric properties of this instrument (e.g., validity, reliability) are not described by the Applicant. While all subjects reported that the training materials were sufficient or very sufficient to explain self-administration, 11 subjects (19.7%) felt the idea of self-injection was stressful or very stressful. Interestingly, however, 53 subjects (94.6%) indicated that self-administration of icatibant was preferable or very preferable to clinic-based administration. Nearly the entire
L682: sample (55 subjects = 98.3%) considered assembly and handling of the pre-filled syringe to be easy or very easy, with 49 subjects (87.5%) stating it was easy or very easy to self-inject icatibant and 46 subjects (82.1%) indicating it was convenient or very convenient to carry the medication for self-injection. Finally, 49 subjects (87.5%) reported that they were satisfied or very satisfied with how icatibant relieved their symptoms compared to their usual treatment. In general, the results of this unvalidated questionnaire support the convenience of self-administered icatibant.
L683: Investigator Global Assessment of Symptoms: Investigator-based symptom assessments at the 48-hour post-icatibant follow-up clinic visit were consistent and slightly lower in severity ratings, compared to self-assessments by patients. Complete resolution of skin edema was noted by investigators in 49 subjects (87.5%), while 48 subjects (85.7%) were considered to have no abdominal symptoms at 48 hours post-dosing. Only two subjects were felt to have persistent severe abdominal symptoms at 48 hours post-dosing. Of those subjects judged by investigators to have persistent mild to moderate symptoms, approximately half of these patients felt their attacks had resolved or improved to a satisfactory degree by this time point. During the naive-treatment phase, only one subject was felt to have persistent symptoms at the 48-hour follow-up (moderate skin edema).
L684: Although no symptoms of laryngeal edema were recorded at 48 hours post-dosing, only a single subject reported laryngeal symptoms during their self-treated attack. This subject was a 59 year-old man with Type II HAE who was instructed to come to the clinic for physician-directed treatment after the initial reporting of his laryngeal symptoms, but he refused due to his distance from the clinic site. Although he agreed to seek medical care at a closer facility following icatibant self-administration, he did not do this, noting that his symptoms began to improve at 4 hours following this single icatibant dose and were completely resolved by 14 hours post-dose, with no symptom recurrence noted at the 48-hour time point.
L685: Sensitivity Analyses for Main Efficacy Measures: Sensitivity analyses were conducted to assess the potential effects of rescue medication use on the main efficacy measures in FAST-4. Rescue medication use was reported in 11 subjects (19.6%), consisting primarily of analgesics (e.g., ibuprofen, acetaminophen, diclofenac), gastrointestinal medications (e.g., metoclopramide, hyoscine, buscopan), and other treatments for HAE (e.g., C1-INH replacement, tranexamic acid, commercial Firazyr). Rescue medication use did not appear to impact self-reported time to onset of symptom relief based on VAS-3 score (2.0 hrs, 95% CI: 1.8-10.0 hrs) or primary symptom score (3.5 hrs, 95% CI: 2.0-6.3 hrs). Subjects who reported no rescue medication use (45 subjects = 80.4%) had a median time to onset of symptom relief based on VAS-3 of 3.4 hrs (95% CI: 2.0-4.0 hrs), with 97.6% reporting symptom relief, and 2.0 hrs (95% CI: 1.3-2.0) based on primary symptom score, with 97.5% reporting symptom relief. In addition, when subjects who received rescue medications were censored from the efficacy analysis at the time of their rescue medication use, the median time to primary
L686: symptom relief remained similar to that of the main efficacy analysis: 2.0 hrs, with 92% of subjects reporting symptom relief. These censored analyses were similar for time frames based on VAS-3: 2.3 hrs, with 90.4% reporting symptom relief.
L687: Sensitivity analyses were also performed to evaluate the potential impact on the primary efficacy endpoints of major protocol violations, which may have led to subject misclassification. The exclusion of three subjects who failed to return used icatibant syringes to the study site (as a confirmation of medication compliance) from the analyses of data generated during the self-administration phase of the trial did not significantly impact time to onset of symptom relief or summary VAS symptom scores.
L688: Gender Subgroup Analyses: Time to onset of symptom relief based on VAS-3 was the same for icatibant-experienced male subjects (2.0 hrs, 95% CI: 1.1-4.0) and female subjects (2.0 hrs, 95% CI: 1.8-4.0), in contrast to the gender subgroup findings noted in the pivotal Phase 3 efficacy trials. Time to onset of symptom relief was longer in the icatibant-naïve group for both genders, particularly for males (male subjects: 5.1 hrs, 95% CI: 4.0-6.2 hrs; female subjects: 3.5 hrs, 95% CI: 2.0-6.0 hrs). This contributed to longer lag times noted in the overall samples for male (4.0 hrs, 95% CI: 1.1-6.0 hrs) versus female (2.3 hrs, 95% CI: 2.0-4.0 hrs) subjects, which was consistent with overall findings from the other Phase 3 trials.
L689: 7.4.6 Immunogenicity
L690: Immunogenicity assessments were conducted across the three pivotal Phase 3 efficacy trials. Serum samples from FAST-1, FAST-2, and FAST-3 were analyzed by both screening and confirmatory ELISA for icatibant-specific IgG. In addition, samples from FAST-3 were additionally tested for icatibant-specific IgE. Samples drawn both pretreatment and post-treatment (at Day 14 post-dosing for the initial HAE attack) were tested and considered positive if evidence of either drug-specific IgG or IgE was detected by both screening and confirmatory ELISA.
L691: Across all three trials, a total of 121 icatibant-recipients (with 192 pretreatment and 214 post-treatment samples) and 108 placebo or tranexamic acid-recipients (with 163 pretreatment and 189 post-treatment samples) were evaluated. As expected, no placebo or tranexamic acid-recipients had positive icatibant-specific antibody results at either time point. In contrast, two icatibant-recipients were noted to be antibody positive at baseline, although only one of these subjects was antibody positive at post-treatment. However, this subject did not have a detectable increase in drug-specific antibody titer post-dosing, and follow-up immunogenicity samples over the next 5 months were all negative. In addition, the Applicant states that the icatibant treatment response was maintained in this patient during subsequent HAE attacks that occurred after the detection of icatibant-specific antibodies. Thus, the Applicant suggests these positive findings were related to nonspecific background noise for both subjects, which appears to be a reasonable hypothesis.
L692: During the open-label extension phases of the three trials, two additional subjects from FAST-2 had two sporadic antibody (icatibant-specific IgG)-positive post-treatment samples. However, in both cases, these titers were transient, with documentation of multiple negative follow-up samples, and did not exceed the minimum required dilution of the assay. The Applicant also indicated that icatibant treatment-responses were maintained in both of these patients with subsequent HAE attacks that occurred following the detection of icatibant-specific antibodies.
L693: Of note, no anaphylactic or otherwise significant hypersensitivity reactions were noted for any subject during the controlled or open-label extension phases of the three pivotal Phase 3 trials. Therefore, icatibant appears to have a low likelihood of immunogenicity.
L694: Results Specific for FAST-3
L695: Immunogenicity samples were uniformly negative for all subjects tested in FAST-3, regardless of randomized or open-label treatment assignment during both the blinded treatment phase and open-label extension phase.
L696: 7.5 Other Safety Explorations
L697: 7.5.1 Dose Dependency for Adverse Events
L698: Dose explorations were limited in the icatibant development program to Phase 1 and 2, with only the 30 mg SC dose evaluated in the randomized treatment portion of the pivotal Phase 3 safety and efficacy trials. Although during the open-label extension phases of all three pivotal efficacy trials, up to three 30 mg icatibant injections were allowed within a 24-hour period (each separated by at least 6 hours) for persistent signs and symptoms of HAE, relatively few patients were treated with more than one dose of icatibant for any given HAE attack in the Phase 3 program. Thus, it is difficult to evaluate AE data for dose dependency. However, from the limited safety data available, no dose-dependent patterns emerged.
L699: 7.5.2 Time Dependency for Adverse Events
L700: From the safety data presented, rates for the majority of AEs do not appear to be time-dependent. However, local injection site reactions (regardless of AE designation) appeared to occur immediately or soon after subcutaneous icatibant dosing.
L701: 7.5.3 Drug-Demographic Interactions
L702: Incidences of total and severe AEs recorded during the 14-day post-dosing observation period for initial HAE attacks are shown within key demographic strata in Table 48.
L703: When safety data were stratified by age group or gender, AE rates in icatibant-recipients were similar across all strata and were noted to be lower than in placebo-recipients for each subgroup. In contrast, a higher rate of AEs were observed in subjects classified as white versus non-white, although the number of non-white subjects was limited in the pooled safety population (icatibant = 6 subjects, tranexamic acid = 0 subjects, placebo = 7 subjects). Similarly, when AE rates were stratified by weight groups, there appeared to be an increase in AE rates as weight categories increased. However, in all subgroups defined by race or weight, AE rates within each stratum were similar to or lower than rates in the corresponding subgroup of placebo-recipients. Therefore, overall icatibant does not appear to induce disproportionate rates of AEs in specific demographic subgroups at rates greater than observed with placebo treatment.

L707: 7.5.4 Drug-Disease Interactions
L708: The Applicant seeks an indication for the treatment of acute HAE attacks in adults, without specific reference to severity or edema location, which is generally supported by results of the Phase 3 clinical program. Across the three pivotal Phase 3 efficacy trials, HAE attacks ranging from mild to severe were treated with blinded study treatment, with moderate to severe cutaneous and/or abdominal HAE attacks randomly in all three trials and mild to moderate laryngeal HAE attacks randomly treated in FAST-3 only. Thus, the distribution of blinded treatments by severity of HAE attacks was largely limited to moderate to severe HAE attacks: icatibant (N = 113): mild = 1.8%, moderate = 46.9%, severe = 51.3%; tranexamic acid (N = 38): mild = 0%, moderate = 28.9%, severe = 71.1%; placebo (N = 75): mild = 0%, moderate = 54.7%, severe = 45.3%. Safety data from these clinical trials do not suggest an attack-severity dependency for AEs, with similar or lower AE rates observed in icatibant versus placebo-recipients within each severity grade stratum.
L709: In terms of edema location, a range of cutaneous, abdominal, and laryngeal HAE attacks was treated across the three trials, with the majority being cutaneous and abdominal HAE attacks: icatibant: cutaneous = 56.6%, abdominal = 37.2%, laryngeal = 6.2%; tranexamic acid: cutaneous = 60.5%, abdominal = 39.5%, laryngeal = 0%; placebo: cutaneous = 52.0%, abdominal = 46.7%, laryngeal = 1.3%. An attack-site dependency was not noted for AE data in these trials, other than an expected preponderance of specific System Organ Class AEs associated with the different edema sites, e.g., gastrointestinal AEs noted more frequently in abdominal HAE attacks (7.1%) versus cutaneous attacks (1.6%). Of note, acute AE rates were higher with abdominal HAE attacks (acute = 42.9%; observation period = 45.2%) compared to cutaneous attacks (acute = 18.8%; observation period = 40.6%) in the icatibant group. Although the underlying pathophysiology of a potential association is unclear, this pattern may reflect a drug-disease interaction specific to icatibant, as a preponderance of abdominal attack-associated AEs was not observed in placebo-recipients, for whom AEs were more common with cutaneous attacks (acute = 48.7%; observation period = 66.7%) compared to abdominal attacks (acute = 31.4%; observation period = 45.2%). Overall, however, it is difficult to draw conclusions regarding the clinical significance of differences in AE rates by edema location, given the limited sample size of the Phase 3 program. This is particularly true of laryngeal HAE attacks, which were much lower in number in the icatibant development program, compared to other edema sites.
L710: Icatibant was also studied in patients with mild to moderate hepatic and renal impairment in pharmacokinetic trials. Although a formal renal impairment study was not conducted as part of the clinical program, an earlier trial previously reviewed in the original NDA submission (JE049-2002) studied the pharmacokinetics of intravenously administered icatibant in 10 of 37 subjects with hepatorenal syndrome and glomerular filtration rates below 60 mL/min. Another trial (JE049-2001) evaluated the pharmacokinetics of icatibant in eight subjects with hepatic insufficiency compared to
L711: eight healthy volunteers. Based on the findings from these trials, no significant accumulation of icatibant or its principal metabolites (M1 and M2) was noted in patients with renal or hepatic impairment, and no distinct AE patterns related to renal or hepatic impairment were observed.
L712: 7.5.5 Drug-Drug Interactions
L713: Formal drug-drug interaction studies were not performed in the icatibant Phase 3 clinical program; however, nonclinical in vitro studies did not reveal significant inhibition (e.g., 1A2, 2A6, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, 2E1, 3A4) or induction (e.g., 1A2 or 3A4) of CYP450 enzymes by icatibant. Of note, the Applicant recognizes the theoretical potential for icatibant to attenuate the antihypertensive effects of ACE-inhibitors via antagonism of bradykinin, which is known to accumulate as a result of ACE-inhibitor therapy, thereby leading to vasodilation and antihypertensive effects. Given that ACE-inhibitors are known to induce angioedema in idiosyncratic fashion, however, their use is generally contraindicated in HAE patients. Therefore, this theoretical interaction is unlikely to pose significant risk in clinical practice, given expected prescribing patterns for the two drugs.
L714: 7.6 Additional Safety Evaluations
L715: 7.6.1 Human Carcinogenicity
L716: The Applicant acknowledges a theoretical risk of induction or worsening of malignancies with icatibant, based on the in vitro mitogenic potential of icatibant-mediated bradykinin inhibition observed in nonclinical studies. However, this safety signal has not emerged in the icatibant clinical program or in reports from the medical literature. Moreover, the expected infrequent and intermittent use of icatibant would presumably lessen a risk of human carcinogenesis in most HAE patients. Of note, however, one subject in FAST-2 received a total of 142 doses of icatibant for 142 separate HAE attacks, reflecting the striking variability in the clinical presentation of HAE. Thus, long-term (2-year) carcinogenicity studies in rat and mouse are currently ongoing (mouse carcinogenicity studies were originally halted by the Applicant due to severe injection reactions and were then re-started at lower icatibant doses).
L717: 7.6.2 Human Reproduction and Pregnancy Data
L718: No formal studies of icatibant in pregnancy have been conducted. Active pregnancy and breastfeeding were exclusion criteria in the icatibant Phase 3 program. However, five medically confirmed pregnancies have occurred to date in clinical trial participants, who had received icatibant prior to being withdrawn from their respective trials. Three of these pregnancies resolved without complication or sequelae. One was terminated in
L719: an elective abortion in the eighth week of pregnancy, and follow-up information was not available for the fifth. A sixth pregnancy in an icatibant-recipient was described in a spontaneous report to the Applicant, but follow-up information was unavailable.
L720: Collectively, nonclinical studies of icatibant in rats and dogs demonstrated delayed maturation of reproductive organs, impaired uterine implantation, delayed parturition leading to fetal death, atrophy/degeneration in the uterus, ovary, testis, and prostate, and decreased levels of testosterone (but no clinically significant changes in luteinizing hormone). Studies in rabbits further demonstrated premature births and abortions associated with icatibant exposure. The Pharmacology/Toxicology Review Team noted that these toxicities were associated with daily dosing of test animals, which is much higher than the expected dosing for humans in clinical practice. Of note, adverse histopathological findings were not observed in dogs treated biweekly for nine months in the pivotal dog toxicity study. In addition, a Phase 1 clinical trial (HGT-FIR-062) is currently ongoing to evaluate the effects of icatibant on reproductive hormone levels in 10 male and 10 premenopausal female subjects.
L721: 7.6.3 Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
L722: Icatibant was not evaluated in subjects less than 18 years of age, and the Applicant is not currently seeking an indication for pediatric use in the treatment of acute HAE attacks.
L723: 7.6.4 Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal and Rebound
L724: Nonclinical studies of icatibant indicated that it does not distribute to brain tissue and does not display pharmacologic attributes of drugs prone to overuse, abuse, or withdrawal syndromes. The Applicant states that icatibant has no addictive potential, and intentional overdose is unexpected. In turn, withdrawal phenomena have not been evaluated for icatibant. However, with regard to potential effects of icatibant overdose, one spontaneous postmarketing report from the European Union (where icatibant is currently approved for the treatment of HAE attacks) documented an extreme elevation in hepatic enzymes in a 23 year-old man who received a total of eight icatibant injections (two of which were 60 mg in dose) for a presumed laryngeal HAE attack on a background of multi-organ failure secondary to septic shock. Although this particular patient received an icatibant overdose totaling 300 mg over 5 days, the clinical context of this case suggests the ultimate outcome of death (and possibly the extreme elevation in hepatic enzymes) was related to the underlying condition of septic shock and multi-organ failure, rather than icatibant overdose. This case is discussed in detail in Section 8 Postmarket Experience.
L725: 7.7 Additional Submissions / Safety Issues
L726: On June 30, 2011, the Sponsor submitted a 120-day Safety Update, which included safety data from the ongoing open-label extension phase of FAST-3 and the ongoing icatibant self-administration trial FAST-4. With a data cut-off date of April 18, 2011, this 120-day Safety Update encompasses data from 84 subjects from the ongoing FAST-3 extension phase and 95 subjects enrolled in FAST-4. In addition, newly reported postmarketing AE data are included, which are discussed in Section 8 Postmarket Experience. Overall, the data in this 120-day Safety Update are consistent with the safety profile observed in the original Complete Response submission, and no new safety signals emerged during this reporting period.
L727: FAST-3 Extension Phase Safety Update
L728: Data were summarized for those subjects in the FAST-3 extension phase who had received treatment for up to five HAE attacks from the time of the original database lock (October 1, 2010) through April 18, 2011. This included 11 subjects treated for a first attack, 18 subjects for a second attack, 20 subjects for a third attack, 18 subjects for a fourth attack, and 13 subjects for a fifth attack. Narratives for all SAEs were provided. The demographic and baseline characteristics of the FAST-3 Safety Update population were similar to those of the safety population reported in the original submission (e.g., female predominance, nearly all White). In addition, similar to what was reported in the original FAST-3 extension phase safety analysis, 92.5% (74 subjects) of subjects treated during this 120-day reporting period received a single injection of icatibant for any given HAE attack, with only one subject receiving three injections for a single, prolonged HAE attack. AEs were again categorized according to whether they occurred during the acute observation period (within 24 hours of icatibant dosing) or during the overall 14-day observation period.
L729: During the acute post-treatment period, three SAEs were reported in this population (one each after a first, second, and fourth HAE attack). Severe AEs were similarly rare with only two events reported, one after a second HAE attack and one after a third HAE attack. No deaths were reported during the acute observation period, although two subjects were hospitalized for either a prolonged HAE attack or further clinical observation, and one subject discontinued during this reporting period due to an arrhythmia that was considered related to treatment of a fourth HAE attack. The overall AE patterns over the full 14-day observation period were similar: four SAEs (two worsening HAE episodes, one arrhythmia, and one non-cardiac chest pain), three severe AEs (two episodes of worsening HAE and one episode of non-cardiac chest pain), no deaths, three hospitalizations (due to a prolonged HAE attack, further clinical observation, and non-cardiac chest pain that was considered treatment-related), and one discontinuation due to an arrhythmia. Consistent with safety data from the original submission, worsening or recurrent HAE was the most commonly listed AE, while local
L730: injection site reactions that resolved spontaneously were observed almost universally with icatibant dosing across all five sequential HAE attacks (89-100%), as expected. In addition, a single episode of transient transaminemia was observed, as well as one episode of elevated creatine kinase levels. In addition, one subject developed a weak IgG antibody response to icatibant during the 120-day reporting period at approximately 5 months after a second icatibant-treated HAE attack; no follow-up immunogenicity testing results were available for this patient. Of note, no clinically relevant changes in vital signs or physical examination findings were reported. However, one episode of generalized pruritus and cutaneous burning sensation occurred after icatibant treatment for an eighth HAE attack, although there were no associated rashes, respiratory symptoms, or vital sign abnormalities reported in this subject. Moreover, this patient reported no other signs or symptoms related to hypersensitivity during seven prior and two subsequent icatibant-treated HAE attacks. Thus, this episode does not appear to represent an event of anaphylaxis.
L731: FAST-4 Ongoing Treatment Phase Update
L732: In this 120-day Safety Update, cumulative safety data are reported for FAST-4 from the initiation of this trial (September 2009) through April 18, 2011. Thus, in addition to the original 56 subjects reported in the original Complete Response submission, this Safety Update adds 39 new subjects, for a total of 95 subjects. With regard to icatibant self-administration, 88 of these 95 subjects are reported in the self-injection safety database, as 32 additional subjects had self-administered icatibant (per protocol) during the 120-day reporting period. The remaining seven subjects had received an initial physician-administered icatibant dose but had not yet self-administered icatibant at the time of the original FAST-4 database lock (October 20, 2010). The demographics and baseline characteristics of this expanded population was comparable to that reported for the initial 56 subjects in the original submission. Eighty (90%) of the 88 self-treated subjects received only a single icatibant injection for their HAE attack, while seven subjects required a second dose administered in the clinic, and one subject required two additional doses. The AE profile for this updated safety population was similar to that reported in the original submission, with worsening or recurrent HAE being the most commonly reported AE in 25% of subjects, with no new or unexpected AEs reported, in comparison to the prior safety database. One or more local injection site reactions were observed in all subjects, with most severe reactions becoming mild to moderate within 8 hours. There were no AE-related trial discontinuations, no SAEs, and no deaths reported. Two subjects were reported as having had severe AEs during the 120-day reporting period (compared to four severe AEs in the original submission), both of which were cases of severe HAE.
L733: 8 Postmarket Experience
L734: The Applicant based a postmarketing safety review on information submitted to the Shire Global Safety Systems (SGSS) database, as monitored by Shire's Global Pharmacovigilance and Risk Management Department. This review included all serious and non-serious cases (other than non-serious cases from clinical trials) entered into the SGSS for the first two years of postmarketing experience in the European Union through July 11, 2010, where the Applicant estimates that 5,379 patient exposures to Firazyr have occurred cumulatively since the time of E.U. approval on July 11, 2008. From the Applicant's postmarket review, a total of 21 cases were identified, with 11 from spontaneous reporting and ten drawn from the literature. These accounted for 37 adverse drug reactions (ADRs), seven of which were unlisted, with two of these seven ADRs considered serious. Per the MedDRA classification system, the majority of these ADRs fell under the General Disorders and Administration Site Conditions System Organ Class, which primary included local injection site reactions (erythema, irritation, pruritus, pain, and swelling) that were also commonly reported in the icatibant clinical program, as well as one non-serious unlisted report of polyuria.
L735: Serious and Unlisted ADRs
L736: Two serious and unlisted ADRs have been reported:
L737: 1) Extremely elevated hepatic enzymes and eventual death in a 23 year-old man following icatibant use at greater than recommended doses. Patient first presented for surgical resection of tonsillar abscess, which was followed by severe throat and facial swelling that required fasciotomy and tracheotomy two days later. HAE was suspected and patient received C1-INH replacement therapy (1000 U Berinert) but required repeat fasciotomy for persistent throat abscess. Hepatic enzymes noted to be elevated at that time (AST = 63 U/L, NML: 0-35 U/L; GGT = 74 U/L, NML: 0-55 U/L), with abnormal platelets, aPTT, and D-dimer levels. Septic shock subsequently diagnosed with multi-organ failure (including renal failure) and intracranial ischemia. Subjects received first dose of icatibant 30 mg SC at this time for a presumed laryngeal HAE attack, with only minor, transient improvement in throat edema. Second, third, and fourth doses of icatibant 30 mg SC were subsequently given, as well as another 1000 U of C1-INH, along with vancomycin and metoclopramide, with additional elevations observed in hepatic function tests (AST = 86 U/L; ALT = 42 U/L, NML: not reported; GGT = 378 U/L; ALK Phos = 199 U/L, NML: 35-130 U/L; LDH = 491 U/L, NML: 0-248 U/L). Over two more days, several additional doses of icatibant were given, including fifth and sixth doses of 60 mg each and seventh and eighth doses of 30 mg each, for a total dose of 300 mg SC icatibant administered over 5 days. Hepatic enzymes rose sharply over this period and peaked after the seventh dose of icatibant (AST = 13,780 U/L; ALT = 4,477 U/L), with abdominal CT scanning revealing multiple areas of hepatic ischemia. After the eighth and final
L738: dose of icatibant, hepatic enzymes began to decrease, although multi-organ failure continued to progress, and the patient eventually died with evidence of widespread brain, liver, and spleen infarctions, thrombosis of the hepatic vein and inferior vena cava, and severe transverse and ascending colitis, with suspected abdominal perforation. The Applicant has suggested that these hepatic enzyme elevations were due to the patient's underlying septic shock and malperfusion of the liver, rather than from acute drug toxicity, despite icatibant dosing beyond current recommendations.
L739: 2) Life-threatening acute myocardial infarction in a 23 year-old man with hypertension, significant adiposity, and a family history of coronary artery disease, which occurred one day after receipt of icatibant for an acute HAE attack. Patient had received an initial dose of icatibant one month prior for an acute HAE attack without adverse reaction. Following this episode, patient received a cardiac stent and beta blocker, clopidogrel, statin, and ACE inhibitor therapy, prior to starting rehabilitation. Although this patient had several cardiac risk factors, his young age and the temporal relationship of icatibant administration to the myocardial infarction raise the possibility of a causal relationship.
L740: A Firazyr Patient Registry (Icatibant Outcomes Survey) has also been initiated that is open to all patients receiving icatibant treatment, which focuses on the safety of long-term icatibant treatment, with special attention to cardiac ischemic events, generalized reactions (hypotension, mucosal edema, bronchoconstriction, and aggravation of pain), effects on sexual maturation in adolescents, potential hypersensitivity reactions, and the safety and effectiveness of icatibant in laryngeal HAE attacks. Although outcome data from this registry are not included in this Complete Response submission, a total of 250 patients have been registered from eight countries, as of December 2010.
L741: Postmarketing 120-day Safety Update
L742: One serious, unexpected spontaneous event was reported in the 120-day postmarketing period. This case was a 39 year-old woman with HAE who developed arthralgia, hand paresthesia, localized pain, and extremity edema 15 minutes after treatment with a single dose of icatibant 30 mg SC. Following hospitalization, these symptoms resolved by the next morning without sequelae. This AE was considered an HAE event and was classified as serious due to the hospitalization. The reporter considered this event to be probably related to icatibant. In contrast, the Applicant considered this event unrelated to icatibant and attributable to the underlying disorder of HAE.
L743: 9 Appendices
L744: 9.1 Literature Review/References
L745: A literature review was provided by the Applicant of icatibant-related safety data documented in the MEDLINE database, which spans the period from 1950 to November 2010. Details of the search process, as provided by the Applicant, included the drug-specific terms “icatibant,” “firazyr,” and “HOE 140” paired with the following search terms for safety-related topics pertaining to human drug exposure: abnormalities, drug induced; breast feeding; breast milk; congenital malformation (exploded); death (exploded); Adverse drug interactions (exploded); drug interaction (exploded); drug overdose; drug toxicity (exploded); lactation (exploded); milk, human; overdose; pregnancy (exploded); pregnancy complications (exploded); pregnant women; side effect (exploded); substance abuse (exploded); drug abuse (exploded); substance-related disorders (exploded); teratogens; teratogenicity; treatment failure; drug treatment failure. A total of 128 publications were identified by these search terms, including journal articles, commercial reports, letters, editorials, short surveys, notes, trade journals, and documentation of U.S. government and non-government research support. The Applicant identified eight of these publications as containing relevant safety data, but only five of these publications reported AE data in human recipients of icatibant outside of the icatibant development program.
L746: These five publications identified by the Applicant described the following adverse drug reactions in humans related to icatibant (in vitro and nonclinical effects are not described in this Clinical Review): 1) local injection site reactions (erythema, pruritus) noted in a series of eight emergency department patients treated with icatibant 30 mg SC for ACE-inhibitor-induced angioedema; 2) local injection site reaction (erythema, edema, burning sensation) following icatibant treatment in a 49 year-old man with acquired C1-INH deficiency or acquired angioedema who had also received treatment with C1-INH replacement therapy; 3) attenuation of the hypotensive effect of ACE-inhibitor therapy as studied in 20 normotensive and seven hypertensive patients who were co-administered icatibant and captopril; 4) reduction in arterial flow dependent dilation noted in 10 healthy volunteers treated with icatibant; and 5) resolution of acute HAE symptoms in six patients, with subsequent local injection site reactions (erythema, burning sensation, swelling) in all patients, as well as severe dizziness in one patient. Other than the single report of dizziness, these post-dosing reactions are all consistent with the adverse effect profile and known physiologic effects of icatibant described in the Phase 3 clinical program.
L747: Key References Provided by the Applicant:
L748: 1) Bas, M., et al. "Therapeutic efficacy of icatibant in angioedema induced by angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors: A case series." Ann Emerg Med. 2010; 56(3):278-282.2) Bright, P., et al. "Successful treatment of acquired C1 inhibitor deficiency with icatibant." Clin Exp Derm. 2010; 35(5):553.3) Gainer, J., et al. "Effect of bradykinin-receptor blockade on the response to angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitor in normotensive and hypertensive subjects." New Eng J Med. 1998; 339(18):1285-1292.4) Hornig B., et al. "Role of bradykinin in mediating vascular effects of angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors in humans." L749: A separate literature review of the PubMed database from January 1, 1977, through January 1, 2011, was conducted as part of this Clinical Review to search for additional reports of complications or adverse events related to icatibant exposure in humans. For this search, the following terms were pooled into two separate searches:
L750: 1) icatibant OR firazyr OR HOE 140 (1418 citations)2) adverse event OR adverse effect OR complication OR sequela OR sequella OR sequelae OR sequellae (2,324,830 citations) [incorrect spelling variants included to capture additional citations]
L751: These searches were then combined to identify overlapping citations, which limited to references in humans, produced a final tally of 17 references. A review of the available abstracts for these citations identified no additional descriptions of adverse events associated with icatibant exposure in humans that were not already referenced in the Applicant's literature review or the safety data submitted for this Complete Response. Of note, one manuscript not produced by the Applicant's literature review (Pretorius, M., et al. "A pilot study indicating that bradykinin B2 receptor antagonism attenuates protamine-related hypotension after cardiopulmonary bypass." Clin Pharmacol Ther. 2005; 78(5):477-485) detailed the use of intravenous icatibant (HOE 140) after cardiopulmonary bypass, in order to minimize the potential hypotensive complications associated with protamine reversal of heparin-induced anticoagulation. This small trial of 16 adult male patients noted a decrease in the activity of tissue plasminogen activator following administration of icatibant versus saline placebo, as well as a
L752: reduction in protamine-induced hypotension. No complications associated with bleeding or fibrinolytic imbalance were noted in this trial. Thus, no clinical correlates of this finding were identified, although this result raises the hypothesis of potential thrombotic complications associated with bradykinin receptor blockade.
L753: 9.2 Labeling Recommendations
L754: A formal review of the proposed trade name Firazyr by DMEPA concluded that this name is acceptable. Proposed package labeling has been included in this Complete Response submission. The Applicant seeks an indication for the “treatment of acute attacks of HAE in adults” at a dose of 30 mg of subcutaneously injected icatibant, with two additional doses of icatibant permitted for persistent or recurrent symptoms, with all doses administered at least 6 hours apart, with the total number of injections in a 24-hour period not to exceed three. The proposed dosing is consistent with the results of the icatibant dose-selection program, as discussed in Section 4.4 Clinical Pharmacology and Section 6.1.8 Analysis of Clinical Information Relevant to Dosing Recommendations. The proposed indication for icatibant self-administration is supported by the results of FAST-4.
L755: At the time of this review, labeling discussions are pending. Key elements of the Prescribing Information, with proposed revisions by the Division, are described below:
L756: • Highlights of Prescribing Information○ Indications and Usage indicates that the pharmacologic drug class of icatibant will be listed as a bradykinin B2 receptor antagonist. The SEALD Review Team deferred this determination to the Office of Clinical Pharmacology, which agreed with this designation of a new pharmacologic class. In addition, the proposed indication for the treatment of acute attacks of HAE in adults has been clarified to indicate the treatment of patients 18 years of age and older.○ The proposed Dosage and Administration specifications are acceptable and supported by the results of the icatibant dose-selection program (limited dose-ranging information from Phase 2 trials and pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic modeling using data generated by a human intravenous bradykinin challenge model) and FAST-4 (icatibant self-administration). In addition, safety and efficacy data from the icatibant Phase 3 program (namely, the results of FAST-3, with supportive results from FAST-1 and FAST-2) confirm the appropriateness of the proposed 30 mg subcutaneous dose of icatibant. Further dose exploration is not expected, as the proposed dosage is acceptable.
L757: ○ Warnings and Precautions have been updated to instruct patients to seek immediate medical care for laryngeal attacks, following use of Firazyr, while the warnings regarding cautious use in patients with ischemic heart disease and stroke have been revised to remove the phrase (b) (4) from this section. Section 5 has been similarly revised.○ Adverse Reactions have been revised to include common AEs occurring in (b) (4) patients, consistent with the presentation of clinical trial AE data.• Section 6, Adverse Reactions: Section 6.1, Clinical Trials Experience has been revised to focus only on subjects from the three Phase 3 controlled trials, with a request for the Applicant to describe the demographics of this specific safety population. Table 1, based on pooled AE data from FAST-1 and FAST-3, has been revised to include only those AEs occurring in \geq 1\% of patients and at a higher rate with icatibant versus placebo. Safety results from FAST-2 are described in the text with a summary statement. Similarly, safety data from the open label extension phases of these trials, as well as from FAST-4, are also described in the text.• Section 7, Drug Interactions: This section has been expanded to include a subsection on potential interactions with ACE inhibitors.• Section 8, Use in Specific Populations: This section has been revised by the Pharmacology/Toxicology Review Team to better organize teratogenicity and reproductive findings from animal studies, which have resulted in a Pregnancy Category C classification for icatibant. In addition, a section has been added regarding the use of icatibant during labor and delivery. Potentially promotional language regarding the likelihood of icatibant to be passed in breast milk has been removed from Section 8.3, Nursing Mothers. Finally, the section on hepatorenal impairment has been divided into separate sections for each of these special patient populations, describing clinical trial data from patients with hepatic and renal insufficiency.• Section 11, Description: The wording of this section has been altered slightly to indicate that the trade name Firazyr collectively refers to the injection formulation of icatibant supplied in a single-use prefilled syringe.• Section 12, Clinical Pharmacology: Section 12.1, Mechanism of Action and Section 12.2, Pharmacodynamics have been re-written to increase clarity. The Agency's QT-Interdisciplinary Review Team has also provided draft labeling
L758: language describing the results of a thorough QT study submitted with this Complete Response. In addition, a sentence has been added to Section 12.2 to indicate that the clinical significance of the pharmacodynamic findings described in the proposed label is unknown at this time. Several sections describing pharmacokinetic data in special patient populations and demographic subgroups (e.g., based on age and gender) were added by the Clinical Pharmacology Review Team.

In addition, the

L762: 9.3 Advisory Committee Meeting
L763: A Pulmonary-Allergy Drugs Advisory Committee (PADAC) Meeting was held on June 23, 2011, for this Complete Response submission, as icatibant is a new molecular entity that is unapproved in the U.S. for any indication. Icatibant was not reviewed by the PADAC during the initial review cycle for the original NDA, and the goal of this meeting was to discuss the icatibant program's efficacy and safety results with a panel of 13 external experts in a public forum. In addition to formal presentations by the Applicant, the DPARP's Primary Clinical Reviewer, and the Division of Biometric's Statistical Team Leader, the meeting included personal testimonies from HAE patients and patient advocates that highlighted the need for a convenient, self-administered treatment for this life-threatening and often debilitating condition.
L764: The six questions posed to the Committee and a tabulation of votes are shown in Table 49. Of note, Question #6 was created during the PADAC Meeting itself upon agreement by the PADAC Chair, the Agency, and a favorable consensus among committee members, at the behest of several physician committee members who sought to make their opinions on icatibant self-administration known through an official vote.
L765: Table 49: Questions and voting results for June 23, 2011, Pulmonary-Allergy Drugs Advisory Committee Meeting on icatibant
L766: QuestionsVotes

YesNoAbstain



1. Discuss the efficacy and safety data for icatibant.(Discussion Question)Non-voting

2. Do the data provide substantial and convincing evidence of a clinically meaningful benefit for icatibant in the treatment of acute attacks of hereditary angioedema? (If not, what further data should be obtained?)1210

3. Has the safety of icatibant been adequately assessed for the treatment of acute attacks of hereditary angioedema? (If not, what further data should be obtained?)1111
L767: 4. Do the efficacy and safety data provide substantial evidence to support approval of icatibant for the treatment of acute attacks of hereditary angioedema in patients 18 years of age and older? (If not, what further data should be obtained?)1210

5. Discuss the potential impact of self-administration on the safety and efficacy of icatibant, if any. (Discussion Question)Non-voting

6. Do the data support the self-administration of icatibant? (If not, what further data should be obtained?)1111
L768: In response to the non-voting discussion questions, the PADAC noted the following points:
L769: • Safety and efficacy data have been generated from a relatively small and homogeneous clinical trial population, particularly with regard to ethnic distribution. However, given the orphan nature of the disease and the difficulty in conducting large clinical trials, the majority of the committee members considered these data generally acceptable, as a basis for drug approval.
• A limited number of committee members noted that the clinical development program did not include an adequate number of patients with comorbidities that could pose a particular risk with icatibant treatment (e.g., ischemic heart disease).
• Phase 3 clinical trial participants and investigators were likely unblinded as to treatment assignment, given the nearly universal development of local injection site reactions in icatibant-recipients, versus far lower rates in control subjects.
• Comparisons of time to onset of symptom relief are inherently affected by protocol-specified assessment schedules, in which symptom assessments were minimal between Hours 12 and 48 post-dosing. Thus, in the present database, the onset of symptom relief within a limited post-dosing time frame (suggested by one committee member as 8 hours) provides a more robust efficacy outcome measure versus calculations over a longer duration, which are complicated by large gaps in assessment time points.
• The validation program submitted by the Applicant for the VAS appears adequate to establish VAS-based outcome measures as appropriate efficacy endpoints, although single symptom scores may introduce less bias than composite symptom scores, such as the VAS-3.
L770: • The treatment effect of icatibant (i.e., time to onset of symptom relief) appeared consistent across the Phase 3 clinical trials, as well as with open-label and self-administered icatibant treatment.• Large differences in placebo effects appeared to account for the conflicting efficacy results of FAST-1 and FAST-3, with potential bias introduced by differences in the time from attack onset to study administration in the placebo groups, which was nearly twice as long in FAST-1 (~10 hours) compared to FAST-3 (~5 hours).
L771: With regard to icatibant self-administration, overall the PADAC expressed strong support for this proposed use, noting that most emergency departments do not have adequate staff training or the most recently approved treatments for acute HAE attacks. Supported by comments from several HAE patients and patient advocates during the Open Public Hearing portion of the meeting, the committee members overwhelmingly felt that HAE patients were capable of self-diagnosing acute HAE attacks. Of note, the need for patient education in proper administration techniques was stressed, and the possibility of a potential delay in seeking medical care due to self-dosing at home was discussed. However, several committee members noted that self-administered icatibant posed less of a safety risk than self-administered epinephrine, which has been approved for many years for the treatment of acute anaphylaxis.
L772: Thus, the PADAC recommended approval of icatibant on the basis of the available efficacy and safety data, with favorable voting results. In addition, while it was recognized that the single icatibant self-administration trial, FAST-4, was ongoing, the committee members nonetheless indicated that the available data support self-administration of icatibant, given the caveat that the Division will review additional data from the ongoing FAST-4 trial at a later date and respond appropriately. In turn, the Applicant indicated that the final data analysis from FAST-4 is projected to be available by September 2011.
L773: Of note, concerns were consistently raised by the Consumer Representative committee member that the available data may not be generalizable to the larger HAE patient population who would ultimately use the drug, given the relatively small size of the icatibant clinical program. Given the demographics of the Phase 3 participants, this concern was cited with particular regard to certain patient subgroups, including ethnic minorities, obese patients, pregnant women, and those with ischemic heart disease and other comorbidities. This resulted in consistent “No” votes by the Consumer Representative on all voting questions. The Patient Representative committee member also expressed these same concerns, and as a result, abstained from voting for Question #3, stating that insufficient data had been presented to support the safety of icatibant in these patient subgroups. In response to these concerns, several committee members supported the development of a postmarketing patient registry for icatibant.

L787: REVIEW SUMMARY:
L788: This is a medical officer review of materials submitted in anticipation of an End-of-review (EOR) meeting for NDA-22-150, Icatibant. The NDA was submitted October 22, 2007, and reviewed under Priority Review. A decision of Not Approvable was made, and an action letter was issued on April 23, 2008, citing multiple clinical and CMC deficiencies. The clinical deficiencies cited included the following:
L789: The submitted data from your clinical program do not provide substantial evidence that icatibant is sufficiently safe and effective for the proposed indication of the treatment of acute attacks of HAE. The uncertain efficacy of the comparator drug, TAE, in the treatment of acute attacks of HAE complicates interpretation of the results of Study JE049-2102. Study JE049-2103 failed to demonstrate a statistically significant treatment difference between placebo and icatibant. In addition, there are concerns regarding the validity of the primary endpoint used in both studies (time to onset of symptom relief using the VAS). Without substantial evidence of the efficacy of the proposed dose of icatibant, we cannot evaluate if there is appropriate safety. Before icatibant may be approved, you must submit sufficient evidence of the efficacy of icatibant for the treatment of patients with acute attacks of HAE. This evidence must be generated by using a reliable instrument to assess efficacy and an appropriate control arm. You will need to demonstrate safety for the dose shown to be efficacious.
L790: The Applicant has requested an EOR meeting for further clarification of the items listed in the Not Approvable letter. Responses to these questions are provided. Details of the NDA review can be found in the individual reviews from each discipline.
L791: OUTSTANDING ISSUES: None
L792: Questions
L793: 1. The sponsor believes that the validation studies conducted to establish the validity of the PRO diary in measuring HAE systems are consistent with the requirements of the draft PRO guidance. Does the Agency agree?
L794: Division response: The validation studies outlined in Table 1 appear to be consistent with the requirements of the draft guidance, but whether the results of these studies confirm the validity of the PRO instrument is a review issue. As noted, some of the study reports were not submitted in the NDA for review and, therefore, we cannot comment on their content. Based on the information provided in the original NDA, we concluded that the VAS has its limitations which may have contributed to the non-significant results of FAST-1.
L795: Should you elect to conduct another Phase 3 efficacy and safety study, you could use the VAS as a primary efficacy variable; however, you should include additional supportive efficacy variables, such as a composite symptom score like the VDS to support the efficacy of icatibant and the validity of the VAS. Alternatively, you could consider a different primary endpoint, such as a composite symptom score and include the VAS as a key secondary endpoint to facilitate cross-study comparisons. In either case, all data to support the chosen instrument should be included at the time of submission.
L796: 2. Based on the additional re-analyses conducted by the sponsor, the FAST-1 study demonstrated a statistically significant and clinically relevant treatment difference between placebo and icatibant which confirms the efficacy of Firazyr. Does the Agency agree that the results from FAST-1 and FAST-2 are adequate to support the approval of Firazyr?
L797: Division response: No, we do not agree. While the additional efficacy analyses are supportive, post hoc analyses do not replace or outweigh the results generated from a pre-specified statistical analysis plan. An additional confirmatory study will be required to support approval of Firazyr.
L798: 3. The sponsor believes the additional evidence presented based on pathophysiological rationale for use of anti-fibrinolytic agents and the comparison of FAST-2 study with literature data on natural time course of HAE attacks adequately ameliorates any concern of possible adverse effects of tranexamic acid in the treatment of acute HAE attacks. Does the Agency agree that the FAST-2 study data can be used as evidence of efficacy and safety for Firazyr?
L799: Division response: We agree that FAST-2 generally supports the efficacy and safety of icatibant; however, based upon the totality of the information provided in the NDA, a confirmatory placebo-controlled study is required. Replicate findings of efficacy from at least two studies are required to support approval. Your application contained only one study with statistically significant results, and this issue remains the primary deficiency of the application.

L804: SUMMARY REVIEW OF REGULATORY ACTION
L805: Date: April 23, 2008From: Badrul A. Chowdhury, MD, PhDDirector, Division of Pulmonary and Allergy Products,CDER, FDASubject: Division Director Summary ReviewNDA Number: 22-150Applicant Name: Jerini AGDate of Submission: October 26, 2007PDUFIA Goal Date: April 26, 2008Proprietary Name: FirazyrEstablished Name: IcatibantDosage form: InjectionStrength: 30 mgProposed Indications: Hereditary angioedema (HAE)Action: Not Approval
L806: 1. Introduction
L807: Jerini submitted this 505(b)(1) application for use of icatibant for the treatment of acute attacks hereditary angioedema (HAE) in patients 18 years of age and older. The proposed dose is 30 mg by subcutaneous injection. The application is based on clinical efficacy and safety studies. This summary review will provide an overview of the application, with a focus on the clinical efficacy and safety studies.
L808: 2. Background
L809: HAE is a rare inherited disease characterized by intermittent and unpredictable attacks of angioedema involving various organs, particularly the skin, intestine, and upper airway. HAE attacks are potentially life-threatening, particularly cases that involve the upper airway. The treatment options for HAE are usually divided into three categories – chronic long-term therapy, short-term prophylaxis to prevent attacks, and treatment of acute attacks1. Androgenic steroids are the only drug class approved for use in patients with HAE in the United States (US). Danazol is approved and marketed in the US with the label indication “prevention of attacks of angioedema.” The drug is also used for chronic long-term therapy1,2. Stanzanol and oxymetholone are also approved with similar indication, but are no longer marketed in the US. Elsewhere in the world epsilon aminocaproic acid (EACA) and tranexamic acid (TA) are approved for use in HAE patients. EACA and TA are used as chronic long-term therapy in HAE, but these are not
L810: 1 MM Frank. Hereditary angioedema: The clinical syndrome and its management in the United States. Immunol Allergy Clin N Am 2006; 26:653-668.
L811: 2 MM Frank, Jiang H. New therapies for hereditary angioedema: Disease outlook changes dramatically. J Allergy Clin Immunol 2008; 121:272-280.
L812: thought to be effective in acute attacks1,2. Fresh frozen plasma is often used for short-term prophylaxis to prevent acute attacks and for treatment of acute attacks, but the use of fresh frozen plasma in HAE is controversial as it can worsen an attack by providing more substrate that can be acted on to release additional mediators such as high molecular weight kininogens1.
L813: At present there are no drugs approved in the US or elsewhere for treatment of acute attacks of HAE. Icatibant is a new molecular entity proposed for the treatment of HAE. The applicant initially proposed a broad HAE indication, but during the review period modified the indication to limit to acute attacks of HAE. The modified indication is consistent with the clinical program that studied efficacy during acute attacks only. The putative mechanism of action of icatibant is inhibition of the bradykinin pathway by blocking the bradykinin type 2 receptor. The bradykinin pathway is not directly responsible etiologically for HAE, but is thought to play an important role in causing the symptoms of HAE when the complement pathway is activated due to deficiency of C1 inhibitor (C1-INH) in these patients.
L814: The Agency and the applicant had various interactions dating back to 2003 when the applicant first came to the Agency seeking an orphan drug designation for icatibant, which was granted. This product was initially assigned to the Division of Gastrointestinal and Coagulation Drug products, and in 2005 the product was assigned to this Division. There were many issues discussed at various meetings, the major issue was the validity of the primary efficacy endpoint of time to onset of symptom relief assessed by a Visual Analogue Scale (VAS) that was used in the two phase 3 studies. In response to a Special Protocol Assessment (SPA) for one of two phase 3 studies (the placebo-controlled study), it was stipulated that the primary endpoint was acceptable, provided adequate validation of the VAS was provided in the NDA. The applicant conducted an observational study in HAE patients to define the Minimum Clinically Significant Difference (MCSD) and submitted that study with the NDA. During the pre-NDA meeting the Division communicated to the applicant concerns that the phase 3 efficacy data were not convincing with the placebo-controlled study failing to show statistically significant efficacy. In addition, the Division questioned the appropriateness of using TA as an active control for acute attack in the other phase 3 study. The same comments were also communicated in the filing letter.
L815: 3. Chemistry, Manufacturing, and Controls
L816: Firazyr is supplied as a sterile solution in pre-filled syringe containing 30 mg of icatibant in 3 mL of isotonic acetate buffer solution (10 mcg icatibant base/mL). The drug substance icatibant acetate is a synthetic decapeptide with a structure similar to the nonapeptide hormone bradykinin. In addition to the drug substance, each mL of the drug product contains (b) (4) sodium chloride (b) (4) acetic acid (b) (4), and (b) (4) sodium hydroxide (b) (4), and water for injection, adjusted to pH of 5.5 \pm (b) (4). The solution contains no preservatives. The proposed expiry period is 18 months for drug product stored (b) (4) (do not freeze). The proposed expiry period and the storage conditions are not supported by the submitted data due to
L817: temperature dependent peptide hydrolysis, with one of the degradants testing positive in the genotoxicity study. In addition, the proposed specifications for the drug substance and drug product and the stability testing protocols are not adequate and have to be re-submitted with supporting data. Main issues include drug substance and drug product impurities, stability imbalance between the assay and total impurities, control of biological activity and tertiary structure of the peptide and deficient stability protocols. The applicant will need to revise specifications to address the issues pertaining to impurities or provide results from supporting qualification studies.
L818: Establishment evaluation requests (EER) were made for the two sites manufacturing the drug substance (b) (4) and the drug product (b) (4) for five sites associated with testing of the drug substance, and two sites associated with testing of the drug product. The drug substance and drug product manufacturing sites in (b) (4) respectively, have their inspections completed. Drug product manufacturing site and its associated two testing sites have acceptable inspection status, but the drug substance inspection report is still pending. Two analytical establishments in (b) (4) are assigned for inspection but have not been inspected. These pending inspections will not preclude taking action on this NDA because it is likely to take the applicant more than one year to respond to the clinical deficiencies.
L819: 4. Nonclinical Pharmacology and Toxicology
L820: The applicant submitted results of nonclinical toxicology studies lasting 13 weeks in duration. The primary toxicities were injection site irritation, and immature male and female reproductive organs. Injection site irritation was not of concern because it can be monitored in humans. For the reproductive organ toxicities, a NOAEL was identified in rats that provided an adequate margin of safety, while no NOAEL was identified in dog studies. The dog study did, however, achieve systemic exposures that covered the potential clinical exposures. A complete genetic toxicology program was conducted, which was negative. Carcinogenicity studies have not been conducted but are currently underway. A complete battery of reproductive toxicology studies was conducted and the results support a Pregnancy Category C designation. Although there were no observed teratogenic effects, there were signs of embryotoxicity, and dose-related decreases in post-implantations and total number of live fetuses. Additionally, icatibant prolonged gestation, resulting in spontaneous abortions and litter deaths. The Division accepted the limited toxicology program for submission of the application because there are no effective therapies for acute attacks of HAE. The Division also informed the applicant that treatment of acute attacks of HAE is considered chronic intermittent use, therefore, to avoid labeling restrictions, the applicant was asked to conduct chronic repeat dose toxicity studies of 6 month in duration in rats and 9 months in duration in dogs, and standard carcinogenicity studies. These were communicated to the applicant at the Pre-NDA meeting, in the filing letter, and also will be included in the NDA action letter.
L821: 5. Clinical Pharmacology and Biopharmaceutics
L822: The applicant submitted results of an adequate clinical pharmacology program with the application. There are no outstanding clinical pharmacology issues.
L823: Icatibant is absorbed within 30 minutes after injection and eliminated with a half-life of 0.6 to 1.5 hours. The clearance is primary non-renal with only 5-6% of parent drug excreted in the urine. The metabolic pathway is not certain, but in vitro studies suggest that the metabolism is CYP450 independent. Icatibant does not inhibit or induce the major CYP450 enzymes, implying low potential for drug-drug interaction. There is a theoretical pharmacodynamic interaction possibility of icatibant with ACE inhibitors. But this possible interaction is of little concern because of the general avoidance of ACE inhibitors in HAE patients due to their potential for angioedema.
L824: Icatibant exposure seems to be dependent on age and gender. Subjects over 65 years of age had 66% to 116% increase in AUC and 12% to 15% increase in Cmax compared to younger subjects. Young female subjects had approximately 2.3-fold increase in both AUC and Cmax compared to young males. The reasons for these differences are not known. These differences did not seem to impact clinical efficacy. But the subject numbers in these subgroups are too small to make a firm conclusion.
L825: A thorough QT/QTc study was not conducted. Given the nature of the disease and proposed indication and possible use of the drug, a thorough QT/QTc study was not deemed necessary. The applicant assessed serial ECGs from other studies. Analyses of these ECGs suggest possible QT prolongation and ST/T wave changes with icatibant. One subject had 120 msec QTcF prolongation with ST/T wave change after dosing and another subject had 38 msec QTcF prolongation after dosing.
L826: 6. Clinical Microbiology
L827: The drug product is (b) (4) Controls around these processes are adequate. The applicant has not provided container closure integrity study and sterility assurance information on the needle to be included in the final packaging of the product. These deficiencies will be communicated in the action letter.
L828: 7. Clinical and Statistical – Efficacy
L829: a. Overview of the clinical program
L830: The clinical program submitted with this application consists of two relatively small sized phase 3 studies. The scope of the clinical program and the size of the studies are reasonable for this orphan indication. Some characteristics of the pivotal studies are shown in Table 1. The design and conduct of these studies are briefly described below, followed by efficacy findings and conclusions. Safety findings are discussed in the following section.
