L835: b. Design and conduct of the studies
L836: The pivotal clinical studies included a small open-label proof-of-concept dose-ranging study (study 2101), and 2 phase 3 studies (study 2102 for FAST 2, and study 2103 for FAST 1). Study 2101 was small and of limited value and is not discussed further in this document.
L837: The general design of the phase 3 studies (study 2101 and study 2103) was similar. Both were randomized, double-blind, double-dummy, and multi-center in design conducted in HAE patients in a physician supervised setting during acute attacks. A key difference was the use of controls; study 2102 used TA as the active control; study 2103 was placebo controlled. Patients were dosed within 6 hours of onset of symptoms and were observed for up to 48 hours for symptom assessments. Patients with laryngeal attacks were not randomized but received icatibant 30 mg SC. The primary efficacy endpoint was the time from the beginning of receiving treatment to the onset of symptom relief as measured by a visual analogue scale (described further below). Secondary efficacy endpoints included response rate at 4 hours, time to relief of each symptoms present at pre-dose, time to almost complete symptom relief, assessment of each symptom on a 0-5 scale (none, mild, moderate, severe, very severe), and investigator global assessment. No composite symptom scoring was performed by patients. Safety assessment included recording of adverse events, vital signs, clinical laboratory measures, ECG, and physical examination. Patients treated in the double-blind phase were then continued into open-label extension phase.
L838: There are major concerns with the design and conduct of the phase 3 studies noted below:
L839: Primary efficacy variable
L840: The primary efficacy variable in the phase 3 studies was time to onset of symptom relief assessed by a VAS for the single most severe presenting symptoms before treatment. The VAS is a 100 mm horizontal line with 0 mm as no symptom and 100 mm as worst possible symptom. The symptoms rated included cutaneous swelling, cutaneous pain, abdominal pain, and nausea. Time to onset of symptom relief was defined as the time between injection and the time of symptom relief (defined as an absolute reduction from pretreatment VAS of \geq 20 mm if pre-treatment VAS was \geq 30 and \leq 50, or of \geq 30 mm if pre-treatment VAS was &gt; 50 mm). This rather complicated definition of symptom relief is not intuitive. It is quite removed from actual patient report of symptom scores. Also it is based on identification of one predominant symptom, which may not capture the extent of a whole HAE attack.
L841: Study 4102 was an observational non intervention study to define the Minimum Clinically Significant Difference (MCSD) of the VAS. During HAE attacks, patients completed VAS, and a patient rated Verbal Description Scale (VDS) that rated symptom change from baseline as “much less,” “a little less,” “the same,” “a little more,” or “much more.” The applicant designated the VDS as the “gold standard.” Based on comparison of VAS to VDS, a 9 mm change in VAS was identified as the MCSD for symptom relief. Changes in the VAS corresponded to changes in VDS (r=0.76; p&lt;0.0001). The problem with this validation is that VDS is another patient reported outcome that itself does not seem to be validated. Nevertheless, VDS seems to be more intuitive and close to patient report of symptoms. If VDS is indeed the “gold standard,” it begs the question as to why VDS was not itself used as the primary endpoint, or even a secondary endpoint in the phase 3 studies.
L842: The applicant attempted to provide further validation of the VAS by pooling data from the two phase 3 studies and comparing VAS to physician assessment to assess the test-retest reliability and clinical validity. These analyses did not provide reliable results and VAS appeared to have limited test-retest validity.
L843: Choice of active control
L844: The selection of TA as an active control is questionable. The efficacy of antifibrinolytic agents such as TA or EACA for the treatment of acute attack of HAE is not established. TA is approved in some EU countries and South Africa for HAE. The package insert and existing literature seems to support its use for chronic long term therapy, but not for acute attacks. Extensive literature search shows 3 studies that used TA for acute attacks. In one double-blind study involving 5 patients treated with TA or placebo for 2-4 months, 2 patients received acute intermittent treatment at start of acute attack and 3 patients received continuous treatment. One of the 2 patients receiving acute treatment is reported to have benefited.3 In an open label study involving 7 patients, 4 patients received TA during acute attacks, and 3 patients received TA during an acute attack and also received continuous TA treatment. Six of these 7 patients are reported to have improved during
L845: 3 Blohme G. Treatment of hereditary angioneurotic edema with tranexamic acid: a random double-blind cross-over study. Acta Med Scand 1972; 192:293-298.
L846: acute attacks by TA treatment.4 In another open label longitudinal study, 27 patients are reported to have less severe attacks with a high dose of TA (1 g every 3 to 4 hours) given during acute attacks.5 There are no published studies reporting benefit with EACA in acute attacks of HAE.
L847: The existing data do not support use of TA as a valid active control. With no data supporting it use, an important question is whether TA could perform worse than placebo. The applicant submitted expert opinion (b) (4)
L848: stating that TA is not worse than placebo. The results of the phase 3 studies do not support this assertion. TA performed appreciably worse in one phase 3 study compared to placebo in the other phase 3 study (data shown in the subsequent section). While the validity of cross study comparison is limited, the comparison nevertheless does not support the expert opinion assertion.
L849: Another problem of using TA is the fact that TA is dosed orally. It is not known to what extent absorption of TA can be effected (either increased or decreased) in patients with HAE presenting with abdominal symptoms with presumed intestinal wall edema.
L850: Blinding
L851: Both phase 3 studies were designed to be blinded, but there is no assurance that the blinding succeeded. Icatibant is associated with a high rate of injection site reaction, and TA is associated with a high frequency of nausea, vomiting, and diarrhea, as well as color vision disturbance. It is not possible to predict whether a compromise of blinding would favor icatibant or the comparator group. Nevertheless, possible incomplete blinding somewhat undermines the strength of the efficacy findings.
L852: Selection of dose
L853: Dose selection was based on PK-PD modeling using bradykinin challenge in healthy subjects. Doses ranging from 0.005 mg/kg to 3.2 mg/kg IV icatibant were used to establish a dose response to bradykinin, based on measurement of blood pressure, heart rate, and cutaneous blood flow. The phase 3 dose was selected based on expected systemic bradykinin concentration anticipated during an HAE attack. With that assumption no formal dose-ranging study was performed, and it may be difficult to do given the limited number of subjects available for study, and the subjective nature of efficacy measure. Although PK-PD modeling data suggest that a higher dose is unlikely to increase efficacy substantially, it is unclear how representative the modeling is in HAE patients. It may be prudent for the applicant to explore higher doses if the 30 mcg dose ultimately fails to show substantial efficacy.
L854: 4 Ohela K. Treatment of hereditary angioneurotic edema with tranexamic acid and cinnarazine. Acta Dermatovener 1976; 56:61-67.
L855: 5 Agostoni A, Cicardi M. Hereditary and acquired C1-inhibitor deficiency: biological and clinical characteristics in 235 patients. Medicine 1992; 71:206-215.
L856: c. Efficacy findings and conclusions
L857: Findings of studies 2102 and 2103 do not support efficacy of icatibant at a dose 30 mg SC for treatment of acute attacks of HAE.
L858: Findings of the primary efficacy endpoint for the primary endpoint and for the two major organ systems are shown in Table 2. Icatibant was statistically superior to TA, but not to placebo. Subgroup analysis for study 2102 suggested a differential response according to gender. The time to onset of symptom relief was 3.5 hours in male patients compared to 1.6 hours in female patients. No difference was observed by stratifying patients on body weight or by race in this study. Subgroup analysis in study 2103 did not show any difference according to gender. Secondary efficacy endpoint analyses were generally consistent with the primary endpoint analysis. Efficacy data to support laryngeal attacks were limited. There were 3 patients in study 2102 with laryngeal symptom, 2 of whom reported regression of symptoms by 0.3 hour and 1 hour post treatment and 1 patient was intubated and unable to complete symptom scoring. In study 2103, there were 8 patients with laryngeal attacks and the median time to regression was 0.6 hours.
L859: The two controlled studies are suggestive of icatibant's efficacy for treating acute attacks of HAE, but the data provided from these two studies are not convincing or adequate to support approval. The placebo controlled study (study 2103) showed numerical trends in favor of icatibant, but did not reach statistical significance of superiority over placebo. The active controlled study showed statistically significant difference in favor of icatibant, but the conclusion is questionable and not sufficient to support approval because of issues with choice of the active control as discussed above. Both studies have issues with the primary efficacy endpoint, and blinding as discussed above. To support approval, the applicant will need to conduct a clinical program to show superiority of icatibant over placebo. A reasonable active control that can be used in a clinical program possibly does not exist. The clinical program should use a patient centric efficacy measure, such as the VDS scale, and also use the VAS to link back to the clinical studies already conducted.

L863: 8. Safety
L864: a. Safety database
L865: The safety database for icatibant 30 mg is based primarily on data from two phase 3 studies (Table 1). The safety database includes 63 patients treated in the controlled portions of the studies. Additional safety data is obtained from the open-label portions of these studies that included patients rolled over from the controlled portions and other patients enrolled after completion of double-blind portions. The database is limited, but adequate for this orphan disease and the limited scope of treatment of acute attacks of HAE.
L866: b. Safety findings and conclusion
L867: The safety data were generally supportive, but the major limitation is that efficacy has not been established. Safety will ultimately be assessed in the context of an efficacious dose and a successful program. Nevertheless, in the clinical program no significant finding of concern was seen. There were some serious adverse events, but the temporal relationship to dose did not suggest causality, and some serious adverse events were difficult to interpret in the presence of acute HAE attacks. The most common adverse event was injection site irritation, which was expected with the preclinical toxicology study showing the same finding.
L868: c. REMS/RiskMAP
L869: REMS and RiskMAP will likely not be necessary for this drug. A final decision will be made later prior to approval.
L870: 9. Advisory Committee Meeting
L871: A Pulmonary and Allergy Drug Advisory Committee (PADAC) meeting was scheduled for February 20, 2008. The meeting was scheduled as soon as the NDA was received and before any review of the data because this was a priority review. It was thought that a meeting would be necessary because icatibant is a new molecular entity and first-in-the class, the indication is unique with no other drugs approved to treat acute attacks of HAE, and the disease is rare with an orphan status. As the review proceeded it became evident that the submitted studies do not provide adequate evidence of efficacy of icatibant. Thus the PADAC meeting was cancelled and the applicant notified of the reason for the cancellation in a teleconference on January 11, 2008.
L872: 10. Pediatric
L873: The Pediatric Research Equity Act is not triggered because of the orphan status of the application. The phase 3 clinical studies enrolled patients 18 years of age and older. HAE occurs in pediatric patients and it is expected that younger patients will be evaluated once efficacy and safety in adults is shown. At the pre-NDA meeting the applicant stated that it plans to study pediatric patients later. This plan is reasonable and acceptable.
L874: 11. Other Relevant Regulatory Issues
L875: a. DSI Audits
L876: DSI audited two sites recommended by the clinical review team. These two sites enrolled the largest number of patients in the pivotal phase 3 studies. Audit of these sites showed some compliance violations and inadequate clinical monitoring of study sites; however, the observations did not appear to significantly alter the overall study outcome. Review of the application did not identify any irregularities that would raise concerns regarding data integrity. No ethical issues were present. All studies were conducted in accordance with accepted ethical standards.
L877: b. Financial Disclosure
L878: The applicant submitted acceptable financial disclosure statements. The applicant certified that no investigator entered into any financial arrangements that could affect the outcome of the study.
L879: c. Others
L880: There are no outstanding issues with consults received from DDMAC, DMETS, or from other groups in CDER.
L881: 12. Labeling
L882: a. Proprietary Name
L883: The proposed proprietary name Firazyr was reviewed by DMETS and found to be acceptable. The name was also found to be acceptable to DDMAC from a promotional perspective.
L884: b. Physician Labeling
L885: The labeling was not reviewed in detail during review of this application because the application cannot be approved based on the submitted data. The applicant will need to conduct at least one more phase 3 study to support approval, and based on that study or other studies the label will be substantially different than the present version. It will be more useful to do a comprehensive label review and discuss with the applicant with a label that is expected to be final or close to final.
L886: c. Carton and Immediate Container Labels
L887: These were reviewed by various disciplines of this Division, and DMETS, and found to be generally acceptable. The drug product name needs to be changed from (b) (4) to “Firazyr (icatibant) Injection, 30* mg” (b) (4)
L888: Also the carton labels contain a graphic symbol that should be removed as it decreases the prominence of the proprietary name and established name.
L889: d. Patient Labeling and Medication Guide
L890: There is not patient labeling for this product as it is proposed to be administered by a health care provider.
L891: 13. Action and Risk Benefit Assessment
L892: a. Regulatory Action
L893: The submitted clinical data has failed to demonstrate substantial evidence of efficacy for icatibant 30 mg subcutaneous injection for acute treatment of HAE. The action on this application will be Not Approval.
L894: The demonstrated efficacy of icatibant over TA in one study is not adequate because efficacy of TA in acute attacks in HAE is not known. The placebo control study failed to demonstrate statistically significant difference between icatibant and placebo. In addition, there are concerns regarding validity of the use of VAS as the primary efficacy variable. Without substantial evidence of efficacy of the proposed dose of icatibant, safety cannot be assumed. To support approval of this product the applicant will need to conduct a clinical program showing efficacy of icatibant over placebo in acute attacks of HAE. The study should use reliable measures of efficacy, such as the VDS described by the applicant as the “gold standard” and also include the VAS as frame of reference to the phase 3 studies already conducted.
L895: Icatibant is likely to be used in setting outside the usual healthcare delivery setting, such as use by patients at home. The applicant will need to provide data to show that icatibant can be safely used at such setting.
L896: Data from the clinical program indicate that both age and gender have effects on icatibant exposure. The applicant will need to explore the scientific basis of these differences in exposure, and also justify the same dose regardless of age and gender.
L897: There are also substantial comments from other disciplines, notably Chemistry, Manufacturing, and Controls, and Nonclinical Pharmacology and Toxicology, which will be included in the action letter.
L898: b. Risk Benefit Assessment
L899: The overall risk and benefit assessment of icatibant does not support its approval as discussed above in section 13 a.
L900: c. Post-marketing Risk Management Activities
L901: Not relevant because the application will not be approved.
L902: d. Post-marketing Study Commitments
L903: Not relevant because the application will not be approved.

L906: Badrul Chowdhury4/23/2008 01:22:12 PMMEDICAL OFFICER
L907: Summary Basis for Regulatory Action
L908: DateApril 22, 2008FromCurtis J Rosebraugh, MD, MPHActing Director, Office of Drug Evaluation IISubjectSummary ReviewNDA/BLA #22-150Supp #Proprietary /Established(USAN) NamesFirazyrIcatibantDosage Forms /StrengthInjection30 mgProposedIndication(s)Hereditary angioedema (HAE)Action:Not Approval
L909: 1. Introduction and Discussion
L910: This review will be a brief summary of the basis for the regulatory action on icatibant and I refer the reader to the reviews in the action package for a more detailed discussion. Jerini US Inc. is seeking licensing approval under the orphan drugs provisions (CFR 316) for icatibant, which is a synthetic decapeptide competitive antagonist of the bradykinin type 2 (B2) receptor, for use in the treatment of acute attacks of hereditary angioedema (HAE) in patients 18 years of age and older. Currently domestically approved treatments for HAE include the androgenic steroid Danazol, but it is only approved for the prevention of attacks of angioedema. There are no approved products for use in acute attacks of HAE although effective therapies for acute attacks of HAE are sorely needed. In other countries (European Union and South Africa), epsilon aminocaproic acid (EACA) and tranexamic acid (TA) are approved for use in HAE, although foreign package inserts do not specify whether the indication is for chronic or acute treatment. The review team performed a literature search to evaluate the possible efficacy of TA in acute attacks, as this is an important issue regarding the sponsor's use of TA as a comparison group, but were unable to find literature that demonstrate adequate trials for the efficacy of TA for acute HAE. Literature reviewed by Dr. Chowdhury would indicate that TA may not be effective in acute attacks (the reader is referred to Dr. Chowdhury's review for further details).
L911: HAE is caused by deregulation of the complement cascade. The activity of the first component of complement, C1, is controlled by a serine protease inhibitor known as C1 inhibitor (C1INH). Inherited deficiency (autosomal dominant) of C1INH results in excessive activation of C2 and C4 which ultimately results in the symptoms of HAE which are episodic, localized, and recurrent edema of skin, gastrointestinal tract and upper airway. It is believed by the sponsors, and others, that a consequence of the uncontrolled activated complement cascade is the uncontrolled release of the vasoactive peptide bradykinin which is thought to be partially or wholly responsible for the symptoms. Therefore, icatibant, while not affecting the
L912: disease or the cascade pathway itself, was designed to block the product of the cascade pathway (bradykinin) and its biologic effects by competitively blocking the B2 receptor.
L913: The main reason for a 'not approval action' is because the sponsor has not adequately demonstrated efficacy. I will discuss that below, but would like to note that there were other deficiencies or noted safety issues, none of which insurmountable or a cause for a 'not approval' action (although some of the CMC issues may have resulted in an 'approvable' action):
L914: Regarding CMC, there are some issues regarding qualification of impurities in the drug substance and product, tightening of acceptance criteria, stability protocol data requests and data requests to support the 18 month expiry which is not presently supported by the submitted stability data.
L915: Regarding Pharm/Tox, the sponsor has not completed chronic, repeat-dose toxicity studies. Although they have completed non-clinical studies supporting acute use of their product, they will need to have animal data to support the chronic intermittent clinical dosing which will clearly occur should this product ever receive approval.
L916: Regarding Pharmacology and biopharmaceutics, icatibant has not been evaluated in a thorough QT study. This is because in clinical study, icatibant has demonstrated a prolongation of QT. As such, a thorough study is moot and the label should indicate the QT and EKG findings that were demonstrated in the clinical trials.
L917: A clinical development program for HAE at present relies on patient reported outcomes (PRO) as there are no objective measures available for evaluation in this situation that are predictive of disease activity. Due to the limited population and limited interest by others in HAE, there has not been a great deal of work done in this area, so there is not a well-established development program that the sponsors could follow. The clinical program itself had very limited exposure of drug to patients, but that is also to be expected in a program seeking an orphan indication. The demonstration of efficacy for HAE rests on two small trials. These trials are discussed in the excellent reviews of Drs. Limb, Seymour and Chowdhury and I would refer the reader to those for a detailed description.
L918: The two trials were of similar design, except one trial compared icatibant to placebo and the other trial compared icatibant to TA. Both of these trials would have probably had some blinding issues as the injection of icatibant is irritating due to the drug and not the vehicle. This will probably always be an issue, even with future trials. Both trials also relied on a patient symptoms measure developed by the sponsor that was a Visual Analog Scale (VAS) based on a 100mm horizontal line, which was itself based on a patient rated Verbal Description Scale (VDS) which has been used in the past in clinical research. The sponsor was using the VAS to measure the highest rated single symptom (vs. a complex) to the patient (i.e. cutaneous swelling or pain, abdominal pain or nausea). The primary endpoint was time to onset of symptom relief based on a derived clinically important change in the VAS. The minimal clinically significant difference in VAS scores is discussed in detail in the other
L919: reviews but I would comment that the formula the sponsor has arrived at is not intuitive or easily understandable.
L920: Results from these two studies are summarized below in the table from Dr. Seymour's excellent review:
L921: Primary Endpoint - Median Time to Onset of Symptom Relief (hours)

Icatibant30mg SCTranexamicAcidPlacebop value



Study 2102N=36N=38

All attacks2.012.0&lt;0.001

Cutaneous attacks2.5 (n=24)18.2 (n=23)&lt;0.001

Abdominal attacks1.6 (n=12)3.5 (n=15)0.026

All attacks2.54.60.142

Cutaneous attacks3.4 (n=14)10.0 (n=13)0.221

Abdominal attacks2.0 (n=13)6.0 (n=16)0.159
L922: In study 2103, the sponsor failed to demonstrate overall statistical significance. The difference in time to onset of their definition of clinical important relief was 2.1 hours. This is in contrast to study 2102 where the sponsor did demonstrate overall statistical significance when compared to TA. It has been noted that across studies, the time to relief for TA was much longer than that of placebo (12 hours compared to 4.6 hours). This could lead one to wonder if TA, while having little evidence in the literature of its efficacy in acute HAE, is actually detrimental in treating HAE. Although it is tempting to make cross study comparisons, this should only been done as a hypothesis generation exercise and with great caution, particularly in small studies where one cannot rely on large numbers in the randomization to avoid any potential biases. It could be that there were imbalances in the numbers of persons with cutaneous attacks vs abdominal attacks or gender differences (women seem to respond sooner to treatment) such that tranexamic seems to have worse effects when in reality it is a matter of confounding due to assignment (more men with cutaneous attacks in the tranexamic acid arm) and limited trial size. However, the results for study 2102 must be kept in mind, as well as the limitations, and do not convincingly demonstrate that icatibant has clinical efficacy, or that TA is detrimental. Most of the secondary endpoints revealed the same type of results as discussed above. Safety is thoroughly discussed in all the reviews and no safety signals that would preclude marketing were noted, but it was a limited data base.
L923: 2. Conclusions and Recommendations
L924: HAE is a rare disorder that is in need of effective therapies for when patients have acute attacks. The sponsor has submitted a database that does not demonstrate efficacy to the extent that would allow marketing. As such, the sponsor will need to provide further evidence that icatibant has the purported effect.
L925: This may be accomplished with one trial instead of two, but it would have to be robust as the two existing would only be minimally supportive and we have questions regarding the evaluation instrument. Further development will also have to include a PRO that we feel can adequately monitor for clinically important efficacy for the disease complex. This application is a Not Approval as further trials will be needed.

L929: MEDICAL OFFICER REVIEW


Division of Pulmonary, Allergy, and Rheumatology Products

Applicant: Shire
Proprietary Name: Firazyr


Clinical Reviewer: Brian Oscar Porter, M.D., Ph.D., M.P.H.
USAN Name: Icatibant


Team Leader: Susan Limb, M.D.
Category: Bradykinin B2 Receptor Antagonist


Review Date: April 13, 2011
Route: Subcutaneous Injection


SUBMISSIONS REVIEWED IN THIS DOCUMENT


Document Date
Submission Type


February 25, 2011
NDA 22-150 SD-21


Comments



Electronic submission




REVIEW SUMMARY: This is a Complete Response Planning Review of a Complete Response to a Not Approvable letter issued for NDA 22-150 on April 23, 2008, for the decapeptide bradykinin receptor antagonist icatibant with a proposed indication of the treatment of acute attacks of hereditary angioedema, delivered as a subcutaneous injection. Icatibant is not approved in the U.S. for any other indication, although it is currently approved in the European Union for this indication. The Complete Response submission includes data to support this proposed indication from a new placebo-controlled Phase 3 clinical trial (HGT-FIR-054) that was not included in the original NDA submission.
The clinical review concludes that the Complete Response is adequate for review.



OUTSTANDING ISSUES: None


RECOMMENDED REGULATORY ACTION: None
L930: I. General Information and Background
L931: Jerini, U.S. originally submitted NDA 22-150 via the 505(b)(1) pathway on October 22, 2007, to seek approval for the synthetic decapeptide bradykinin B2 receptor antagonist icatibant (Firazyr) for the treatment of hereditary angioedema (HAE), later revising this indication to the treatment of acute attacks of HAE. The Division subsequently issued a Not Approvable action letter on April 23, 2008, which cited multiple CMC deficiencies (discussed in the CMC Planning Review by Dr. Eugenia Nashed), the lack of long-term nonclinical toxicity studies (discussed in the Pharmacology/Toxicology Planning Review by Dr. Timothy Robison), age and gender effects on icatibant pharmacokinetics (discussed in the Clinical Pharmacology Planning Review by Dr. Partha Roy), as well as the following deficiencies in the icatibant clinical program:
L932: 1. The submitted data from your clinical program do not provide substantial evidence that icatibant is sufficiently safe and effective for the proposed indication of the treatment of acute attacks of hereditary angioedema (HAE). The uncertain efficacy of the comparator drug, tranexamic acid, in the treatment of acute attacks of HAE complicates interpretation of the results of Study JE049-2102. Study JE049-2103 failed to demonstrate a statistically significant treatment difference between placebo and icatibant. In addition, there are concerns regarding the validity of the primary endpoint used in both studies (time to onset of symptom relief using the Visual Analog Scale). Without substantial evidence of the efficacy of the proposed dose of icatibant, we cannot evaluate if there is appropriate safety. Before icatibant may be approved, you must submit sufficient evidence of the efficacy of icatibant for the treatment of patients with acute attacks of HAE. This evidence must be generated by using a reliable instrument to assess efficacy and an appropriate control arm. You will need to demonstrate appropriate safety for the dose shown to be efficacious.2. Firazyr injection is likely to be used in settings outside the usual healthcare delivery environment, such as self-injection by patients. Submit data to show that Firazyr can be safely used in such settings.3. Dose selection should be further defined in sufficient patients based on the clinical endpoint or other biomarkers that are validated to be related to the clinical endpoint.
L933: On February 25, 2011, Shire (on behalf of Jerini) submitted a Complete Response to this Not Approvable letter, which addressed the concerns above as follows:
L934: Demonstration of Clinical Efficacy
L935: With regard to establishing the clinical efficacy of icatibant, the original NDA submission included two Phase 3 clinical trials of patients presenting with acute HAE attacks who were treated with either icatibant 30 mg SC (up to 3 injections per attack, given at least 6 hours apart), placebo, or tranexamic acid (1000 mg administered orally up to 3 times over 2 days, given at least 6 hours apart), depending on the particular trial. JE049-2103 (FAST-1) was a placebo-controlled trial of icatibant in 64 patients (56 with cutaneous or abdominal edema, who were randomized to icatibant versus placebo and 8 patients with laryngeal edema, who were treated with open-label icatibant). JE049-2102 (FAST-2) was an active-controlled trial of icatibant versus tranexamic acid in 77 patients (74 with cutaneous or abdominal edema, who were randomized to icatibant versus tranexamic acid and 3 patients with laryngeal edema,
L936: who were treated with open-label icatibant). A total of 72 subjects were treated with icatibant during the extension phase of FAST-1, while 54 subjects were treated during the extension phase of FAST-2.
L937: In brief, although a numerical trend in favor of icatibant efficacy was observed in FAST-1, statistically significant differences were not observed in the primary efficacy endpoint of time to onset of symptom relief, as determined by self-reported Visual Analogue Scale (VAS) ratings of the primary symptom manifestation based on the predominant location of the HAE attack (e.g., skin, abdomen). In contrast, FAST-2 demonstrated a statistically significant decrease in the time to onset of symptom relief in the icatibant arm versus the active-control tranexamic acid arm. However, upon initial review of this NDA, the Division indicated that tranexamic acid was an inappropriate comparator for the study of HAE, given that it is not an approved treatment for acute HAE attacks in the U.S., and it was unclear whether this agent could potentially lengthen the time to symptom relief in the control arm, thereby inflating any positive effect seen with icatibant. For these reasons, the original Phase 3 program was considered insufficient to establish the efficacy of icatibant for the proposed indication.
L938: As noted in the Not Approvable action letter, the Division recommended that the Applicant conduct an additional placebo-controlled trial of icatibant of similar scope as FAST-1 and FAST-2, using the same efficacy endpoint (time to symptom onset based on the VAS primary symptom rating) in order to establish replicate efficacy results in support of the proposed claim. The Applicant responded by completing trial HGT-FIR-054 (FAST-3), which was a placebo-controlled trial conducted in 93 subjects and similar in design to FAST-1, with the main difference being the selection of the primary efficacy endpoint. Despite recommendations by the Division during the post-NDA follow-up meeting dated December 15, 2008, to use the same primary endpoint in FAST-3 as was used in FAST-1 and FAST-2, the Applicant designated a new primary efficacy endpoint of time to onset of symptom relief based on a composite VAS rating (denoted as VAS-3), which encompassed skin pain, skin swelling, and abdominal pain, as the collective symptom components. However, time to onset of symptom relief based on the primary symptom complex VAS rating (as in FAST-1 and FAST-2) was still examined and reported as a secondary endpoint. A complete Clinical Study Report has been submitted for FAST-3 with this Complete Response, which appears to adequately address this deficiency for the purposes of initiating review. The major findings of FAST-3 are discussed in Section V of this review.
L939: Of note, concerns were also raised by the Division in the Not Approvable action letter regarding the validity of the VAS rating scale used to operationalize the primary efficacy endpoints for FAST-1 and FAST-2. The Applicant responded to these concerns by completing a series of PRO validation studies that were communicated to the Division in a request for Special Protocol Assessment for the FAST-3 protocol, dated February 13, 2009. In a No Agreement SPA letter dated April 2, 2009, the Division indicated that these validation studies were consistent with the draft Agency guidance on PRO development.
L940: Safety of Self-administration
L941: With regard to demonstrating the safety of icatibant for self-administration in emergency settings, the Applicant conducted an additional Phase 3 clinical trial (JE049-3101) in 56 subjects to assess the safety, tolerability, and patient convenience of self-administered icatibant outside of a medical setting. This multicenter, open-label, uncontrolled trial of single-dose icatibant 30 mg SC also assessed efficacy as a secondary endpoint. At the outset of the trial, patients with documented Type I or Type II HAE (eight icatibant-naïve subjects and 48 previously treated with icatibant) were trained in the self-administration of icatibant using a placebo-filled syringe. Non-naïve subjects were given a single icatibant syringe for
L942: self-administration and a symptom diary and were given permission to self-administer this dose at the outset of their next HAE attack. Naive subjects were treated at the investigator's medical institution at the time of their first post-enrollment HAE attack, where icatibant was administered by a healthcare professional. Following resolution of this initial attack, these subjects were similarly given a single icatibant syringe for self-administration and a new symptom diary and were then allowed to self-administered icatibant at the onset of a second attack, followed by evaluation at the clinical site within 48 hours of treatment (where up to two more injections of icatibant could be administered every 6 hours, as needed). One subject was lost to follow-up after completing self-treatment, and 95 additional subjects were enrolled in the trial, trained in the use of icatibant, but never experienced an HAE attack and therefore did not self-administer icatibant. The Applicant suggests that the nature and severity of adverse events documented in this trial following self-administration of open-label icatibant were similar to what was observed in prior clinical trials of icatibant administered in medical settings by healthcare professionals and identified no new safety signals. A full Clinical Study Report was submitted for JE049-3101 as part of this Complete Response, which appears to adequately address this deficiency for the purposes of initiating a review.
L943: Dose Selection based on Clinical Endpoint or Biomarkers
L944: The Applicant states that icatibant dose selection was originally based on several clinical pharmacology studies, pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic modeling, and data generated from a human bradykinin challenge model. An integrated analysis of these data are presented in this Complete Response, in which the Applicant argues that the 30 mg SC dose of icatibant carried forward into three pivotal Phase 3 clinical trials is safe and effective for the proposed indication and no further dose definition is unnecessary. The Applicant also describes findings from two additional clinical pharmacology studies (HGT-FIR-061, a QT prolongation study, and HGT-FIR-065, a pharmacokinetic study in healthy adults) that were conducted since the original NDA was denied approval, which are said to further support the designated dose selection. Complete study reports for both of these new studies are included in this Complete Response submission and the information submitted appears adequate to initiate review.
L945: II. Icatibant Clinical Development Program
L946: The Phase 1 clinical program for icatibant consisted of five Phase 1 trials (four placebo-controlled and one open-label) of icatibant in healthy subjects: two single dose trials and three repeat dose trials. As noted in Table 1, this program assessed the effects of icatibant on QT prolongation, reproductive hormone effects, pharmacokinetics, and bioavailability. The Phase 2 program consisted of a single open-label proof-of-concept trial in patients with HAE that assessed the efficacy, safety, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamic properties of icatibant as acute treatment for HAE attacks. All of these Phase 1 trials were submitted and reviewed with the original NDA submission, except for HGT-FIR-061 (QT prolongation study), which along with the Phase 2 trial HGT-FIR-065 (pharmacokinetic study in healthy adults), was completed after issuance of the Not Approvable action letter and submitted with this Complete Response.
L947: The Phase 3 clinical program for icatibant in the acute treatment of HAE attacks in adults 18 years of age and older was based on four trials. The first two of these trials (FAST-1 and FAST-2) were submitted and reviewed in the original application for NDA 22-150, whereas a complete study report for FAST-3 is included in the current Complete Response submission. In addition, a fourth Phase 3 trial
L948: (JE049-3101) was conducted in response to the clinical deficiency noted in the Not Approvable letter indicating that additional data were needed to establish the safety of SC icatibant for self-administration.

L952: III. Foreign Marketing and Regulatory History
L953: Icatibant was approved for use in the European Union on July 11, 2008, and is currently approved in 36 countries worldwide for the treatment of HAE attacks. A brief timeline of key regulatory events and Division guidance regarding NDA 22-150 is presented below:
L954: • November 25, 2003: Orphan Drug Designation granted
• June 15, 2004: Fast Track Designation granted
• September, 2005: Re-assignment of IND from Division of Gastrointestinal and Coagulation Drug Products to DPARP
• October 22, 2007: NDA 22-150 submitted by Jerini, U.S. for acute treatment of HAE attacks
• April 23, 2008: Division issues Not Approvable letter (see Section I for description of clinical deficiencies)
• December 15, 2008: End-of-Review Meeting to discuss approach to VAS PRO endpoint validation and requirements to establish icatibant efficacy for proposed indication (i.e., the need for an additional placebo-controlled trial)
• February 13, 2009: Request for Special Protocol Assessment submitted for an additional pivotal Phase 3 trial, FAST-3; submission also included PRO validation studies for the VAS.
• April 2, 2009: No Agreement Letter issued by Division in response to request for Special Protocol Assessment for FAST-3, citing lack of agreement with proposed primary and secondary efficacy endpoints and trial participation criteria; however, PRO validation studies for VAS noted by Division to be consistent with draft Agency guidance on PRO development.
• April 13, 2009: Comments sent from the Division regarding Clinical Protocol JE049-3101 (safety assessment of self-administered icatibant), indicating reasonableness of approach, while suggesting that similar efficacy endpoints be assessed as for FAST-1 and FAST-2, as well as the Visual Descriptor Scale (VDS) for validation purposes.
• June 2, 2009: Teleconference held in which Division recommended that FAST-3 keep the same primary endpoint as used in FAST-1 and FAST-2, with a similar sample size, include the VDS as an assessment tool, and incorporate the blinded evaluation of icatibant for the treatment of acute laryngeal HAE attacks, which was not included in the designs of FAST-1 and FAST-2.
• February 25, 2011: Complete Response submitted by Shire on behalf of Jerini
L955: IV. Items Required for Review Planning (21 CFR 314.50)
L956: The following items pertinent to the NDA Complete Response were included in this submission:

L958: Reviewer's Comment: Icatibant has postmarketing safety data from Europe since its approval in July of 2008, with no apparent addiction safety signals. Given its intended infrequent episodic use for acute HAE attacks, its abuse potential appears limited.

L960: V. Newly Submitted Pivotal Clinical Trial
L961: A. Title
L962: HGT-FIR-054 (FAST-3): A Phase III Randomized Double-blind, Placebo-controlled Multicenter Study of Icatibant for Subcutaneous Injection in Patients with Acute Attacks of Hereditary Angioedema
L963: B. Responsible Medical Officer
L964: Bradley J. Bloom, M.D.
L965: C. Primary Objective
L966: To compare efficacy of icatibant versus placebo on time to symptom relief using a composite VAS during moderate to very severe acute cutaneous and/or abdominal attacks in patients with type I or type II HAE
L967: D. Trial Design
L968: Randomized, placebo-controlled, double-blind, multicenter, parallel group, Phase 3 efficacy and safety trial
L969: E. Dosing Schedule/Materials
L970: • Single dose of icatibant 30 mg SC versus placebo following first moderate to severe abdominal/cutaneous HAE attack• Single dose of icatibant 30 mg SC versus placebo following first mild to moderate laryngeal HAE attack• Single dose of icatibant 30 mg SC following first severe laryngeal HAE attack• Up to 3 doses of icatibant 30 mg SC (separated by at least 6 hours) following any subsequent HAE attack
L971: F. Trial Summary
L972: 1. Overview/Conduct
L973: FAST-3 was a randomized, placebo-controlled, double-blind, multicenter, parallel group, Phase 3 efficacy and safety trial of icatibant for the acute treatment of abdominal, cutaneous, and laryngeal HAE attacks. Adult patients with a documented diagnosis of Type I or II HAE were screened and upon presentation of their first post-screening moderate to severe abdominal or cutaneous HAE attack or first mild to moderate laryngeal attack were randomized to receive a single dose of either icatibant 30 mg SC or placebo. Patients presenting with an initial severe laryngeal attack were treated with a single dose of open-label icatibant 30 mg SC. Subjects then continued into an open-label extension phase in which all HAE attacks were treated with up to three doses of icatibant 30 mg, each given up to every 6 hours. Efficacy was assessed as time to onset of symptom relief based on a patient-reported composite VAS rating comprised of skin swelling, skin pain, and abdominal pain.
L974: 2. Population
L975: Adults with diagnosed HAE who experience moderate to severe abdominal or cutaneous attacks or mild to severe laryngeal attacks.
L976: 3. Treatment groups
L977: Upon the first moderate to severe abdominal or cutaneous or mild to moderate laryngeal HAE attack, subjects were randomized to receive a single dose of either icatibant 30 mg SC or placebo. Initial severe laryngeal attacks were treated with a single dose of open-label icatibant 30 mg SC. All subsequent attacks of any area were treated with open-label icatibant 30 mg SC at up to three injections per attack every 6 hours.
L978: 4. Inclusion/Exclusion
L979: Pertinent Inclusion Criteria
L980: • Males or females \geq 18 years• Diagnosis of Type I/II HAE confirmed by decreased C4 levels and/or immunogenic/functional C1-INH deficiency or by medical history• Current HAE attack in cutaneous, abdominal, or laryngeal areas• Cutaneous or abdominal HAE attacks must have been moderate to very severe by investigator global assessment• At least 1 VAS score \geq 30 mm• Commenced treatment within 6 hours of attack becoming at least mild (laryngeal) or moderate (abdominal and/or cutaneous) in severity, but not &gt; 12 hours after onset• Women of childbearing potential must have negative urine pregnancy test and use appropriate pregnancy prevention methods during study• Voluntary informed consent
L981: Pertinent Exclusion Criteria
L982: • Diagnosis of angioedema other than HAE• Prior icatibant treatment• Previous participation in clinical trial and received another investigational product within past 30 days• Treatment with pain medication since onset of current angioedema attack• Treatment with ACE inhibitor• Evidence of CAD (unstable angina, severe coronary heart disease, Class 3-4 CHF) based on history at screening• Serious concomitant illness/condition that investigator feels would be contraindication to participation in trial• Pregnancy or breastfeeding• Unable to understand nature, scope, and consequences of protocol or unlikely to comply with protocol assessments and follow-up
L983: 5. Concomitant Medications
L984: Attenuated androgens for prophylactic treatment of HAE were allowed at a stable or decreased dose, but doses could not be increased after screening. Prior replacement therapy (fresh frozen plasma or C1-INH) was allowed if given &gt; 5 days prior to onset of current HAE attack. Other HAE rescue medications were permitted, but were avoided for as long as possible (ideally for 8-9 hours) following the initial injection of icatibant. Morphine sulfate 0.05 mg/kg or equivalent low-dose narcotic was permitted for analgesia. Anti-emetics, FFP, epinephrine, C1-INH, and NSAIDs were all permitted. Antihistamines and glucocorticoids were also permitted as concomitant, rather than rescue medications.
L985: 6. Outcome measures (Safety/Efficacy/PK)
L986: Efficacy parameters
L987: • Primary: Time to onset of symptom relief (50% reduction from baseline) based on symptom rating with composite patient-reported VAS in the non-laryngeal intention to treat population, based on three symptoms (skin swelling, skin pain, abdominal pain)• Secondary: Efficacy in the non-laryngeal and laryngeal study populations was also assessed using patient- and investigator-assessed symptom scores for 10 symptoms, investigator global assessment of abdominal, cutaneous, and laryngeal symptoms, patient- and investigator-assessed clinical global impression and improvement, and patient- and investigator-assessed time to initial primary symptom improvement.
L988: Safety parameters
L989: • Local tolerability based on injection site reactions (e.g., erythema, swelling, burning, itching, warmth, pain)• Adverse events (including worsened HAE symptoms within 48 hours of onset of attack)• Immunogenicity• Clinical laboratory results (chemistry, hematology, urinalysis)• Physical examination and vital signs• ECG
L990: 7. Statistical Considerations
L991: A sample of 80 subjects (40 per treatment arm) was calculated to provide 80% power using the log-rank test for equality of survival curve at a 2-sided significance level of 0.05. The primary efficacy endpoint of time to symptom relief was analyzed via the Kaplan-Meier method. Median time to onset of symptom relief and corresponding sign-test based 2-sided 95% confidence intervals were summarized by treatment group for the intention to treat and per protocol populations for subjects experiencing either non-laryngeal or laryngeal attacks. Time to onset of symptom relief based on primary VAS symptom rating was similarly analyzed, which mirrored the primary endpoints of the earlier FAST-1 and FAST-2 pivotal trials. Adverse event data and other safety outcomes were summarized via descriptive statistics. The primary safety adverse event analysis consisted of events that occurred within 16 days of treatment with study drug.
L992: 8. Results
L993: 8.1. Demographics and patient disposition
L994: A total of 369 subjects were screened, of which 42 failed to meet entry criteria and 229 did not experience a qualifying HAE attack during the trial period. Of the 98 subjects who experienced an initial eligible attack, 93 were randomized to double-blind treatment for moderate to severe abdominal or cutaneous attacks and mild to moderate laryngeal attacks, while five subjects were treated to open-label icatibant for severe laryngeal attacks. Three subjects randomized to placebo subsequently received open-label icatibant for progressive HAE symptoms. In addition, one placebo recipient died, while one recipient of open-label icatibant for a severe laryngeal attack was lost to follow-up. A total of 58
L995: subjects were treated for subsequent HAE attacks with open-label icatibant during the extension phase. Protocol violations were noted in two open-label icatibant-recipients and two placebo-recipients.

L1000: 8.2. Treatment Adherence and Exposure
L1001: As randomized treatment was administered by health care providers in an observed clinical setting, subject compliance was not an issue, and all subjects received randomized treatment as intended. During the double-blind treatment phase, four subjects were treated outside of the intended treatment window (i.e., within six hours of symptoms becoming moderate and within 12 hours of the onset of the attack) at 6.5, 6.7, 12.4, and 14 hours. In addition, four placebo-recipients subsequently received a single dose of icatibant as rescue medication. During the open-label extension phase of the trial, all subjects received a single dose of icatibant, except for one subject who received a total of 2 injections.
L1002: 8.3. Efficacy Results
L1003: Nearly all subjects in the non-laryngeal intent to treat population experienced symptom relief (43 of 43 icatibant-recipients and 42 of 45 placebo-recipients), with a median time to onset of symptom relief (50% reduction in composite VAS symptom rating) of 2.0 hours in the icatibant group and 19.8 hours in the placebo group (p &lt; 0.001). Similarly, 43 of 43 icatibant-recipients and 41 of 45 placebo-recipients experienced primary symptom relief, with a median time to onset of 1.5 hours for the icatibant group and 18.5 hours for the placebo group (p &lt; 0.001).
L1004: In contrast to the primary efficacy analysis of FAST-1 (time to onset of primary symptom relief based on VAS), the results of FAST-3 demonstrated a significant decrease from placebo. The following table represents the summary findings of all three pivotal Phase 3 trials (FAST-1, FAST-2, and FAST-3), including time to onset of symptom relief based on both composite 3-symptom VAS score and primary symptom VAS score. As shown, the icatibant groups in all three trials demonstrated significant decreases in the former endpoint versus either placebo- or tranexamic acid-treated controls, whereas only FAST-3 demonstrated a statistically significant decrease in time to onset of primary symptom relief in the icatibant group.
L1005: Reviewer's Comment: The Applicant presents summary efficacy data from each Phase III trial for median time to onset of symptom relief as defined by both the VAS-3 composite score and the VAS primary symptom score. Symptom relief in FAST-3 was defined as a 50% reduction from baseline in VAS score, i.e., VAS-3 and possibly primary symptom VAS score, although there is discrepant information in the FAST-3 final study report to this effect. This methodology was also used to analyze time to onset of symptom relief based on composite VAS scores (VAS-3 and VAS-5) in the Integrated Summary of Efficacy presented by the Applicant. However, in FAST-1 and FAST-2, symptom relief based on primary symptom VAS score was defined as a reduction in VAS score to any value to the right and below the line defined as Y = 6/7 X - 16 with X \geq 30 mm, where X = pre-treatment (baseline) VAS in mm and Y = post-treatment VAS in mm. This corresponded to a reduction by 31 mm at a baseline VAS of 100 mm and by 21 mm at a baseline VAS of 30 mm. The Sponsor also states that this methodology was used to analyze this efficacy endpoint in the Integrated Summary of Efficacy. However, the median times to onset of primary symptom relief presented in summary Table 3 below (taken directly from the ISE) are inconsistent with those presented in the primary study reports for FAST-1 and FAST-2.

L1009: 8.4. Safety Results
L1010: Adverse Events
L1011: The safety population used for the primary safety analysis of FAST-3 consisted of 98 subjects: 46 randomized to icatibant, 46 randomized to placebo, and 6 treated with open-label icatibant. The total icatibant-treated population consisted of 75 icatibant-treated subjects: 46 randomized to icatibant for first attacks and 29 treated with open-label icatibant for their first icatibant-treated attack. The following table summarizes the overall adverse event experience for the safety population, as well as the most common treatment-emergent adverse events reported within 16 days of blinded treatment in 2 or more subjects within a given treatment group and at a greater frequency in icatibant-treated patients than in placebo-treated patients.

L1016: As noted above, one death occurred in a placebo recipient who experienced a fatal myocardial infarction 10 days after receiving blinded placebo treatment, which was reported as unrelated to study drug. A complete narrative is provided. No SAEs occurred in the icatibant group during the blinded treatment phase, while the rate of severe AEs was lower in the icatibant group (4.3%) compared to the placebo group (21.7%). However, both severe AEs in the icatibant group were considered to be drug-related (although neither was considered to be an SAE). Of note, one subject who received open-label icatibant for a third HAE attack experienced severe cholecystitis 4 days post-treatment and severe pneumonia 8 days post-treatment. A second subject treated with open-label icatibant developed a pulmonary embolus of moderate severity 10 days post-treatment. None of these events were considered by the Applicant to be related to study-drug.
L1017: VI. Brief Review of Proposed Labeling
L1018: Revised product labeling has been submitted by the Applicant, which incorporates findings from FAST-3 in support of the proposed indication for the treatment of acute HAE attacks in adults. While a more
L1019: extensive review of the proposed label will be conducted during the review process, a preliminary review identified the following potential issues:
L1020: • Highlights, Dosage and Administration: Findings from JE049-3101 have been submitted in this Complete Response in support of the assertion that icatibant is suitable for self-administration. Section 6.2 Adverse Reactions in Other Studies indicates that the safety profile of self-administered icatibant is similar to that administered by healthcare professionals, and Section 14 Clinical Studies cites efficacy data from this trial.
L1021: (b) (4)
L1022: Reviewer's Comment: During the Response Planning Meeting, it was discussed that rat and dog reproductive toxicity data will be required in the product labeling. In addition, a nonclinical carcinogenicity study will likely be cited as a postmarketing requirement by the Pharmacology/Toxicology Review Team.
L1023: VII. DSI Review/Audit
L1024: Initial review of the application did not raise any data integrity concerns. Data for FAST-3 were obtained from 67 study sites in the United States and 10 foreign countries (Australia, Canada, Hungary, Israel, Mexico, Romania, Russia, South Africa, Turkey, Ukraine), with the vast majority of subjects recruited domestically (219 out of 369 screened subjects for a total of 65 enrollees). Of the foreign study sites, the greatest number of subjects were enrolled in Israel (n = 10), whereas 0-5 subjects were enrolled from each of the other countries. No investigators associated with FAST-3 were listed as having any financial conflicts of interest. (One financial disclosure is made for a German-based investigator involved in one of the prior trials of icatibant that was reviewed in the original NDA submission.) As icatibant is a new NME, a DSI audit was already conducted for the original NDA. In contrast to the original application, however, the large majority of study sites for FAST-3 were domestic. A preliminary statistical review of the raw efficacy data from FAST-3 by the Biometrics Review Team did not detect any outliers in performance by individual study site. No additional DSI audit is recommended by the Clinical Team at this time.
L1025: VIII. Pediatrics
L1026: The Applicant states that pediatric subjects were not included in the icatibant Phase 3 development program, as the reproductive effects of icatibant remain under investigation. The Complete Response
L1027: does not include any other trials conducted in pediatric subjects. Given the orphan drug status of icatibant for the proposed indication, PREA requirements do not apply. However, during previous discussions with the Agency (Pre-NDA meeting with the Division of Gastrointestinal and Coagulation Drug Products on March 1, 2005; Pre-NDA meeting with DPARP on January 24, 2007), the Applicant stated an intent to evaluate its pediatric use during the postmarketing stage, pending the approval of icatibant for use in adults, which the Division stated was a reasonable approach.
L1028: IX. Consults Needed
L1029: The following consults will be requested:
L1030: • CDRH: Evaluate patient use of prefilled syringes with regard to self-administration outside of clinical settings (e.g., user errors, device malfunctions, extractables).• QT-Interdisciplinary Review Team: Evaluate new Thorough QTc Trial (HGT-FIR-061) that was conducted since the time of issuance of the Complete Response to the original NDA.• Microbiology Review
L1031: X. Advisory Committee
L1032: As icatibant is a NME and is under review for a novel indication, a Pulmonary and Allergy Drugs Advisory Committee Meeting has been scheduled for June 23, 2011, which will focus on the requirements for demonstrating efficacy and safety for the proposed indication.
L1033: XI. Conclusions
L1034: The study report for HGT-FIR-054 appears complete, and the information is appropriately indexed for review. No review planning issues are identified.
L1035: XII. Comments for the Applicant
L1036: The Clinical Review Team has no comments for the Applicant at this time, although the Statistical Review Team will seek clarification as to how the Applicant defined time to onset of symptom relief for the primary and secondary efficacy endpoints in the Integrated Summary of Efficacy and the final study reports for the individual Phase III trials, as the final study report for FAST-3 contains discrepancies in this regard. In addition, the Clinical Pharmacology Review Team will also request that electronic datasets be submitted for all population PK data, as well as individual study data from the two additional PK trials that were conducted since the time of issuance of the Complete Response to the original NDA (i.e., HGT-FIR-061 and HGT-FIR-065).

L1068: Cross-Discipline Team Leader Review
L1069: DateMarch 20, 2008FromSally Seymour, MDSubjectCross-Discipline Team Leader ReviewNDA/BLA #, Supplement#22-150ApplicantJerini AGDate of SubmissionOctober 22, 2007PDUFA Goal DateApril 28, 2008Proprietary Name / Established (USAN) namesFirazyr (b) (4)/icatibantDosage forms / StrengthSolution for injection / 30mgProposed Indication(s)Treatment of acute attacks of hereditary angioedemaRecommended:Not Approvable
L1070: 1. Introduction
L1071: Jerini AG submitted a new drug application (NDA# 22-150) on October 22, 2007, for icatibant acetate for injection (30mg) for the treatment of hereditary angioedema (HAE) in patients 18 years of age and older. Icatibant is a bradykinin type 2 (B2) receptor antagonist proposed for administration by subcutaneous (SC) injection. The proposed trade name is Firazyr. HAE is a rare genetic disease characterized by intermittent attacks of swelling of extremities, face, trunk, abdominal viscera, and upper airway. The attacks can be serious and life threatening. Icatibant is not currently marketed for any indication in the US or other countries. Although Jerini originally requested an indication for the treatment of HAE, the clinical program was designed to assess the efficacy of icatibant for the treatment of acute HAE attacks. The Division raised this issue in the January 8, 2008, filing letter. In a submission dated January 28, 2008, Jerini agreed to revise the indication to reflect the treatment of acute attacks of HAE, rather than the treatment of HAE. Currently there are no approved treatments for acute HAE attacks in the US. The Applicant requested a Priority Review and since there are no safe and effective treatments for acute HAE attacks, a Priority Review was granted.
L1072: The primary review issue for this application is the lack of substantial evidence of efficacy. To support the proposed indication, this application includes the results of two phase 3 clinical trials in patients with HAE. One clinical trial was placebo-controlled and the second clinical trial was active-controlled with tranexamic acid (TA). The placebo-controlled trial did not show a statistically significant difference between icatibant and placebo for the primary endpoint, time to onset of symptom relief. The active control used in the second trial is not approved for the treatment of HAE in the US and there is limited data supporting the efficacy of TA for the treatment of acute HAE attacks. The lack of substantial evidence of efficacy is discussed in detail in Section 6. Concern regarding the adequacy of the clinical program to support the proposed indication was raised during the January 24, 2007, Pre-NDA meeting with the Applicant and again in the January 8, 2008, NDA filing letter.
L1073: Since the application is for a new molecular entity (NME) and is a Priority Review, a Pulmonary Allergy Drugs Advisory Committee (PADAC) meeting was planned as soon as the NDA was submitted. The PADAC meeting was organized and scheduled for February 20, 2008. However, as the review proceeded, it became clear that the application did not provide substantial evidence of the efficacy of icatibant for the proposed indication. Thus, the PADAC meeting was cancelled. The Applicant was notified of the reason for the cancellation in a teleconference on January 11, 2008. Of note, the Applicant requested a 90-day meeting to discuss the NDA, which was held on March 5, 2008.
L1074: This memo will provide an overview of the NDA, with a focus on the review issues that warrant discussion, primarily the lack of substantial evidence of efficacy. There are no significant disagreements between the primary and secondary reviewers to expand upon.
L1075: 2. Background
L1076: Icatibant is a bradykinin type 2 (B2) receptor antagonist and is not currently marketed for any indication in the US or other countries. Currently there are no approved treatments for the treatment of acute HAE attacks in the US. Danazol is approved in the US for the prevention of HAE attacks and oxandrolone is used off-label in the US as an alternative to danazol. Jerini conducted one Phase 3 study with an active comparator, tranexamic acid (TA). TA is an antifibrinolytic related to epsilon-aminocaproic acid that inhibits the activation of plasminogen to plasmin and is approved in the US for the prophylaxis and treatment of hemorrhage in patients with hemophilia. TA is not approved in the US for treatment of acute HAE attacks, but is approved in a few countries (e.g. EU) for treatment of HAE. However, the product label does not specify whether the indication is for chronic or acute treatment of HAE and does not include information from clinical studies in HAE patients. In addition, the published literature does not provide support for the use of TA for treatment of acute HAE attacks.
L1077: The IND for icatibant was initially opened (April 8, 2004) in the Division of Gastrointestinal and Coagulation Drug Products (DGCDP), but was later reassigned to the Division of Pulmonary and Allergy Drug Products (DPAP). Because of the potential life-threatening nature of HAE attacks and no approved effective therapies, icatibant was granted fast track designation on June 15, 2004, and orphan drug status on November 25, 2003. The following are pertinent aspects of the regulatory history:
L1078: • June 11, 2004 - Jerini requested a Special Protocol Assessment for Study 2103 (Placebo-Controlled, Phase 3 study)○ In a letter dated July 29, 2004, DGCDP responded to Jerini's SPA questions in which the design, endpoints, sample size, and monitoring were deemed acceptable, with the caveat that onset of symptom relief should be clinically meaningful. No significant concerns with the protocol were raised.• March 1, 2005 – PreNDA meeting held with DGCDP○ Proposed QT prolongation study (Study 1103) deemed acceptable○ Dose selection (30mg) questioned• August 16, 2005 – IND transferred to DPAP• September 8, 2005 and February 9, 2006– Jerini requested feedback regarding the VAS validation protocol. DPAP and the SEALD group reviewed the protocol and provided
L1079: responses. In facsimiles dated January 26, 2006, and August 24, 2006, the Agency raised concerns regarding the VDS.
L1080: • January 24, 2007 – PreNDA meeting held with DPAP○ Jerini informed that DPAP considers treatment of acute attacks of HAE chronic intermittent use; therefore, chronic repeat dose toxicity studies of 6 and 9-month duration, and carcinogenicity studies are required.○ DPAP raised concern that Jerini did not have two confirmatory studies that won on primary endpoint and one phase 3 study conducted with active comparator that is not approved in the US.• January 30, 2007 – QT IRT group consult○ Study 1103 not a thorough QT study○ Because evidence of QT prolongation was noted in clinical studies, a thorough QT study is not required. This was conveyed to the Sponsor. Evidence of QT prolongation included a subject who had a 120msec increase in QTcF 59 minutes post-dose of icatibant and another who had a 38msec increase in QTcF 55 minutes post dose of icatibant.
L1081: 3. CMC/Device
L1082: General product quality considerations
L1083: Icatibant acetate for injection contains the active drug substance, icatibant, as well as the following inactive ingredients: glacial acetic acid, sodium hydroxide, sodium chloride, and water for injection. The drug product is a pre-filled single dose syringe, containing 3 mL of a 10mg/mL sterile solution for SC use manufactured by (b) (4)
L1084: The drug substance is a synthetic decapeptide with a structure similar to bradykinin. (b) (4)
L1085: The proposed expiry is 18 months stored at/below 25°C. However, according to the CMC reviewer, Dr. Eugenia Nashed, the proposed storage and expiry period are not supported by the submitted data. (b) (4) one of the degradants (b) (4) tested positive for genotoxicity; therefore the CMC reviewer recommends storage in the refrigerator.
L1086: In addition to the storage issue, the CMC reviewer identified several additional deficiencies, including the following: inadequate controls for drug substance, including inadequate specifications for impurities (see below); inadequate controls for drug product, including biological activity, impurity profile, and leachable testing; stability protocols; and inadequate microbiological controls for the product as described below.
L1087: A pharmacology/toxicology consult was obtained regarding impurities and leachables identified in the drug product. (b) (4)
L1088: According to the pharmacology/toxicology review team, the specifications for most of the impurities in the drug substance and all of the impurities in the drug product exceed the acceptable qualification threshold of (b) (4) for peptides (recommendations from the 2005 TIDES Conference). The pharmacology/toxicology review team recommends that the Applicant conduct an adequate qualification study or the Applicant should modify the proposed acceptance criterion to less than (b) (4)
L1089: A microbiology consult was obtained because sterility and endotoxin content of the drug product were identified as critical issues. Dr. Anastasia Lolas identified multiple product quality microbiology deficiencies in her review dated March 11, 2008. The deficiencies included the following: container-closure integrity, sterility assurance of needle, (b) (4), discrepancy of validation of bacterial endotoxin method reports, and lack of description of microbiological monitoring program. These comments will be conveyed to the Applicant.
L1090: The CMC and Microbiology deficiency comments will be conveyed to the Applicant.
L1091: Facilities review/inspection
L1092: Establishment evaluation requests (EER) were made. Two analytical establishments (b) (4) were assigned for inspection and inspections are currently pending at the drug product (b) (4) and drug substance (b) (4) manufacturing sites. The inspections are pending at finalization of this review.
L1093: 4. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L1094: The Applicant submitted the standard battery of required nonclinical toxicology studies with the exception of chronic toxicity studies and carcinogenicity studies. In the January 24, 2007, Pre-NDA meeting, DPAP informed Jerini that the treatment of acute attacks of HAE is considered chronic intermittent use; therefore, chronic repeat dose toxicity studies of 6 and 9-month duration, and carcinogenicity studies would be required. Because this determination had not been previously conveyed and the lack of effective therapies for the treatment of acute HAE attacks, DPAP allowed Jerini to submit the NDA prior to completion of these studies.
L1095: Although the pharmacology/toxicology review is pending at the time of finalization of this memo, the nonclinical program for icatibant is considered complete with the exception of the chronic toxicity studies and carcinogenicity studies. The focus of this discussion is on the most relevant findings from the toxicology program. Jerini submitted nonclinical studies that included 13 week repeat dose toxicology studies in Sprague-Dawley rats and dogs. In these studies icatibant was administered SC once daily for 13 weeks. The majority of the toxicology findings were observed in the dog studies, which indicate the following targets of toxicity:
L1096: • skin - local injection site irritation• reproductive tract – immature sexual organs and decreased weights (testes, prostate, epididymides, mammary gland, ovaries, uterus, vagina)○ testes - arrest of maturation, oligospermia, necrosis○ epididymus – oligospermia○ decreased testosterone, HL and FSH in males
L1097: The local reactions are considered monitorable and were noted in the clinical program. The reproductive tract findings were noted primarily in studies conducted with sexually immature animals and were dose schedule dependent. The reproductive findings in animals should be considered in the context of the disease being treated as well as the fact that the animals were dosed daily, while patients will administer icatibant intermittently with a limit in the number of doses recommended (b) (4). Chronic toxicology
L1098: studies are pending and may provide additional information regarding these reproductive findings.
L1099: The genetic toxicology studies were negative. The reproductive toxicology studies show that icatibant is not teratogenic, but icatibant affects the uterine implantation process and uterine stability in early pregnancy as well as delayed parturition in late pregnancy.
L1100: Outstanding issues include the chronic toxicity studies and carcinogenicity studies. The Applicant indicates that the chronic toxicity studies will be available in September 2008 and the carcinogenicity study in 2011.
L1101: 5. Clinical Pharmacology/Biopharmaceutics
L1102: The Applicant submitted multiple clinical pharmacology studies that were reviewed in detail by the Clinical Pharmacology reviewer, Dr. Partha Roy. The pertinent findings from the clinical pharmacology program are described here, but detailed discussion can be found in Dr. Roy's clinical pharmacology review.
L1103: Following SC administration, icatibant is absorbed within 30 minutes and displays linear pharmacokinetics. Protein binding is approximately 44%. Icatibant has an elimination half life of approximately 0.6 to 1.5 hours. The clearance of icatibant is primarily non-renal as only 5-6% of the parent drug is excreted in the urine. No accumulation was noted following SC administration of 30mg icatibant every 6 hours for 3 doses. Icatibant is extensively metabolized into two inactive metabolites, M1 and M2. M1 (b) (4) and M2 is (b) (4). The metabolism pathway is not certain, but in vitro studies suggest that the metabolism of icatibant is P450-independent.
L1104: There are some differences in icatibant exposure based upon age and gender. Clearance was decreased and half-life was prolonged in elderly subjects compared to younger subjects. Elderly subjects (&gt;65 years) showed an increase in AUC (66% to 116%) compared to younger subjects, but the effect on Cmax was less (12-14% increase). Young female subjects showed approximately 2.3-fold increase in both Cmax and AUC values compared to young males. Elderly females exhibited similar increase in Cmax (~2.3-fold) while AUC was increased by about 1.8-fold compared to elderly males. The reason for the difference in exposure in males and females is not clear.
L1105: The pharmacokinetics of icatibant in patients with HAE is similar to those in healthy subjects. The pharmacokinetics of icatibant do not appear to be significantly affected by renal or hepatic impairment. In vitro studies suggest that icatibant does not inhibit any relevant drug metabolizing CYP450s or induce CYP450 enzymes such as CYP1A2 and CYP3A4, implying that there is a low potential for metabolic drug-drug interactions with icatibant. Formal drug-drug interaction studies were not performed for icatibant. The Applicant postulated a theoretical pharmacodynamic interaction between icatibant and ACE inhibitors, suggesting that icatibant may compromise the antihypertensive effects of ACE inhibitors via bradykinin antagonism. Clinical trials excluded subjects taking ACE inhibitors. Dr. Limb addressed this issue in her clinical review as follows. Given the intermittent use of icatibant, the life-threatening potential of HAE attacks, and the general avoidance of ACE inhibitors in HAE
L1106: patients due to their potential for angioedema, the risk of this particular drug-drug interaction does not seem significant. I concur with this conclusion.
L1107: The Applicant assessed the effects of icatibant on ECGs with serial ECGs. The results of a clinical pharmacology study (Study 1103) were reviewed by the Interdisciplinary Review Team for QT Studies (IRT-QT). The IRT-QT determined that there were some ECG effects (ST/T wave changes and QT prolongation) associated with icatibant and a thorough QT study was not necessary. According to the IRT-QT review dated January 30, 2007, following icatibant 30mg SC one subject was noted to have a 120msec increase in QTcF at 59 minutes post-dosing and another subject was noted to have an increase in QTcF from 376 to 414 msec at 55 minutes post-dosing. Both subjects had ST-T wave changes. Since there is no thorough QT study to evaluate effect of icatibant on the QT interval, these findings should be described in the product label.
L1108: 6. Clinical Microbiology
L1109: Clinical microbiology is not applicable for this NDA.
L1110: 7. Clinical/Statistical- Efficacy
L1111: Jerini proposes the use of icatibant for the indication of the treatment of HAE. The proposed dosing regimen is 30 mg icatibant administered subcutaneously, repeated at intervals of not less than 6 hours and not to exceed 3 injections in 24 hours. To support this indication, this application includes the results of 3 clinical trials in patients with HAE (Studies 2101, 2102, 2103), one study to evaluate the effect of icatibant on the QT interval (Study 1103), two phase 1 studies in healthy volunteers, and one observational PRO validation study (Study 4102). The table below displays the clinical studies pertinent to this program, which will be addressed in this review. For detailed review of the clinical program, refer to Dr. Susan Limb's Clinical Review.
