L1114: The pertinent regulatory interactions were outlined in Section 2. Concern regarding the adequacy of the clinical program to support the proposed indication was raised during the January 24, 2007, Pre-NDA meeting with the Applicant and in the January 8, 2008, NDA filing letter.
L1115: Primary Efficacy Variable – Visual Analog Scale
L1116: The clinical development program for a medication for the treatment of acute HAE attacks is not well-established. The Applicant chose to use a patient reported outcome instrument called the Visual Analog Scale (VAS) to measure patient symptoms during an attack as the primary efficacy variable. The VAS has a 100mm horizontal line where 0 mm = no symptoms and 100mm = worst possible symptoms. Patients rate the intensity of symptoms by marking a line on the horizontal linear scale. The Applicant conducted Study 4102 to determine the minimal clinically significant difference (MCSD) in the VAS score.
L1117: Study 4102 was an observational, non-interventional study in 80 adult HAE patients presenting with an acute abdominal and/or cutaneous HAE attack. Patients received the standard of care and were asked to complete patient diaries until complete resolution of their symptoms or 48 hours from baseline, whichever occurred first. In addition to patient symptom scores, patients were asked to complete the VAS and a Verbal Descriptor Scale (VDS). Based on comparison to the VDS, a 9 mm change in VAS was identified as the MCSD for “onset of symptom relief.”
L1118: There are several issues worth noting about the VAS and the determination of the MCSD. The content validity of the VAS has not been established, which is an important step in the development/use of a PRO as described in the February 2006, Draft Guidance for Industry: Patient-Reported Outcome Measures: Use in Medical Product Development to Support Labeling Claims. The development program for icatibant predated the draft guidance, so
L1119: adherence to the draft guidance is not necessarily expected. That being said, to establish the efficacy of icatibant using the VAS, it is important that the VAS is an accurate measure of patient symptoms.
L1120: Study 4102 raised some concerns with the use of the VAS. First, as Dr. Limb noted, the Applicant indicated that the VDS was the gold standard, by which to determine the MCSD, but did not include the VDS in the pivotal studies. In addition, data from some of the patients were not included in the analyses because there was discordance between the VAS and VDS, which raises the question of the validity of the VAS or VDS. In addition, the results of Study 4102 identified 9mm as the MCSD on the VAS; however, one could question the relevance of moving only 9mm on the VAS (e.g. going from 90mm to 81mm).
L1121: For the clinical program, the Applicant chose the primary endpoint of the time to onset of symptom relief of the first moderate to very severe cutaneous (swelling, skin pain) or abdominal (abdominal pain, nausea) edema attack by patient assessed VAS. A response to the right and below a line Y = 6/7 X - 16 with X \geq 30mm, where X is the pre-treatment VAS in mm and Y is the post-treatment VAS in mm. This corresponds to a reduction by 30 mm at a baseline VAS = 100 mm and by 21 mm at a baseline VAS = 30 mm.
L1122: A scatter plot titled 'Symptom Relief' showing the relationship between Baseline VAS (mm) on the x-axis and Outcome (mm) on the y-axis. The x-axis ranges from 0 to 100 in increments of 10. The y-axis ranges from 0.0 to 100.0 in increments of 20.0. A regression line is plotted with the equation y = 0.8571x - 16. Data points are plotted at approximately (30, 10), (40, 15), (50, 25), (60, 35), (70, 45), (80, 55), (90, 65), and (100, 75).
L1123: Best Available Copy
L1124: In addition to concerns regarding the validity of the VAS, use of the time to onset of symptom relief with a complex definition is problematic as it adds an additional layer of complexity. Despite concerns with the VAS and VDS, if the Applicant performs an additional study, inclusion of the VAS and VDS as efficacy variables is recommended to provide additional information about these endpoints and correlate with Studies 2102 and 2103.
L1125: Proof of Concept (Study 2101)
L1126: The Applicant conducted one proof of concept/dose ranging study, Study 2101, to identify a dose and route of administration to further evaluate in Phase 3 studies. Study 2101 was a multicenter, randomized, open-label single dose study in 15 patients 18 years and older with HAE. Fifteen patients with 20 unique abdominal (GI) or cutaneous HAE attacks were randomized to one of the following 5 treatment groups: 0.4mg/kg IV over 2 hours; 0.4mg/kg IV over 30 minutes; 0.8mg/kg IV over 30 minutes; 30mg SC; or 45mg SC. Efficacy was evaluated using a variety of measures of patient symptoms. The VAS was used as the primary efficacy variable. Symptom relief was defined by an absolute reduction of
L1127: • \geq 20 mm if baseline \geq 30 mm and \leq 50 mm or• \geq 30 mm if baseline &gt; 50 mm
L1128: Attacks with VAS &lt; 30 mm were not assessed by this evaluation. Patients also rated onset of symptom relief. Pertinent results are shown in the following table.

L1130: There was no placebo control group, which limits interpretation of the results and 5 patients were treated twice for separate HAE attacks. In general, the patients in the IV treatment group had a longer median time from onset of symptoms to treatment. Using patient reports, median onset of relief occurred in less than 1.5 hours and appeared to be earlier in the SC groups. Using the VAS, there does not appear to be a dose related treatment effect. The VAS and the onset of symptom relief as reported by the patient do not correlate well, which calls into question the validity of the VAS. Because of these issues, it is difficult to distinguish between the doses. Based upon this study and PK/PD modeling, the Applicant has chosen to pursue icatibant 30mcg SC.
L1131: Phase 3 Clinical Trials
L1132: The Applicant conducted two Phase 3 clinical trials for this application: Study 2102 and 2103. Both clinical trials are multicenter, randomized, double-blind, controlled, single-dose clinical trials with an open label extension in patients with HAE 18 years of age and older. The icatibant treatment group in both trials was 30mg SC. The main difference between the two clinical trials was the control groups. Study 2102 was active controlled with tranexamic acid (TA) as the control group and Study 2103 was placebo controlled. Issues with use of TA have been discussed in Section 2. In Study 2102, TA 1000mg was administered every 6 to 8 hours for a total of 4 doses. In Study 2103, the placebo group used a vehicle control solution matched to study drug.
L1133: Patients 18 years of age and older with Type I or Type II HAE (history and/or function C1INH &lt; 50% normal) were enrolled. As Dr. Limb noted C1q levels were not measured so there is a potential that patients with acquired HAE could have been enrolled. Patients with moderate to very severe acute abdominal or cutaneous HAE attacks who presented within 6 hours of onset of attack were randomized 1:1 to icatibant or control group. Patients who presented with laryngeal HAE attacks received open-label icatibant. Icatibant and placebo were administered SC in the abdomen. Following 14 days after the initial attack, patients were rolled over into the one year OLE, in which patients could receive open-label icatibant 30mg SC for acute HAE attacks. Patients could receive a repeat dose within 48 hours with a maximum of 3 injections per attack at least 6 hours apart.
L1134: The primary efficacy endpoint was the time to onset of symptom relief of the first moderate to very severe cutaneous (swelling, skin pain) or abdominal (abdominal pain, nausea) edema
L1135: attack by patient assessed Visual Analogue Scale. Secondary efficacy variables included investigator scores, response rate at 4 hours, time to relief of each symptom present, investigator global assessment, and symptom scores by investigator and patient. Laryngeal edema was scored separately with a symptom score (0-4, none to very severe) and was not included in the primary analysis.

L1137: In Study 2102, icatibant was statistically significant compared to TA for the primary endpoint; however, the efficacy of TA for the treatment of acute HAE attacks is not established. Although approved in other countries for the treatment of HAE, the data supporting this indication and the published literature do not provide convincing evidence that TA is effective for the treatment of acute HAE attacks. The Applicant argues that use of TA is acceptable even if the benefit of TA is uncertain. Cross study comparison shows that icatibant performed similarly (median time to onset of symptom relief of 2-2.5 hours), but the comparator groups performed quite differently with TA having a much longer time to onset of symptom relief (12.0 hrs) compared to placebo (4.6 hrs). Although cross-study comparison has limitations, this difference in how the comparators performed calls into question whether TA is worse than placebo. Secondary endpoints were generally statistically significant for icatibant compared to TA in Study 2102. Adequacy of the blinding is Study 2102 is worth noting as icatibant causes local injection site reactions and TA is associated with nausea. Adequate blinding is imperative with a primary efficacy variable that is subjective like the VAS. Overall, there are limitations with Study 2102; however, the results of the study suggest that icatibant may be of some benefit for the treatment of acute HAE attacks.
L1138: In Study 2103, icatibant was not statistically significant compared to placebo for the primary endpoint although the results were numerically favorable for icatibant. Similarly, secondary endpoints were numerically favorable for icatibant compared to placebo, but did not consistently reach statistical significance. Although some of the secondary endpoints reached statistical significance, the results of Study 2103 are not robust. The Applicant asserts that the treatment difference was less in patients with abdominal versus cutaneous attacks and there were more patients having abdominal attacks in Study 2103 than in Study 2102 and this may have contributed to the loss of statistical significance. This post hoc theory to explain the findings in Study 2103 is purely speculative. If the assessment of the abdominal symptoms versus the cutaneous symptoms is problematic using the VAS, this calls into question the validity of the VAS instrument. Study 2103 suggests there may be a treatment benefit with icatibant; however, the results are not robust.
L1139: Patients with laryngeal HAE attacks were treated in an open-label fashion, were assessed differently, and there is limited efficacy data available for these types of attacks.
L1140: The clinical pharmacology data showed that females have a higher exposure of icatibant compared to males. While in Study 2102 and 2013, females treated with icatibant had a shorter time to onset of symptom relief (1.6-2.0 hours) compared to males (2.7-3.5 hours), there was a greater discrepancy in time to onset of symptom relief in the placebo group (females 3.3. hours compared to males 23 hours). Because of the small size of the two phase 3 studies, no definitive conclusions can be made about any differences in response to icatibant in males versus females.
L1141: In summary, the Applicant submitted two phase 3 clinical trials that suggest that icatibant has some benefit for the treatment of acute HAE attacks; however, the clinical program has limitations including the questionable validity of the VAS and appropriateness of TA as an active comparator. The submitted clinical trials do not provide substantial evidence of efficacy necessary to support the approval of icatibant. Dr. Limb has concluded that the data submitted in this application are not substantial evidence of the efficacy of icatibant for the treatment of acute HAE attacks and that additional efficacy data is necessary. Another placebo controlled clinical trial is recommended. I concur with Dr. Limb's determination.
L1142: 8. Safety
L1143: The safety database for icatibant 30mg is based primarily on data from the two phase 3 trials (Study 2102 and Study 2103), including both the controlled and uncontrolled portions of the trials. The safety database includes 63 patients who received 30 mg icatibant in the controlled portion of the trials. Additional safety data is obtained from the open-label portions of the Phase 3 trials that included patients rolled over from the controlled portion and other patients enrolled after completion of the double-blind portion. The open label experience provided data with repeat dosing of icatibant for acute HAE attacks. During the controlled and open-label portions of the phase 3 trials, over 600 HAE attacks were treated with icatibant.
L1144: Although the protocol allowed for up to 3 doses of icatibant for treatment of a single attack, the majority of attacks were treated with a single dose of icatibant. While the safety database is somewhat small, due to the limited HAE population, the safety database for 30mcg icatibant is acceptable. However, the size of the safety database is not adequate to assess for uncommon adverse events. In addition, there is limited safety data for treatment with more than one dose of icatibant for a single HAE attack.
L1145: Safety assessments included adverse events, laboratories, vital signs, and ECG assessments. No deaths were reported in any patients who received icatibant. A few SAEs were noted, however, the temporal relationship with icatibant does not suggest causality. In addition, ongoing HAE attacks confounded interpretation of some of the SAEs (e.g. pancreatitis with abdominal HAE attack). There were no discontinuations from the controlled portions of the phase 3 trials due to adverse events. The safety data shows that the most common adverse reactions were local injection site reactions and HAE attacks (worsening of HAE symptoms). The reporting of HAE attacks as adverse events is difficult to interpret, but is more likely a reflection of limited efficacy. Other safety assessments included laboratory, ECGs, and vital
L1146: signs, the results of which do not suggest a safety signal with icatibant 30mg. Based on a clinical pharmacology study, icatibant has an effect on the QT interval, which should be described in the product label. Refer to Section 5 for more detailed discussion.
L1147: The primary clinical reviewer, Dr. Susan Limb, has concluded that the safety of icatibant 30mg for the treatment of acute HAE attacks is generally supported by the safety data submitted in this application, but acknowledges the limitations of the size of the safety database. I concur with Dr. Limb's determination.
L1148: 9. Advisory Committee Meeting
L1149: Since this application is for a new molecular entity (NME) and is a Priority Review, a Pulmonary Allergy Drugs Advisory Committee (PADAC) meeting was planned as soon as the NDA was submitted. The PADAC meeting was organized and scheduled for February 20, 2008. However, as the review proceeded, it became clear that the application did not provide substantial evidence of the efficacy of icatibant for the proposed indication (see Section 7). Thus, the PADAC meeting was cancelled. The Applicant was notified of the reason for the cancellation in a teleconference on January 11, 2008.
L1150: 10. Pediatrics
L1151: Icatibant for the treatment of attacks of HAE was given orphan drug status on November 25, 2003. The Pediatric Research Equity Act is not applicable for an indication with orphan drug designation. No pediatric studies or pediatric plan were submitted with this application. The phase 3 clinical trials enrolled patients 18 years of age and older. However, HAE occurs in pediatric patients and the safety and efficacy of icatibant in patients less than 18 years of age should be evaluated. In the Pre-NDA meeting on February 22, 2007, Jerini agreed to address pediatric studies after approval.
L1152: 11. Other Relevant Regulatory Issues
L1153: DSI Audit
L1154: Because icatibant is a new molecular entity, a DSI audit was requested. During the preparation for the DSI audit, it was noted that Jerini did not have a US base of operations. The Applicant's address provided in the application for Morristown, New Jersey did not exist when inspectors went to visit. The Applicant was notified and during the review period has set up the appropriate base of operations in the US.
L1155: The information submitted by the Applicant did not indicate a financial conflict of interest with any clinical investigators. Preliminary review of the efficacy data by the statistician did not indicate a treatment by center interaction. Therefore, the sites for the DSI audit were chosen based upon the clinical investigators who enrolled the most patients in the pivotal studies: Study 2102, Site #070 (Dr. Werner Abere) in Austria enrolled 7 patients; Study 2103, Site #040 (Dr. Alejandro Malbran) in Argentina enrolled 14 patients. At the time of finalization of this review, the results of the DSI audit are pending.
L1156: 12. Labeling
L1157: The proposed proprietary name is Firazyr. The Applicant submitted the proposed trade name on October 13, 2006. A consult to the Division of Medication Errors and Technical Support (DMETS) was submitted regarding the proposed trade name and at that time DMETS had no objection to the proposed trade name.
L1158: The Applicant submitted labeling in the Physician Labeling Rule (PLR) format. DDMAC and DMETS consults were requested upon submission of the NDA. In a consult dated November 20, 2007, DDMAC reviewer Michelle Safarik provided comments about the product labeling. In a consult dated March 10, 2008, DMETS reviewer Richard Abate noted that DMETS continued to have no objection with the trade name, Firazyr. DMETS recommended consistent expression of the trade name, established name and strength throughout the labeling and also provided detailed comments about the syringe, blister, and carton labeling.
L1159: The carton label submitted by Jerini (partially shown below) contains a graphic which should be removed as it decreases the prominence of the trade name and established name. A comment regarding this issue was conveyed in the January 8, 2008, Filing letter to the Applicant.
L1160: A redacted image of a carton label, with a grey box covering the main content. A small '(b) (4)' label is visible in the top right corner of the redacted area.
L1161: During the review period, the review team determined that this application contained insufficient evidence of the efficacy of icatibant. Since additional clinical trial information is required, the review team decided that detailed strike out of the label was not warranted as the label may change significantly with additional clinical data. However, the labeling was reviewed and the following are some general issues with the proposed label:
L1162: • Indication of treatment of HAE is not acceptable as the clinical program evaluated the treatment of acute HAE attacks. This comment was also conveyed in the January 8, 2008, Filing letter and in the March 5, 2008, 90-Day meeting package, the Applicant agreed to revise the indication.• The label should specify that injections should be administered by a health care professional as this is how icatibant was administered in the clinical trials.
L1163: • Adverse reactions table should not include Phase 2 data as originally proposed by the Applicant.
L1164: 13. Recommendations/Risk Benefit Assessment
L1165: • Recommended regulatory action
L1166: The Applicant submitted two phase 3 clinical trials that suggest that icatibant is effective for the treatment of acute HAE attacks; however, the clinical program has limitations as described in Section 6 and the trials do not provide substantial evidence of efficacy necessary to support the approval of icatibant. The safety of icatibant 30mg for the treatment of acute HAE attacks is generally supported by the safety data submitted in this application. However, safety cannot be established without assurance of efficacy at the proposed dose. Because of the lack of substantial evidence of efficacy, the recommended regulatory action is Not Approvable under 21 CFR 314.125(b)(5).
L1167: • Risk Benefit Assessment
L1168: Because the efficacy of icatibant has not been established, the risk benefit profile for icatibant is not established. However, if the efficacy of icatibant 30mg is established, the safety data for icatibant 30mg suggests a favorable risk benefit profile.
L1169: • Recommendation for Postmarketing Risk Management Activities
L1170: Because the recommendation for this application is Not Approvable, there are no recommendations regarding risk management activities at this time.
L1171: • Recommendation for other Postmarketing Study Commitments
L1172: Because the recommendation for this application is Not Approvable, there are no recommendations regarding post-marketing studies. PREA is not triggered by this application because icatibant for the treatment of acute HAE attacks was granted Orphan Drug Status.
L1173: • Recommended Comments to Applicant
L1174: The following clinical deficiency should be conveyed to the Applicant:
L1175: The submitted data from your clinical program do not provide substantial evidence that icatibant is sufficiently safe and effective for the proposed indication of the treatment of acute hereditary angioedema (HAE) attacks. The uncertain efficacy of tranexamic acid in the treatment of acute attacks of HAE complicates interpretation of the results of Study JE049-2102. Study JE049-2103 used a placebo control, but failed to demonstrate a statistically significant treatment difference between placebo and icatibant. In addition, there are concerns regarding the validity of the primary endpoint used in both studies (time to onset of symptom relief using the Visual Analog Scale). Without substantial evidence of the efficacy of the proposed dose of icatibant, safety cannot be assured. To support the approval of icatibant for the treatment of acute attacks of HAE, you will need to conduct at least one placebo-controlled clinical study in HAE patients using a reliable measure to assess efficacy.

L1188: 1 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
L1189: 1.1 Recommendation on Regulatory Action
L1190: The clinical recommendation for this application is Not Approvable. The application requires additional evidence of efficacy to support the proposed indication, “treatment of hereditary angioedema in adults ages 18 years of age and older.”
L1191: This is a 505(b)(1) application for Icatibant (b) (4) for the treatment of hereditary angioedema (HAE) in patients 18 years of age and older. HAE is a rare, inherited condition characterized by intermittent, unpredictable attacks of angioedema. HAE attacks are potentially life-threatening, particularly in cases of airway compromise. Currently, there are no drug products approved for the treatment of acute attacks of HAE. Several drug products are available for prophylaxis, although their effectiveness in preventing acute attacks is limited.
L1192: Icatibant is a new molecular entity. The clinical development program included two small Phase 3 studies, one active-controlled and one placebo-controlled study. Although numerically favorable, the placebo-controlled study failed to show a statistically significant difference for icatibant over placebo for the primary efficacy endpoint, time to onset of symptom relief for an acute cutaneous or abdominal HAE attack as assessed by a Visual Analogue Scale (VAS). The active-controlled study did demonstrate a statistically significant benefit for icatibant, but used an active comparator, tranexamic acid, whose efficacy is not established. Tranexamic acid is not approved for use in HAE in the US, and literature to support the efficacy of tranexamic acid for acute attacks of HAE is limited. As a result, the benefit of icatibant in this trial is more difficult to assess. An additional study, preferably placebo-controlled, is needed to confirm the efficacy of icatibant for the treatment of acute HAE attacks.
L1193: The safety of icatibant at the proposed dose of 30 mg is supported by the submitted clinical study data. Safety data showed that icatibant is most commonly associated with transient, local injection site reactions. The size of the safety database is small due to the rarity of HAE and the difficulty of conducting controlled trials to evaluate unpredictable, acute HAE attacks. As a result, the safety assessment is limited. However, given the potential severity of HAE and the lack of effective treatment alternatives, the safety profile for the proposed dose is acceptable.
L1194: Therefore, although the Applicant has provided some evidence of the efficacy and safety of icatibant for the treatment of acute attacks of HAE, the efficacy data remains insufficient. An additional well-controlled efficacy and safety trial is needed to confirm efficacy findings. As a result, the recommendation of the clinical review is Not Approvable.
L1195: 1.2 Recommendation on Postmarketing Actions
L1196: The recommendation of the clinical review is Not Approvable, so no recommendations on post-marketing actions are made at this time.
L1197: 1.3 Summary of Clinical Findings
L1198: 1.3.1 Brief Overview of Clinical Program
L1199: Icatibant is a synthetic decapeptide, competitive antagonist of the bradykinin type 2 (B2) receptor. The structure is similar to bradykinin, but contains 5 non-proteinogenic amino acids. The proposed dose and route of administration is icatibant 30 mg by subcutaneous injection. The proposed indication is treatment of HAE in patients 18 years of age and older.
L1200: The Applicant's clinical development program relies on two pivotal Phase 3 studies, one performed in Europe (Study JE049-2102; FAST-2; N=72) and one multinational study with sites in the US (Study JE049-2103; FAST-3; N=56). Only Study 2103 was placebo-controlled; Study 2102 used tranexamic acid as an active-control. Both studies used the same efficacy endpoint, time to onset of symptom relief as measured by a Visual Analogue Scale (VAS). These studies are presented in further detail below. In addition to these Phase 3 studies, the Applicant has submitted study reports for pharmacokinetic studies in healthy volunteers (Studies 1101, 1102, and 1103), an open-label proof-of-concept study in HAE patients (Study 2101; N=8), and a non-interventional study to establish the Minimum Clinically Significant Difference (MCSD) for the patient-reported outcome instrument, the VAS (Study 4102; N=60).
L1201: The safety database includes 63 patients who received one 30 mg SC injection of icatibant for acute abdominal or cutaneous HAE attack during the two Phase 3 studies and 11 patients who received open-label icatibant for laryngeal attacks. The Applicant has also provided safety data up to September 2007 from 118 subjects who were treated for a total of 602 attacks from ongoing open-label extension studies conducted after each of the Phase 3 studies. During the OLE studies, patients have been permitted to receive up to 3 x 30-mg icatibant injections per attack, separated by an interval of 6 hours or more in a 24-hour period.
L1202: 1.3.2 Efficacy
L1203: The Applicant submitted reports for two Phase 3 efficacy and safety studies for icatibant: Study 2102 and Study 2103. HAE is a rare condition characterized by intermittent, unpredictable attacks of angioedema that are potentially life-threatening, and there are no drug products currently approved for the treatment of acute attacks of HAE. Studies 2102 and 2103 are suggestive of icatibant's efficacy for this novel indication, but the data provided from these two studies are not convincing or adequate to support approval. A brief overview of each study and the major review issues are summarized below.
L1204: Study 2102
L1205: Study 2102 was a randomized, multi-center, double-blind, double-dummy, active-controlled trial of icatibant 30 mg QD single dose versus tranexamic acid 1000 mg every 6-8 hours for a total of 6 doses administered within 6 hours of onset of an acute cutaneous or abdominal HAE attack of at least moderate severity. Patients presenting with laryngeal symptoms were not randomized but received open-label 30 mg icatibant. Tranexamic acid, the active comparator, is not
L1206: approved for HAE in the US and there is scant literature to support its efficacy in the treatment of acute HAE attacks.
L1207: The primary efficacy endpoint was the time to onset of symptom relief as assessed by the VAS. A primary symptom was determined and assessed by the patient using the VAS. Onset of symptom relief was defined by graphical formula as a response to the right and below a line Y = 6/7 X - 16 with X \geq 30\text{mm}, where X = pre-treatment VAS in mm and Y = post-treatment VAS in mm. Important secondary endpoints included response rate at 4 hours, time to relief of each symptom present in pre-dose VAS other than the primary symptom, time to almost complete symptom relief, and patient symptom severity scores. Descriptive narratives and symptom scores were provided for laryngeal attack patients.
L1208: Study 2102 demonstrated a statistically significant difference between icatibant and tranexamic treatment arms (2.0 vs. 12.0 hours in time to onset of symptom relief; P &lt; 0.0001). Secondary endpoints were generally supportive as well, if not statistically significant. However, as the efficacy of the active comparator, tranexamic acid, is not established, the study results are not conclusive. Theoretically, the treatment benefit observed for icatibant may be due to tranexamic acid exacerbating the HAE attack. While an effect worse than placebo seems unlikely, the uncertain efficacy of tranexamic acid makes the results more difficult to interpret. Furthermore, both primary and secondary efficacy endpoints rely on patient-reported outcomes with limited validation. This issue is compounded by the fact that icatibant is associated with ubiquitous local injection site reactions, compromising blinding. Support for the efficacy of icatibant in laryngeal attacks is even more limited, as only 3 laryngeal attack patients were treated in the original study, with 4 additional patients in the OLE phase. In terms of laryngeal attacks, the study results are favorable, but the selection of unvalidated subjective endpoints combined with the small sample size make efficacy for this particular type of HAE attack difficult to assess. Given these considerations, Study 2102 provides substantial but not conclusive evidence for icatibant's efficacy and the proposed indication.
L1209: Study 2103
L1210: Study 2103 was a randomized, multi-center, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial of icatibant 30 mg QD single subcutaneous dose versus placebo in the treatment of acute cutaneous or abdominal HAE attacks. As in Study 2102, patients presenting with laryngeal symptoms were not randomized but received open-label 30 mg icatibant subcutaneously. The primary and secondary efficacy endpoints were the same endpoints defined in Study 2102. Laryngeal attack patients scored symptom severity separately and descriptive narratives were provided.
L1211: Although numerically supportive, Study 2103 did not show a statistically significant benefit for icatibant over placebo (2.5 vs. 4.6 hours, respectively; p = 0.142). The Applicant reasons that a greater proportion of patients in Study 2103 compared to Study 2102 presented with abdominal pain as the primary symptom assessed by the VAS. The Applicant states that pain symptoms in general are more likely to respond to placebo treatments, hence a robust placebo effect in this study that minimized the treatment difference from placebo. Whether this assertion explains the lack of benefit is difficult to verify; review of cutaneous pain VAS scores do not show a placebo effect of the same magnitude. At the very least, this explanation indicates a potential
L1212: shortcoming in the VAS. If the “sensitivity/specificity” of the instrument varies significantly with the specific symptom assessed, the VAS may not be an appropriate instrument for evaluating multiple types of acute HAE attack symptoms. Secondary endpoints were generally supportive but not robust. Of particular concern was the durability of response, which appeared to be limited for both abdominal and cutaneous HAE attacks. Additional study of dose or dosing interval may address this issue, although based on the pharmacodynamic information available, the utility of a higher dose appears limited.
L1213: In summary, Study 2103 does not provide adequate support for the efficacy of icatibant in the treatment of acute attacks of HAE. Although the data are suggestive of efficacy, at least one more placebo-controlled trial is needed to confirm the findings. If the VAS is used as the primary endpoint for another trial, additional validation is recommended. Alternatively, other symptom scoring scales could be used potentially with the VAS included as a key secondary endpoint to facilitate comparison with Studies 2102 and 2103.
L1214: 1.3.3 Safety
L1215: The safety database includes 63 patients who received 30 mg icatibant in the randomized phase of the Phase 3 studies and 11 additional patients treated with open-label icatibant during the controlled phase of the study for laryngeal attacks at baseline. A total of 118 patients with 602 attacks collectively were included in the open-label portions of the Phase 3 studies. The OLE studies include patients who had been treated during the controlled phase of the Phase 3 studies who were automatically enrolled in the OLE and other patients who enrolled after completion of the double-blind phase. From the total safety database, 47 patients have been treated with icatibant for 1 attack to date, 68 patients for 2 to 5 attacks, 14 patients for 6 to 10 attacks, and 12 patients have experienced more than 10 attacks for which they have received icatibant. The majority of attacks have been treated with a single dose of icatibant (611 out of 665 attacks total). Fifty-six attacks in 27 patients have been treated with 2 x 30-mg icatibant doses, and 4 attacks in 4 patients have been treated with 3 x 30-mg doses. The Applicant has also provided information from 15 HAE patients treated in an open-label, proof-of-concept Phase 2 study (Study 2101) for additional support of safety.
L1216: The safety of 30 mg icatibant for the treatment of acute HAE attacks is generally supported by safety data from the Phase 2 and Phase 3 clinical development program. Aside from self-limited local injection site reactions, the frequency of other adverse events was low and the relationship to treatment was indeterminate. HAE was reported as the second most common adverse event, but the overall frequency was comparable to the frequency observed in the comparator arms and most likely reflects limited efficacy rather than a worsening of the underlying disease. Overall, the safety assessment was limited by the small sample size, particularly for repeat and chronic dosing. The conduct of an additional placebo-controlled trial may facilitate more reliable analysis of infrequent adverse events as well as provide confirmatory data for the use of icatibant in repeat and chronic dosing. However, the reviewer acknowledges that larger studies may not be feasible due to the rare nature of HAE. Because of the life-threatening potential of HAE attacks and the lack of other efficacious drugs currently available for this indication, if the
L1217: efficacy of icatibant is established, the safety data suggests a favorable risk:benefit profile for icatibant at the 30 mg dose. If doses higher than 30 mg or alternative dosing regimens are shown to be efficacious, additional safety data to support higher dosing will be required.
L1218: 1.3.4 Dosing Regimen and Administration
L1219: The proposed dosing regimen is 30 mg icatibant administered subcutaneously, repeated at intervals of not less than 6 hours and not to exceed 3 injections in 24 hours.
L1220: In general, the efficacy of icatibant at this dose is not convincingly established, and an additional placebo-controlled study will be needed to confirm efficacy for the treatment of acute attacks. In addition, the proposed dosing regimen should reflect the limitations of the clinical studies. For example, there is little data to support repeat and chronic dosing in the current NDA submission.
L1221: (b) (4)As data to support long-term, repeat use is limited at this time, dosage recommendations should reflect these limitations. The regimen also does not specify the circumstances for appropriate use, (b) (4) as was done in the supporting clinical trials. Formal recommendations on dosage and administration from the clinical review may be altered should the Applicant elect to perform another Phase 3 study and submit additional data.
L1222: 1.3.5 Drug-Drug Interactions
L1223: No formal drug interaction studies were conducted with icatibant. Review of adverse event reports does not reveal any apparent interactions between icatibant and other drug products often used in the treatment of HAE patients, including opioids for pain relief, antiemetics, gonadotropins, C1 INH replacement therapy, and corticosteroids. The Applicant cites a theoretical interaction between icatibant and ACE inhibitors, whereby icatibant may attenuate the antihypertensive effect of ACE inhibitors by blockade of bradykinin receptors. Patients taking ACE inhibitors were excluded from the icatibant studies. The proposed labeling does not contain any restrictions, however, on the concomitant use of ACE inhibitors. However, given the expected, limited manner of use of icatibant, the life-threatening potential of HAE attacks, and the general avoidance of ACE inhibitors in HAE patients due to their potential for angioedema, the risk of this particular drug-drug interaction does not seem significant.
L1224: 1.3.6 Special Populations
L1225: No formal studies in special populations have been conducted with icatibant. Review of data from the Phase 3 studies, particularly Study 2102, suggests a potential difference in efficacy between male and female patients, with females experiencing a slightly quicker time to onset of symptom relief. The small sample size limits the utility of subgroup analysis, but there is no clear explanation for the differences seen in male versus female patients. Pharmacokinetic assessments show higher plasma levels in females, but primarily elderly females, who comprised only a minor fraction of the population enrolled. In terms of types of attacks, a similar
L1226: proportion of patients were male versus female in the icatibant cutaneous and abdominal attack subgroups in Study 2102 (see Table 26). No differences were noted in terms of ethnicity, although the diversity of the patient population was restricted given the small sample size and study site locations. As mentioned, pharmacokinetic assessments suggest higher plasma levels in the elderly but no dose adjustments are recommended. Icatibant clearance does not appear to be affected by hepatic or renal impairment.
L1227: 2 INTRODUCTION AND BACKGROUND
L1228: 2.1 Product Information
L1229: The established name of the subject product of this application is icatibant and the proposed trade name is Firazyr® (b) (4). The established name will be used in this review to refer to the product. Icatibant is supplied as a pre-filled syringe containing 30 mg icatibant acetate in 3 mL solution. In addition to the active ingredient, the sterile, acetate buffer solution contains (b) (4) sodium chloride, (b) (4) acetic acid, and (b) (4) sodium hydroxide/ml and water for injection adjusted to pH 5.5 (b) (4). The solution contains no preservatives.
L1230: The active ingredient is a new molecular entity, a novel decapeptide antagonist directed against the bradykinin type-2 receptor.
L1231: The proposed indication for icatibant is the treatment of HAE of patients 18 years of age and older. The proposed dosing regimen is 30 mg icatibant injected subcutaneously for the treatment of an HAE attack. In cases of insufficient relief or recurrence of symptoms, up to two additional 30-mg injections may be administered at intervals of no less than 6 hours. No more than 3 injections within a 24-hour period are recommended.
L1232: 2.2 Currently Available Treatment for Indications
L1233: Currently, there are no drug products approved for the treatment of acute attacks of HAE in the US. Several drug products are available for prophylaxis, although their effectiveness in preventing acute attacks is limited. Danazol (NDA 74-582) is approved for the prevention of attacks of hereditary angioedema of all types (cutaneous, abdominal, and laryngeal). Oxymetholone (b) (4) and stanozolol (NDA 12-885) had similar indications but are no longer marketed in the US. Another androgen, oxandrolone, is used off-label in the US as an alternative to danazol.
L1234: 2.3 Availability of Proposed Active Ingredient in the United States
L1235: Icatibant is not currently marketed in the US.
L1236: 2.4 Important Issues With Pharmacologically Related Products
L1237: No other members of the pharmacologic class are currently marketed.
L1238: 2.5 Presubmission Regulatory Activity
L1239: The opening IND (April 8, 2004) was initially reviewed in the Division of Gastrointestinal and Coagulation Drug Products prior to reassignment to the Division of Pulmonary and Allergy Products in September 2005. A timeline of pertinent regulatory proceedings is included below:
L1240: July 31, 2003 Request for Orphan Drug DesignationNovember 25, 2003 Orphan Drug Designation #03-1762 grantedFebruary 6, 2004 Pre-IND meeting:Discussion focused on selection and validation of symptom-based endpoints, including justification of the minimum important difference. The Applicant was also reminded that two adequate, well-controlled studies were the usual requirement for a new indication. The ongoing, open-label, proof-of-concept study, #2101, being conducted at that time in Europe was unlikely to meet this requirement. Other issues discussed included eligibility for Fast Track Designation and Priority Review.April 8, 2004 Opening IND:The proposed study was a multi-center, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial in 56 adult subjects with moderate to severe cutaneous or abdominal symptoms of HAE. The primary efficacy endpoint was time to onset of symptom relief assessed by a Visual Analogue Scale (VAS) completed by patients; symptom score and global assessment score were secondary efficacy outcomes. Patients with laryngeal symptoms were enrolled in an open-label arm of the study and their outcomes were to be reported descriptively. Comments communicated to the Applicant following review were to include adequate dose-ranging studies in the clinical development program, as well as validating the VAS instrument and justifying a time to onset of symptom relief that is clinically meaningful.June 11, 2004 Request for SPA (SN 004); Protocol JE049 #2103 (SN 005):The Applicant amended the opening IND protocol and requested a SPA. The main changes were addition of an open-label extension phase, assessment of laryngeal symptoms using a 5-point symptom score, and new pharmacoeconomic and safety secondary endpoints. In a response dated July 29, 2004, the Division confirmed that the proposed endpoints and sample size were acceptable, with the caveat that onset of symptom relief should be clinically meaningful. The Division also advised that safety data should be recorded in detail from the extension phase of the study.June 15, 2004 Fast Track Designation grantedJuly 1, 2004 EOP2 CMC meetingMarch 1, 2005 Pre-NDA meeting with Div. of GI and Coagulation Drug ProductsMajor clinical issues raised:• QTc data should be collected in the proposed PK study JE049 #1103 with additional time points at or near Cmax
L1241: • Data obtained from the open-label, Phase 2 study JE049 #2101 did not fully support the 30 mg SC dose used in the Phase 3 pivotal trials• Pediatric use to be assessed in post-marketing phaseSeptember 2005 Reassignment of IND to Division of Pulmonary and AllergySeptember 8, 2005 Protocol JE049 #4102 (PRO endpoint validation) submitted (SN 0013)The study was intended to estimate the Minimum Clinically Significant Difference (MCSD) in VAS scores. Patients were asked to score their symptom response using the Verbal Descriptor Scale (VDS), classifying their symptom as “much more,” “a little more,” “about the same,” “a little less,” or “much less” compared to baseline. The MCSD was defined as the VAS change most predictive of the VDS categories of “a little less” and “a little more” for each symptom. The MO review dated January 26, 2006, and SEALD consult cited several concerns with the protocol:• The VDS required more rigorous definition. Incorporation of a temporal element in the VDS makes this measure less reliable.• The patient population in the validation study was not representative of the patients participating in the phase 3 study.• Patient diaries used in Phase 3 studies should be included in the validation protocol.• Potential labeling limitations for claims supported solely by the VASFebruary 9, 2006 Response to Division/SEALD review regarding validation study (SN 015). Reference to other clinical studies that have used a Verbal Descriptor Scale to determine the MCSD in VAS scores.June 1, 2006 SEALD review of February 9, 2006 response. Patient diaries and planned subject enrollment deemed acceptable. Concern raised that the VAS is not a comprehensive measure of HAE symptoms and does not correlate clearly with the proposed indication.June 20, 2006 SAP for Study #2103 (FAST-1) submitted via emailThe Statistical review team asked the Applicant to provide the assumed MCSD used to calculate the sample size of N=56. The review team recommended that the Applicant prespecify alternative analyses to be used if the proportional hazards assumption was not supported by the observed data.October 10, 2006 Revised SAP submitted (SN 0026)October 13, 2006 Proposed trade name application submitted (SN 0027)January 24, 2007 Pre-NDA meeting. L1242: 2.6 Other Relevant Background Information
L1243: Icatibant has not yet been approved for marketing for any indication [2.5.5.13]
L1244: 3 SIGNIFICANT FINDINGS FROM OTHER REVIEW DISCIPLINES
L1245: 3.1 CMC (and Product Microbiology, if Applicable)
L1246: The CMC reviewer's recommended action on this application at the time of this review is pending. Based on preliminary review, the CMC reviewer has noted that the Applicant has limited manufacturing experience with icatibant, and current specifications for impurities do not meet the TIDES Oligonucleotide and Peptide Technology Product and Development Conference 2005 guidelines. Also, recent changes have been made in the drug sterilization process that may affect the microbiology standards. GMP inspection of the manufacturing sites is pending and is expected to be completed by the end of March 2008.
L1247: 3.2 Animal Pharmacology/Toxicology
L1248: The Pharmacology/Toxicology reviewer's recommended action on this application at the time of this review is pending. Based on preliminary review, the Pharmacology/Toxicology reviewer has identified several major issues. The Applicant has provided preclinical data to support human dosing only up to 13 weeks. Updates regarding chronic dosing studies (6-month in rat and 9-month in dog via the subcutaneous route) as well as carcinogenicity toxicology studies have been requested from the Applicant. The Applicant has also been asked to qualify impurities and/or degradants exceeding the qualification threshold of (b) (4) for peptides.
L1249: No apparent issues are identified upon preliminary review of the genotoxicology battery. The safety battery is complete and preliminary review is notable for dose-schedule-dependent effects on male and female reproductive organs in sexually immature rats and dogs. In SC dosing studies, these effects included arrest in the maturation of reproductive organs in both males and females. After the recovery period, the females had recovered but half of the male dogs still had immature prostate and testes. Also, decreases in testosterone, LH, and FSH were observed in male animals. Based on reproductive organ findings, no NOAEL was established.
L1250: Specific reproductive toxicology studies are currently under review, but preliminary review indicates that icatibant increases the frequency of spontaneous abortions and fetal resorptions.
L1251: Reviewer's Comment: The clinical relevance of the reproductive findings is unclear, given the fact that most patients would use a single dose of icatibant on intermittent occasion. Additional chronic toxicology studies are pending and may provide additional information regarding these reproductive findings. The Applicant did assess FSH and LH levels in the pharmacokinetic study, Study 1103, and did not observe any clinically relevant changes.
L1252: 4 DATA SOURCES, REVIEW STRATEGY, AND DATA INTEGRITY
L1253: 4.1 Sources of Clinical Data
L1254: The primary source of clinical data for this review was the studies conducted by Jerini AG, presented in the clinical study reports. Data from literature reports referenced by the Applicant, expert opinions solicited and compiled by the Applicant, and literature reports collated from a PubMed literature search performed by the reviewer were also used. Prior communications with the Applicant were used as regulatory references.
L1255: 4.2 Tables of Clinical Studies
L1256: Table 1 shows the main clinical studies presented by the Applicant in support of this application. Studies 2102 and 2103 are the major efficacy (“pivotal”) studies in this application. Each of these studies was followed by open-label extensions (OLE) to obtain additional safety and efficacy data on repeat administration. In addition to these Phase 3 studies, the Applicant has submitted study reports for pharmacokinetic studies in healthy volunteers (Studies 1101, 1103, and 1103), an open-label proof-of-concept study in HAE patients (Study 2101), and a non-intervention study to define the Minimum Clinically Significant Difference (MCSD) as measured by the VAS instrument (Study 4102).

L1258: 4.3 Review Strategy
L1259: The two Phase 3 studies, the open-label proof-of-concept study, and the PRO validation study presented in Table 1 were reviewed, with greater emphasis placed on the two Phase 3 efficacy and safety studies, 2102 and 2103. Data from the OLE phases of both pivotal studies was included in the review. The proof-of-concept study was briefly reviewed to assess the extent of dose-ranging performed in the clinical development program and for additional safety and efficacy information, given the small number of patients exposed in the overall clinical development program. Study 4102 was reviewed to assess the validity of the VAS instrument, the basis of the primary efficacy endpoint used in both Phase 3 studies.
L1260: Reviews of the studies are based primarily on the Jerini AG study reports, original protocols, and statistical analysis plans. The Applicant's summary data tables were reviewed in details. Appendix tables were also reviewed in varying amounts of detail, depending upon the endpoint and review issue. Case report forms (CRFs) were also reviewed.
L1261: The Applicant provided bibliographies within the study reports and expert opinion reports in the application. These references in addition to the results of a literature search conducted by the reviewer were reviewed to the extent of their relevance to the review.
L1262: 4.4 Data Quality and Integrity
L1263: The Applicant states that no debarred investigators participated in the study, and all investigators certified to having no financial conflicts of interest.
L1264: The Division requested an audit by the Division of Scientific Investigations (DSI) for this NDA since icatibant is a new molecular entity and the majority of the supporting efficacy data had been obtained from international study sites. A DSI audit was requested for the study sites which enrolled the most patients in Studies 2102 and 2103, Sites 070 and 014, respectively. Study 2102, Site 070 (Dr. Werner Abere) in Austria enrolled 7 patients; Study 2103, Site 040 (Dr. Alejandro Malbrán) in Argentina enrolled 14 patients.
L1265: In Study 2103, the Applicant identified Study Site 070 (n=10; 5 assigned to icatibant) as an outlier. At this study site, patients receiving icatibant were noted to have a poorer response to icatibant (time to onset of symptom relief \geq 10 hours) compared to other study sites. Post hoc analysis performed excluding this site resulted in a statistically significant treatment difference between icatibant and placebo (2 vs 6 hours; p=0.026). According to the Applicant's own audit of data, one patient from this site (007-008) was wrongly assigned skin pain as the primary symptom instead of skin swelling. A second patient who received icatibant for an abdominal attack was later revealed to have narcotic drug addiction, violating inclusion/exclusion criteria.
L1266: Reviewer's comment: While the protocol violations at Study Site 070 in Study 2103 are noteworthy, there does not appear to be evidence of systematic bias at this study site to merit full exclusion of these site results. Further discussion of the post hoc analyses are included in Section 7 of this review.
L1267: 4.5 Compliance with Good Clinical Practices
L1268: The Applicant states that all Phase 1, 2, and 3 clinical trials performed by Jerini were conducted in accordance with applicable standards and ICH-GCP [2.5.1.4]. However, the individual study reports note several GCP violations. In Study 2102, Study Site 020 (n=6) was found to have some data entered retrospectively by the investigator for one patient. Study Site 050 (n=2) had lost source documents and both patients enrolled at this site signed informed consents after the screening visit. In Study 2103, Study Site 090 was noted to have missing source data for the two patients enrolled at this site.
L1269: 4.6 Financial Disclosures
L1270: Based upon the information provided by Jerini AG, no disclosable financial arrangements occurred for Studies 2101, 2102, and 2103.
L1271: 5 CLINICAL PHARMACOLOGY
L1272: The Applicant has submitted clinical pharmacology data generated from 4 studies in healthy subjects conducted by Jerini (1001, 1101, 1102, and 1103), and one study in patients with HAE (2101). Pharmacokinetics in patients with hepatic and/or renal impairment were assessed in Studies 2001 and 2002 as part of the development programs for other indications. The application also includes summaries of 3 preliminary studies using continuous IV infusions conducted by Hoechst in the early 1990's in anticipation of development for other indications (Studies 9101, 9103, and 9106). This review briefly focuses on the studies based on the subcutaneous formulation (Studies 1102, 1103, and 2101) to provide an overview of the formulation and dose exploration performed for icatibant and the proposed HAE indication.
L1273: 5.1 Pharmacokinetics
L1274: Icatibant has linear pharmacokinetics, with a dose-proportional increase in mean Cmax and mean AUC0-t. The elimination profile is triphasic, with the majority of the drug eliminated with a half-life of ~1 hour. There appears to be a difference in gender that is not readily explained by body weight differences, with women achieving higher plasma levels of drug. The principal findings of the main clinical pharmacology studies are summarized below.
L1275: Study 1102
L1276: Study 1102, a randomized, controlled, open crossover study in 24 subjects, assessed the bioavailability of a single SC dose of icatibant compared to IV infusion in healthy subjects. Results of the study are summarized in Table 2. The study report concludes that mean icatibant Cmax values after IV infusion are approximately 2-fold higher than after SC injection with no relevant differences found in the extent of exposure between the different administration routes or concentrations. Cmax was reached in approximately 0.5 h. Elimination half-lives were similar for both routes of administration. The pharmacokinetics of the M2 metabolite, the major metabolite (inactive), were similar between routes as well.
L1277: Table 2 Pharmacokinetic parameters for single-dose SC icatibant (Study 1102)*
