L1280: Source: je049-1102-report-body.pdf, Section 10.2.1, Table 7
L1281: Study 2101
L1282: Similar values were obtained in Study 2101, an open-label, proof-of-concept study conducted in HAE patients that evaluated single doses of 30 mg and 45 mg SC icatibant. The mean Cmax for the 30 mg dose was 1278.3 ng/ml, compared to 2229.8 ng/ml with the 45 mg dose. The mean AUC0-t for the 30 mg dose was 2555.1 ng*h/ml compared to 4886.1 ng*h/ml for the 45 mg dose. Cmax was reached in approximately 0.5 h.
L1283: Study 1103
L1284: Study 1103 was a randomized, double-blind study in 32 healthy subjects intended to evaluate the pharmacokinetics of icatibant after SC injection by gender and age. Eight males and 8 females ages 19 to 40 years and 8 males and 8 females &gt;65 years were enrolled. Patients received 5 doses of 30 mg icatibant on Day 1, and single injections of 30 mg icatibant on Days 8 and 15. No gender-specific difference in plasma exposure was observed. Females had a 1.6 to 2.4-fold higher AUC0-t (3818.6 vs. 1853.4 ng*h/ml) and 1.6 to 2.2-fold higher Cmax (1814 vs. 805.39 ng/ml) compared to males. No gender differences were noted for tmax or t1/2, but t1/2 was longer in elderly patients (&gt;1.1h) compared to younger subjects (0.5h). Multiple dose administration did not lead to accumulation of icatibant.
L1285: As part of the safety evaluation, plasma LH and FSH levels were assessed at pre-dose, 0.5, 2, 4, 6, 12, 12.5, and 14h post-dose on Day 1 and at pre-dose, 0.5, and 2h post-dose on Day 15. No consistent effect on these hormonal levels was reported.
L1286: Study 1103 included 12-lead standard ECG and Holter ECG measurements. Standard ECGs were recorded at Screening, pre-dose, 0.5, 1, and 4h after the first dose, pre-dose and 2h after each following dose on Day 1, before and 0.5, 1, and 4h post-dose on Days 8 and 15, and at the first follow-up visit (within 36 hours). Holter ECGs were recorded for 24 h on Days -1 and 1 and for 12 h on Days 8 and 15. ECGs were analyzed by a central reader. The Applicant concluded that no consistent relationship between icatibant and QT interval prolongation was observed. However, this study was reviewed by the Interdisciplinary Review Team for QT Studies who cited several examples of ST/T wave changes and/or QT prolongation in healthy patients receiving 5 doses of icatibant 30 mg SC on 3 separate days. Details of the IRT's review can be found in the review dated January 30, 2007 under IND 68,214.
L1287: Reviewer's comment: This study was reviewed by the Interdisciplinary Review Team for QT Studies, who concluded that icatibant showed evidence of QT prolongation and thus a thorough QT study was not warranted. At the pre-NDA meeting, these comments were communicated to the Applicant, and the Applicant was informed that QT prolongation would be included in product labeling unless the Applicant chose to refute these findings. The Applicant has not provided any additional data to refute these findings.
L1288: Other studies
L1289: The Applicant reports that in vitro studies have demonstrated that icatibant does not inhibit or significantly induce CYP450 isoenzymes [summary-clin-pharm.pdf, Section 2.7.2.3.1].
L1290: The pharmacokinetics of icatibant do not appear to be altered in patients with hepatic impairment or impaired renal function secondary to hepatorenal syndrome (Studies 2001 and 2002). More detailed discussion of these studies may be found in the Clinical Pharmacology reviewer's analysis.
L1291: Reviewer's comment: The gender difference in plasma exposure may be partly responsible to for the gender difference in efficacy noted between male and female patients in the Phase 3 studies, particularly Study 2102.
L1292: 5.2 Pharmacodynamics
L1293: Study 2101 evaluated several pharmacodynamic parameters at baseline, 0, 4, and 20 hours post-dose: C1 inhibitor, C4, C1q, and bradykinin. No changes in C1 INH, C4, or C1q were observed over time. Reduction of bradykinin levels from baseline was observed at 4 hours post-dose for both the 30 mg and 45 mg SC doses. In the 30 mg dose group, mean bradykinin decreased from 63.25 to 37.93 pmol/L. In the 45 mg dose group, bradykinin decreased from 82.00 to 70.67 pmol/L. The normal range for bradykinin ranges from 2.2 to 7.1 pmol/L, according to the assay used in the analysis. Presumably, the elevated bradykinin levels are due to the underlying HAE attack. Based on the time-course, icatibant does not appear to have increased bradykinin levels.
L1294: 5.3 Exposure-Response Relationships
L1295: Dose selection was based on pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic modeling using bradykinin challenge. Doses ranging from 0.005 mg/kg to 3.2 mg/kg IV icatibant in earlier studies were used to establish a dose response curve to bradykinin, based on measurements of blood pressure, heart rate, and cutaneous blood flow. The 0.4 mg/kg IV dose was selected as being sufficiently high to counteract the elevated systemic bradykinin concentrations anticipated during an HAE attack for up to 6 to 12 hours. The PK/PD modeling suggested that repeat administration was more likely to prolong the duration of effect rather than higher doses. In addition, higher doses administered subcutaneously were more likely to elicit stronger injection site reactions.
L1296: Aside from the assessments of the 30 and 45 mg SC doses performed in Study 2101, no formal dose-ranging study in HAE patients was performed. Pharmacodynamic data suggest that higher
L1297: dosing is unlikely to increase efficacy substantially and no safety issues are identified with the proposed 30 mg dose. Furthermore, establishing a true dose-response curve for icatibant may not be feasible, given the highly subjective nature of the efficacy measurements. However, it is unclear how representative the pharmacodynamic modeling is of HAE attacks. Thus, clinical dose ranging data is necessary. Alternative dosing regimens may improve the durability of response and are recommended as a consideration for future clinical trials.
L1298: 6 INTEGRATED REVIEW OF EFFICACY
L1299: 6.1 Indication
L1300: The proposed indication for icatibant is “the treatment of hereditary angioedema” in patients 18 years of age and older.
L1301: Reviewer’s comment: The proposed indication is not supported by the Phase 3 studies, which were designed to assess the efficacy of icatibant in acute attacks of HAE only, not prophylaxis or chronic treatment.
L1302: 6.1.1 Methods
L1303: The efficacy review is based on the two, Phase 3 studies conducted by Jerini, Study 2102 and Study 2103. Study 2102 was a randomized, double-blind, active-controlled trial in the treatment of patients presenting with acute cutaneous and abdominal HAE attacks with icatibant 30 mg SC versus oral tranexamic acid as an active comparator. Study 2103 had a similar study design to Study 2102 but used a placebo control. Both studies provided open-label treatment to patients presenting with laryngeal attacks and both studies were followed by open-label extension phases which permitted repeat dosing with icatibant. Detailed reviews of the individual studies are provided in the Appendix. Study 2101, a Phase 2 proof-of-concept study in HAE patients, and Study 4102, a non-interventional validation study of the patient-reported outcome instrument used in the efficacy studies, are briefly reviewed as well. The other pharmacokinetic studies discussed in Section 5 are not included in the efficacy review.
L1304: 6.1.2 General Discussion of Endpoints
L1305: The primary and secondary endpoints evaluated in Studies 2102 and 2103 were based on patient-reported (PRO) and investigator-reported outcomes. Given the variable nature of HAE attacks, more objective endpoints have not been established and PRO-based efficacy variables are considered acceptable by this reviewer if accompanied by appropriate validation and consistency between different measures is observed.
L1306: The endpoints used in the Phase 3 studies were previously discussed with the Division of Gastrointestinal and Coagulation Drug Products as part of a Special Protocol Assessment for Study 2103 and predate the publication of the Guidance for Industry: Patient-reported Outcomes Measures: Use in Medical Product Development to Support Labeling Claim. The primary
L1307: efficacy endpoint, time to onset of symptom relief, is based on a Visual Analogue Scale (VAS). At the time of the SPA, the review division stipulated that this endpoint was acceptable if adequate validation of the VAS instrument was provided in the NDA application. To meet this requirement, the Applicant has submitted results of Study 4102, a non-interventional, observational study in HAE patients intended to define the Minimum Clinically Significant Difference as measured by the VAS. The application also includes a third-party analysis (Mapi Values) of VAS and patient symptom scores collected during the Phase 3 trials (Report No. JE049-5110) and a briefing document that discusses the conceptual framework for the endpoints selected (Report No. JE049-5105). A brief review of these results and additional study reports is presented below. Study 4102 is discussed in further detail in the Appendix as well.
L1308: The primary efficacy endpoint was the time to onset of symptom relief as measured by the Visual Analogue Scale (VAS) for the single most severe, presenting symptom. The VAS is a 100 mm horizontal line with 0mm = no symptoms and 100mm = worst possible symptom. Patients mark on the line to rate the intensity of each symptoms of at baseline and pre-determined time points throughout the treatment period. The symptoms rated include the following: cutaneous swelling, cutaneous pain, abdominal pain and nausea. For cutaneous attacks, the time to onset of symptom relief was defined by “swelling” or “pain,” whichever was the most severe presenting symptom. If both were equally severe, “pain” was used as the primary endpoint. For abdominal attack patients, abdominal pain was used to assess onset of symptom relief. Time to onset of symptom relief is defined as the time between injection and time of first documented onset of symptom relief, as defined below, at 3 consecutive measurements. Subjects with no documented onset of symptom relief were to be censored at the time of their last symptom assessment.
L1309: Onset of symptom relief was originally defined as follows: Absolute reduction from pre-treatment VAS of \geq 20 mm if pre-dose VAS \geq 30 and \leq 50, or of \geq 30mm if VAS &gt; 50mm.
L1310: A modified definition of onset of relief was presented in the SAP dated August 24, 2006 for both Studies 2102 and 2103 as follows (Figure 1):
L1311: • A response to the right and below a line Y = 6/7 X - 16 with X \geq 30mm.○ X = pre-treatment VAS in mm○ Y = post-treatment VAS in mm• Corresponds to a reduction by 30 mm at a baseline VAS = 100 mm and by 21 mm at a baseline VAS = 30 mm.
L1312: Figure 1 Study 2102: Definition of onset of symptom relief by VAS as presented in the statistical analysis plan dated August 24, 2006
L1313: The figure is a scatter plot titled "Symptom Relief". The x-axis is labeled "Baseline VAS (mm)" and ranges from 0 to 100. The y-axis is labeled "Outcome (mm)" and also ranges from 0 to 100. There are 10 data points plotted, showing a positive linear correlation. A regression line is drawn through the points with the equation y = 0.8571x - 16.Baseline VAS (mm)Outcome (mm)3010351540204525503055356040654570507555806085659070957510080Figure 1: A scatter plot titled 'Symptom Relief' showing the relationship between Baseline VAS (mm) on the x-axis and Outcome (mm) on the y-axis. The data points follow a linear trend with the regression line y = 0.8571x - 16. The x-axis and y-axis both range from 0 to 100 mm.
L1314: Source: je049-2102-statistical.pdf, Section 3
L1315: Secondary endpoints included time to relief of each symptom present in pre-dose VAS other than the primary symptom, time to almost complete symptom relief (0-10 mm on VAS), response rate at 4 hours, regression of symptoms (start of improvement), and individual symptom severity scoring on a 5-point scale of none to very severe. Investigators also scored individual symptoms, as well as making Global Assessments, Clinical Global Impression, observable regression of symptoms, and overall patient improvement. Laryngeal attacks were treated on an open-label basis. Patients and investigators scored symptoms on a similar 5-point severity scale, rating dysphagia and voice change. Investigators made additional assessments of breathing difficulties, stridor, and asphyxia.
L1316: Reviewer's comment: While the use of a PRO for measuring symptoms is reasonable for HAE attacks, the Applicant's definition of symptom relief is quite complicated and not entirely intuitive. Time to onset of symptom relief is farther removed from the measured symptom scores and is not easily described. Also, the primary endpoint is based on identification of a predominant symptom, which may not capture the extent of an HAE attack or may not be possible for different patient presentations. No formal composite symptom scoring by patients was assessed, which would be a useful indicator of the patient's overall response to treatment.
L1317: Study 4102
L1318: Study 4102 was an observational, non-interventional study in 80 adult HAE patients presenting with an acute abdominal and/or cutaneous HAE attack of at least moderate severity. The objective of the study was to identify the MCSD for the VAS instrument. Patients received the standard of care as determined by the physician and were asked to complete patient diaries until complete resolution of their symptoms or 48 hours from baseline, whichever occurred first. In addition to patient symptom scores, patients were asked to complete the VAS and a Verbal Descriptor Scale (VDS), which the Applicant designated as the "gold standard". Based on comparison to the VDS, a 9 mm change in VAS was identified as the MCSD for "onset of symptom relief." This cut-point provided 88.24% specificity and 82.61% sensitivity at 6 hours post baseline. Changes in the VAS corresponded to changes in the VDS (r=0.7576; p&lt;0.0001). A more complete discussion of Study 4102 is located in the Appendix.
L1319: Reviewer's comment: Although the study demonstrated statistically significant correlation between the VAS and VDS, the value of this correlation is uncertain. The Applicant proposes the VDS as the “gold standard,” which begs the question as to why the VDS was not used as a primary endpoint or even a key secondary endpoint. Furthermore, the study did not include any patient interviews to substantiate the terms used in the VAS or VDS, nor did it provide illustrative examples for patients filling out patient diaries. For example, the concept of “a little less” symptom may vary widely among individuals with HAE. In addition, the Applicant has not reconciled the discordance in VAS and VDS observed in several study participants' diaries (n=7 out of 80 patients who completed the study). Whether the discordance reflects a lack of comprehension on the part of the patient or error in data collection is uncertain. In either circumstance, the observed discordance with the proposed gold standard is problematic and raises concerns regarding the validity of the VAS instrument for HAE attacks.
L1320: Post-hoc analyses of patient diary data from pivotal studies (Study Report 5110) and PRO validation briefing document (Study Report 5105)
L1321: The application includes a study report, “Validation of PRO endpoints using pooled clinical trial data from the FAST hereditary angioedema studies,” Report No. JE049-5110. Post-hoc analyses patient diary entries from Studies 2102 and 2103 were compared to physician assessments to assess the test-retest reliability and clinical validity. The VAS appeared to have limited test-retest validity compared to patient individual symptom scores, although the retest validity was not formally assessed. The VAS and patient symptom scores performed comparably in terms of clinical validity and responsiveness when compared against various physician assessments.
L1322: In addition, the application includes a briefing document that provides an overview of the conceptual framework for the endpoints selected. The study report cites market research in HAE patients that identifies the key symptoms and attack locations relevant to acute HAE attacks as represented in the VAS PRO. Third-party translation and back-translation was used for most countries, except for Argentina (Spanish), Canada (Canadian French), Austria, and Israel (Israel) where back translation was not performed. No patient testing of the translations was conducted.
L1323: Reviewer's comment: In general, the VAS is difficult to interpret. Although the time to onset of symptom relief statistically correlates with patient-reported start of improvement, the actual times reported vary. As a result, it is unclear whether the VAS measurement or the patient symptom score reflects the true onset of symptom relief. The time to onset of symptom relief as assessed by the VAS is not entirely intuitive or easily described. Validation of this PRO instrument is limited by the lack of cognitive debriefing interviews and patient-based test-retest evaluations.
L1324: 6.1.3 Study Design
L1325: 6.1.3.1 General study design of Phase 3 studies
L1326: The general study design was similar for both Phase 3 studies. Studies 2102 and 2103 were randomized, double-blind, multicenter safety and efficacy studies. The key difference between
L1327: the studies was the controls used; Study 2102 used an active control, oral tranexamic acid, while Study 2103 used a placebo control. The appropriateness of tranexamic acid as an active control is discussed in Section 6.1.3.2.
L1328: In both studies, patients 18 years of age and older presenting with an acute abdominal or cutaneous HAE attack of at least moderate severity within 6 hours of onset of symptoms were randomized to icatibant or the other treatment group. Patients were then observed for up to 48 hours, during which time clinical assessments were made at regular intervals. Patients presenting with a laryngeal edema attack were not randomized but were eligible to receive a single dose of icatibant 30 mg SC. Patients who participated in the double-blind treatment phase or received open-label treatment for a laryngeal attack were then eligible to participate in an open-label extension (OLE) phase. For the OLE, any attack severe enough to warrant treatment qualified for treatment with icatibant 30 mg SC. If the attack worsened within 48 hours of initial treatment, additional injections were permitted (maximum of 3 injections per attack at least 6 hours apart). The OLE was later further modified to enroll patients who met original study criteria but who had not participated in the double-blind phase or who did not have an attack sufficiently severe to qualify during the double-blind phase. The OLE studies are ongoing and presently there is no limitation to the total number of attacks for which a patient may receive icatibant.
L1329: Reviewer's comment: The general study design is acceptable for demonstrating the efficacy of icatibant for the treatment of acute HAE attacks. However, the proposed indication, treatment of HAE, is broad and not supported by the design of the Phase 3 studies. A general treatment indication would be expected to include studies addressing chronic and short-term prophylaxis of attacks, in addition to treatment of acute attacks. Dose-ranging data to support the 30 mg SC icatibant dose is limited, as discussed in Section 5.
L1330: 6.1.3.2 Selection of control groups
L1331: Only one of the two pivotal studies used a placebo-control design. Study 2102 used a double-dummy design with oral tranexamic acid (1g by mouth every 6 to 8 hours for 2 days) as an active comparator. The efficacy of tranexamic acid, a synthetic antifibrinolytic related to epsilon-aminocaproic acid, for treatment of acute HAE attacks is not established. Currently, tranexamic acid is not approved for HAE treatment in the US. It is marketed in the US under the trade name, Cyklokapron®, for the prophylaxis and treatment of hemorrhage in hemophiliac patients undergoing tooth extraction. Tranexamic acid is approved in other countries for other indications related to its antifibrinolytic properties, such as dysfunctional uterine bleeding. Tranexamic acid is approved in a few countries, including the European Union and South Africa, for hereditary angioedema. The foreign package inserts do not specify whether the indication is for chronic or acute treatment of HAE. In general, the literature to support the use of tranexamic acid for acute intervention is very limited. A PubMed literature search using the terms “tranexamic acid,” “hereditary angioedema,” and “hereditary angioneurotic (o)edema” yields few references with clinical trial data. The three studies referenced by this reviewer includes a single blinded trial conducted by Blohme et al, which demonstrated that 1 out of 2 patients treated benefited from treatment of tranexamic acid during an acute HAE attack. Ohela et al. observed that 6 out of 7 patients who received open-label tranexamic acid during a period that
L1332: ranged from 3 to 22 months appeared to have acute attacks of lessened severity. Finally, in a longitudinal study of HAE patients, Agostoni et al. reported that 27 patients appeared to have less severe attacks when given tranexamic acid for acute attacks.
L1333: Reviewer's comment: The treatment difference between tranexamic acid and icatibant is difficult to interpret, since the efficacy of tranexamic acid in acute HAE attacks is not established. Also, oral bioavailability may be compromised or unpredictable in cases of mucosal edema resulting in altered gut absorption, in addition to vomiting and diarrhea. Theoretically, a statistically significant treatment difference between tranexamic acid and icatibant could be attributed to tranexamic acid worsening an HAE attack. The Applicant has provided an expert opinion summarizing that at the very least, tranexamic acid is not worse than placebo. Most likely, this statement is correct but the data to support this assertion is limited. For example, tranexamic acid in Study 2102 appears to perform appreciably worse than the placebo arm in Study 2103. While the validity of such a cross-study comparison may be limited, the comparison certainly does not support the Applicant's assertion.
L1334: 6.1.3.3 Patient selection criteria
L1335: Both studies included adult patients with a documented diagnosis of either Type I or Type II HAE. The diagnosis could be confirmed either by functional and immunogenic C1-INH results from the central laboratory or by medical history. Inclusion on medical history alone was permitted if a clear diagnosis had been made on all of the following criteria:
L1336: • Family history• Characteristic attack manifestations, recurrent attacks• Historical functional C1-INH &lt;50%• Exclusion of other forms of angioedema
L1337: Patients with C1-INH &gt;50% could be included if an acceptable rationale for the higher level was provided.
L1338: Reviewer's comment: These inclusion/exclusion criteria could be more stringent for confirming the diagnosis of HAE as opposed to acquired angioedema (AAE). Although both functional C1-INH and C4 levels were collected, the inclusion criteria were based only on the C1-INH level. C1q levels, which are ordinarily depressed in AAE but normal in HAE, were not obtained for the study and were not routinely documented. Although a diagnosis of acquired angioedema (AAE) is an exclusion criterion, it is possible that patients with AAE who have previously been presumed to have HAE may have been enrolled in this study.
L1339: 6.1.3.4 Blinding
L1340: Although both studies were blinded, the adequacy of the blinding may have been compromised by the near ubiquity of local injection site reactions associated with icatibant in contrast to placebo injections. Furthermore, tranexamic acid is associated with GI disturbances (nausea, vomiting, and diarrhea), as well as a distinctive adverse effect of color vision disturbances, which may have further compromised blinding.
L1341: Reviewer’s comment: As the primary and secondary endpoints are based on subjective symptom assessments, incomplete blinding further undermines the strength of the efficacy findings.
L1342: 6.1.4 Efficacy Findings
L1343: 6.1.4.1 Patient population and disposition
L1344: Table 3 shows the enrollment and patient disposition in each of the two efficacy studies. A total of 63 patients received icatibant 30 mg SC, 38 patients received tranexamic acid, and 29 patients received placebo in the two Phase 3 studies. An additional 3 patients in Study 2102 and 8 patients in Study 2103 received open-label icatibant for an initial HAE attack with laryngeal symptoms.

L1347: 6.1.4.2 Baseline characteristics
L1348: Table 4 summarizes the demographics and baseline HAE characteristics of the patients enrolled in both studies. The patient populations for the two studies differed slightly. The patients enrolled in Study 2102 were slightly older (mean age 41.1 years) compared to patients in Study 2103 (mean age 34.9 years). Study 2102 was racially homogenous, whereas Study 2103 enrolled several non-Caucasian patients. The two studies were comparable in overall gender distribution. In addition, Study 2103 had a balanced number of abdominal versus cutaneous attacks, whereas Study 2102 had a proportionally greater number of cutaneous attacks. The Applicant states that this disparity in attack site may explain the lack of statistically significant findings in Study 2103, which is discussed in more detail in Section 6.1.4.3. Baseline attack frequency, as assessed by attack history over the prior 6 months, appeared comparable between the two studies and suggested that most patients had fairly active disease with regular attacks.

L1352: 6.1.4.3 Primary efficacy findings
L1353: 6.1.4.3.1 Primary efficacy findings: Study 2102
L1354: Onset of symptom relief for the ITT population is shown in Table 5. For all attacks, the treatment difference between icatibant and tranexamic groups was 10.0 hours (p &lt; 0.001). Also shown in the table are the median times to onset of symptom relief for the subpopulation of patients with cutaneous versus abdominal HAE attacks.

L1358: Reviewer's comment : The icatibant group had a statistically significant faster onset of symptom relief compared to the tranexamic acid group overall, although the treatment difference between icatibant and tranexamic acid is much less in the abdominal attack subgroup compared to the cutaneous group (1.9 hours versus 15.7 hours, respectively). According to the Applicant, pain symptoms in general are more likely to respond to treatment, including placebo. Since abdominal pain was the abdominal symptom used for VAS scoring, the Applicant states that abdominal attacks were more likely to show improvement after any treatment. Whether this explanation is true is uncertain. When looking at time to onset of symptom relief for individual symptoms aside from the primary symptom, skin pain does not appear to have the same "placebo response." The literature does not indicate that abdominal attacks differ from cutaneous attacks in terms of bradykinin pathophysiology.
L1359: Subgroup analysis suggested a differential response according to gender. The time to onset of symptom relief was greater in male patients (3.5 hours) compared to female (1.6 hours) patients treated with icatibant. The small sample size limits the utility of subgroup analysis, but there is no clear explanation for the differences seen in male versus female patients. In terms of types of attacks, a similar proportion of patients were male versus female in the icatibant cutaneous and abdominal attack subgroups (see Table 4). Pharmacokinetic assessments show higher plasma levels in females, but pharmacodynamic data from clinical pharmacology studies suggest that a 30 mg dose should be sufficient for maximal bradykinin inhibition in both sexes. No differences were observed between patients weighing 50-75 kg and 75-100kg. As the entire study population was reported as Caucasian, no subgroup analysis by race was provided.
L1360: Reviewer's comment: As shown in Section 6.1.4.3.2, it appears that male patients appear to have slower resolution of HAE attacks based on data from the placebo group. Review of the literature does not indicate any gender-based differences in terms of HAE attack severity, frequency, or course, so the reason for the observed discrepancy remains unknown.
L1361: 6.1.4.3.2 Primary efficacy findings: Study 2103
L1362: Onset of symptom relief for the ITT population is shown in Table 6. For all attacks, the treatment difference between icatibant and placebo groups was 2.1 hours (p=0.142). Also shown in the table are the median times to onset of symptom relief for the subpopulation of patients with cutaneous versus abdominal HAE attacks.

L1366: Reviewer's comment: Although the VAS results were numerically favorable to icatibant, icatibant failed to demonstrate a statistically significant quicker onset of symptom relief compared to placebo. The clinical relevance of the 2.1 hour treatment difference is difficult to assess, as HAE attacks may be quite variable and the natural course for most attacks is spontaneous resolution. There did not appear to be any clear differences in icatibant efficacy in cutaneous versus abdominal HAE attacks.
L1367: The Applicant states that an exaggerated placebo response was observed in Study 2103 due to the higher number of patients with abdominal versus cutaneous attacks enrolled compared to Study 2102. According to the Applicant, pain symptoms in general are more likely to be responsive to placebo. Since abdominal pain was the base symptom scored on the VAS, patients presenting with abdominal attacks were more likely to show improvement, regardless of allocation to treatment group. Whether this explanation is true is uncertain.
L1368: Unlike Study 2102, subgroup analysis did not show any statistically significant differences between male and female patients. When divided by weight, patients weighting between 75 to 100kg treated with icatibant were observed to have a faster onset of symptom relief compared to their placebo counterparts (3.9 v. 23.0 hours; p=0.030). No statistically significant differences were noted in patients weighing 50 to 100 kg (n=3) or patients &gt;100 kg (n=9). These differences do not appear clinically relevant and more likely are a reflection of the small sample size in the study.
L1369: 6.1.4.4 Secondary efficacy findings
L1370: 6.1.4.4.1 Secondary efficacy findings of Study 2102
L1371: The Applicant performed analysis on multiple secondary endpoints, including response rate at 4 hours, time to relief on non-primary symptoms, time to almost complete symptom relief (VAS 0-10mm), assessment of each symptom by severity, durability of response, start of improvement, and rescue medication use. Several of these endpoints were not pre-specified and no adjustment was made for multiplicity. No composite symptom scoring was performed by the patients. In general, the secondary endpoints supported the efficacy of icatibant over tranexamic acid. This portion of the review provides an overview of the patient-derived endpoints that were deemed most clinically relevant and provided added perspective on the primary efficacy variable. Additional patient-reported endpoints and physician assessments are presented in detail in the Appendix.
L1372: Responder analysis
L1373: As a form of responder analysis, the study report presents response rate at 4 hours after start of treatment, using the same definition of response as used in the primary efficacy endpoint. At 4 hours after start of treatment, the response rate based on the primary symptom was 80.0% (n=28) for icatibant versus 30.6% (n=11) for tranexamic acid (p &lt; 0.001). In the cutaneous attack subgroup, the response rate at 4 hours was 73.9% (n=17) versus 17.4% (n=4), respectively (p &lt; 0.001). In the abdominal attack subgroup, the response rate was 91.7% (n=11) versus 53.8% (n=7), respectively (p = 0.073).
L1374: Reviewer's comment: One issue with this definition of responder is that patients who may have persistent moderate-to-severe symptoms would be considered responders, e.g. if a patient has a baseline VAS of 100mm that decreases to 70mm, that patient would be classified as a responder. Also, since the VAS is based on a single primary symptom, a "responder" may have other symptoms that worsen or develop from baseline which would not be captured by this assessment.
L1375: Durability of response
L1376: A durable response was defined as onset of symptom relief for the primary symptom within 8 hours after treatment that lasted for at least 24 hours. In the icatibant group, 68.6% (n=24) reported a durable response, compared to 38.9% (n=14) in the tranexamic acid group (p = 0.017). For cutaneous attacks, 65.2% (n=15) of icatibant patients compared to 21.7% (n=5) of tranexamic acid patients reported a durable response (p = 0.007). For abdominal attacks, 75.0% (n=9) of icatibant patients reported a durable response compared to 69.2% (n=9) of tranexamic acid patients (p = 1.0).
L1377: Reviewer's comment: Icatibant performed favorably in terms of durability of response compared to tranexamic acid for all attacks and the subset of cutaneous attacks. No differences were seen, however, in the abdominal attack group. Overall, less than ¾ of patients reported a durable response. Whether a more durable response could be achieved with repeat dosing is unclear. Of note, the definition does not take into account rescue medication use, although in general, the tranexamic acid group received more rescue medications.
L1378: Assessment of each symptom by severity
L1379: In general, statistically significant differences were observed for individual cutaneous symptoms scored by patients at the various time points between icatibant and tranexamic acid treatment groups. For abdominal symptoms, abdominal pain was scored more favorably by icatibant patients starting at 1 h post-dose and sustained through the Visit 3 timepoint (p=0.001 to 0.045). No statistically significant differences were reported for nausea, vomiting, or diarrhea. This analysis only includes patients who reported the symptom as at least of mild severity at baseline.
L1380: Regression of symptoms (start of improvement) according to the patient
L1381: Icatibant patients reported a median time to start of improvement of 0.8 hours, compared to 7.9 hours for tranexamic acid patients (p&lt;0.001).
L1382: Reviewer's comment: The start of improvement by this symptom index differs from the onset of symptom relief as estimated by the VAS. The Applicant has not conducted any separate studies to validate this patient-reported endpoint. In addition, this particular endpoint is not described in the protocol and the study report does not describe how the information was collected (retrospectively versus prospectively). However, it is worth noting the disparity in this measurement and the time to onset of symptom relief as assessed by the VAS.
L1383: Rescue medication use
L1384: Although not evaluated as a formal secondary efficacy endpoint, the data is reviewed here as a reflection of icatibant's efficacy. Seven patients in the icatibant group and 12 patients in the tranexamic acid group received rescue medication for the initial HAE attack. The most commonly administered rescue medication was C1-INH (n=5 in the icatibant group; n=8 in the tranexamic acid group). Other medications administered included opioids for pain relief and glucocorticoids. Rates of rescue medication usage were similar between cutaneous and abdominal attack groups. The study report states that no patients in the icatibant group required rescue treatment during the first 12 hours after administration of study drug, compared to 5 patients in the tranexamic acid group.
L1385: Reviewer's comment: Rescue medication usage data is supportive of icatibant's efficacy.
L1386: 6.1.4.4.2 Secondary efficacy findings of Study 2103
L1387: The secondary efficacy endpoints evaluated in Study 2103 are the same as those endpoints evaluated for Study 2102. In general, the secondary endpoints were numerically favorable but did not show a statistically significant benefit for icatibant over placebo. The data are suggestive of icatibant's efficacy for acute HAE attacks but are not robust findings.
L1388: Durability of response
L1389: A durable response was defined as onset of symptom relief for the primary symptom within 8 hours after treatment that lasted for at least 24 hours. In the icatibant group, 51.9% reported a durable response, similar to the 50.0% in the placebo group (p=1.0). For cutaneous attacks, 57.1% of icatibant patients compared to 38.5% of placebo patients reported a durable response
L1390: (p=0.449). For abdominal attacks, 46.2% of icatibant patients reported a durable response compared to 60.0% of placebo patients (p=0.705).
L1391: Reviewer's comment: Icatibant and placebo groups performed similarly in terms of durability of response. Results were numerically unfavorable for icatibant for patients with abdominal attacks. These results suggest that icatibant's impact on the overall resolution of an acute attack may be limited.
L1392: Response rate at 4 hours after start of treatment
L1393: At 4 hours after start of treatment, the response rate based on the primary symptom was 66.7% for icatibant versus 46.4% for placebo (0.176). When classified by cutaneous versus abdominal attacks, no statistically significant differences were noted as well (p=0.252 and 0.435, respectively).
L1394: Assessment of each symptom by severity
L1395: No consistent, statistically significant differences were observed for individual cutaneous symptoms scored by patients at the various time points between icatibant and placebo treatment groups. For abdominal symptoms, both abdominal pain and nausea were scored more favorably by icatibant patients starting at 1 h post-dose and sustained through the 12-h timepoint (p=0.001 to 0.033).
L1396: Regression of symptoms (start of improvement) according to the patient
L1397: Icatibant patients reported a median time to start of improvement of 0.8 hours, compared to 16.9 hours for placebo patients (p&lt;0.001).
L1398: Reviewer's comment: As noted for the results of Study 2102, the disparity between this measure and the VAS time to onset of symptom relief for individual patients is noteworthy. To some extent, the "start of improvement" according to the patient is more easily understood and provides a composite score of the patient's overall attack status.
L1399: Rescue medication use
L1400: Although not presented as a formal secondary endpoint, rescue medication use is included here as a reflection of icatibant's efficacy in relieving symptoms of an acute HAE attack. Twenty patients total received rescue medication: 6 patients in the icatibant group and 14 in the placebo group. The most commonly administered rescue medication were opioids for pain relief. One patient (015-006) in the placebo group subsequently developed laryngeal symptoms and was treated with open-label icatibant as a rescue medication.
L1401: Reviewer's comment: On this basis, icatibant compares favorably to placebo. The Applicant has also provided additional subgroup analysis of the primary efficacy endpoint in terms of patients who were using attenuated androgens as a concomitant medication versus those who were not. Icatibant appears to be more efficacious in patients without androgen (p=0.019); plausibly, the concomitant use of androgens may blunt the treatment difference observed for icatibant. However, the numbers are small, making subgroups analysis difficult.
L1402: 6.1.4.5 Laryngeal attack efficacy results
L1403: Overall, the data to support icatibant’s efficacy in laryngeal attacks was limited by the small number of subjects. In Study 2102, 3 patients presented with laryngeal attacks during the controlled phase of the study. In this study, 2 of the 3 patients reported a regression of symptoms by 0.3 and 1 hour post-icatibant. The third patient was intubated and unable to complete symptoms scoring during the acute attack, but was successfully extubated 8 hours later and reported regression of symptoms 24 hours after icatibant administration. Time to onset of symptom relief as assessed by the VAS was not reported.
L1404: In Study 2103, 8 patients were treated with open-label icatibant during the controlled phase, and the median time to regression of symptoms as reported by the patients was 0.6 hours. Symptom scoring by both patients and investigators at 0, 2, and 4 hours after study drug administration is presented below. Patients were asked to score only 2 attributes of a laryngeal attack, voice changes and difficulties in swallowing. Although laryngeal attacks are typically considered the most severe manifestation of HAE, the cases in Study 2103 appeared to be mostly mild in severity.
L1405: Table 7 Study 2103: Laryngeal attack symptom scores as reported by patients (N=8 attacks in 8 patients)


SeverityVoice changeDifficulties in swallowing

L1406: Source: je049-2103-report-body.pdf, Section 11.4.3, Table 43, Page 128
L1407: During the OLE phase of Study 2102 and 2103, an additional 8 attacks in 4 patients and 11 attacks in 6 patients, respectively, were treated with open-label icatibant. A median time to regression of symptoms or onset of symptom relief was not reported for Study 2102’s OLE, but symptom scores reported at 0, 2, and 4 hours suggested that most patients experienced symptom improvement within 4 hours of treatment. For Study 2103’s OLE, the time to regression of symptoms ranged from 0.3 to 1.4 hours, consistent with prior observations. The overall severity of laryngeal attacks in the OLE phase appears to have been more severe than that reported during the controlled phase of Study 2103.
L1408: Reviewer’s comment: The small sample size and lack of placebo control limits the interpretation of these results. Whether the improvements observed over time were attributable to icatibant or reflected the gradual resolution of the attack is difficult to discern, especially in cases where the presenting symptoms were mild at baseline. In one report in the literature (Bork and Barnstedt), patients with untreated laryngeal attacks averaged 100.8h (324 attack episodes) from onset of symptoms to time of symptom resolution. However, the baseline severity of these cases is not well-documented, thus limiting the utility of these cases as a historical control. Furthermore, laryngeal efficacy data in the icatibant Phase 3 program were presented descriptively and time
L1409: to onset of symptom relief or median time to regression were not consistently reported, complicating the comparison of individual cases.
L1410: 6.1.4.6 Open-label extension efficacy data
L1411: In general, the open-label extension data from both Phase 3 studies were consistent with results obtained from the respective controlled-phases of each study and were supportive of icatibant's efficacy, in the absence of formal statistical comparisons. The onset of symptom relief was evaluated in the open-label extension phase by the number of attacks treated with icatibant.
L1412: In Study 2102, the greatest number of attacks for any one patient treated in this portion of the study was 38. (More detailed information regarding total drug exposure is discussed in the Appendix, Section 10.2.2, Drug exposure.) The onset of symptom relief ranged from 1.5 to 12.0 hours for all attacks and 1.6 to 2.5 hours for patients where data was available for 10 or more patients (2nd and 3rd attacks).
L1413: In Study 2103, the greatest number of attacks treated for any one patient in this portion of the study was 12. More detailed information regarding total drug exposure is discussed in the Appendix, Section 10.3.2.5, Safety analysis. The onset of symptom relief ranged from 1.0 to 4.0 hours for all reported attacks and 1.5 to 1.8 hours for patients who experienced 2 to 4 attacks during the OLE (n=59).
L1414: 6.1.4.7 Post hoc efficacy analyses
L1415: In Study 2103, the Applicant identified Study Site 070 (n=10; 5 assigned to icatibant) as an outlier. At this study site, patients receiving icatibant were noted to have a poorer response to icatibant (time to onset of symptom relief \geq 10 hours) compared to other study sites. Post hoc analysis performed excluding this site resulted in a statistically significant treatment difference between icatibant and placebo (2.0 vs 6.0 hours; p=0.026). According to the Applicant's own audit of data, one patient from this site (007-008) was wrongly assigned skin pain as the primary symptom instead of skin swelling. A second patient who received icatibant for an abdominal attack was later revealed during the audit to have narcotic drug addiction, violating inclusion/exclusion criteria. A third patient (015-007) at Study Site 015 was found to have a history of pancreatitis, which may have confounded the reporting of abdominal pain symptoms. Post hoc analysis performed excluding Patients 007-014 and 015-007 and reassignment of Patient 007-008 for the primary symptom resulted in a statistically significant treatment difference between icatibant and placebo (2.0 vs 5.0 hours; p=0.049). Post hoc analysis performed excluding Patients 007-014 and 015-007 results in a treatment difference 2.0 vs. 4.6 hours (p=0.069).
L1416: Reviewer's comment: While the protocol violations at Study Site 070 are noteworthy, there does not appear to be evidence of systematic bias at this study site to merit full exclusion of these site results. Given the small total sample size, post hoc exclusion of 10 patients from the efficacy database, including 3 patients with laryngeal edema attacks, is far from trivial. Also, as the unfavorable direction of the results prompted the data audit for this particular study site, the
L1417: possibility of bias in this type of post hoc analysis remains an issue. From the perspective of the clinical review, these post hoc analyses are considered supportive of efficacy but are not considered as a substitute for the pre-specified analyses based on the ITT population.
L1418: Additional post hoc analysis stratifying the results of Study 2103 by US versus non-US locations indicates that efficacy results were fairly comparable, without any clear geographic differences. Further details regarding post-hoc analysis can be found in the statistical team’s review. According to the statistical reviewer, the median time to onset of symptom relief for icatibant recipients at US and non-US locations was 2.25 and 2.5 hours respectively. In the placebo arm, the times were 7.0 and 3.6 hours, respectively.
L1419: 6.1.5 Clinical Microbiology
L1420: Icatibant is not an antimicrobial drug product, and the application does not contain any clinical microbiology data.
L1421: 6.1.6 Efficacy Conclusions
L1422: The Applicant has provided reports for two Phase 3 efficacy and safety studies, Study 2102 and Study 2103, as well as uncontrolled efficacy data on laryngeal attack treatment and treatment of abdominal and cutaneous HAE attacks during the open-label portions of each study. Only Study 2103 was placebo-controlled. While the results were numerically favorable, this study failed to show a statistically significant benefit for icatibant over placebo in the treatment of acute HAE attacks. Study 2102 did show a statistically significant benefit for icatibant over the active comparator, but the efficacy of the active comparator in the treatment of acute HAE attacks is not established, making the comparison more difficult to interpret. Notably, the time to onset of symptom relief for the tranexamic treatment arm is considerably longer than the median time observed for the placebo arm in Study 2103. While the reliability of such a cross-study comparison may be questionable, the assertion that tranexamic acid should perform no worse than placebo is not reinforced by the information presented in the application. As a result, the application does not provide sufficient data to support the proposed indication, treatment of hereditary angioedema.
L1423: In the absence of a conclusive study in the current clinical program, at least one additional placebo-controlled study with a comparable sample size is required. Ideally, the additional study would use a primary efficacy endpoint that has confirmed test-retest validity and transparency. A composite symptom endpoint, such as the Verbal Descriptor Scale used as the “gold standard” in Study 4102, is a candidate endpoint if supported by validation methods as described in the Guidance for Industry: Patient-reported Outcomes Measures: Use in Medical Product Development to Support Labeling Claims. The VAS could be included as a key secondary endpoint to facilitate comparison with the data obtained from Studies 2102 and 2103. Alternatively, the same primary efficacy endpoint used in Studies 2102 and 2103 may be used in another placebo-controlled study if the Applicant provides additional information to support the validity of the VAS PRO instrument. Of note, such a study would support an indication for treatment of acute HAE attacks. Should the Applicant seek a broader indication such as the one
L1424: proposed in this application, treatment of HAE, the clinical development program will require additional studies that assess the use of icatibant in short-term and chronic prophylaxis as well.
L1425: 7 INTEGRATED REVIEW OF SAFETY
L1426: 7.1 Methods and Findings
L1427: Safety was assessed in the clinical studies with reports of adverse events, laboratory values, vital signs, and physical exams. A total of 63 patients treated with a single injection of icatibant 30 mg SC in the controlled phase of the two Phase 3 studies is the focus of this Integrated Review of Safety. Additional safety information obtained from 118 patients treated for a total of 602 HAE attacks with open-label icatibant during the Phase 3 studies is also reviewed and discussed. Additional discussion is found in the individual study reports (Appendix, Sections 10.2 and 10.3). A few patients treated during the open-label phase received up to 3 30-mg doses for a single attack, but the majority of patients were treated with 1 injection per attack. Exposure data are summarized in Table 8, Table 20, Table 25, Table 35, and Table 36. Table 8 shows the number of patients by the number of attacks treated and doses administered, as well as the number of patients who received 1, 2, or 3 sequential 30-mg icatibant injections for a single attack. Additional safety information from a Phase 2 study in HAE patients was reviewed as well, but given the small number of patients and consistency with the data obtained from the Phase 3 studies, these results are not discussed in detail here but may be found in the Appendix.
L1428: The overall safety profile of icatibant 30 mg was acceptable without any concerning safety signals. The most relevant safety issues for this review were local injection site reactions and recurrent or progressive hereditary angioedema attacks.

L1431: 7.1.1 Deaths
L1432: No deaths were reported in any patients who received icatibant. One death was reported in Study 2102 for a 53-year-old male randomized to the tranexamic acid treatment arm. Forty-one days after drug administration, the patient collapsed and died. An autopsy showed coronary artery disease and aortic stenosis.
L1433: 7.1.2 Other Serious Adverse Events
L1434: Controlled phase
L1435: In addition to the patient death described above in Study 2102, 5 SAEs were reported in 4 patients who received icatibant.
L1436: • Patient 75-013 experienced a severe laryngeal HAE attack requiring tracheostomy 5 months after the last receipt of icatibant.• Patient 90-004 experienced an abdominal HAE attack 2 weeks after initial treatment with icatibant, notably accompanied by hypotension, pyuria, and microhematuria. She was deemed fully recovered by 5 days after treatment with C1 INH concentrate, volume resuscitation, and antibiotic therapy. Additional icatibant was not administered.• Patient 16-005 was hospitalized for viral gastroenteritis and hypertensive crisis 3 months after receipt of icatibant.• Patient 075-014 experienced a laryngeal HAE attack, the same attack that qualified him for treatment with open-label icatibant in the study.
L1437: No SAEs were reported during the controlled phase for Study 2103.
L1438: OLE phase
L1439: In Study 2102's OLE, 5 patients experienced 9 SAEs, including HAE and renal failure requiring hospitalization (4 months after last treatment with icatibant), elective tooth extraction requiring hospitalization for precautionary monitoring, rotavirus-associated diarrhea with increased pancreatic enzymes requiring hospitalization (3 months post-icatibant), and a motor vehicle accident with minor head injury and hospitalization (2 days post-icatibant). The motor vehicle accident occurred in the setting of road flooding and hazardous driving conditions.
L1440: In Study 2103's OLE, 4 SAEs were reported for 4 patients, including 2 events of pancreatitis associated with an abdominal HAE attack in one patient (10 and 14 days post-icatibant; prior history of pancreatitis), worsened abdominal HAE attack despite 3 sequential doses of icatibant, and chest pain accompanied by abdominal and cutaneous HAE symptoms (7 days post-icatibant).
L1441: Phase 2 (Study 2101)
L1442: Two SAEs were reported for Study 2101. One patient with a longstanding history of schizoaffective disorder and 8 prior psychiatric hospitalizations experienced an acute manic episode 7 days after infusion of 0.4 mg/kg IV over 2 hours. CK levels were reported as elevated (level not reported), as had been noted in past psychiatric hospitalizations for the patient. A second patient reported recurrence and worsening of HAE symptoms 20 hours after treatment with 30 mg SC icatibant and was hospitalized for an additional 12 hours prior to resolution of his symptoms.
L1443: Reviewer's comment: The timing of the SAEs makes a causal relationship with icatibant appear less likely. In the case of the CK abnormalities observed in the Phase 2 study, CK elevations during psychotic episodes have been previously reported in the literature and seem unlikely to have been caused by a single dose exposure to icatibant.
L1444: 7.1.3 Dropouts and Other Significant Adverse Events
L1445: 7.1.3.1 Overall profile of dropouts
L1446: Of patients treated with icatibant, 1 patient discontinued prematurely from Study 2102 due to a desire to become pregnant. One patient discontinued early from Study 2102's OLE due to unavailability for follow-up. In Study 2103, one patient discontinued early after completion of Visit 6. According to the study report, the patient is reported as discontinuing for "other" reasons but according to the CRF narrative, the patient withdrew consent. During the OLE phase, 4 patients were lost to follow-up, 1 patient completed Visit 6 but was unable to attend the 6-month follow-up visit due to a motor vehicle accident and scheduling problems, 1 patient withdrew citing lack of efficacy, and 4 patients withdrew consent after Visit 4 for reasons not specified.
L1447: No patients from the controlled phase of either study discontinued due to adverse events. No premature discontinuations were reported for the Phase 2 study, Study 2101.
L1448: 7.1.3.2 Adverse events associated with dropouts
L1449: No dropouts associated with adverse events were reported.
L1450: 7.1.3.3 Other significant adverse events
L1451: No other significant adverse events are reported by the Applicant or noted in the safety database.
L1452: 7.1.4 Other Search Strategies
L1453: Icatibant and pharmacologically related products are not currently marketed for any indication, so no post-marketing data or additional clinical reports of safety in the literature are available.
L1454: 7.1.5 Common Adverse Events
L1455: 7.1.5.1 Eliciting adverse events data in the development program
L1456: In both Studies 2102 and 2103, an adverse event was defined as any untoward medical occurrence in a patient administered a pharmaceutical product, and which did not necessarily have a causal relationship with this treatment. All pre-existing medical conditions were to be documented at the Screening Visit, and all AEs occurring from Visit 1 to the study assessment were recorded in the CRF, whether the AE was deemed treatment-related or not. During the controlled phase of the studies, HAE was to be documented as an AE only if there was worsening of symptoms during a treated attack. New HAE attacks that were not treated with icatibant were to be recorded in the CRF as "Occurrence of HAE attack," and were not considered AEs, although the Applicant notes that some investigators may have reported new
L1457: attacks as AEs, leading to an over-reporting of HAE attack as an AE. HAE attacks with laryngeal symptoms that occurred during the OLE phase were considered AEs. AEs were elicited from patients at all study visits, including at the 24-hour follow-up, Weeks 2, 5, 12, 24 and the final follow-up telephone call. Formal checklists were not employed. Investigators were instructed to follow-up all AEs until they had stabilized or resolved, or until the end of the protocol-defined study period, whichever came first.
L1458: Reviewer's comment: The categorization of HAE as an AE is problematic. Worsening of symptoms during a treated attack may be difficult to discern clinically and may merely be a reflection of the natural course of an attack. Also, it appears that there was some variability among investigators in how HAE attacks were reported. It is unclear why the Applicant made the distinction that only HAE attacks with laryngeal symptoms would be considered an AE. While laryngeal attacks have more life-threatening potential, the pathophysiology of these attacks versus attacks at other sites is presumably the same and it is arguable whether a mild laryngeal attack should be considered an AE instead of a severe GI or cutaneous attack.
L1459: 7.1.5.2 Appropriateness of adverse event categorization and preferred terms
L1460: Adverse events were coded by the Applicant using Version 9.1 of the MedDRA coding dictionary. Preferred terms and primary system organ class designation for each event were reviewed and spot-checked against case narratives, particularly for the SAEs. In general, the Applicant appears to have categorized AEs appropriately, although the categorization of HAE as an AE appears to have been handled somewhat inconsistently among the various study investigators as discussed in Section 7.1.5.1.
L1461: 7.1.5.3 Incidence of common adverse events
L1462: The Applicant's report and display of AEs were consistent within each study report and in the Integrated Summary of Safety (Revised ISS; dated December 20, 2007). The Applicant tabulated all AEs, but classified local injection site reactions separately as these reactions occurred in almost all subjects who received icatibant.
L1463: In the ISS, the Applicant combined data from the two Phase 3 trials. Although the study design and dose regimens were comparable, the studies used different comparator arms, limiting the pooling of data. As a result, data from the studies were reviewed separately then compared for consistency. Open-label data from both studies was pooled. Safety data from the Phase 2 Study 2101 was presented separately and is reviewed in the Appendix. Phase 1 data was not reviewed in depth, as these data did not present any unique safety signals and the IV formulation was used in these early studies.
L1464: 7.1.5.4 Common adverse event tables
L1465: Adverse events in the controlled phase of the Phase 3 studies, Study 2102 and Study 2103, occurring more commonly in the icatibant treatment group compared to tranexamic acid or placebo are shown in Table 9 and Table 10, respectively.

L1472: Reviewer's comment: HAE was to be recorded only in the event of worsening of symptoms during a treated attack. In Study 2102, HAE was reported by 7 patients (19.4%) in the icatibant group as an AE and 6 patients (15.8%) in the tranexamic acid group. In Study 2103, HAE was reported by 3 patients (11.1%) in the icatibant group as an AE and 5 patients (17.2%) in the placebo group. The AE frequency reported for icatibant contradicts the efficacy results reported for icatibant over tranexamic acid but favors the efficacy of icatibant over placebo.
L1473: Laryngeal attack patients
L1474: Injection site reactions were reported by all 11 subjects who received open-label icatibant in both studies. The type of reaction and duration were similar to those reactions reported for abdominal and cutaneous attack patients. Other AEs reported in this group included HAE attacks (n=4), headache (n=2), viral infection (n=1), thermal burn (n=1), epistaxis (n=1), dermal cyst (n=1), and urticaria (n=1). The case of urticaria does not appear to be treatment-related as the patient developed the urticaria nearly 10 months after the initial administration of icatibant.
L1475: OLE phase of Study 2102 and Study 2103
L1476: Seventy-nine (66.9%) of patients in the OLE reported at least one AE during the study. Similar types of AEs were reported during the OLE phases, with local injection site reactions predominating. Hereditary angioedema was reported in 27 (22.9%) of patients, which could be interpreted to be mean that icatibant lacked efficacy, caused an exacerbation of symptoms, a late relapse occurred, or a secondary attack occurred. Other AEs occurring in \geq 3\% of patients included the following: headache (n=13, 11%), nasopharyngitis (n=8, 6.8%), urinary tract infection (n=8, 6.8%), pharyngitis (n=6, 5.1%), chest pain (n=5, 4.2%), pyrexia (n=5, 4.2%), vomiting (n=4, 3.4%), and urticaria (n=4, 3.4%). Nine SAEs for 5 patients described above were also reported during the OLE phase.
L1477: 7.1.5.5 Identifying common and drug-related adverse events
L1478: A wide range of adverse events were reported for the icatibant arms of both Phase 3 studies; however, the majority of these AEs were reported in a single patient. Comparison of incidence rates across the studies does not highlight specific adverse events which are likely treatment-related, with the exception of local injection site reactions which were nearly ubiquitous. The reporting of hereditary angioedema attacks as AEs is difficult to interpret, but most likely reflects limited efficacy of icatibant rather than HAEs attacks worsened or triggered by icatibant. This assumption is supported by data from Study 2103, the placebo-controlled trial.
L1479: Stratification of these events by attack site (cutaneous and abdominal), age, or gender, does not indicate any clinically relevant differences. Review of the laryngeal attack patients also does not suggest any clinically relevant differences. Subgroup analysis by ethnicity is limited by the small number of non-White study participants.
L1480: 7.1.5.6 Additional analyses and explorations
L1481: Local injection site reactions were nearly ubiquitous across all the studies, occurring in patients of both sexes, all ethnicities, and age groups and comparable among cutaneous, abdominal, and
L1482: laryngeal attack patients. The reactions were self-limited and immediate, generally resolving within a few hours after dose administration. The main characteristics were local erythema, swelling, burning, itching, warmth, and cutaneous pain. Based on the few patients who received additional doses for separate attacks during the OLE, there did not appear to be any intensification or diminishment of local injection site symptoms upon repeat dosing. During the Phase 2 study, Study 2101, both the 30-mg and 45-mg SC treatment groups (N=5 in each dose group) reported injection site reactions without a clear dose dependency for severity or duration of the reaction. No injection site reactions were reported for intravenous dosing groups.
L1483: 7.1.6 Less Common Adverse Events
L1484: The size of the safety database for the two Phase 3 studies is unlikely to provide an adequate evaluation of less common adverse events. Aside from local injection site reactions, 31 (49.2%) patients in the icatibant group reported an AE, in comparison to 19 patients (65.5%) in the placebo group and 16 (42.1%) in the tranexamic acid group. With the exception of local injection site reactions and hereditary angioedema, all of the other adverse events were reported in 1 or 2 patients during the controlled phases of each study. Evaluation of these rare events and their relationship to drug treatment is difficult.
L1485: In the OLE phase, 79 (66.9%) of patients reported at least one AE. As in the controlled phase, the relationship of the less common AEs to treatment is difficult to ascertain. Of the AEs reported, 6 cases of urticaria in 5 patients are noted. In 4 of the patients, the urticaria was noted to occur 1, 4, 9, and 11 days after treatment with icatibant; in the 5th patient, the urticaria developed at 9 months after the last injection of icatibant. Patient 40-010 had urticaria 4 days after icatibant on 2 consecutive occasions. Allergic dermatitis was reported in 1 patient (Patient 004, Study 2102), but occurred approximately 6 months after receipt of icatibant. The long time interval between receipt of icatibant and the skin eruptions for some of these reactions makes a relationship to icatibant unlikely. However, for the episodes occurring within several days, including the patient who experienced urticaria twice after 2 consecutive treatments with icatibant, a causal relationship remains a possibility.
L1486: 7.1.7 Laboratory Findings
L1487: 7.1.7.1 Overview of laboratory testing in the development program
L1488: Routine laboratory testing was performed regularly during each study and as dictated by individual AEs. In the Phase 3 studies, blood sampling for safety monitoring was performed as Screening, Time 0h, Day 2, and 14 during the controlled phase. During the OLE, laboratory testing for safety was performed at Time 0 and Day 14. The range of normal values for hematology and chemistry parameters varied by study site; no uniform cutoff values were applied for identifying patients with high or low values or for categorization of extreme laboratory values as adverse events.
L1489: • Hematology: complete blood cell count with white cell count differential
L1490: • Coagulation parameters• Hepatic chemistries: alanine aminotransferase (ALT), aspartate aminotransferase (AST), gamma-glutamyl transferase (GGT), alkaline phosphatase (ALK PHOS), and total bilirubin (TBILI)• Renal: uric acid, blood urea nitrogen (BUN), creatinine• Metabolic: glucose, creatinine phosphokinase (CPK)• Urinalysis by dipstick (recorded as normal/abnormal)• Urine pregnancy test in women of childbearing age at Screening, Day 2, and during the OLE
L1491: Reviewer's comment: The routine laboratory assessments were adequate.
L1492: 7.1.7.2 Selection of studies and analyses for drug-control comparisons of laboratory values
L1493: The two Phase 3 studies were the focus for the review of drug-control comparisons of laboratory values, with particular attention paid to Study 2103, the placebo-controlled study. Data from the OLE of each Phase 3 study are reviewed also, although the duration of follow-up and degree of exposure varied among individuals. Longer-term data on icatibant is not available.
L1494: 7.1.7.3 Standard analyses and explorations of laboratory data
L1495: 7.1.7.3.1 Analyses focused on measures of central tendency
L1496: 7.1.7.3.1.1 Hematology
L1497: Mean hematology values were similar among the icatibant, placebo, and tranexamic acid treatment groups. A small reduction in mean hemoglobin, hematocrit, and WBC count at the 24-hour and Day 14 visits was observed in all groups, most likely reflecting acute changes in volume status during the onset and recovery from an acute HAE attack. Evaluation of other laboratory parameters such as bilirubin does not suggest acute hemolysis. Similar changes were observed in the OLE.

L1501: 7.1.7.3.1.2 Chemistry
L1502: Mean chemistry values were similar among the icatibant, placebo, and tranexamic acid groups. Small reductions in glucose, uric acid, ALT, AST, and GGT at the 24-hour and Day 14 time points were observed in all three treatment groups. The magnitude of changes was limited and unlikely to be clinically relevant.

L1506: 7.1.7.3.2 Analyses focused on outliers or shifts from normal to abnormal
L1507: The Applicant has not provided formal analyses of outliers or shifts from normal to abnormal. A glossary of normal laboratory ranges specific to each laboratory is provided. Upon review of individual patient data listings, the following shifts from normal to abnormal values were noted.
L1508: 7.1.7.3.2.1 Hematology
L1509: No clinically significant changes from normal to abnormal were noted for the icatibant treatment group.
L1510: 7.1.7.3.2.2 Chemistry
L1511: The following shifts from normal to abnormal values were noted in the metabolic evaluations from the Phase 3 studies.
L1512: Study 2102
L1513: Patient 001-004 had an increase in total bilirubin from 15.2 to 22.9 μmol/L from pre-treatment to Day 14. The bilirubin returned to the normal range by 2 days later when the patient presented for a new HAE attack in the OLE.
L1514: Study 2103
L1515: • Patient 055-002 was noted to have an increase in CK from pre-treatment to the 24-hour follow-up (81 → 166 U/L). Upon subsequent treatment for 3 additional HAE attacks over the ensuing 7 months in the OLE, the CK fluctuated (112-215 U/L). No other follow-up for this abnormality is reported.• Patient 071-002 had an elevated CK level (249 U/L) at the 14-day follow-up but no pre-treatment value is available for comparison. The level had returned to normal range by the time the patient returned for treatment of a second HAE attack in the OLE phase 3 months later.
L1516: Patients with marked laboratory abnormalities are discussed in Section 7.1.7.3.3.
L1517: Reviewer's comment: In the absence of formal analysis, review of the individual patient data listings for various laboratory parameters do not indicate any consistent laboratory abnormalities associated with icatibant treatment or any clear differences between the icatibant and respective comparator groups.
L1518: 7.1.7.3.3 Marked outliers and dropouts for laboratory abnormalities
L1519: No laboratory abnormalities led to subject discontinuation from the study. Several laboratory abnormalities were reported as adverse events, however. The criteria used for designating laboratory abnormalities as AEs are not described in the study protocols.
L1520: Study 2102
L1521: • Patient 016-005 was reported to have blood in the urine which resolved by the time of follow-up 40 days later. This patient had proteinuria noted at pre-treatment.• During the OLE, Patient 070-004 presented with rotavirus gastroenteritis accompanied by a transaminitis 3 months after open-label treatment for her 3rd HAE attack. Three days following hospital discharge, she received icatibant 30 mg SC for an HAE attack. Five days later she was readmitted for persistent diarrhea, this time accompanied by an increase in pancreatic enzymes (amylase 110 U/L and lipase 63 U/L). The patient was discharged 1 week later and reported as recovered.
L1522: Study 2103
L1523: • Patient 017-002 was reported to have a mild elevation of ALT (77→164 U/L), AST (50→80 U/L), and GGT (60→77 U/L) from pre-treatment to Day 6 which spontaneously returned to baseline by 2-week follow-up.• During the OLE, Patient 015-007 in Study 2103 developed severe pancreatitis requiring hospitalization that was reported as an AE. The episode occurred approximately 10 days after receipt of open-label icatibant. During the hospitalization, several laboratory derangements consistent with severe pancreatitis (elevated WBC count, increased cholesterol) were reported.• Patient 040-003 was noted to have increases in CK (175→309 U/L), total bilirubin (8.55→82.08 mmol/L), and AST (52→136 U/L) from pre-treatment to Day 17. At 1 month follow-up, the total bilirubin and AST had returned to baseline but the CK remained elevated at 278 U/L.
L1524: • Patient 022-003 was noted to have an elevation in CK from baseline to 1-month follow-up (177→280 U/L). Additional follow-up was not provided.
L1525: Reviewer's comment: There is no clear association between the laboratory abnormalities reported as adverse events and ictaibant, although the two cases of elevated CK enzymes appear temporally related. CK enzyme elevation has been reported with use of anabolic androgens, including those agents used for prophylaxis in HAE. Based on the individual patient listings, Patients 040-003 and 022-003 do not have any androgens recorded as concomitant or rescue medications during the study period. A review of the literature does not suggest a relationship between CK elevations or pancreatitis in HAE. Conceivably, mucosal edema could lead to transient pancreatic obstruction and subsequent pancreatic enzyme leak, but such an explanation is speculative.
L1526: 7.1.7.4 Additional analyses and explorations
L1527: No formal dose-ranging studies were performed for icatibant aside from the limited dose exploration performed in the Phase 2 study, Study 2101. Likewise, no drug-demographic, drug-disease, or drug-drug interaction studies were performed in the clinical development program for icatibant. Information on drug-drug interactions is extrapolated from in vitro data, showing no relevant inhibition or induction of CYP450 enzymes. The Applicant has postulated based on the mechanism of action that icatibant may attenuate the antihypertensive effect of ACE inhibitor drugs, but there is no clinical data regarding this issue.
L1528: Reviewer's comment: Given the expected, limited manner of use of icatibant, the life-threatening potential of HAE attacks, and the general avoidance of ACE inhibitors in HAE patients due to their potential for angioedema, the risk of this particular drug-drug interaction does not seem significant.
L1529: 7.1.7.5 Special assessments
L1530: No special laboratory assessments for safety were reported.
L1531: 7.1.8 Vital Signs
L1532: 7.1.8.1 Overview of vital signs testing in the development program
L1533: Vital signs, including blood pressure, sitting pulse rate, respiratory rate, and body temperature were assessed as Screening, Time 0h, 0.5h, 24h, Week 2, and Week 24 during the controlled phased of the study. During the OLE, vital signs were assessed at Time 0h, 0.5, and Week 2. These results were not summarized by treatment group in the ISS provided by the Applicant. An information request was made on February 14, 2008 and additional summary statistics and tables were provided by the Applicant on February 26, 2008. According to the additional information, the following were considered normal ranges for vital signs: pulse (60-100 beats/min), systolic
L1534: blood pressure (90-140 mmHg), diastolic blood pressure (60-90 mmHg), and temperature (36.5-37.2°C).
L1535: 7.1.8.2 Selection of studies and analyses for overall drug-control comparisons
L1536: The two Phase 3 studies were the primary basis for the review of drug-control comparisons of vital signs, with particular attention paid to the placebo-controlled study, Study 2103.
L1537: 7.1.8.3 Standard analyses and explorations of vital signs data

L1543: Review of individual patient data listings did not reveal any clinically relevant outliers or shifts from normal to abnormal.

L1545: Review of individual patient data listings did not reveal any marked outliers. No patients discontinued prematurely from the studies due to vital sign abnormalities.
L1546: 7.1.8.4 Additional analyses and explorations
L1547: No other additional analyses or data explorations were performed for vital signs results.
L1548: 7.1.9 Electrocardiograms (ECGs)
L1549: 7.1.9.1 Overview of ECG testing in the development program, including brief review of preclinical results
L1550: In terms of ECG assessment, the Applicant did not conduct a formal thorough QT study. The clinical pharmacology study, Study 1103 (Section 5), showed several examples of ST/T wave changes and/or QT prolongation in healthy patients receiving 5 doses of icatibant 30 mg SC on 3 separate days. This study was reviewed by the Interdisciplinary Review Team for QT Studies (IRT-QT), who concluded that icatibant showed evidence of QT prolongation and a thorough QT study was not warranted. At the pre-NDA meeting, these comments were communicated to the Applicant, and the Applicant was informed that QT prolongation would be included in product labeling unless the Applicant chose to refute these findings. The Applicant has not provided any additional data to refute these findings.
L1551: In the Phase 3 program, a 12-lead ECG was performed on all patients enrolled in Studies 2102 and 2103 at Time 0h prior to study drug administration, 0.5h post-dose, 24h post-dose, and at Week 2 follow-up. ECG interpretation was performed by a central reader, Pierre Maison-Blanche, who prepared an ECG expert report for each individual study separate from the Applicants' ISS. The ECG variables – PQ, QRS, QT, and QTc (Bazett, Fridericia, and Framingham) intervals and changes from baseline – were summarized by treatment group. Categorical ECG results (normal and abnormal) were presented by frequencies and percentages. The results of the ECG analysis from the Phase 3 studies are discussed in more detail in this section of the review.
L1552: 7.1.9.2 Selection of studies and analyses for overall drug-control comparisons
L1553: The clinical development program does not include any clinical studies with a positive control for QT prolongation. The two Phase 3 studies were the primary basis for the review of drug-control comparisons of vital signs, with particular attention paid to the placebo-controlled study, Study 2103. The clinical pharmacology study, 1103, which indicates a QT prolongation effect was also reviewed, although this study was an open-label, uncontrolled study.
L1554: 7.1.9.3 Standard analyses and explorations of ECG data
L1555: 7.1.9.3.1 Analyses focused on measures of central tendency
L1556: The mean QTcF interval (msec) for each study is presented. In Study 2102, the mean change from baseline at 30 minutes and 24-48 hours post-icatibant was -5.7 msec and -3.1 msec, respectively, compared to 1.4 msec and -4.4 msec for the tranexamic acid treatment group. For
L1557: Study 2103, the mean changes from baseline for the icatibant group were 5.3 msec and -3.8 msec, respectively, compared to 1.9 msec and -2.2 msec for the placebo group.
