L1560: 7.1.9.3.2 Analyses focused on outliers or shifts from normal to abnormal
L1561: No persistent shifts from normal to abnormal were reported.
L1562: 7.1.9.3.3 Marked outliers and dropouts for ECG abnormalities
L1563: No marked outliers or dropouts for ECG abnormalities were reported from the Phase 3 studies.
L1564: In Study 1103, several examples of icatibant-associated ST-T wave changes and QT prolongation were noted by the IRT-QT consult.
L1565: Subject 203 was noted to have 120 msec increase in QTcF at 59 minutes post-dosing. The QTcF at this time point was 530 msec. Significant ST-T waves changes were also noted. These changes resolved by ~2 hours post dosing, with the QTcF interval measuring 412 msec.
Subject 106 was noted to have ST-T wave changes and an increase in QTcF interval from 376 to 414 msec at 55 minutes post-dosing. These changes were resolved on subsequent ECGs and the QTcF interval returned to 385 msec.
L1566: 7.1.9.4 Additional analyses and explorations
L1567: No additional analyses or explorations were performed on the ECG data.
L1568: 7.1.10 Immunogenicity
L1569: The study report states that an assay for detection of icatibant antibodies remains under development at this time and data will be reported separately when available. There are no reports of anaphylaxis or allergic reactions to study drug administration. There are 5 reports of urticaria; the relationship to study drug administration remains uncertain.
L1570: Reviewer's comment: As a peptide drug product, albeit a small peptide, the formation of blocking antibodies may theoretically decrease efficacy of icatibant with repeat administration.
L1571: 7.1.11 Human Carcinogenicity
L1572: Long-term human carcinogenicity data is not available for icatibant. During the Phase 2 and Phase 3 clinical development program up to September 30, 2007, no new cases of malignancy were reported during the clinical trials.
L1573: Reviewer's comment: Given the mechanism of action and the limited exposure expected with the use of the drug for intermittent HAE attacks, a tumorigenic effect seems unlikely.
L1574: 7.1.12 Special Safety Studies
L1575: No special safety studies were conducted for icatibant.
L1576: 7.1.13 Withdrawal Phenomena and/or Abuse Potential
L1577: No special studies regarding withdrawal phenomena and/or abuse potential have been conducted for icatibant, as these issues were not considered to pertain to this class of drugs.
L1578: 7.1.14 Human Reproduction and Pregnancy Data
L1579: There is no drug exposure data in pregnancy for icatibant. No formal studies of icatibant in pregnancy have been conducted, and pregnancy was an exclusion criterion for the clinical studies. No pregnancies have been reported after receipt of icatibant.
L1580: Preclinical studies have demonstrated delayed maturation of reproductive organs in sexually immature rats and dogs, with decreased testosterone and FDS levels. In the clinical pharmacology study, Study 1103, no clinically relevant changes in FSH and LH were observed.
L1581: Reviewer's comment: Given the proposed intermittent use of icatibant, clinically relevant suppression of the gonadal pituitary axis is not expected.
L1582: 7.1.15 Assessment of Effect on Growth
L1583: The clinical development program included adults ages 18 years and older, so no formal assessment of icatibant's effect on growth was made.
L1584: 7.1.16 Overdose Experience
L1585: There is no overdose experience with icatibant. The clinical pharmacology study, Study JE049 1001, showed that a dose of 3.2 mg/kg administered by IV infusion over 1 hour caused symptoms of partial B2 receptor agonism, including erythema, flushing, hypotension, and generalized pruritus at the time of maximum plasma concentration. These effects were transient and self-limited. Similar effects might be expected in the event of an overdose with the SC formulation. As of now, icatibant is not intended for patient self-administration and the potential for overdose with SC formulation in the hands of a healthcare provider appears low.
L1586: 7.1.17 Postmarketing Experience
L1587: Icatibant is currently not approved for marketing in the US or overseas. No postmarketing experience is available for report.
L1588: 7.2 Adequacy of Patient Exposure and Safety Assessments
L1589: 7.2.1 Description of Primary Clinical Data Sources (Populations Exposed and Extent of Exposure) Used to Evaluate Safety
L1590: 7.2.1.1 Study type and design/patient enumeration
L1591: The primary source of clinical data for the safety review were the two Phase 3 studies conducted by Jerini, Study 2102 and Study 2103. This review focuses primarily on data collected during the controlled phase of each study with additional support from data collected during the OLE portion of each study. Phase 1 studies were briefly reviewed but their contribution to the safety database is limited given the type of patient enrolled (healthy volunteers). Safety data from the Phase 2 study, Study 2101, in HAE patients was evaluated in more detail (Appendix) and is discussed in terms of the consistency of findings with the Phase 3 data. Per patient data was available from individual patient data listings in the NDA, case narratives in the NDA, electronic data sets provided for statistical review, and patient CRFs. Literature reports referenced by the Applicant were reviewed briefly. In addition, the reviewer performed an electronic PubMed search [search term: icatibant; search restrictions; human] that yielded 184 literature reports. These reports were reviewed briefly and did not suggest additional safety concerns.
L1592: Table 1 summarizes the study design and subject enrollment of the key clinical studies relevant to this application. Additional detail of the individual studies can be found in the individual study reviews included in the Appendix.
L1593: 7.2.1.2 Demographics
L1594: Demographic information for the Phase 1 studies (Studies 1101, 1102, and 1103) in the HAE development program are presented below. All Phase 1 participants were healthy volunteers.

L1601: 7.2.1.3 Extent of exposure (dose/duration)
L1602: The Phase 3 studies used the same dose of icatibant, a single 30 mg SC injection. During the OLE, patients were permitted up to 3 injections separated by a minimum of 6 hours in a 48 hour period per HAE attack. Based on the number of attacks and response to icatibant, patients therefore had a range of exposures during the OLE. The exposure is detailed in the individual study reports in the Appendix. From the total safety database, 47 patients have been treated with
L1603: icatibant for 1 attack to date, 68 patients for 2 to 5 attacks, 14 patients for 6 to 10 attacks, and 12 patients have experienced more than 10 attacks for which they have received icatibant. The majority of attacks have been treated with a single dose of icatibant (611 out of 665 attacks total). Fifty-six attacks in 27 patients have been treated with 2 x 30-mg icatibant doses, and 4 attacks in 4 patients have been treated with 3 x 30-mg doses.
L1604: 7.2.2 Description of Secondary Clinical Data Sources Used to Evaluate Safety
L1605: No additional studies were reviewed for this application. Postmarketing experience for icatibant is not available. The referenced literature was reviewed primarily for background information on HAE and the rationale for icatibant as a treatment for HAE; no clinical safety data from the literature was available.
L1606: 7.2.2.1 Other studies
L1607: No additional studies were reviewed for this application.
L1608: 7.2.2.2 Postmarketing experience
L1609: Icatibant is not marketed for any indication at the time of this review.
L1610: 7.2.2.3 Literature
L1611: The Applicant provided 279 literature references, and for certain reports, electronic copies of articles regarding hereditary angioedema, the role of bradykinin, and icatibant. In addition, the reviewer performed an electronic PubMed search [search term: icatibant; search restrictions: human] that yielded 184 literature reports, the majority of which overlapped with the references provided by the Applicant. These reports were reviewed briefly and did not suggest additional safety concerns.
L1612: 7.2.3 Adequacy of Overall Clinical Experience
L1613: Due to the rarity of HAE, the overall number of patients evaluated in the clinical development program for icatibant was limited but appears comparable in scope to the clinical developments programs currently underway for other drug products intended for a similar indication. In addition, there are presently no drug products approved for the treatment of acute HAE attacks, which are potentially life-threatening. Given these considerations, the total number of patients in the safety database is sufficient to support the safety of single-dose 30 mg icatibant for the intermittent treatment of acute cutaneous and abdominal HAE attacks. However, safety data to support repeat dosing within a 24-hour interval is limited (only 4 patients treated with 3 x 30 mg doses for an attack) and additional clinical information on icatibant used in this manner is desirable.
L1614: The number of patients treated for laryngeal HAE attacks is limited as well, although the safety of icatibant for laryngeal attacks can likely be extrapolated from the cutaneous and abdominal
L1615: attacks. Safety data on the subset of laryngeal patients in the clinical development program appears comparable to the data collected for the other patients. Data to support the efficacy of icatibant for laryngeal attacks is a separate issue and is discussed in Section 6 of this review.
L1616: 7.2.4 Adequacy of Special Animal and/or In Vitro Testing
L1617: At the time of this review, the Division was awaiting updates from the Applicant on preclinical chronic dosing and carcinogenicity studies. The Applicant has provided preclinical data to support dosing for up to 13 weeks.
L1618: 7.2.5 Adequacy of Routine Clinical Testing
L1619: The laboratory parameters and vital signs and the timing of these assessments were adequate.
L1620: In terms of ECG assessment, the Applicant did not conduct a formal thorough QT study. Limited ECG monitoring performed in the Phase 3 studies described in Section 7.1 did not show any QT prolongation effects. However, the clinical pharmacology study, Study 1103, showed several examples of ST/T wave changes and/or QT prolongation in healthy patients receiving 5 doses of icatibant 30 mg SC on 3 separate days. This study was reviewed by the Interdisciplinary Review Team for QT Studies, who concluded that icatibant showed evidence of QT prolongation and a thorough QT study was not warranted. At the pre-NDA meeting, these comments were communicated to the Applicant, and the Applicant was informed that QT prolongation would be included in product labeling unless the Applicant chose to refute these findings. The Applicant has not provided additional data to refute these findings.
L1621: 7.2.6 Adequacy of Metabolic, Clearance, and Interaction Workup
L1622: The pharmacokinetic evaluation of icatibant is discussed in more detail in Section 5 and the Clinical Pharmacology review. Formal drug-drug interaction studies were not conducted and limited data has been collected on special populations; however, given the proposed indication and intended manner of use, the pharmacokinetic evaluation of icatibant is generally adequate.
L1623: 7.2.7 Adequacy of Evaluation for Potential Adverse Events for Any New Drug and Particularly for Drugs in the Class Represented by the New Drug; Recommendations for Further Study
L1624: As discussed above in Section 7.2.5, clinical data indicate a QT prolongation effect for icatibant. The Applicant has not submitted additional data to refute these findings and product labeling in the future should reflect this potential toxicity unless other data is provided.
L1625: Otherwise, the safety profile for intermittent 30 mg single-dose use of icatibant is acceptable. Data for repeat dosing in a 24-hour period is limited, and additional clinical data to support such a dosing regimen is desirable. This data may be obtained in the ongoing OLE studies, or preferably, in an additional placebo-controlled study.
L1626: 7.2.8 Assessment of Quality and Completeness of Data
L1627: The data and form of the data provided for the safety review are described in Section 7.2.1. Initially, the Applicant pooled Phase 2 and 3 data together in the ISS. The Applicant submitted a revised ISS on December 20, 2007, summarizing the Phase 2 and Phase 3 data separately. In general, the Applicant provided limited formal analysis of safety monitoring assessments (laboratory parameters, vital signs, and ECG findings) in the initial submission. Several clinical information requests were made, and the Applicant provided the requested information in amendments to the NDA (Letter dates: December 28, 2007, February 20, 2008, and February 26, 2008).
L1628: 7.2.9 Additional Submissions, Including Safety Update
L1629: The initial NDA submission contained safety information up to July 26, 2006. In the revised ISS submitted December 28, 2007, the Applicant included updated safety data up to September 30, 2007. The clinical review is based on safety information up to the September 30, 2007 cutoff date. The clinical review team also made several information requests to clarify the presentation of the safety data. Responses to these requests were received on February 11, 2008, February 18, 2008, February 20, 2008, and February 26, 2008 and the information is incorporated into this review.
L1630: 7.3 Summary of Selected Drug-Related Adverse Events, Important Limitations of Data, and Conclusions
L1631: 7.3.1 Local injection site reactions
L1632: Local injection site reactions occurred in nearly all patients receiving icatibant by subcutaneous injection. These reactions appeared self-limited and generally resolved within a few hours of treatment. More serious adverse events were not associated with these reactions. No special monitoring is recommended for these reactions, but information regarding these reactions should be included in the icatibant product label.
L1633: 7.3.2 Hereditary angioedema
L1634: Hereditary angioedema was the second most commonly reported adverse event, as discussed in Sections 7.1.5.4 and 7.1.5.5. HAE was to be reported as an AE only in the event of a new attack during treatment or significant worsening of an attack during treatment; however, these distinctions are difficult to make clinically during an acute attack. Given the overall limited support for efficacy and the mechanism of action of icatibant, the most likely conclusion is that HAE AEs reflect the underlying disease condition and limited efficacy of icatibant rather than a treatment-related exacerbation of the condition.
L1635: 7.4 General Methodology
L1636: 7.4.1 Pooling Data Across Studies to Estimate and Compare Incidence
L1637: 7.4.1.1 Pooled data vs. individual study data
L1638: The Applicant pooled Phase 3 data for the ISS, providing safety assessments for the individual studies in the respective study reports. The two studies differed in terms of the comparator arms, so both pooled data and individual study data were examined for the purpose of this review. Incidence rates for local injection site reactions were comparable. HAE as an adverse event was reported more frequently in Study 2102 compared to Study 2103; the frequency reported for the icatibant arm in Study 2102 rivaled the tranexamic acid arm in that study and the rate reported for the placebo arm in Study 2103. As mentioned in Section 7.3.2, interpretation of HAE as an AE is unclear and a causal relationship is not established. Other adverse events were much less common in both studies, supporting the overall safety profile of icatibant. Overall, the pooling of data did not appear to hide any substantial informative differences. Phase 2 data consisted of data from a single study that did not indicate any additional safety signals. Phase 1 data from healthy volunteers also did not raise any additional safety concerns, although the utility of the data was somewhat limited by the use of an alternate drug formulation (IV versus SC).
L1639: 7.4.1.2 Combining data
L1640: When data were combined, the numerators and denominators were simply combined.
L1641: 7.4.2 Explorations for Predictive Factors
L1642: 7.4.2.1 Explorations for dose dependency for adverse findings
L1643: Limited dose exploration was performed for icatibant. There is some evidence to suggest that local injection site reactions are dose-dependent. There does not appear to be any other consistent relationships with other reported adverse events.
L1644: 7.4.2.2 Explorations for time dependency for adverse findings
L1645: Local injection site reactions appear to follow immediately after dosing. Data from the clinical studies do not suggest a clear time dependency for other adverse events.
L1646: 7.4.2.3 Explorations for drug-demographic interactions
L1647: Pharmacokinetic evaluation suggests higher systemic exposure in female patients and older patients. Given the low incidence of individual adverse events (besides local injection site reactions) as well as the low number of non-white study participants and patients &gt;65 years of
L1648: age, a stratification of adverse events by gender, age, or ethnicity was not possible. Local injection site reactions were ubiquitous and do not appear to be more severe in a particular demographic. In terms of HAE attacks, which were reported as AEs, icatibant appeared to be more efficacious in female patients, which may be related to the higher exposure observed in PK studies.
L1649: 7.4.2.4 Explorations for drug-disease interactions
L1650: The population enrolled in both studies was adult HAE patients with acute cutaneous, abdominal, or laryngeal attacks. Data from the clinical studies do not suggest an attack-site dependency for adverse events.
L1651: 7.4.2.5 Explorations for drug-drug interactions
L1652: Formal drug-drug interaction studies were not performed for icatibant. In vitro study does not show any significant inhibition or induction of CYP450 enzymes. The Applicant has postulated a theoretical interaction between icatibant and ACE inhibitors, suggesting that icatibant may compromise the antihypertensive effects of ACE inhibitors via bradykinin antagonism. However, given the expected, limited manner of use of icatibant, the life-threatening potential of HAE attacks, and the general avoidance of ACE inhibitors in HAE patients due to their potential for angioedema, the risk of this particular drug-drug interaction does not seem significant.
L1653: 7.4.3 Causality Determination
L1654: Of the adverse events described, local injection site reactions are clearly drug-related. HAE attacks, which were sometimes reported as AEs, are more difficult to interpret. Given the limited efficacy demonstrated in the Phase 3 trials, it appears that HAE as an AE is most likely a reflection of limited efficacy and the natural course of HAE attacks rather than a treatment-related exacerbation of underlying disease. Other adverse events were reported at such low frequency that their relationship to treatment is difficult to ascertain without further study.
L1655: 8 ADDITIONAL CLINICAL ISSUES
L1656: 8.1 Dosing Regimen and Administration
L1657: The proposed dose of icatibant is 30 mg SC, which may be repeated for an additional 2 doses at intervals of 6 hours or more in a 24-hour period. The application does not provide convincing evidence of efficacy for this dose for the treatment of acute HAE attacks. Lack of efficacy may be due in part to unequal bioavailability in male versus female patients. Alternatively, a different dosing regimen may provide more consistent inhibition of bradykinin effects. Pharmacodynamic data suggests that higher doses are unlikely to augment bradykinin inhibition, but repeat dosing, which has not been formally evaluated for efficacy against a control, may improve the efficacy profile. Should a higher dose or alternative dosing regimens prove more efficacious in future clinical studies, additional safety information will be needed to support their use.
L1658: 8.2 Drug-Drug Interactions
L1659: Formal drug-drug interaction studies were not performed for icatibant. In vitro study does not show any significant inhibition or induction of CYP450 enzymes. The Applicant has postulated a theoretical interaction between icatibant and ACE inhibitors, suggesting that icatibant may compromise the antihypertensive effects of ACE inhibitors via bradykinin antagonism. However, given the expected, limited manner of use of icatibant, the life-threatening potential of HAE attacks, and the general avoidance of ACE inhibitors in HAE patients due to their potential for angioedema, the risk of this particular drug-drug interaction does not seem significant and no dosing adjustment is recommended for this scenario.
L1660: 8.3 Special Populations
L1661: Pharmacokinetic data suggests higher bioavailability in female and older patients. No clinically relevant pharmacokinetic differences were observed in patients with hepatic and renal impairment. On the basis of safety, no dosing adjustments are recommended for any patient subgroup; however, alternative doses and/or dosing schedules may be a consideration to improve efficacy, particularly in male patients. No information on icatibant in pregnancy or lactation is available.
L1662: 8.4 Pediatrics
L1663: Icatibant was granted Orphan Drug status on November 25, 2003 and is subsequently exempt from the submission of pediatric studies. No pediatric data was included in the NDA submission. In a prior discussion with the Division of Gastrointestinal and Coagulation Drug Products (March 1, 2005 – February 22, 2007 Pre-NDA Meeting Minutes), the Applicant stated that any plans for pediatric studies would be discussed pending NDA approval. In a later pre-NDA meeting held with the Division of Pulmonary and Allergy Products (February 22, 2007 – January 24, 2007 Pre-NDA Meeting Minutes), the Applicant agreed to submit their pediatric plan prior to NDA submission. As of the time of this review, the Applicant has not submitted a pediatric plan for review.
L1664: 8.5 Advisory Committee Meeting
L1665: An Advisory Committee meeting related to this application was originally planned but later cancelled as the ongoing review indicated insufficient evidence of efficacy.
L1666: 8.6 Literature Review
L1667: The Applicant provided 279 literature references, and for certain reports, electronic copies of articles regarding hereditary angioedema, the role of bradykinin, and icatibant. In addition, the reviewer performed an electronic PubMed search [search term: icatibant; search restrictions: human] that yielded 184 literature reports, the majority of which overlapped with the references provided by the Applicant. These reports were reviewed briefly and did not suggest additional safety concerns.
L1668: 8.7 Postmarketing Risk Management Plan
L1669: No post-marketing risk management plan was submitted with this application.
L1670: 8.8 Other Relevant Materials
L1671: Consultations were requested for this application by the Division of Drug Marketing, Advertising, and Communication (DDMAC); the Division of Medication Errors and Technical Support (DMETS), Study Endpoints and Label Development group (SEALD), and the Division of Scientific Investigation (DSI). At the time of finalization of this review, the consults are pending.
L1672: 9 OVERALL ASSESSMENT
L1673: 9.1 Conclusions
L1674: This reviewer finds the data submitted by the Applicant inadequate to support the indication proposed by the Applicant: “the treatment of hereditary angioedema.” The application does not contain two adequate, well-controlled studies demonstrating substantial evidence of effectiveness. Study 2102 used an active comparator, tranexamic acid, whose efficacy in acute HAE attacks is not established. While results of Study 2102 supported the efficacy of icatibant over tranexamic acid, the validity of the comparative difference is unconfirmed. Study 2103 used a placebo control but failed to demonstrate a statistically significant benefit for icatibant over placebo. While results were numerically favorable, the results were not statistically robust. In addition, the VAS PRO instrument used for the primary efficacy variable in both studies would benefit from further validation. While a PRO-based endpoint is acceptable for studies in HAE, the primary endpoint based on the VAS is difficult to interpret. Other PRO-based endpoints, perhaps with the VAS as a supportive secondary endpoint, may be more appropriate for future clinical trials.
L1675: Given these issues, this review concludes that the Applicant has not sufficiently demonstrated icatibant’s efficacy for the treatment of acute HAE attacks. Another placebo-controlled trial with a valid primary endpoint and statistically robust results is recommended to support efficacy. Other concerns include the very small sample size to support repeat dosing for a single attack and to support chronic use in the absence of preclinical data to support chronic dosing beyond 13 weeks. The reviewer acknowledges that the size of the clinical database is inherently limited due to the rarity of HAE and agrees with the Applicant that single-dose administration may be extrapolated to a certain extent. However, clinical data on repeat and chronic dosing obtained from another placebo-controlled trial will permit a more thorough evaluation of non-single-dose regimens.
L1676: This reviewer agrees with the Applicant’s conclusion that the safety profile of 30 mg icatibant is acceptable for the proposed patient population and the treatment of acute HAE attacks. If higher
L1677: doses or alternative dosing regimens are shown to be efficacious, additional safety information will be required to support other doses.
L1678: 9.2 Recommendation on Regulatory Action
L1679: From the clinical standpoint, the recommended action is Not Approvable.
L1680: The data submitted in this application provide suggestive but not conclusive evidence of efficacy for icatibant in the treatment of acute HAE attacks. Additional evidence from another placebo-controlled trial is required to support icatibant's efficacy. While no particular safety concerns were identified in the course of this review, additional evidence of safety from another clinical trial will bolster the safety findings of the current, limited clinical database.
L1681: 9.3 Recommendation on Postmarketing Actions
L1682: 9.3.1 Risk Management Activity
L1683: No risk management activities are recommended.
L1684: 9.3.2 Required Phase 4 Commitments
L1685: As the recommended action of this review is Not Approvable, no Phase 4 commitments are recommended at this time.
L1686: 9.3.3 Other Phase 4 Requests
L1687: As the recommended action of this review is Not Approvable, no other Phase 4 requests are recommended at this time.
L1688: 9.4 Labeling Review
L1689: As the recommended action of this review is Not Approvable, a broad overview of the major changes needed for the proposed labeling is presented here. A line-by-line review is not included in this review.
L1690: Proposed package labeling has been included in this submission [1.14]. The Applicant seeks an indication for the treatment of hereditary angioedema.
L1691: 1. The efficacy and safety studies conducted in support of this application addressed the treatment of acute attacks of HAE specifically, not a general treatment indication.2. The Dosage and Administration describes the subcutaneous use of icatibant, but does not specify that injections should be administered by a trained health professional in a monitored setting, i.e. not for home use or self-administration.3. The Adverse Reactions section should be revised to present Phase 3 safety data only.
L1692: 4. Under Clinical Studies, the proposed label (b) (4)[Redacted]5. Although numerically supportive, Study 2103, the single placebo-controlled study in the application, did not demonstrate a statistically significant benefit for icatibant over placebo. The Clinical Studies section presents the results (b) (4)[Redacted]
L1693: 9.5 Comments to Applicant
L1694: The clinical recommended action for this application is Not Approvable. The major deficiencies of the application to be communicated to the Applicant are outlined below:
L1695: The submitted data from your clinical program do not provide substantial evidence that icatibant is sufficiently safe and effective for the proposed indication of the treatment of hereditary angioedema (HAE). The clinical development program does not include at least two well-controlled, adequate studies that provide substantial evidence of icatibant's efficacy in the treatment of acute attacks of hereditary angioedema. Study JE049-2102 used an active comparator, tranexamic acid. As the efficacy of tranexamic acid in the treatment of acute attacks of HAE is not well established, the efficacy comparison between tranexamic acid and icatibant is more difficult to assess. Study JE049-2103 used a placebo control, but failed to demonstrate a statistically significant treatment difference between placebo and icatibant. Although the results were numerically favorable for icatibant, the results were not statistically robust. In addition, the primary endpoint used in both studies (time to onset of symptom relief using the Visual Analog Scale) was not adequately validated, further complicating interpretation of study results.
L1696: The above deficiencies may be addressed by the following:
L1697: • Provide data from at least one adequate and well-controlled (placebo) study to demonstrate the efficacy and safety of icatibant for the treatment of acute hereditary angioedema attacks. The additional study(ies) should include the following elements:○ Stringent inclusion and exclusion criteria for hereditary angioedema. In addition to a consistent medical history, patients should have documented laboratory values to support the diagnosis, including a low functional C1 inhibitor level, depressed C4 levels, and normal C1q levels.○ Stratification of patients by attack site, including patients with laryngeal edema in the randomization.○ A primary endpoint with adequate validation. A patient-reported outcome, such as the VAS, may be acceptable but should have reasonable content validity, test-retest validity and the results should be supported by secondary endpoints in a clinically meaningful way (i.e. not just statistically significant correlation). Should you elect to use a different efficacy endpoint, we recommend inclusion of “the time to onset of symptom relief” as assessed by the VAS as a key secondary
L1698: endpoint to facilitate cross-study comparisons with the other Phase 3 studies in your development program.
L1699: • Revise the proposed indication to more accurately reflect your clinical program, e.g. the treatment of acute attacks of hereditary angioedema.
L1700: 10 APPENDICES
L1701: 10.1 Individual Study Report: Study 2101
L1702: 10.1.1 Study protocol: Study 2101 – Proof of concept study to investigate in patients with HAE the efficacy and safety of icatibant administered intravenously at various infusion rates and subcutaneously at different doses
L1703: 10.1.1.1 Study administrative information
L1704: • Study sites: Multicenter
L1705: (b) (4)
L1706: • Study initiation date: July 2003• Study completion date: August 2004• Study report date: April 18, 2007
L1707: 10.1.1.2 Objectives/rationale
L1708: • Assess the efficacy and tolerability of icatibant when administered as an IV infusion at various rates or as a SC injection in subjects with acute HAE attacks involving cutaneous and gastrointestinal areas• Assess and compare the onset and duration of action of icatibant to guide dose selection and investigate the kinetic profile in HAE patients
L1709: 10.1.1.3 Design overview
L1710: Study 2101 was an open-label, multi-center, sequential, single dose study in HAE patients, divided into 5 sequential dose groups. Following initial screening, patients presenting with an acute cutaneous or gastrointestinal HAE attack received a single dose of icatibant in one of 5 possible dosing IV or SC dosing regimens (Section 10.1.1.5.1). The first 2 subjects at each study site were observed for 20 h following infusion/injection. Other subjects were observed for a minimum of 4 h. Patients completed symptom scores, visual analogue scales (VAS), and diaries. Investigators recorded global clinical impression (GCI). Other assessments performed included physical exams, vital signs, clinical laboratory tests, C1q/C4/C1 INH/bradykinin levels, and photographic documentation of swelling in some cases. Serum was also collected for pharmacokinetic analysis.
L1711: 10.1.1.4 Study population
L1712: 15 adult patients with documented HAE presenting with an acute cutaneous or GI HAE attack.
L1713: Inclusion criteria
L1714: • Ages 18-65 years
L1715: • Documented history of HAE• HAE attack frequency of at least 1 every 3 months• Presentation with moderate to severe HAE attack of any location, except laryngeal, within 10 hours of the start of the attack• If of child-bearing potential, reliable contraception and negative urine pregnancy test at baseline
L1716: Exclusion criteria
L1717: • Laryngeal edema• Concomitant diseases or medication which may interfere with the study, at the discretion of the investigator, including the following:○ Medical condition capable of altering the metabolism or elimination of drugs○ History of allergy or hypersensitivity to any medication
L1718: 10.1.1.5 Study treatments
L1719: 10.1.1.5.1 Dosing materials
L1720: • IV infusion: IV infusion of icatibant was prepared ex tempore under aseptic conditions and infused via an indwelling venous cannula in the forearm• SC injection: 10 mg/ml icatibant solution
L1721: 10.1.1.5.2 Treatment groups
L1722: • Group I: 0.4 mg/kg IV infused over 2 h• Group II: 0.4 mg/kg IV infused over 30 min• Group III: 0.8 mg/kg IV infused over 30 min• Group IV: 30 mg SC injection• Group V: 45 mg SC injection
L1723: 10.1.1.5.3 Randomization
L1724: Allocation to treatment groups was done in chronological fashion upon presentation with an acute attack of HAE.
L1725: 10.1.1.5.4 Blinding
L1726: Open-label study.
L1727: 10.1.1.5.5 Administration
L1728: Within 10 hours of onset of an acute HAE attack, patients were administered icatibant per dosing group assignment in a monitored study center.
L1729: 10.1.1.5.6 Treatment compliance
L1730: Treatment was administered by the investigator or sub-investigators.
L1731: 10.1.1.6 Study procedures
L1732: 10.1.1.6.1 Concomitant medications
L1733: Other concomitant medications were not permitted for the duration of the study with the exception of oral contraceptives. Subjects requiring treatment with another drug were to be withdrawn if the investigator and Sponsor deemed that the drug may affect the outcome of the study. All previous treatments within 2 weeks of the study treatment were to be documented in the CRF.
L1734: 10.1.1.6.2 Assessments and evaluations
L1735: In addition to symptom scoring, VAS, physical exams, vital signs, injection/infusion site exams, and elective photographic documentation of swelling, patient underwent serum sampling for clinical laboratory evaluations (screening, baseline, discharge, and follow-up at 36-60h post infusion), pharmacodynamic measurements (baseline, end of infusion, 4 h post-infusion, and 20 h post-infusion), and PK profiling. Pharmacodynamic sampling included assays for levels of C1q, C4, C1 INH, and bradykinin.
L1736: 10.1.1.7 Efficacy parameters
L1737: The study was intended as an exploratory, proof-of-concept study. Efficacy was evaluated in terms of onset of symptom relief, almost complete symptom relief, and complete symptom relief on the basis of the following parameters:
L1738: Skin swelling score
L1739: • Assessed as baseline and end of infusion (time 0) for Groups I, II, and III and for all groups at 2 and 4 h post-drug administration. For Groups IV and V, skin swelling scores were also assessed at 0.5 and 1 h post-dose.• For patients observed for 20 h post-dose, additional scoring was performed at 6, 10, and 20 h post-dose.
L1740: Abdominal score
L1741: • Assessed as baseline and end of infusion (time 0) for Groups I, II, and III and for all groups at 2 and 4 h post-drug administration. For Groups IV and V, skin swelling scores were also assessed at 0.5 and 1 h post-dose.• For patients observed for 20 h post-dose, additional scoring was performed at 8 and 20 h post-dose.
L1742: Visual analogue scale (VAS)
L1743: • A patient reported outcome (PRO) symptom scale. The VAS is a 100 mm horizontal line with 0mm = no symptoms and 100mm = worst possible symptom. Patients mark on the line to rate the intensity of each symptoms of at baseline and pre-determined time points throughout the treatment period. The symptoms rated include the following: cutaneous swelling, cutaneous pain, abdominal pain and nausea.• Performed at baseline and 2, 4, 8, 20, 48, 72, 96, and 120 h post-dose.• For Groups IV and V, also performed at 0.5 and 1 h post-dose.• Later time points completed only if clinically meaningful symptoms present.• Symptom relief defined as an absolute reduction of○ \geq 20 mm if baseline \geq 30 mm and \leq 50 mm or
L1744: ○ \geq 30 mm if baseline &gt; 50 mm• Attacks with VAS &lt; 30 mm deemed not assessable by this evaluation
L1745: Photographic documentation of swelling
L1746: • Taken at baseline and 4 h post-dose for all groups and electively at 20 h post-dose.
L1747: Subject diary
L1748: • Used to record symptoms, concomitant medications, VAS, and AEs after patients discharged from the clinic and returned at the follow-up visit.
L1749: Global Clinical Impression (GCI)
L1750: • GCI was assessed by the physician at baseline and 4 h post-dose.• Severity of attack scored from 0 to 4 (not relevant to severest intensity).• Individual symptom scoring: facial edema (0-3), extremities edema (0-4), abdominal edema (0-4), and laryngeal edema (0-4).
L1751: 10.1.1.8 Pharmacokinetic parameters
L1752: Plasma samples were collected before, during and after infusions/injections, with a total volume ranging from 20-52 ml. The following variables were analyzed: Cmax, tmax, AUC_{0-t}, AUC_{0-\infty}, \lambda z and t_{1/2}.
L1753: 10.1.1.9 Safety parameters
L1754: 10.1.1.9.1 Adverse events
L1755: Any adverse events were recorded from the time of admission to the unit until follow-up 60-120 hours after the end of the infusion/injection. AEs occurring outside of the clinic were recorded in patients diaries first prior to recording in the CRF.
L1756: 10.1.1.9.2 Laboratory assessments
L1757: Laboratory assessments included the following: routine clinical blood chemistries and complete blood counts, coagulation panels, serology for HIV, HBsAg, and HCV, urinalysis, and urine pregnancy test for women of childbearing age.
L1758: 10.1.1.9.3 Physical exams
L1759: Physical exams were performed at screening, 4 h post-dose, and at follow-up. Particular attention was placed on infusion/injection site reactions, which were scored at baseline, 2, and 4-hr post-dose.
L1760: 10.1.1.9.4 Vital signs
L1761: Vital signs (blood pressure, pulse) were performed at screening, baseline, during infusion, and hourly for 8 hours after infusion, as well as 10, 20, and 36 hours post infusion.
L1762: 10.1.1.9.5 ECGs
L1763: ECGs were assessed at baseline, 4 h post-dose and 20 h post-dose for those patients observed for 20 h. Detailed ECG evaluation for QTc effects was performed by a central ECG lab (Prof. Pierre Maison-Blanche at Hospital Lariboisiere in Paris, France).
L1764: 10.1.1.10 Statistical plan
L1765: No formal inferential tests were performed. Efficacy, safety, and PK data were reported using standard descriptive statistics. No formal sample size calculation was used.
L1766: 10.1.2 Results
L1767: 10.1.2.1 Protocol amendments
L1768: Five amendments were made to the protocol, listed by date:
L1769: • July 7, 2003○ Addition of study site○ Typographical error correction○ Elaboration of subject diary procedures• August 11, 2003 – Addition of study site• December 5, 2003 – Dose adjustment for Group III based on results of preceding Groups I and II. Original protocol proposed a dose range of 0.2 – 0.8 mg/kg; revised protocol allowed for 0.8 mg/kg over 30 min.• March 17, 2004○ Group IV route of administration changed from IV to SC○ Four additional subjects added to constitute Group V• May 5, 2004 – Addition of study site
L1770: 10.1.2.2 Protocol deviations
L1771: No subjects were excluded from analysis due to protocol deviations. The following deviations were noted:
L1772: • The dose of 30 mg in Group IV was administered as 3 abdominal SC injections of 1 ml each (10 mg/ml) rather than as a single injection. The dose of 45 mg in Group V was administered as 2 abdominal SC injections of 2.25 ml each instead of a single injection.• Subject 303 received study drug at 10 h 20 min after the onset of attack, rather than 10 h after onset of attack.• Subjects 411 and 412 were enrolled although their HAE type was not documented at screening.
L1773: Reviewer's comment: The protocol deviations noted are unlikely to have had major impact on overall conclusions of efficacy and safety from this small, proof-of-concept study.
L1774: 10.1.2.3 Treatment compliance
L1775: Treatment was administered in all cases by the investigator or sub-investigators.
L1776: 10.1.2.4 Datasets analyzed
L1777: Analysis is based on 20 attacks in all 15 patients enrolled.
L1778: 10.1.2.5 Study patients
L1779: Fifteen subjects with a total of 20 HAE attacks were treated in the study. The patients ranged in age from 20 to 61 years (median 42 years). Five patients were male, 10 were female. All patients were Caucasian. Ten subjects experienced one attack; 5 subjects experience two attacks and were treated in a higher dose group for the second attack. These subjects received separate subject ID numbers for each of the two treatments received. Of the 20 attacks, 10 were cutaneous, 7 were cutaneous and abdominal, and 3 were abdominal attacks. Of the 15 subjects, 12 were HAE Type I, 1 subject was HAE Type 2, and no information was provided for 2 subjects. An additional subject was initially classified as Type I then classified as Type 2 during a second attack.

L1782: Reviewer's comment: Patients were eligible to re-enroll in the same study in a higher dose group and were assigned new subject ID numbers (103/201, 104/502, 203/501, 302/402, 304/503). Inclusion of the same patients in multiple stages of the study is a potential confounder of study results, given the possibility of “double-counting” adverse events or biasing enrollment in terms of drug responders.
L1783: 10.1.2.6 Concomitant medications
L1784: Fifteen subjects in Groups I (n=3), II (n=4), III (n=3), IV (n=2), and V (n=3) took concomitant medication during the treatment period and follow-up, including medications intended for the treatment of HAE, 1 patient was taking danazol and 5 patients were reported to have received Berinert P, a plasma-derived C1 INH concentrate licensed in Germany, Austria, Switzerland, and Japan. At least 2 of these patients are documented to have received multiple infusions of Berinert P.
L1785: Reviewer's comment: The concomitant use of medications for HAE, particularly replacement C1 INH therapy, is problematic and obscures the efficacy findings. Although not currently approved in the US at the time of this review, it is reasonable to assume that replacement therapy could alter the natural course of an attack and potentially alter efficacy results.

L1792: 10.1.2.8 Efficacy endpoint outcomes
L1793: 10.1.2.8.1 Onset of symptom relief as reported by subject
L1794: Symptom relief as reported by the subject and according to the VAS is summarized in Table 19. For the SC treatment groups, the onset of symptom relief was between 27 and 35 minutes. As assessed by the VAS, median onset of symptom relief ranged from 2 to 5 hours for all treatment groups. Of note, the SC treatment groups had on average a shorter duration between onset of attack and treatment in comparison to the IV groups.

L1798: Reviewer's comment: SC appears to be superior to IV administration, although on average the interval between onset of attack and treatment was shorter in the SC groups, which may account for this difference. In terms of individual patients who enrolled in more than one phase of the study (Patient ID 103/201, 104/502, 203/501, 302/402, 304/503), there does not appear to be a major difference in onset of relief as reported by the patient by administration route or by dose.
L1799: 10.1.2.8.2 Symptom relief according to VAS
L1800: Symptom relief according to VAS, defined as an absolute reduction from the pre-treatment VAS of \geq 20 mm if baseline VAS was \geq 30 mm and \leq 30 mm if baseline VAS was &gt; 50 mm, is summarized in Table 19. Almost complete symptom relief was defined as duration from start time of injection to the first time point with a VAS score of 0-10 mm for at least 3 consecutive measurements for all 5 symptoms. Complete symptom relief was defined as the first time point with a VAS of 0 for all 5 symptoms assessed. The onset of symptoms relief according to VAS appeared comparable among treatment groups, although there appeared to be a large degree of individual variability, and ranged from 2:00 to 20:00 h. Similarly, the time to almost complete
L1801: relief ranged from 2:00 to 22:00 h. Time to complete relief ranged greatly, from 8:30 h to 84:00 h, without any clear differences among dose groups and routes of administration.
L1802: Reviewer's comment: Based on the VAS assessments, there do not appear to be any clear dose response or differences in efficacy in terms of routes of administration. Dose group III may also be a potential treatment, but the differences between the IV and SC formulation are not great and the SC is a more patient-friendly route of administration. For some individuals, the onset of symptom relief according to the VAS versus as reported by the patient did not match, which calls into question the underlying validity of the VAS instrument.
L1803: 10.1.2.8.3 Symptom relief according to GCI
L1804: The GCI was assessed at 4 and 20 hours post infusion/injection. According to the study report, the GCI at 4 indicated a “considerable reduction” in symptoms at 4 hours that correlated with the VAS.
L1805: Reviewer's comment: According to the Applicants calculations, the VAS correlated more closely with the GCI rather than patient's symptom scoring. However, the GCI is presented as a composite severity score that is difficult to interpret. Information regarding the individual components of the score is not clearly provided and illustrative examples of the severity ranking are not provided. The intra- and inter-observer reliability of the scoring is uncertain.
L1806: 10.1.2.8.4 Additional treatment with C1 INH
L1807: Five patients received C1 inhibitor replacement therapy within 28 hours of receiving icatibant treatment: Subjects 304/503, 411, and 531. All three subjects reportedly experienced onset of relief with icatibant prior to receiving replacement C1 INH for recurrence of symptoms. According to the study report, C1 INH was not administered as a rescue therapy for worsening symptoms.
L1808: 10.1.2.8.5 Pharmacodynamic parameters
L1809: No changes were noted in C1-INH, C, or C1q levels following administration of icatibant. Bradykinin levels were reduced from baseline to 4 hours post-dose, without any differences among treatment groups.
L1810: Reviewer's comment: The role of bradykinin in acute HAE attacks has not been clearly delineated, although there is literature to support the notion that bradykinin may be responsible for many of the clinical manifestations of HAE.
L1811: 10.1.2.9 Safety assessments
L1812: 10.1.2.9.1 Extent of exposure
L1813: The extent of exposure for individual patients is summarized in Table 20.

L1815: 10.1.2.9.2 Adverse events
L1816: 10.1.2.9.2.1 Serious adverse events
L1817: No deaths were reported. Two SAEs were reported. One patient in Group I (Patient 101) was admitted to the hospital for an acute manic episode of a pre-existing schizoaffective psychoses seven days after icatibant infusion. The other patient in Group IV (Patient 411) was classified as having a prolonged hospitalization due to an infusion of C1 INH replacement therapy that was administered 21 hours after initial icatibant injection.
L1818: 10.1.2.9.2.2 Discontinuations from the study due to adverse events
L1819: No patients were discontinued from the study due to adverse events.
L1820: 10.1.2.9.2.3 Other adverse events
L1821: A total of 11 subjects reported 41 AEs during the course. The majority of AEs occurred in the SC injection groups (Group IV – 4 subjects reporting 17 AEs and Group V – 4 subjects reporting 19 AEs). Thirty-three of these 36 AEs were related to local injection site reactions, including feeling of warmth, pruritus, erythema, burning sensation, itching, swelling, and pain. All of these resolved spontaneously. No local reactions were reported after IV infusion. The other main AE was coded as HAE, which was meant only to include a separate recurrence of symptoms rather than a progression or exacerbation of initial presenting symptoms.
L1822: Reviewer's comment: The majority of AEs attributable to drug appear to be local injection site reactions. The two SAEs do not appear to be directly related to icatibant, although the recurrence of symptoms in Patient 411 may be related to poor efficacy. Likewise, the other main AE of HAE may reflect limited efficacy of the drug.
L1823: 10.1.2.10 Vital signs
L1824: No changes in blood pressure or pulse from screening were noted.
L1825: 10.1.2.11 Physical exams
L1826: Pertinent physical exam findings including local injection site reactions for all patients who received SC icatibant, as described in the section above. No other changes in physical exam findings are reported.
L1827: 10.1.2.12 Laboratory parameters
L1828: No systematic changes from screening baseline were observed for any of the clinical chemistry or hematology parameters.
L1829: 10.1.2.13 ECGs
L1830: The Applicant states that no significant differences were found between pre- and post-dose ECGs in any of the treatment groups. A statistical report on ECG parameters read by a central reader is not provided.
L1831: Reviewer's comment: Study 2101 does not show any obvious QT prolongation effects, but the study was not designed to assess this issue methodically.
L1832: 10.1.3 Study summary and conclusions
L1833: As a proof-of-concept study, Study 2101 provides some support for the efficacy of icatibant. However, the small number of patients evaluated and the use of the same individuals in multiple phases of the study limit the generalizability of the results. As a dose-ranging study, the data to support the dose selected for pivotal trials is limited. The 30 mg SC dose appears to have had similar results as the 45 mg dose, and lower doses were not studied. Durability of response was not assessed in a systematic fashion.
L1834: In terms of safety data, no significant safety issues are identified. The most common AE, local injection site reactions for the patients treated subcutaneously, does not appear to have been severe or to have had any persistent effects on patients.
L1835: 10.2 Individual Study Report: Study 2102
L1836: 10.2.1 Study protocol: Study 2102
L1837: Title: A randomized, double-blind, controlled, parallel group multicenter study of a SC formulation of icatibant versus oral tranexamic acid for the treatment of HAE
L1838: 10.2.1.1 Study administrative information
L1839: • Study sites: 31 active centers (1 Austria, 4 France, 8 Germany, 1 Hungary, 2 Switzerland, 1 Ireland, 3 Israel, 3 Italy, 1 Lithuania, 1 Poland, 4 UK, 2 Sweden)• Study dates: March 1, 2005 to July 25, 2006• Date of study report: May 10, 2007
L1840: 10.2.1.2 Objectives/rationale
L1841: • Assess the efficacy of icatibant compared with tranexamic acid on the onset of symptom relief from moderate to very severe acute cutaneous and/or abdominal HAE attacks• Assess the rate of response, time to almost complete relief, global outcome, severity of each symptom, and safety and tolerability of icatibant• Assess the efficacy and safety of repeated administration of icatibant in an open-label extension phase (OLE)• Assess the efficacy and safety of open-label icatibant in laryngeal HAE attacks• Assess the relative pharmacoeconomic impact of icatibant
L1842: 10.2.1.3 Design overview
L1843: Study 2102 was a multicenter, randomized, double-blind, double-dummy, active-controlled, Phase 3 study. The study also included open-label treatment for patients with laryngeal HAE attacks and an open-label extension phase for all patients who experienced a recurrent HAE attack and a modified open-label extension phase for patients who were not treated during the double-blind treatment phase of the study. Results of the modified open-label extension phase were provided separately.
L1844: Double-blind treatment phase
L1845: • Patient presentation with moderate-to-severe cutaneous and/or abdominal edema no later than 6 hours after the time the current attack became moderate in severity (Visual Analogue Scale \geq 30 mm)• Hospitalization for a minimum of 12 hours during acute attack, with follow-up on Day 2 and Day 14• If no symptom relief by 12 hours post-dose, hospitalization extended for an additional 3 hours with additional symptom assessment at 14 hours after injection• Rescue medications to be avoided if possible, otherwise documented in the CRF
L1846: Open label treatment with icatibant for laryngeal edema attack
L1847: • Similar study procedures as those summarized above for patients enrolled in the controlled portion of the study
L1848: Open-label extension phase (OLE)
L1849: • After completion of Visit 4 (Day 14) follow-up of initial attack, patients enrolled in OLE• Any attack severe enough to warrant treatment can qualify for treatment with icatibant 30 mg SC• Hospitalization for 5 hours, with follow-up on Day 2 and Day 14 and long term follow-up by telephone every 4 months and clinic visit every 6 months• Repeated dosing○ If attack worsens within 48 hours of initial treatment, additional injections permitted○ Maximum of 3 injections per attack at least 6 hours apart• Duration: 1 year after closure of double-blind phase
L1850: 10.2.1.4 Study population
L1851: The study population included patients 18 years of age and older with a documented diagnosis of either Type I or II HAE, presenting with moderate to severe cutaneous or abdominal HAE attacks. Patients with laryngeal HAE attacks were treated separately with open-label icatibant.
L1852: Inclusion criteria
L1853: • Age 18 years of age and older• Documented diagnosis of HAE Type I or II. The diagnosis could be confirmed either by functional and immunogenic C1-INH results from the central laboratory or by medical history. Inclusion on medical history alone were permitted if a clear diagnosis had been made on all of the following criteria:○ Family history○ Characteristic attack manifestations, recurrent attacks○ Historical functional C1-INH &lt;50%○ Exclusion of other forms of angioedema• Patients with C1-INH &gt;50% could be included if an acceptable rationale for the higher level was provided• Current moderate to severe HAE attack in the cutaneous, abdominal, and/or laryngeal area• For patients with cutaneous and/or abdominal attacks, a VAS \geq 30mm at baseline• Able to complete baseline assessments and commence treatment no later than 6 hours after the current attacks became moderate• Negative urine pregnancy test for women of childbearing potential
L1854: Exclusion criteria
L1855: • Diagnosis of angioedema other than HAE• Participation in a clinical trial of another investigational agent within the past month• Treatment with any pain medication since onset of the current attack• Treatment with replacement therapy, including C1-INH, within 3 days from the onset of the current edema attack• Treatment with tranexamic acid within 7 days• Any contraindications for tranexamic acid, including hypersensitivity, severe renal failure, active thromboembolic disease, massive bleeding in upper urinary tract, impaired color vision• Treatment with ACE inhibitors• Evidence of coronary artery disease based on medical history or screening examination• Congestive heart failure (Class III or IV)• Serum creatinine \geq 250 \mu\text{mol/L}• Other serious concomitant illness or mental condition per investigator's discretion• Pregnancy or breastfeeding
L1856: Reviewer's comment: Although both functional C1-INH and C4 levels were collected, the inclusion criteria were based only on the C1-INH level. C1q levels were not obtained for the study and were not routinely documented. Although a diagnosis of acquired angioedema (AAE)
L1857: is an exclusion criterion, it is possible that patients with AAE who have previously been presumed to have HAE may have been enrolled in this study.
L1858: 10.2.1.5 Study treatments
L1859: 10.2.1.5.1 Dosing materials
L1860: • Icatibant acetate 30 mg SC x 1 in the abdominal region (3 mL of 10 mg/ml sterile, isotonic, acetate-buffered solution for injection containing no preservative (pH 5.5; packaged in 5 ml glass ampules)• Placebo injection matched to icatibant solution, 3 ml SC injection• Tranexamic acid 500 mg over-encapsulated film tablet, 1000 mg every 6 to 8 hours apart for 2 days (total of 6 doses)• Placebo capsule matched to tranexamic acid
L1861: 10.2.1.5.2 Treatment groups
L1862: Double-blind treatment phase
L1863: • 30 mg icatibant acetate SC x 1 and placebo capsule every 6 to 8 hours for 2 days (6 doses)• Tranexamic acid 1000mg every 6 to 8 hours for 2 days (6 doses) and placebo SC injection
L1864: Open-label treatment for laryngeal edema
L1865: • 30 mg icatibant SC x 1
L1866: Open-label extension phase
L1867: • 30 mg icatibant SC x 1. If attack worsened within 48 hours of the initial treatment, a maximum of 2 additional doses could be administered at least 6 hours apart.• Worsening symptoms or new symptoms occurring more than 48 hours after initial treatment were considered new attacks.• No more than 8 injections of icatibant could be given within any 4-week period
L1868: Reviewer's comment: The lack of a separate placebo arm is a significant review issue. Tranexamic acid is not approved for HAE in the US, and data to support its efficacy for this indication has not been formally evaluated. As a result, any observed benefit of icatibant over tranexamic acid may be due to tranexamic acid worsening the patient's condition, rather than improvement attributable to icatibant, i.e. tranexamic acid may be worse than placebo.
L1869: 10.2.1.5.3 Randomization
L1870: Seventy-four patients were randomized 1:1 to receive treatment with icatibant or tranexamic acid and were stratified according to attack site, cutaneous versus abdominal. Random assignments were made through a centralized, automated system via allocation of a kit number of the study medication to be used for treatment based on a stochastic minimization procedure.
L1871: Patients with both cutaneous and abdominal symptoms were to be allocated to the abdominal group if at least one abdominal symptom (pain, vomiting, diarrhea, nausea) was moderate to very severe. An exception was made in the SAP dated August 24, 2006, stating that if patients have moderate diarrhea as the only abdominal symptom along with moderate-to-severe cutaneous patients, these patients were to be categorized as cutaneous attack patients.
L1872: Patients with laryngeal symptoms were allocated to the open-label treatment group regardless of coexistent cutaneous or abdominal symptoms.
L1873: 10.2.1.5.4 Blinding
L1874: Ampoules of study drug were masked to blind both investigators and patients for the double-blind treatment phase of the study. Tranexamic acid tablets were over-encapsulated to conceal their identity.
L1875: Reviewer's comment: Blinding of study treatments was incomplete, as is acknowledged by the Applicant. Subcutaneous injection of icatibant is associated with a high rate of local injection site reactions. The Applicant states that for ethical reasons, a placebo injection with similar irritant properties could not be used. Furthermore, oral tranexamic acid is known to cause nausea, vomiting, and diarrhea, in addition to a distinctive adverse effect of color vision disturbances. Since the primary and secondary endpoints are subjective, self-reported outcomes, the blinding issue may have had a direct impact on the study results.
L1876: 10.2.1.5.5 Treatment compliance
L1877: Study medications were administered under direct observation at the study sites for the first 12 hours. The 4th, 5th, and 6th doses of oral study medication were dispensed to patients at the time of discharge with instructions. Patients were to return blisters/unused medication at follow-up for drug accountability. Icatibant was not self-administered.
L1878: 10.2.1.6 Study procedures
L1879: 10.2.1.6.1 Concomitant and rescue medications
L1880: • Concomitant medications were defined as any medication other than rescue medications which were taken during the trial.• Antipyretics, corticosteroids, and antihistamine use was discouraged but permitted at the discretion of the investigator.• Narcotics and other pain medications were to be avoided if possible for the first 8-9 hours of study treatment. If required, the medication was recorded in the CRF as rescue medication. The narcotic of choice was morphine sulfate 0.05 mg/kg• Use of androgens for regular prophylaxis was permitted at stable or decreased doses. Increases in doses were not permitted and those patients were excluded from the efficacy analysis of the double-blind portion of the study.• Receipt of replacement C1-INH within 3 days from the onset of a new attack was an exclusion criterion.
L1881: 10.2.1.6.2 Assessments and evaluations
L1882: Double-blind phase
L1883: Table 21 and Table 22 describe the schedule of assessments for the double-blind treatment phase of Study 2102 starting at Screening (Visit 0). Aside from open-label treatment with icatibant, laryngeal patients were expected to follow the same sequence of procedures and assessments.

L1901: 10.2.1.7 Efficacy parameters
L1902: 10.2.1.7.1 Primary efficacy variable
L1903: The primary efficacy endpoint was the time to onset of symptom relief as measured by the Visual Analogue Scale (VAS). The VAS is a 100 mm horizontal line with 0mm = no symptoms and 100mm = worst possible symptom. Patients mark on the line to rate the intensity of each symptoms of at baseline and pre-determined time points throughout the treatment period. The symptoms rated include the following: cutaneous swelling, cutaneous pain, abdominal pain and nausea. For cutaneous attacks, the time to onset of symptom relief was defined by “swelling” or “pain,” whichever was the most severe presenting symptom. If both were equally severe, “pain” was used as the primary endpoint. For abdominal attack patients, abdominal pain was used to assess onset of symptom relief. Time to onset of symptom relief is defined as the time between injection and time of first documented onset of symptom relief, as defined below, at 3 consecutive measurements. Subjects with no documented onset of symptom relief were to be censored at the time of their last symptom assessment.
L1904: Onset of symptom relief was originally defined as follows:
L1905: • Absolute reduction from pre-treatment VAS of \geq 20 mm if pre-dose VAS \geq 30 and \leq 50, or of \geq 30 mm if VAS &gt; 50 mm
L1906: A modified definition of onset of relief was presented in the SAP dated August 24, 2006 as follows (Figure 1 Study 2102: Definition of onset of symptom relief by VAS as presented in the statistical analysis plan dated August 24, 2006):
L1907: • A response to the right and below a line Y = 6/7 X - 16 with X \geq 30 mm.○ X = pre-treatment VAS in mm○ Y = post-treatment VAS in mm• Corresponds to a reduction by 30 mm at a baseline VAS = 100 mm and by 21 mm at a baseline VAS = 30 mm.
L1908: Figure 2 Study 2102: Definition of onset of symptom relief by VAS as presented in the statistical analysis plan dated August 24, 2006
L1909: Baseline VAS (mm)Outcome (mm)301040205030604070508060907010080Figure 2: A scatter plot titled 'Symptom Relief' showing the relationship between Baseline VAS (mm) on the x-axis and Outcome (mm) on the y-axis. The x-axis ranges from 0 to 100, and the y-axis ranges from 0.0 to 100.0. A regression line is plotted with the equation y = 0.8571x - 16. Data points are plotted at approximately (30, 10), (40, 20), (50, 30), (60, 40), (70, 50), (80, 60), (90, 70), and (100, 80).
L1910: Source: je049-2102-statistical.pdf, Section 3
L1911: 10.2.1.7.2 Secondary efficacy variables
L1912: • Response rate at 4 hours○ Proportion of patients with onset of symptom relief for the primary symptom within 4 hours after treatment• Time to relief of each symptom present in pre-dose VAS other than the primary symptom• Time to almost complete symptom relief (0-10 mm on VAS for at least 3 consecutive measurements)• Global Assessment○ Investigators made a global assessment of all HAE symptoms, using the 5-point symptom scale described below.• Assessment of each symptom by severity○ 5-point symptom scale▪ 0 = none
L1913: ▪ 1 = mild▪ 2 = moderate (moderate interference with daily activities and no other interventions required)▪ 3 = severe (severe interference with daily activities and intervention may or may not be required)▪ 4 = very severe (very severe interference with daily activities and intervention required)○ Scored by patient: cutaneous swelling, cutaneous pain, abdominal pain, nausea, erythema, irritation, vomiting, and diarrhea○ Scored by investigator: cutaneous swelling, cutaneous pain, abdominal pain, nausea, erythema, abdominal tenderness, vomiting, and diarrhea• Clinical global impression/improvement○ Investigators made assessments of global impression on the patients acuity using a 7-point scale from “normal, not ill” to “among the most extremely ill patients.”○ Investigators made assessments of improvement on a 7-point scale from “very much improved” to “very much worse.”• Regression of symptoms (start of improvement) according to the patient (details not provided in the protocol)• Observable regression of symptoms according to the physician• Overall patient improvement according to the physician• Patient satisfaction
L1914: 10.2.1.7.3 Laryngeal efficacy parameters
L1915: Laryngeal attacks were treated on an open-label basis. Patients and investigators scored symptoms on a similar 5-point severity scale, rating dysphagia and voice change. Investigators made additional assessments of breathing difficulties, stridor, and asphyxia. The protocol also states that aside from the open-label treatment, other study procedures were to be the same as those for patients enrolled for cutaneous and abdominal attacks.
L1916: 10.2.1.8 Safety parameters
L1917: • Number and proportion of AEs• Vital signs• 12-lead ECGs at baseline, 0.5h, and 24 hours, read by a central reader (b) (4)• Local injection site tolerability: erythema, swelling, burning sensation, itching/pruritus, warm sensation, pain, and other• Laboratory assessments○ Hematology parameters: hemoglobin, hematocrit, red blood cell, white blood cell count and differential, platelet count, mean corpuscular hemoglobin, and mean corpuscular volume○ Clinical chemistry parameters: glucose, creatinine, uric acid, urea, total bilirubin, AST, ALT, GGT, and CPK○ Urinalysis by dipstick○ Urine pregnancy test for women of childbearing potential
L1918: • Physical exams and vital signs• Antigenicity• Complement C3a-desArg
L1919: 10.2.1.9 Statistical plan (SAP dated August 24, 2006)
L1920: Efficacy analysis
L1921: The primary efficacy endpoint was calculated using the Wilcoxon version of the log-rank test, at a 2-sided significance level of 5%. Secondary endpoints described above were evaluated without adjustment for multiplicity. Outcomes for laryngeal attacks were evaluated using descriptive statistics. A sample size of 74 patients was calculated to provide 80% power to detect a difference of 4 hours between treatment groups and assuming a 10% dropout rate.
L1922: Safety analysis
L1923: Safety endpoints were analyzed using descriptive statistics. Statistical evaluation of ECG QT intervals was performed by Paraxel International GmbH.
L1924: 10.2.1.10 Protocol amendments
L1925: Study 2101 was formally amended twice. In addition, the Applicant included other modifications of the original protocol in the Statistical Analysis Plan dated August 24, 2006.
L1926: • October 25, 2005 Amendment○ Clarification of inclusion and exclusion criteria, including contraindications to tranexamic acid○ Inclusion of patients with a medical history of HAE alone if patient also has a consistent family history, characteristic, recurrent attack manifestations, historical function C1-INH level &lt;50% and exclusion of other forms of edema. Subsequent confirmation of diagnosis to be made by functional C1-INH measurement in central laboratory.○ Specification of use of a medically acceptable form of contraception for the duration of the study in all women of childbearing age○ Clarification that laryngeal edema patients would follow the same study procedures as patients on double-blind treatment○ Omission of interim analysis• June 2, 2006 amendment○ Addition of Modified OLE to permit patients who were not otherwise eligible to receive icatibant in the double-blind phase of the study or the follow-up OLE○ Addition of study results from Study JE049-2101○ Modification of the primary endpoint to a more stringent definition (See Section 10.2.1)• Statistical analysis plan dated August 24, 2006○ Modified definition of the Onset of Symptom Relief definition to the following:▪ A response to the right and below a line Y = 4/7 X - 50/7 with X=30\text{mm}.
L1927: • X = pre-treatment VAS in mm• Y = post-treatment VAS in mm▪ Corresponds to a reduction by 50 mm at a baseline VAS = 100 mm and by 20 mm at a baseline VAS = 30 mm.○ Modification of stratification of patients. Patients with moderate diarrhea as the only abdominal symptom with moderate to severe cutaneous symptoms were to be classified in the cutaneous group.
L1928: 10.2.2 Results
L1929: 10.2.2.1 Protocol deviations
L1930: The Applicant reports a total of 16 protocol deviations for 14 patients (8 patients in the icatibant group, 6 patients in the tranexamic acid group. Eight major protocol deviations resulted in exclusion from the per-protocol population dataset. These deviations included failure to record a pre-dose VAS, mishandling of source data, mishandling of informed consent procedures, pre-dose VAS &lt;30mm, failure to dispense oral study medication, and mishandling of patient diary.
L1931: 10.2.2.2 Treatment compliance
L1932: The study report does not specifically describe treatment compliance, although it is noted that 4 patients did not receive oral study drug on the second day in violation of the protocol. In Section 11.3 of the study report, the Applicant states that drug accountability was assessed by used blister packs and unused medication at Visit 4 of the protocol.
L1933: 10.2.2.3 Datasets analyzed
L1934: The Applicant has defined the following analysis populations:
L1935: • ITT population: intent-to-treat, all randomized patients who received double-blind trial medication• Per protocol population: all patients from the ITT population without major deviations from the protocol. Patients with a pre-dose VAS of &lt;30mm for the primary symptom were excluded.• Safety population: all randomized patients who received trial medication and for whom a safety assessment was available• Patients with laryngeal symptoms at Baseline: all patients who received icatibant open-label for laryngeal attack• Patients treated in the controlled phase: all randomized patients and patients with laryngeal symptoms at baseline• Patients treated in the OLE: all patients who received treatments for second or subsequent attacks, including patients with laryngeal symptoms as Baseline
L1936: 10.2.2.4 Study patients
L1937: 10.2.2.4.1 Patient disposition
L1938: A total of 74 patients were enrolled and treated in the double-blind portion of the study.
L1939: Patient disposition for the double-blind phase of Study 2102 is presented in Table 24. One patient (081-003) withdrew from the icatibant arm due to her desire to become pregnant. In the tranexamic acid arm, 1 patient discontinued due to pregnancy (082-005), patient 096-001 discontinued due to death, and Patient 096-004 was lost to follow-up. None of the 3 patients enrolled in the open-label treatment portion of Study 2102 for laryngeal symptoms discontinued prematurely.

L1942: The OLE phase of Study 2102 consisted of 35 patients (34 from the double-blind portion, 1 patient from the laryngeal symptoms at baseline portion). One patient (020-012) discontinued prematurely after one administration during the OLE due to unavailability for follow-up.
L1943: 10.2.2.4.2 Patient study drug exposure
L1944: Thirty-six patients received 1 SC injection of icatibant during the controlled phase of the study; an additional 3 patients with laryngeal symptoms at baseline received 1 dose of open-label icatibant.
L1945: During the OLE, a total of 122 attacks in 35 patients were treated with 131 SC injections of icatibant (49 abdominal, 65 cutaneous, and 8 laryngeal). Six of 35 patients (10 attacks) enrolled in the OLE were treated with more than 1 dose of icatibant for a single attack. The majority of patients experienced (N=16) experienced 1 additional attack during the OLE and were treated with a single icatibant dose. At the other extreme, one patient received 41 injections of icatibant for 38 HAE attacks. Extent of exposure during the OLE is summarized in Table 25.

L1949: Reviewer's comment: The amount of data on repeated exposures in Study 2102 is limited to 6 patients. Only 1 patient received 3 doses for a single attack.
L1950: 10.2.2.4.2 Demographics and baseline characteristics
L1951: A total of 74 patients ranging in age from 19 to 68 years were randomized in this study. The majority of patients had Type I HAE (n=67, 90.5%), and had positive family histories (n=62, 83.7%). The demographics and baseline characteristics of the ITT population for the controlled phase of Study 2102 are shown in Table 26.

L1954: Reviewer's comment: The demographic and baseline disease characteristics appear comparable between the treatment groups. One outlier patient in the tranexamic acid group reported a total of 72 abdominal HAE attacks in the previous 6 months, indicative of individual variability in attack frequency. The three laryngeal patients also had similar baseline characteristics and did not appear to have a more severe history or frequency of HAE attacks.
L1955: 10.2.2.4.3 Concomitant medication use
L1956: At baseline, 9 patients in the icatibant group and 6 patients in the tranexamic acid group reported use of antigonadotropins and androstan derivatives for chronic HAE prophylaxis upon study entry. The most common concomitant medication was danazol. Ten patients in the icatibant group and 8 patients in the tranexamic acid group reported receiving C1 INH replacement therapy for prior attacks; during Study 2102, 4 patients in the icatibant group and 5 patients in the tranexamic acid received C1-INH upon entering the study.
L1957: For laryngeal attack patients, 1 patient was on danazol prior to treatment with icatibant.
L1958: Reviewer's comment: The study report distinguished between the use of C1-INH as a concomitant medication and as a rescue medication (discussed in Section 10.2.2.6.2). Details are not provided, although presumably C1-INH as a concomitant medication was used primarily for prophylactic purposes, e.g. prior to surgery.
L1959: 10.2.2.5 Efficacy endpoint outcomes
L1960: 10.2.2.5.1 Primary endpoint analysis
L1961: The primary endpoint was the time to onset of symptom relief resulting from moderate-to-severe cutaneous and abdominal HAE attacks. All analyses and discussion are based on the ITT population, unless otherwise indicated.
L1962: Onset of symptom relief for the ITT population is shown in Table 5 and Figure 3. For all attacks, the treatment difference between icatibant and tranexamic groups was 10.0 hours (p &lt; 0.001). Also shown in the table are the median times to onset of symptom relief for the subpopulation of patients with cutaneous versus abdominal HAE attacks.

L1966: Figure 3 Study 2102: Time of onset of symptom relief (hours)
L1967: The figure is a Kaplan-Meier survival plot titled 'Time of onset of symptom relief (hours)'. The y-axis is labeled 'Survival Function' and ranges from 0.0 to 1.0. The x-axis is labeled 'Time to onset of symptom relief for Primary symptom (hours)' and ranges from 0 to 50.
