The Icatibant group (blue line) shows a faster onset of relief compared to the Tranexamic acid group (black line).
L1968: Source: je049-report-body.pdf, Section 16, Figure 8.1a, P 3173
L1969: Reviewer's comment : The icatibant group had a statistically significant faster onset of symptom relief compared to the tranexamic acid group overall, although the treatment difference between icatibant and tranexamic acid is much less in the abdominal attack subgroup compared to the cutaneous group (1.9 hours versus 15.7 hours, respectively). According to the Applicant, pain symptoms in general are more likely to respond to treatment, including placebo. Since abdominal pain was the abdominal symptom used for VAS scoring, the Applicant states that abdominal attacks were more likely to show improvement after any treatment. Whether this explanation is true is uncertain. When looking at time to onset of symptom relief for individual symptoms aside from the primary symptom, skin pain does not appear to have the same "placebo response." The literature does not indicate that abdominal attacks differ from cutaneous attacks in terms of bradykinin pathophysiology.
L1970: Durability of response
L1971: A durable response was defined as onset of symptom relief for the primary symptom within 8 hours after treatment that lasted for at least 24 hours. In the icatibant group, 68.6% (n=24) reported a durable response, compared to 38.9% (n=14) in the tranexamic acid group (p=0.017). For cutaneous attacks, 65.2% (n=15) of icatibant patients compared to 21.7% (n=5) of tranexamic acid patients reported a durable response (p=0.007). For abdominal attacks, 75.0% (n=9) of icatibant patients reported a durable response compared to 69.2% (n=9) of tranexamic acid patients (p=1.0).
L1972: Reviewer's comment: Icatibant performed favorably in terms of durability of response compared to tranexamic acid for all attacks and the subset of cutaneous attacks. No differences were seen, however, in the abdominal attack group. Overall, less than ¾ of patients reported a durable response. Whether a more durable response could be achieved with repeat dosing or a different dose is unclear. Of note, the definition does not take into account rescue medication use, although in general, the tranexamic acid group received more rescue medications.
L1973: Time to onset of symptom relief – subgroup analysis
L1974: The time to onset of symptom relief was greater in male patients (3.5 hours) compared to female (1.6 hours) patients treated with icatibant. No differences were observed between patients weighing 50-75 kg and 75-100kg. As the entire study population was reported as Caucasian, no subgroup analysis by race was provided.
L1975: Reviewer's comment: The small sample size limits the utility of subgroup analysis, but there is no clear explanation for the differences seen in male versus female patients. In terms of types of attacks, a similar proportion of patients were male versus female in the icatibant cutaneous and abdominal attack subgroups (see Table 26).
L1976: Open-label extension phase
L1977: The onset of symptom relief was evaluated in the open-label extension phase by the number of attacks treated with icatibant. The greatest number of attacks treated for any one patient in this portion of the study was 38. More detailed information regarding total drug exposure is discussed in Section 10.2.2.4.2, Drug exposure. The onset of symptom relief ranged from 1.5 to
L1978: 12.0 hours for all attacks and 1.6 to 2.5 hours for patients where data was available for 10 or more patients (2nd and 3rd attacks).
L1979: Reviewer's comment: The efficacy data from the OLE are generally consistent with results obtained from the double-blind portion of the study.
L1980: 10.2.2.5.2 Secondary endpoints analyses
L1981: Time to onset of symptom relief using a modified definition of response
L1982: The study report presents time to onset of symptom relief using a more stringent definition of response as a secondary endpoint analysis. The modified definition is as follows:
L1983: • A response to the right and below a line Y = 4/7 X - 50/7 with X=30\text{mm}.
      
○ X = pre-treatment VAS in mm
○ Y = post-treatment VAS in mm


• Corresponds to a reduction by 50 mm at a baseline VAS = 100 mm and by 20 mm at a baseline VAS = 30 mm.
L1984: Based on the modified definition, the median time to onset of symptom relief for the icatibant group versus tranexamic acid is 2.5 versus 21.3 hours (p &lt; 0.001), with a treatment difference of 18.8 hours.
L1985: Reviewer's comment: A more stringent definition for onset of symptom relief increases the treatment difference seen between the two arms of the study.
L1986: Response rate at 4 hours after start of treatment
L1987: At 4 hours after start of treatment, the response rate based on the primary symptom was 80.0% (n=28) for icatibant versus 30.6% (n=11) for tranexamic acid (p &lt; 0.001). In the cutaneous attack subgroup, the response rate at 4 hours was 73.9% (n=17) versus 17.4% (n=4), respectively (p &lt; 0.001). In the abdominal attack subgroup, the response rate was 91.7% (n=11) versus 53.8% (n=7), respectively (p = 0.073).

L1994: Source: je049-2102-report-body.pdf, Section 11.4.2.9, Table 42, P 124
L1995: Global Assessment
L1996: Statistically significant differences in Global Assessment as scored by the investigator on a 5-point scale were observed between icatibant and tranexamic acid groups at 4, 12, 14, and 24 hours in favor of icatibant (p &lt; 0.001 to 0.013).
L1997: Clinical global impression/improvement
L1998: As Baseline, the majority of patients in both treatment groups were scored as “moderately ill”: 16 patients (44.4%) in the icatibant group and 16 patients (42.1%) in the tranexamic acid group. At 4 hours, 18 patients (50.0%) in the icatibant were reported to be “very much improved,” 15 patients (41.7%) were “much improved,” and 3 (8.3%) were minimally improved. In the tranexamic acid group, 3 patients (7.9%) was “very much improved,” 7 (18.4%) were “much improved,” 11 (28.9%) were “minimally improved,” 10 (26.3%) had “no change,” 5 (13.1%) were “minimally worse,” 1 (2.6%) was “much worse,” and 1 (2.6%) was “very much worse”.
L1999: Assessment of each symptom by severity
L2000: In general, statistically significant differences were observed for individual cutaneous symptoms scored by patients at the various time points between icatibant and tranexamic acid treatment groups. For abdominal symptoms, abdominal pain was scored more favorably by icatibant patients starting at 1 h post-dose and sustained through Visit 3 timepoint (p = 0.001 to 0.045). No statistically significant differences were reported for nausea, vomiting, or diarrhea.
L2001: Reviewer's comment: This analysis only includes patients who reported the symptom as at least of mild severity at baseline.
L2002: Regression of symptoms (start of improvement) according to the patient
L2003: Icatibant patients reported a median time to start of improvement of 0.8 hours, compared to 7.9 hours for tranexamic acid patients (p &lt; 0.001).
L2004: Reviewer's comment: The start of improvement by this symptom index differs from the onset of symptom relief as estimated by the VAS. The Applicant has not conducted any separate studies to validate this patient-reported endpoint. In addition, this particular endpoint is not described in the protocol and the study report does not describe how the information was collected (retrospectively versus prospectively).
L2005: Observable regression of symptoms according to the physician
L2006: Investigators reported a median time to visible regression of symptoms of 1.7 hours for icatibant patients, compared to 8.0 hours for tranexamic acid patients (p &lt; 0.001).
L2007: Overall patient improvement (time to regression/start of improvement) according to the physician
L2008: Investigators reported a median time to start of improvement of 1.5 hours for icatibant patients, compared to 6.9 hours for tranexamic acid patients (p &lt; 0.001).
L2009: Rescue medication use
L2010: Seven patients in the icatibant group and 12 patients in the tranexamic acid group received rescue medication for the initial HAE attack. The most commonly administered rescue medication was C1-INH (n=5 in the icatibant group; n=8 in the tranexamic acid group). Other medications administered included opioids for pain relief and glucocorticoids. Rates of rescue medication usage were similar between cutaneous and abdominal attack groups. The study report states that no patients in the icatibant group required rescue treatment during the first 12 hours after administration of study drug, compared to 5 patients in the tranexamic acid group.
L2011: Reviewer's comment: In terms of the primary endpoint, the data supports the efficacy of icatibant over tranexamic acid in the treatment of acute HAE attacks. However, in the absence of a placebo arm, assessment of efficacy remains difficult, especially since tranexamic acid is not approved for treatment of HAE in the US. The Applicant has provided multiple post-hoc secondary analyses to support the primary efficacy conclusions, but these additional analyses do not ameliorate this central study design issue. Furthermore, these other analyses rely primarily on unvalidated patient- and investigator-reported outcomes, further complicating the review.
L2012: 10.2.2.5.3 Laryngeal attack efficacy analysis
L2013: Laryngeal attacks in 3 patients were treated with open-label icatibant in the controlled phase of the study. An additional 8 attacks in 4 patients were treated in the OLE phase of Study 2102.
L2014: Laryngeal attack symptom scores – double blind phase
L2015: Patient 075-001 presented with severe voice change and difficulty swallowing. Regression of symptoms was reported by the patient at 1-hour post-icatibant. No rescue medications or ventilatory assistance was required.
L2016: According to the patient narratives, Patient 075-014 was too ill to complete any symptom scoring and was intubated 5 minutes after icatibant administration. The patient was successfully extubated 8 hours later. Regression of symptoms was reported at 24 hours post-dose by Patient 075-014.
L2017: Patient 078-001 presented with severe voice change and difficulty swallowing. The patient reported regression of symptoms at 0.3 hours post-icatibant and absence of any symptoms by 4 hours post-dose.
L2018: Reviewer's comment: The small sample size limits any conclusions of efficacy. Also, whether the improvements observed over time are attributable to icatibant or reflect the gradual resolution of the attack cannot be ascertained in the absence of a placebo. The Applicant has not provided VAS scores for the laryngeal attack patients, although the protocol dated April 10, 2006, states that all study procedures described in Section 6.1 for the double-blind study participants would be followed.
L2019: Laryngeal attack symptom scores – OLE
L2020: A total of 4 patients were treated for 8 laryngeal attacks during the OLE.
L2021: Reviewer's comment: Based on symptom scores reported at 0, 2, and 4 hours, the data suggest that most patients experienced some improvement of symptoms within 4 hours of icatibant administration. Whether this improvement is attributable to icatibant is difficult to determine. Also, a median time to regression of symptoms or onset of symptom relief is not provided to facilitate comparison with cutaneous and abdominal attacks.
L2022: 10.2.2.6 Safety assessments
L2023: 10.2.2.6.1 Adverse events
L2024: Serious adverse events and death
L2025: One death (Patient 096-001) occurred in the tranexamic acid group. The cause of death is reported as sudden cardiac death due to aortic valve sclerosis. This patient died suddenly approximately 41 days after study drug administration. He was out walking and suddenly lost consciousness and died.
L2026: In addition to the patient death described above in Study 2102, 5 SAEs were reported in 4 patients who received icatibant.
L2027: • Patient 75-013 experienced a severe laryngeal HAE attack requiring tracheostomy 5 months after the last receipt of icatibant.• Patient 90-004 experienced an abdominal HAE attack 2 weeks after initial treatment with icatibant, notably accompanied by hypotension, pyuria, and microhematuria. She was deemed fully recovered by 5 days after treatment with C1 INH concentrate, volume resuscitation, and antibiotic therapy.• Patient 16-005 was hospitalized for viral gastroenteritis and hypertensive crisis 3 months after receipt of icatibant.• Patient 075-014 experienced a laryngeal HAE attack, the same attack that qualified him for treatment with open-label icatibant in the study.
L2028: During the OLE, 5 patients experienced 9 SAEs, including HAE and renal failure requiring hospitalization (4 months after last treatment with icatibant), elective tooth extraction requiring hospitalization for precautionary monitoring, rotavirus-associated diarrhea with increased pancreatic enzymes requiring hospitalization (3 months post-icatibant), and a motor vehicle accident with minor head injury and hospitalization (2 days post-icatibant). The motor vehicle accident occurred in the setting of road flooding and hazardous driving conditions.
L2029: Reviewer's comment: The disparate nature of the SAEs and the timing of events in relation to study drug suggest that these events were not treatment-related.
L2030: Discontinuations from the study due to adverse events
L2031: No patients discontinued from the study due to adverse events, but one patient (082-005) in the tranexamic acid group was found to be pregnant on Day 14 of the treatment phase of the study and excluded.
L2032: Common adverse events
L2033: Injection site reactions were the most common adverse event and were assessed separately from other AEs in the Applicant's study report. HAE attacks were only to be reported as an AE if there was a worsening of symptoms during a treated attack. New HAE attacks were not to be reported as AEs, but the study report states that some investigators reported new attacks as AEs, leading to an over-reporting of HAE attack as an AE.
L2034: • Double-blind phase
L2035: The most common AE were local injection site reactions, reported by 35 patients (97.2%) in the icatibant treatment group compared to 10 patients (26.3%) who received placebo injection. These reactions were characterized most commonly by erythema (97.2%) and swelling (69.4%), followed by burning (69.4%), itching (30.6%), warmth (47.2%), and cutaneous pain (19.4%). Patients with primary cutaneous HAE attack symptoms reported swelling (83.3%) more commonly than abdominal attack patients (41.7%). The median duration of the reaction was 1.5 hours, with erythema as the principal persistent symptoms for that duration.
L2036: Eighteen patients (50.0%) in the icatibant group reported an adverse event besides a local injection site reaction, compared to 14 patients (36.8%) in the tranexamic acid group. The most common AE aside from local site reaction was HAE attack, reported by 7 patients (19.4%) in the icatibant group and 6 patients (15.8%) in the tranexamic acid group. A table of AEs occurring more commonly in the icatibant group than in the tranexamic acid group is presented below.

L2038: Source: jc049-2102-report-body.pdf, Section 12.2.2.1 and Section 16, Table 6.17.1.1, Page 2843
L2039: Reviewer's comment: Given the statistically significant benefit observed for icatibant over tranexamic acid in terms of the primary efficacy endpoint, the frequency of HAE reported as an AE seems incongruous.
L2040: Laryngeal attack patients
      Injection site reactions were reported by all 3 subjects who received open-label icatibant. The type of reaction and duration were similar to those reactions reported for abdominal and cutaneous attack patients. One patient also reported HAE attack, which was the same qualifying laryngeal attack for the study.
    

OLE phase
      Injection site reactions were reported in all 35 patients who received open-label icatibant during the OLE of Study 2102. Other common AEs occurring in \geq 3\% of patients included the following: HAE attacks (n=5, 14.7%), urinary tract infection (n=4, 11.8%), nasopharyngitis (n=3, 8.8%), pharyngitis (n=3, 8.8%), chest pain (n=2, 5.9%), vomiting (n=2, 5.9%), and dehydration (n=2, 5.9%). Nine SAEs for 5 patients described above were also reported during the OLE phase.
L2041: Reviewer's comment: Adverse event findings are relatively consistent among the different phases of Study 2102. Local injection site reactions are nearly ubiquitous. Other common AEs are HAE attack and headache. Whether HAE attack is a true AE or a reflection of the underlying disease is unclear. In terms of attack site locations – cutaneous, abdominal, and laryngeal – there did not appear to be any differences in the nature or frequency of AEs among these patient subgroups.
L2042: 10.2.2.6.2 Vital signs
L2043: No consistent changes in vital signs from baseline occurred during the study.
L2044: 10.2.2.6.3 Physical exams
L2045: Aside from local injection site reactions and manifestations of the presenting HAE attack, no clinically significant physical exam findings are reported after administration of icatibant.
L2046: 10.2.2.6.4 Laboratory parameters
L2047: Hematology
L2048: No consistent changes in hematology indices or coagulation parameters were observed in either the icatibant or tranexamic acid treatment groups.
L2049: Chemistry laboratory testing
L2050: Decreases in mean CK were observed for both icatibant (19.1 U/L) and tranexamic acid (19.7 U/L) groups at 24 hours after study drug administration. These decreases persisted through the follow-up visit on Day 14. Decreases were also observed for uric acid from baseline to Day 14 in both groups (10.6 mcmol/L and 11.5 mcmol/L, respectively).
L2051: During the OLE, one patient (070-004) with no prior history of pancreatitis had a pancreatic enzyme elevation (amylase 110 U/L) and mild hepatic enzyme elevations in the setting of rotavirus gastroenteritis which resolved in 30 days.
L2052: Urinalysis
L2053: One patient (016-005) was noted to have transient hematuria and proteinuria, which appeared to resolve by Day 40.
L2054: Antigenicity
L2055: The Applicant states that the antigenicity assay had not yet been developed at the time of the study report and that data will be reported separately when available.
L2056: Reviewer's comment: The Applicant reports being unable to induce antibodies to icatibant in animal models to develop antigenicity assays. Theoretically, development of blocking antibodies could diminish efficacy of icatibant over time.
L2057: 10.2.2.6.5 ECGs
L2058: The study report states that there were no significant QT prolongation effects noted after icatibant administration. No patient has a difference from baseline above 60 msec or a QTcF value &gt;500 msec. One patient was noted to have changes consistent with left and right
L2059: ventricular hypertrophy (096-001), who later suffered a sudden cardiac death, presumably secondary to aortic valve sclerosis.
L2060: Reviewer's comments: Overall, the safety profile of icatibant from Study 2102 and the OLE appear acceptable. The main toxicity appears to be self-limited injection site reactions. HAE attacks are the second most common adverse event, which may reflect the underlying disease condition rather than exacerbation caused by the study drug.
L2061: 10.2.3 Study summary and conclusions
L2062: Study 2102 provides support for the efficacy of icatibant in the treatment of acute cutaneous and abdominal HAE attacks, with the caveat that the efficacy of the active comparator used in this trial, tranexamic acid, is not established. Tranexamic acid is not approved for the treatment of HAE in the US and evidence for tranexamic acid's efficacy in HAE in the literature is limited. Secondary efficacy endpoints are generally supportive, although many of these endpoints were assessed as post hoc analyses without any adjustment for multiplicity and based on subjective, unvalidated symptom score scales. Support for the efficacy of icatibant in laryngeal attacks is limited, given the low numbers treated in the trial (n=3 in the original study and 4 patients in the OLE phase).
L2063: The safety profile for 30 mg icatibant appears acceptable, although again this assessment is restricted by the small number of patients evaluated in the Phase 3 program. Aside from local injection site reactions, there do not appear to be any major safety issues. Data on repeated dosing, especially beyond 2 doses, is limited to a few patients; although no concerning safety issues are identified, the safety database in this regard is too limited to make an accurate assessment at this time. Of note, the Applicant has not provided any information on the antigenicity of icatibant in this study report.
L2064: 10.3 Individual Study Report: Study 2103
L2065: 10.3.1 Study protocol: Study 2103
L2066: Randomized, double blind, placebo-controlled multicenter study of a subcutaneous formulation of icatibant for the treatment of hereditary angioedema
L2067: 10.3.1.1 Study administrative information
L2068: • Sites: 1 center in Argentina, 17 US centers, 5 Canada centers, 3 Australia centers• Study dates: December 28, 2004 to July 17, 2006• Date of study report: May 11, 2007
L2069: Reviewer's comment: The Argentinian study site enrolled the highest number of subjects (n=14). Thirty-one patients were enrolled in the US, 6 in Canada, and 6 in Australia. The US site with the highest enrollment was Site 070 (n=10).
L2070: 10.3.1.2 Objectives/rationale
L2071: • Assess the efficacy of icatibant compared with placebo on the time of onset of symptom relief from moderate to very severe acute cutaneous or abdominal HAE attacks• Assess the rate of response, time to almost complete symptom relief, global outcome, severity of each symptoms, safety and tolerability of icatibant compared to placebo• Assess efficacy and safety of repeated treatments in the open-label extension phase• Assess the efficacy and safety of icatibant in open-label use for laryngeal edema• Assess the relative pharmacoeconomic impact of icatibant
L2072: 10.3.1.3 Design overview
L2073: Study 2103 was a multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, single-dose Phase 3 study. The study also included open-label treatment for patients with laryngeal HAE attacks and an open-label extension phase for all patients who experienced a recurrent HAE attack and a modified open-label extension phase for patients who were not treated during the double-blind treatment phase of the study. Results of the modified open-label extension phase were provided separately.
L2074: 10.3.1.4 Study population
L2075: The study population included patients 18 years of age and older with a documented diagnosis of either Type I or II HAE, presenting with moderate to severe cutaneous or abdominal HAE attacks. Patients with laryngeal HAE attacks were treated separately with open-label icatibant.
L2076: Inclusion criteria
L2077: • Age 18 years of age and older• Documented diagnosis of HAE Type I or II. The diagnosis could be confirmed either by functional and immunogenic C1-INH results from the central laboratory or by medical history. Inclusion on medical history alone were permitted if a clear diagnosis had been made on all of the following criteria:○ Family history○ Characteristic attack manifestations, recurrent attacks○ Historical functional C1-INH &lt;50%○ Exclusion of other forms of angioedema• Patients with C1-INH &gt;50% could be included if an acceptable rationale for the higher level was provided• Current moderate to severe HAE attack in the cutaneous, abdominal, and/or laryngeal area• Able to complete baseline assessments and commence treatment no later than 6 hours after the current attacks became moderate• Negative urine pregnancy test for women of childbearing potential
L2078: Exclusion criteria
L2079: • Diagnosis of angioedema other than HAE• Participation in a clinical trial of another investigational agent within the past month• Treatment with any pain medication since onset of the current attack
L2080: • Treatment with replacement therapy, including C1-INH, within 3 days from the onset of the current edema attack• Treatment with ACE inhibitors• Evidence of coronary artery disease based on medical history or screening examination• Congestive heart failure (Class III or IV)• Other serious concomitant illness or mental condition per investigator's discretion• Pregnancy or breastfeeding
L2081: Reviewer's comment: Although both functional C1-INH and C4 levels were collected, the inclusion criteria were based only on the C1-INH level. C1q levels were not obtained for the study and were not routinely documented. Although a diagnosis of acquired angioedema (AAE) is an exclusion criterion, it is possible that patients with AAE who have previously been presumed to have HAE may have been enrolled in this study.
L2082: 10.3.1.5 Study treatments
L2083: 10.3.1.5.1 Dosing materials
L2084: • Icatibant acetate 30 mg SC in the abdominal region (3 mL of 10 mg/mL sterile, isotonic, acetate-buffered solution for injection containing no preservative (pH 5.5; packaged in 5 mL glass ampules)• Placebo injection in the abdominal region (3 mL of sterile, isotonic, acetate-buffered solution for injection without preservative (pH 5.5)
L2085: 10.3.1.5.2 Treatment groups
L2086: Double-blind treatment phase
L2087: • 30 mg icatibant acetate SC x 1• Placebo SC (vehicle matched to study drug)
L2088: Open-label treatment for laryngeal edema
L2089: • 30 mg icatibant SC x 1
L2090: Open-label extension phase
L2091: • 30 mg icatibant SC x 1. If attack worsened within 48 hours of the initial treatment, a maximum of 2 additional doses could be administered at least 6 hours apart.• Worsening symptoms or new symptoms occurring more than 48 hours after initial treatment were considered new attacks.• No more than 8 injections of icatibant could be given within any 4-week period
L2092: 10.3.1.5.3 Randomization
L2093: Fifty-six patients were randomized in a 1:1 ratio to receive icatibant or placebo. Random assignments were made through a centralized, automated system via allocation of a kit number of the study medication to be used for treatment. Patients with both cutaneous and abdominal symptoms were to be allocated to the abdominal group if at least one abdominal symptom (pain,
L2094: vomiting, diarrhea, nausea) was moderate to very severe. An exception was made in the SAP dated August 24, 2006, stated that if patients has moderate diarrhea as the only abdominal symptom along with moderate-to-severe cutaneous patients, these patients were to be categorized as cutaneous attack patients. Patients with laryngeal symptoms were allocated to the open-label treatment group regardless of coexistent cutaneous or abdominal symptoms.
L2095: 10.3.1.5.4 Blinding
L2096: Ampoules of study drug were masked to blind both investigators and patients for the double-blind treatment phase of the study.
L2097: Reviewer's comment: Blinding of study treatments was incomplete due to the ubiquity of local injection site reactions associated with icatibant.
L2098: 10.3.1.5.5 Treatment compliance
L2099: All study medications were administered under direct observation at the study sites. Icatibant was not self-administered.
L2100: 10.3.1.6 Study procedures
L2101: 10.3.1.6.1 Concomitant medications
L2102: • Concomitant medications were defined as any medication other than rescue medications which were taken during the trial.• Antipyretics, corticosteroids, and antihistamine use was discouraged but permitted at the discretion of the investigator.• Narcotics and other pain medications were to be avoided if possible for the first 8-9 hours of study treatment. If required, the medication was recorded in the CRF as rescue medication. The narcotic of choice was morphine sulfate 0.05 mg/kg/• Use of androgens for regular prophylaxis was permitted at stable or decreased doses. Increases in doses were not permitted and those patients were excluded from the analysis of the double-blind portion of the study.• Receipt of replacement C1-INH within 3 days from the onset of a new attack was an exclusion criterion.

L2123: 10.3.1.7 Efficacy parameters
L2124: 10.3.1.7.1 Primary efficacy variable
L2125: The primary efficacy endpoint was the time to onset of symptom relief as measured by the Visual Analogue Scale (VAS). The VAS is a 100 mm horizontal line with 0mm = no symptoms and 100mm = worst possible symptom. Patients mark on the line to rate the intensity of each symptoms at baseline and pre-determined time points throughout the treatment period. The symptoms rated include the following: cutaneous swelling, cutaneous pain, abdominal pain and nausea. For cutaneous attacks, the time to onset of symptom relief was defined by “swelling” or “pain,” whichever was the most severe presenting symptom. If both were equally severe, “pain” was used as the primary endpoint. For abdominal attack patients, abdominal pain was used to assess onset of symptom relief. Time to onset of symptom relief is defined as the time between injection and time of first documented onset of symptom relief, as defined below, at 3 consecutive measurements. Subjects with no documented onset of symptom relief were to be censored at the time of their last symptom assessment.
L2126: Onset of symptom relief was originally defined as follows:
L2127: • Absolute reduction from pre-treatment VAS of \geq 20 mm if pre-dose VAS \geq 30 and \leq 50, or of \geq 30mm if VAS &gt; 50mm
L2128: A modified definition of onset of relief was presented in the SAP dated August 24, 2006 as follows (see Figure 1):
L2129: • A response to the right and below a line Y = 6/7 X - 16 with X \geq 30mm (see Figure 1).
      
○ X = pre-treatment VAS in mm
L2130: ○ Y = post-treatment VAS in mm• Corresponds to a reduction by 30 mm at a baseline VAS = 100 mm and by 21 mm at a baseline VAS = 30 mm.
L2131: Reviewer's comment: The modified definition using more stringent criteria is used in the primary endpoint analysis. For post-hoc analysis, the Applicant has provided results using even more stringent criteria.
L2132: 10.3.1.7.2 Secondary efficacy variables
L2133: • Response rate at 4 hours○ Proportion of patients with onset of symptom relief for the primary symptom within 4 hours after treatment• Time to relief of each symptom present in pre-dose VAS other than the primary symptom• Time to almost complete symptom relief (0-10 mm on VAS for at least 3 consecutive measurements)• Global Assessment○ Investigators made a global assessment of all HAE symptoms, using the 5-point symptom scale described below.• Assessment of each symptom by severity○ 5-point symptom scale▪ 0 = none▪ 1 = mild▪ 2 = moderate (moderate interference with daily activities and no other interventions required)▪ 3 = severe (severe interference with daily activities and intervention may or may not be required)▪ 4 = very severe (very severe interference with daily activities and intervention required)○ Scored by patient: cutaneous swelling, cutaneous pain, abdominal pain, nausea, erythema, irritation, vomiting, and diarrhea○ Scored by investigator: cutaneous swelling, cutaneous pain, abdominal pain, nausea, erythema, abdominal tenderness, vomiting, and diarrhea• Clinical global impression/improvement○ Investigators made assessments of global impression on the patients acuity using a 7-point scale from “normal, not ill” to “among the most extremely ill patients.”○ Investigators made assessments of improvement on a 7-point scale from “very much improved” to “very much worse.”• Regression of symptoms (start of improvement) according to the patient (details not provided in the protocol)• Observable regression of symptoms according to the physician• Overall patient improvement according to the physician• Patient satisfaction
L2134: 10.3.1.7.3 Laryngeal attack efficacy assessment
L2135: Laryngeal attacks were treated on an open-label basis. Patients and investigators scored symptoms on a similar 5-point severity scale, rating dysphagia and voice change. Investigators made additional assessments of breathing difficulties, stridor, and asphyxia. The protocol also states that aside from the open-label treatment, other study procedures were to be the same as those for patients enrolled for cutaneous and abdominal attacks.
L2136: 10.3.1.8 Safety parameters
L2137: Safety endpoints included the following:
L2138: • Number and proportion of AEs• Vital signs• 12-lead ECGs performed at baseline, 0.5h, and 24 hours post-dose• Local injection site tolerability: erythema, swelling, burning sensation, itching/pruritus, warm sensation, pain, and other• Laboratory assessments○ Hematology parameters: hemoglobin, hematocrit, red blood cell, white blood cell count and differential, platelet count, mean corpuscular hemoglobin, and mean corpuscular volume○ Clinical chemistry parameters: glucose, creatinine, uric acid, urea, total bilirubin, AST, ALT, GGT, and CPK○ Urinalysis by dipstick○ Urine pregnancy test for women of childbearing potential• Physical exams and vital signs• Antigenicity• Complement C3a-desArg
L2139: 10.3.1.9 Statistical plan (dated August 24, 2006)
L2140: Efficacy analysis
L2141: The primary efficacy endpoint was calculated using the Wilcoxon version of the log-rank test, at a 2-sided significance level of 5%. Secondary endpoints described above were evaluated without adjustment for multiplicity. Outcomes for laryngeal attacks were evaluated using descriptive statistics. A sample size of 56 patients was calculated to provide 80% power to detect a difference of 5.5 hours between treatment groups and assuming a 10% dropout rate.
L2142: Safety analysis
L2143: Safety endpoints were analyzed using descriptive statistics.
L2144: 10.3.1.10 Protocol amendments
L2145: • Amendment dated September 7, 2004 (prior to screening of first patient). Major changes included the following:○ Administration of the study medication from 3 to 1 SC injection○ Criteria for treatment in the OLE changes from “moderate to severe” attacks to “any attack severe enough to warrant treatment”○ VAS \geq 30mm added to Inclusion Criteria
L2146: ○ Onset of symptom relief definition changed"▪ Original: absolute reduction by \geq 20mm observed at 3 consecutive measurements▪ Amended definition: absolute reduction from pre-treatment VAS of \geq 20 mm if pre-dose VAS \geq 30 and \leq 50, or of \geq 30mm if VAS &gt; 50mm• Amendment dated April 10, 2006○ Addition of Modified OLE to permit patients who were not otherwise eligible to receive icatibant in the double-blind phase of the study or the follow-up OLD○ Modification of the inclusion criterion for diagnosis of HAE to allow inclusion of patients based on medical history alone with later confirmation by C1-INH levels.○ Increase in the time following an attack (8-9 hours) before rescue medication permitted○ Decision to omit planned interim analysis by DSMB• Statistical analysis plan dated August 26, 2006○ Modification of the Onset of Symptom Relief definition to the following:▪ A response to the right and below a line Y = 6/7 X - 16 with X \geq 30mm.• X = pre-treatment VAS in mm• Y = post-treatment VAS in mm▪ Corresponds to a reduction by 30 mm at a baseline VAS = 100 mm and by 20 mm at a baseline VAS = 30 mm.○ Modification of stratification of patients. Patients with moderate diarrhea as the only abdominal symptom with moderate to severe cutaneous symptoms were to be classified in the cutaneous group.
L2147: 10.3.1.11 Datasets analyzed
L2148: The Applicant has defined the following analysis populations:
L2149: • ITT population: intent-to-treat, all randomized patients who received double-blind trial medication• Per protocol population: all patients from the ITT population without major deviations from the protocol. Patients with a pre-dose VAS of &lt; 30mm for the primary symptom were excluded.• Safety population: all randomized patients who received trial medication and for whom a safety assessment was available• Patients with laryngeal symptoms at Baseline: all patients who received icatibant open-label for laryngeal attack• Patients treated in the controlled phase: all randomized patients and patients with laryngeal symptoms at baseline• Patients treated in the OLE: all patients who received treatments for second or subsequent attacks, including patients with laryngeal symptoms as Baseline
L2150: 10.3.2 Results
L2151: 10.3.2.1 Protocol deviations
L2152: Fifteen protocol violations were reported for 9 randomized patients. Of these, 5 violations for 5 patients were considered major violations, and these subjects were excluded from the per-protocol population. These violations included missing source data/lab results/ECGs, receipt of pain medication, pre-dose VAS &lt;30mm, and missing documentation of the time of the attack and last receipt of replacement C1-INH therapy.
L2153: Nine patients were granted waivers to enter the study based on historical data and confirmed diagnoses of HAE, even though laboratory results were unavailable or C1-INH levels were higher than the inclusion criteria: 025-004, 001-002, 001-001, 002-003, 018-001, 018-002, 018-003, 057-001, and 056-003.
L2154: Patient 015-006 was entered into the study for a moderate cutaneous attack and received placebo. Six hours after study drug administration, he developed laryngeal edema and was given icatibant as a rescue medication.
L2155: Patient 02-003 was documented as having severe abdominal HAE attacks every 2-4 days for which she typically received C1-INH replacement. The patient was granted a waiver to enter the study despite receiving C1-INH more recently than 3 days prior to study drug administration, on the condition that a minimum 24-hour washout period for C1-INH was observed.
L2156: 10.3.2.2 Study patients
L2157: 10.3.2.2.1 Patient disposition
L2158: Patient disposition for the double-blind phase of Study 2103 is presented in Table 34. One patient categorized under “Other,” was found to be pregnant upon presentation for the first HAE attack and was never randomized. In addition, 1 of 8 patients enrolled in the open-label treatment portion of Study 2103 for laryngeal symptoms at baseline withdrew consent and discontinued from the study prematurely.

L2176: Source: je049-2103-report-body.pdf. Section 10.5
L2177: The study report notes that the severity of previous attacks by history varied slightly between treatment groups. More patients in the placebo group reported having had a severe cutaneous attack in the past 6 months compared to the icatibant group (12 v. 9 patients). More patients in the icatibant group reported having had a severe laryngeal attack (6 v. 5 patients).
L2178: Reviewer's comments: Demographics and baseline characteristics, including HAE attack history in the previous 6 months, appear comparable between the two treatment groups. Although differences exist, the small sample size is taken into account and the differences do not appear to be significant enough to have impacted the final efficacy results.
L2179: 10.3.2.2.3 Concomitant medications
L2180: At baseline, 6 patients in the icatibant group and 13 patients in the placebo group reported use of antigonadotropins and androstan derivatives for chronic HAE prophylaxis. Two patients in the icatibant group and 3 patients in the placebo group reported receiving C1 INH replacement therapy for prior attacks.
L2181: For laryngeal attack patients, 1 patient was on regular antigonadotropins prior to treatment with icatibant.
L2182: 10.3.2.3 Efficacy endpoint outcomes
L2183: 10.3.2.3.1 Primary endpoint analysis: Onset of symptom relief
L2184: The primary endpoint was the time to onset of symptom relief resulting from moderate-to-severe cutaneous and abdominal HAE attacks, using the following definition: Absolute reduction from pre-treatment VAS of \geq 20 mm if pre-dose VAS \geq 30 and \leq 50, or of \geq 30mm if VAS &gt; 50mm. All analyses and discussion are based on the ITT population, unless otherwise indicated.
L2185: Onset of symptom relief for the ITT population is shown in Table 6. For all attacks, the treatment difference between icatibant and placebo groups was 2.1 hours (p=0.142). Also shown in the table are the median times to onset of symptom relief for the subpopulation of patients with cutaneous versus abdominal HAE attacks.

L2197: Source: je049-2103-report-body.pdf, Section 11.4, Figure 4, P 96
L2198: Reviewer's comment: Although the VAS results were numerically favorable to icatibant and supported by post-hoc responder analysis, icatibant failed to demonstrate a statistically significant quicker onset of symptom relief compared to placebo. The clinical relevance of the 2.1 hour treatment difference is difficult to assess, as HAE attacks may be quite variable and the natural course for most attacks is spontaneous resolution. There did not appear to be any clear differences in icatibant efficacy in cutaneous versus abdominal HAE attacks.
L2199: The Applicant states that an exaggerated placebo response was observed in Study 2103 due to the higher number of patients with abdominal versus cutaneous attacks enrolled compared to Study 2102. According to the Applicant, pain symptoms in general are more likely to be responsive to placebo. Since abdominal pain was the base symptom scored on the VAS, patients presenting with abdominal attacks were more likely to show improvement, regardless of allocation to treatment group.
L2200: Durability of response
L2201: A durable response was defined as onset of symptom relief for the primary symptom within 8 hours after treatment that lasted for at least 24 hours. In the icatibant group, 51.9% reported a durable response, similar to the 50.0% in the placebo group (p=1.0). For cutaneous attacks, 57.1% of icatibant patients compared to 38.5% of placebo patients reported a durable response (p=0.449). For abdominal attacks, 46.2% of icatibant patients reported a durable response compared to 60.0% of placebo patients (p=0.705).
L2202: Reviewer's comment: Icatibant and placebo groups performed similarly in terms of durability of response. Results were numerically unfavorable for icatibant for patients with abdominal attacks. These results suggest that icatibant has limited impact on the overall resolution of an acute attack.
L2203: Time to onset of symptom relief – subgroup analysis
L2204: No statistically significant differences were reported between male and female patients. When divided by weight, patients weighting between 75 to 100kg treated with icatibant were observed to have a faster onset of symptom relief compared to their placebo counterparts (3.9 v. 23.0 hours; p=0.030). No statistically significant differences were noted in patients weighing 50 to 100 kg (n=3) or patients &gt;100 kg (n=9).
L2205: Reviewer's comment: The small sample size limits the utility of subgroup analysis. No formal subgroup analysis was performed by age or ethnicity.
L2206: Open-label extension phase
L2207: The onset of symptom relief was evaluated in the open-label extension phase by the number of attacks treated with icatibant. The greatest number of attacks treated for any one patient in this portion of the study was 12. More detailed information regarding total drug exposure is discussed in Section 10.3.2.4.2, Safety analysis. The onset of symptom relief ranged from 1.0 to 4.0 hours for all reported attacks and 1.5 to 1.8 hours for patients who experienced 2 to 4 attacks during the OLE (n=59).
L2208: Reviewer's comment: The efficacy data from the OLE are consistent with results obtained from the double-blind portion of the study.
L2209: 10.3.2.3.2 Secondary endpoints analyses
L2210: Time to onset of symptom relief using a modified definition of response
L2211: The study report presents time to onset of symptom relief using a more stringent definition of response as a secondary endpoint analysis. The modified definition is as follows:
L2212: • A response to the right and below a line Y = 4/7 X - 50/7 with X=30\text{mm}.
      
○ X = pre-treatment VAS in mm
○ Y = post-treatment VAS in mm


• Corresponds to a reduction by 50 mm at a baseline VAS = 100 mm and by 20 mm at a baseline VAS = 30 mm.
L2213: Based on this more stringent definition, the median time to onset of symptom relief for the icatibant group versus placebo is 2.5 versus 8.1 hours (p=0.058), with a treatment difference of 5.6 hours.
L2214: Reviewer's comment: Although a more stringent definition for onset of symptom relief increases the treatment difference seen between the two arms of the study, the comparison still does not show a statistically significant difference.
L2215: Response rate at 4 hours after start of treatment
L2216: At 4 hours after start of treatment, the response rate based on the primary symptom was 66.7% for icatibant versus 46.4% for placebo (0.176). When divided by cutaneous versus abdominal attacks, no statistically significant differences were noted as well (p=0.252 and 0.435, respectively).

L2223: Global Assessment
L2224: No statistically significant differences in Global Assessment as scored by the investigator on a 5-point scale were observed between icatibant and placebo groups, with the exception of abdominal symptoms at the 24-hour mark alone (p=0.041).
L2225: Clinical global impression/improvement
L2226: As Baseline, 15 patients (55.6%) in the icatibant group were scored as “moderately ill,” compared to 19 patients (65.5%) in the placebo group. At 4 hours, 10 patients (37.0%) in the icatibant group were reported to be “very much improved,” 9 patients (33.3%) were “much improved,” and 5 (18.5%) were minimally improved. In the placebo group, 1 patient (3.5%) was “very much improved,” 10 (34.5%) were “much improved,” 7 (24.1%) were “minimally improved,” 4 (13.9%) had “no change,” 5 (17.3%) were “minimally worse”, and 2 (7%) were “much worse.”
L2227: Assessment of each symptom by severity
L2228: No consistent, statistically significant differences were observed for individual cutaneous symptoms scored by patients at the various time points between icatibant and placebo treatment groups. For abdominal symptoms, both abdominal pain and nausea were scored more favorably by icatibant patients starting at 1 h post-dose and sustained through the 12-h timepoint (p=0.001 to 0.033).
L2229: Regression of symptoms (start of improvement) according to the patient
L2230: Icatibant patients reported a median time to start of improvement of 0.8 hours, compared to 16.9 hours for placebo patients (p&lt;0.001).
L2231: Reviewer's comment: The start of improvement does not correlate clearly with the onset of symptom relief as assessed by the VAS, calling into question the reliability and reproducibility of either measure. This particular endpoint is not described in the protocol and the study report does not describe how the information was collected (retrospectively versus prospectively).
L2232: Observable regression of symptoms according to the physician
L2233: Investigators reported a median time to visible regression of symptoms of 6.5 hours for icatibant patients, compared to 14.0 hours for placebo patients (p&lt;0.240).
L2234: Overall patient improvement according to the physician
L2235: Investigators reported a median time to start of improvement of 1.0 hours for icatibant patients, compared to 5.7 hours for placebo patients (p&lt;0.001).
L2236: Reviewer's comment: The patient- and physician-reported times to start of improvement for placebo patients differs substantially (16.9 versus 5.7 hours, respectively) for unclear reasons. The reliability of a patient-reported outcome versus physician scoring is difficult to assess, although overall the patient-reported time to start of improvement appears to correlate better with the primary efficacy endpoint, onset of symptom relief as assessed by the VAS.
L2237: Rescue medication use
L2238: Twenty patients total received rescue medication: 6 patients in the icatibant group and 14 in the placebo group. The most commonly administered rescue medication were opioids for pain relief. One patient (015-006) in the placebo group subsequently developed laryngeal symptoms and was treated with open-label icatibant as a rescue medication.
L2239: Reviewer’s comment: Although not presented as a formal secondary endpoint, rescue medication use is reviewed here as a reflection of icatibant’s efficacy in relieving symptoms of an acute HAE attack. On this basis, icatibant compares favorably to placebo. The Applicant has also provided additional subgroup analysis of the primary efficacy endpoint in terms of patients who were using attenuated androgens as a concomitant medication versus those who were not. Icatibant appears to be more efficacious in patients without androgen (p=0.019); plausibly, the concomitant use of androgens may blunt the treatment difference observed for icatibant. However, the numbers are small in these subgroups making analysis difficult.
L2240: 10.3.2.3.3 Laryngeal attack analysis
L2241: Laryngeal attacks in 8 patients were treated with open-label icatibant in the controlled phase of the study. An additional 6 attacks in 11 patients were treated in the OLE phase of Study 2103. One patient (024-002) was treated for a laryngeal attack in both phases of the study. These results are discussed separately.
L2242: Laryngeal attack symptom scores – double blind phase
L2243: The median time to regression of symptoms as reported by the patients was 0.6 hours. Symptom scoring by both patients and investigators at 0, 2, and 4 hours after study drug administration is presented below. Patients were asked to score only 2 attributes of a laryngeal attack, voice changes and difficulties in swallowing.

For the most part, even at baseline, the symptoms appeared to have been limited in severity. Whether the improvements observed over time are attributable to icatibant or reflect the gradual resolution of the attack cannot be ascertained in the absence of a placebo. The Applicant has not provided VAS scores for the laryngeal attack patients, although the protocol dated April 10, 2006 states that all study procedures described in Section 6.1 for the double-blind study participants would be followed.

L2254: Reviewer's comment: The results from the OLE phase in laryngeal attack patients appear relatively comparable to those collected during the controlled phase of Study 2103. Again, whether improvement is related to an effect of icatibant versus natural resolution of these symptoms is unclear.
L2255: 10.3.2.3.4 Post hoc analyses
L2256: In Study 2103, the Applicant identified Study Site 070 (n=10; 5 assigned to icatibant) as an outlier. At this study site, patients receiving icatibant were noted to have a poorer response to icatibant (time to onset of symptom relief \geq 10 hours) compared to other study sites. Post hoc analysis performed excluding this site resulted in a statistically significant treatment difference between icatibant and placebo (2.0 vs 6.0 hours; p=0.026). According to the Applicant's own audit of data, one patient from this site (007-008) was wrongly assigned skin pain as the primary symptom instead of skin swelling. A second patient who received icatibant for an abdominal
L2257: attack was later revealed during the audit to have narcotic drug addiction, violating inclusion/exclusion criteria. A third patient (015-007) at Study Site 015 was found to have a history of pancreatitis, which may have confounded the reporting of abdominal pain symptoms. Post hoc analysis performed excluding Patients 007-014 and 015-007 and reassignment of Patient 007-008 for the primary symptom resulted in a statistically significant treatment difference between icatibant and placebo (2.0 vs 5.0 hours; p=0.049). Post hoc analysis performed excluding Patients 007-014 and 015-007 results in a treatment difference 2.0 vs. 4.6 hours (p=0.069).
L2258: Reviewer's comment: While the protocol violations at Study Site 070 are noteworthy, there does not appear to be evidence of systematic bias at this study site to merit full exclusion of these site results. Given the small total sample size, post hoc exclusion of 10 patients from the efficacy database, including 3 patients with laryngeal edema attacks, is far from trivial. Also, as the unfavorable direction of the results prompted the data audit for this particular study site, the possibility of bias in this type of post hoc analysis remains an issue. From the perspective of the clinical review, these post hoc analyses are considered supportive of efficacy but are not considered as a substitute for the pre-specified analyses based on the ITT population.
L2259: Additional post hoc analysis performed by the statistical reviewer stratifying the results of Study 2103 by US versus non-US locations indicates that efficacy results were fairly comparable, without any clear geographic differences. Further details regarding post-hoc analysis can be found in the statistical team's review. The median time to onset of symptom relief for icatibant recipients at US and non-US locations was 2.25 and 2.5 hours respectively. In the placebo arm, the times were 7.0 and 3.6 hours, respectively.

L2262: 10.3.2.4 Safety assessments
L2263: 10.3.2.4.1 Adverse events
L2264: Serious adverse events
L2265: No deaths were reported from Study 2103. No SAEs were reported during the controlled phase of the study. In the OLE, 4 SAEs for 3 patients (3 of 42 patients, 7.1%) were reported. Patient 015-007, who had a prior history of pancreatitis, experienced 2 episodes of severe pancreatitis at 11 and 14 days after the start of the OLE. Patient 007-008 had a worsening, severe HAE attack (the patient's 7th attack) despite 3 sequential 30 mg doses of icatibant. Patient 018-002 developed severe chest pain and abdominal and cutaneous HAE attack symptoms 7 days after
L2266: receipt of icatibant for a second HAE attack. The patient had a negative evaluation for cardiac causes of chest pain and was discharged later that evening.
L2267: Discontinuations from the study due to adverse events
L2268: No patients discontinued from the study due to adverse events.
L2269: Common adverse events
L2270: Injection site reactions were the most common adverse event and were assessed separately from other AEs in the Applicant’s study report. HAE attacks were only to be reported as an AE if there was a worsening of symptoms during a treated attack. New HAE attacks were not to be reported as AEs, but the study report states that some investigators reported new attacks as AEs, leading to an over-reporting of HAE attack as an AE.
L2271: Double-blind phase
L2272: The most common AE were local injection site reactions, reported by 26 patients (96.3%) in the icatibant treatment group and 8 patients (27.6%) in the placebo group. These reactions were characterized most commonly by erythema (96.3%) and swelling (85.2%), following by burning, itching, warmth, and cutaneous pain. The median duration of the reaction was 3.5 hours, with erythema as the principal persistent symptoms for that duration.
L2273: Eleven patients (40.7%) in the icatibant group reported an adverse event besides a local injection site reaction, compared to 18 (62.1%) in the placebo group. The most common AE aside from local site reaction was HAE attack, reported by 3 patients (11.1%) in the icatibant group and 5 patients (17.2%) in the placebo group. A table of AEs occurring more commonly in the icatibant group than placebo is presented below.

L2275: Source: je049-2103-report-body.pdf, Section 12.2.2.1 and Section 16, Table 6.17.1.1, Page 1970
L2276: • Laryngeal attack patientsInjection site reactions were reported by all 8 subjects who received open-label icatibant. The type of reaction and duration were similar to those reactions reported for abdominal and cutaneous attack patients. Other AEs reported in this group included HAE attacks (n=3), headache (n=2), viral infection (n=1), thermal burn (n=1), epistaxis (n=1), dermal cyst (n=1), and urticaria (n=1).• OLE phaseOne-hundred-four AEs other than injection site reactions were reported in 39 patients (out of 42 total) during the OLE phase of the study. Injection site reactions were reported in 41 of 42 (97.6%) patients who received open-label icatibant during the OLE of Study 2103. Other common AEs occurring in \geq 3\% of patients included the following: HAE attacks (n=12, 28.6%), headache (n=11, 26.2%), upper respiratory tract infection (n=5, 11.9%), abdominal pain (n=2, 4.8%), chest pain (n=3, 7.1%), nasopharyngitis (n=4, 9.5%), and sinusitis (n=2, 4.8%). Four SAEs described above were also reported during the OLE phase.
L2277: Reviewer's comment: Adverse event findings are relatively consistent among the different phases of Study 2103. Local injection site reactions are nearly ubiquitous. Other common AEs are HAE attack and headache. Whether HAE attack is a true AE or a reflection of the underlying disease is unclear, although the frequency of this AE was higher in the placebo group than in the icatibant treatment group, suggesting the latter explanation. In terms of attack site locations – cutaneous, abdominal, and laryngeal – there did not appear to be any differences in the nature or frequency of AEs among these patient subgroups.
L2278: 10.3.2.4.2 Vital signs
L2279: No consistent or lasting vital sign changes were noted over the course of visits in the double-blind portion of the study, laryngeal attack patients, or during the OLE phase.
L2280: 10.3.2.4.3 Physical exams
L2281: Aside from local injection site reactions, physical exam findings were consistent the abdominal, cutaneous, and laryngeal manifestations of HAE attacks.
L2282: 10.3.2.4.4 Laboratory parameters
L2283: Hematology
L2284: Drops in hematocrit and total white counts were observed in both icatibant and placebo patients from baseline to time of attack, which appeared to resolve by the 24 hour time-point.
L2285: Reviewer's comment: As the change in hematocrit and WBC count was observed in both treatment groups and rapidly resolved, the change most likely represent changes in fluid volume status during an acute HAE attack, rather than destruction or decreased production of blood cells.
L2286: Clinical chemistry
L2287: No persistent derangements in blood chemistry parameters were reported for the icatibant treatment group versus placebo. In terms of outliers, 1 patient (017-002) was noted to have an
L2288: elevation in LFTs (ALT 77 → 164 U/L, AST 50 → 80 U/L, and GGT 60 → 77 U/L) after study drug administration that resolved spontaneously by 30 days.
L2289: During the OLE phase, one patient (040-003) had a similar transient increase in AST from 52 → 136 U/L. This patient was also reported to have a transient increase in total bilirubin from 8.55 to 82.08 µmol/L, and a more persistent increase in serum CK levels (175 → 309 U/L). Two weeks after treatment, the CK level was 278 U/L and further follow-up is not provided.
L2290: Antigenicity
L2291: The study report states that an assay for detection of icatibant antibodies remains under development at the time of the study report and data will be reported separately when available.
L2292: ECGs
L2293: No consistent derangements in ECGs were noted for patients. Two patients in the icatibant group were noted to have abnormal ECGs at baseline, including a left bundle branch block and Wolff-Parkinson-White Type A abnormality. No QT prolongation was reported in any of the patients.
L2294: 10.3.3 Study summary and conclusions
L2295: Study 2103 provides limited support for the efficacy of icatibant in the treatment of acute attacks of HAE. While numerically favorable, the study did not show a statistically significant difference between icatibant and placebo for the primary efficacy endpoint, time to onset of symptom relief as measured by the VAS. Similarly, the secondary efficacy results were generally numerically favorable for icatibant but were far from robust. Of particular concern was the durability of response, which appeared to be limited for both abdominal and cutaneous HAE attacks. Additional study of dose or dosing interval may address this issue.
L2296: In terms of laryngeal attacks, the study results are favorable, but the selection of unvalidated subjective endpoints combined with the small sample size make efficacy for this particular type of HAE attack difficult to assess. As these patients were treated with open-label icatibant, no placebo comparison is available.
L2297: In terms of safety, the safety profile for icatibant in limited repeat dosing appears acceptable based on the information provided. The most common adverse event that is clearly attributable to the drug is local injection site reaction, which appears to be relatively mild and self-limited in the majority of cases. Several cases of mild transient transaminitis raise hepatotoxicity as a possibility, but the small number of patients assessed in the study does not permit a more thorough evaluation. In consideration of the proposed indication and the intended manner of use, the nature of hepatic enzyme elevations observed to date is not particularly concerning. Of note, safety information on the antigenicity of the drug has not been provided.
L2298: 10.4 Individual Study Report: Study 4102
L2299: 10.4.1 Study protocol: Study 4102
L2300: Title: Study to estimate the minimum clinically significant difference (MCSD) in Visual Analogue Scale (VAS) scores for skin swelling, skin pain, and abdominal pain in subjects with hereditary angioedema (HAE)
L2301: 10.4.1.1 Study administrative information
L2302: • Study dates: June 19, 2006 to March 24, 2007• Study site: Multicenter (20 international study sites)• Study report date: May 31, 2007
L2303: 10.4.1.2 Objectives/rationale
L2304: • To estimate the MCSD in VAS scores as the basis for defining “onset of symptom relief” for cutaneous and abdominal HAE attacks of at least moderate severity.
L2305: Reviewer’s comment: The “time to onset of symptom relief” is the primary efficacy endpoint for the two pivotal Phase 3 trials, Studies 2101 and 2103.
L2306: 10.4.1.3 Design overview
L2307: Non-interventional, observational study to validate the patient-reported outcome tool, the Visual Analogue Scale (VAS), used in the Phase 3 program for icatibant.
L2308: 10.4.1.4 Study population
L2309: 80 adult subjects with HAE, presenting with an acute abdominal and/or cutaneous HAE attack of at least moderate severity
L2310: Inclusion criteria
L2311: • Age of 18 years or older• Documented diagnosis of HAE Type I or II (confirmed by medical history and C1-INH deficiency/&lt;50% functional level). Subjects with higher C1-INH levels permitted to enroll if HAE clearly confirmed clinically and an acceptable rational provided for the higher functional level of C1-INH.• History of angioedema attacks involving the skin or abdominal areas
L2312: Exclusion criteria
L2313: • Other form of angioedema, such as acquired angioedema• Participation in Studies 2102 or 2103 if the current attack is treated with an investigational drug product
L2314: 10.4.1.5 Study treatments
L2315: No study treatments were administered. Standard of care (danazol, C1-INH, fresh frozen plasma, tranexamic acid, or other investigational medications except icatibant) was selected by the primary care physician and documented in the CRF.
L2316: 10.4.1.6 Study procedures
L2317: Study 4102 was a non-interventional, observational study. Patients meeting screening criteria were instructed to come to the clinic or contact by telephones in the event of a cutaneous and/or abdominal HAE attack. Attacks involving only laryngeal edema were excluded. At least one symptom had to be rated as moderate severity in order to qualify for the study. Patients were instructed on the various assessments and asked to complete the following in the patient diaries until complete resolution of symptoms or 48 hours from baseline, whichever occurred first:
L2318: • Visual Analogue Scale (VAS) of skin swelling, skin pain, abdominal pain, and nausea• Verbal Descriptor Scale (VDS) for skin swelling, skin pain, and abdominal pain• Patient Symptom Score (PSS) for skin swelling, erythema, irritation, skin pain, abdominal pain, nausea, vomiting, diarrhea• Physicians completed a Global Assessment, Clinical Global Impression, and Physician Symptom score.
L2319: Based on the severity of the attack, patients were managed in the clinic or were followed by telephone contact, completing the diaries at home. Diaries were filled out at Baseline (0 h), 1, 2, 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, 14, 18, 24, 36, and 48 hours post-baseline.
L2320: All concomitant medications were recorded in the CRF.
L2321: 10.4.1.7 Efficacy parameters
L2322: 10.4.1.7.1 Primary efficacy variables
L2323: • VAS of skin swelling, skin pain, and abdominal pain○ The VAS is a 100 mm horizontal line with 0mm = no symptoms and 100mm = worst possible symptom. Patients mark on the line to rate the intensity of each symptoms of at baseline and pre-determined time points throughout the treatment period.○ The MCSD was defined as a change in VAS corresponding to subject report of “a little less” symptom according to the VDS, used as the basis for defining the “onset of symptom relief” for skin swelling, skin pain, and/or abdominal pain○ For cutaneous attack patients, the MCSD analysis was based on the most severe symptoms; if both equally severe, “pain” used.• VDS for skin swelling, skin pain, and abdominal pain○ Change from baseline rated as “much less,” “a little less,” “the same,” “a little more,” or “much more”
L2324: 10.4.1.7.2 Secondary efficacy variables
L2325: • Severity of symptoms (scored by physician). Physician scores the following on a 5-point scale, with 0=absence of symptoms and 4=very severe: cutaneous swelling, erythema, cutaneous pain, abdominal pain, nausea, abdominal tenderness, vomiting, and diarrhea.• Clinical Global Impression. Physician rates patient’s overall presentation relative to other HAE patients in the physician’s experience on an 8-point scale, with 0=not assessed, 1=normal, to 7=among the most extremely ill patients.
L2326: • Global Assessment (physician). Physician scores the following 3 symptom categories – “cutaneous,” “abdominal,” and “laryngeal” – on 5-point scale, with 0=absence of symptoms and 4=very severe.• VAS for nausea (subject)• PSS for skin swelling, erythema, irritation, skin pain, abdominal pain, nausea, vomiting, diarrhea. Each symptom scored on a scale from 0 to 4, with 0=absence of symptoms and 4=very severe. For vomiting and diarrhea, patients were instructed to assess symptoms over the last 2 hours.
L2327: 10.4.1.8 Safety parameters
L2328: As a non-interventional, observational study, no formal safety evaluations were planned. Patients were treated according to standard of care at the discretion of the physician. Adverse events were recorded in the CRF.
L2329: 10.4.1.9 Statistical plan
L2330: The intended purpose of the study was to identify the change in VAS score that corresponded most closely to a VDS measurement, taken as the “gold standard,” that indicates that a patient has had a clinically meaningful symptom improvement (“a little less”). Receiver Operating Characteristics Curve (ROC) analysis was used to identify an optimum cut-point for the VAS change from baseline that corresponded with the VDS. A sample size of 60 evaluable patients was calculated to provide 80% power to detect a correlation of 0.36 between the VDS (ordinal variable) and the change in VAS from baseline.
L2331: Reviewer’s comment: The Applicant does not explain why the VDS should be taken as the “gold standard.” From the description provided, the VDS is another PRO without any validation.
L2332: 10.4.2 Results
L2333: 10.4.2.1 Protocol amendments
L2334: No formal amendments were made to the original protocol. A change was made on the study population in the statistical analysis plan (March 27, 2007) that utilized a computer algorithm to identify patients with usable symptom data. Patients who did not report any improvement from baseline on the VDS and patients with obvious discordance between the trend in VAS, PSS, and VDS were excluded.
L2335: Reviewer’s comment: The issue of unusable data as defined by the Applicant illustrates potential pitfalls with the VAS as an efficacy endpoint. The Applicant does not explain how these weaknesses might limit interpretation of VAS data or how they might be addressed in practice to maximize the collection of usable data.
L2336: 10.4.2.2 Protocol deviations
L2337: Patient 120-012 was identified as a protocol violation for failure to contact the investigator prior to diary completion.
L2338: 10.4.2.3 Datasets analyzed
L2339: The outcome analyses are based on the evaluable patient population, defined by a diary review algorithm in the statistical analysis plan dated March 27, 2007 and briefly described in Section 10.4.2.1 of this review.
L2340: 10.4.2.4 Study patients
L2341: 10.4.2.4.1 Patient disposition
L2342: An initial 149 patients were enrolled. During the study period, 81 patients experienced a qualifying HAE attack and 80 patients completed the study. Of these 80 patients, 57 patients had diary data which was deemed usable based on the diary review algorithm described in Section 10.4.2.1 of this review.

L2345: 10.4.2.4.2 Demographics and baseline characteristics
L2346: Of 57 evaluable patients, 43 (75.44%) were female, 54 (94.74%) were White and 3 (5.26%) were Asian. The mean age was 41.61 years (range 18.63 to 75.68 years). Four patients were categorized as having Type II HAE; the remainder was categorized as Type I HAE. As a group, the study cohort averaged 10 HAE attacks within the past 6 months, ranging from 0 to 51 attacks.
L2347: 10.4.2.4.3 Concomitant therapies
L2348: Patients received a variety of therapies during the course of the study for treatment of HAE. The most common therapies administered included danazol, C1-INH replacement, aminocaproic acid, and antiemetics.
L2349: 10.4.2.5 Efficacy endpoint outcomes
L2350: 10.4.2.5.1 Primary endpoint analysis
L2351: Based on ROC analysis, a 9 mm change in VAS was identified as the MCSD for “onset of symptom relief.” This cut-point provided 88.24% specificity and 82.61% sensitivity at 6 hours post baseline. Changes in the VAS corresponded to changes in the VDS (r=0.7576; p&lt;0.0001).
L2352: 10.4.2.5.2 Secondary endpoints analyses
L2353: The Applicant has not provided analyses for the other symptom scores collected in the study.
L2354: 10.4.2.6 Safety assessments
L2355: No formal safety assessments were performed.
L2356: 10.4.3 Study summary and conclusions
L2357: Study 4102 provides limited support for the use of the VAS to determine onset of symptom relief. Although the study demonstrates strong correlation between the VAS and VDS, the value of this correlation is uncertain as the VDS is another type of PRO which has not been validated, even though the Applicant proposes the VDS as the “gold standard.” Furthermore, the study did not include any patient interviews to substantiate the terms used in the VAS or VDS, nor did it provide illustrative examples for patients filling out patient diaries. For example, the concept of “a little less” symptom may vary widely among individuals with HAE. In addition, the Applicant has not reconciled the discordance in VAS and VDS observed in several study participants’ diaries (n=7 out of 80 patients who completed the study). Whether the discordance reflects a lack of comprehension on the part of the patient or error in data collection is uncertain. In either circumstance, the observed discordance with the proposed gold standard is problematic and reflects a potential flaw with the VAS instrument.
L2358: 10.5 Line-by-Line Labeling Review
L2359: As the recommended action is Not Approvable, a detailed line-by-line labeling review was not performed. Major issues identified with the proposed labeling are discussed in Section 9.4, Labeling Review.

REVIEW SUMMARY:



This is a 45-day filing and planning review of an NDA for icatibant, a selective competitive antagonist at the bradykinin type 2 (B2) receptor. The Applicant, Jerini AG, has developed icatibant 30 mg subcutaneous injection for acute, intermittent use. The proposed indication is the treatment of hereditary angioedema (HAE). Two pivotal efficacy and safety studies are submitted in support of this application (JE049 2102 and 2103). The Applicant has provided additional data from several uncontrolled studies, including an open-label Phase 2 study of intravenously and subcutaneously administered icatibant in HAE patients (Study 2101) and pharmacokinetic studies in healthy volunteers (Studies 1101, 1102, and 1103). Safety information on repeated, long-term use is presented from the ongoing open-label extension studies for Studies 2102 and 2103 (2102-B and 2103-B, respectively). The Applicant has also provided results of a non-interventional study, 4102, to demonstrate the validity of the patient-completed Visual Analogue Scale (VAS), which was used as the primary efficacy parameter in the Phase 3 studies. These study reports are appropriately indexed and organized to allow review. The Applicant has provided an Integrated Summary of Efficacy, Integrated Summary of Safety, Integrated Summary of Benefits and Risks, copies of proposed labeling, and appropriate case report forms.
The submission is adequate to allow clinical review. The submission is fileable.



OUTSTANDING ISSUES: None.

L2370: 1. GENERAL INFORMATION AND BACKGROUND
L2371: Icatibant is a bradykinin type-2 receptor antagonist under development for treatment of acute attacks of hereditary angioedema (HAE). It was previously developed by Aventis AG under IND (b) (4) for treatment of allergic rhinitis, asthma, and post-operative pain. IND (b) (4) was subsequently withdrawn on August 9, 1996 (not for safety reason per communication dated August 28, 1996). Jerini subsequently acquired the drug product and right of reference to IND (b) (4) opening IND 68,214 (April 8, 2004) for further evaluation of icatibant as a treatment for HAE. (b) (4)
L2372: The proposed dose for acute attacks of HAE is 30 mg icatibant administered subcutaneously.
L2373: The text from the proposed INDICATIONS AND USAGE section of the label follows: (b) (4)
L2374: The 505(b)(1) application is an electronic submission.
L2375: 2. CLINICAL DEVELOPMENT PROGRAM
L2376: The Applicant's clinical development program relies on two pivotal Phase 3 studies, one performed in Europe (Study 2102; FAST-2) and one conducted in the US (Study 2103; FAST-3). Of note, only Study 2103 was placebo-controlled; Study 2101 used tranexamic acid as an active-control. Both studies used the same efficacy endpoint, time to onset of symptom relief. These studies are presented in further detail below. In addition to these Phase 3 studies, the Applicant has submitted study reports for pharmacokinetic studies in healthy volunteers, an open-label proof-of-concept study in HAE patients, and a PRO validation, non-intervention study.

L2379: 3. FOREIGN MARKETING AND REGULATORY HISTORY
L2380: Icatibant has not yet been approved for marketing for any indication [2.5.5.13].
L2381: The opening IND (April 8, 2004) was initially reviewed in the Division of Gastrointestinal and Coagulation Drug Products prior to reassignment to the Division of Pulmonary and Allergy Products in September 2005. A timeline of regulatory proceedings is included below:
L2382: July 31, 2003            Request for Orphan Drug Designation
  November 25, 2003    Orphan Drug Designation #03-1762 granted
  February 6, 2004      Pre-IND meeting:
                                      Discussion focused on selection and validation of symptom-based endpoints, including justification of the minimum important difference. The Applicant was also reminded that two adequate, well-controlled studies were the usual requirement for a new indication. The ongoing, open-label, proof-of-concept study, #2101, being conducted at that time in Europe was unlikely to meet this requirement. Other issues discussed included eligibility for Fast Track Designation and Priority Review.
April 8, 2004            Opening IND:
                                      The proposed study was a multi-center, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial in 56 adult subjects with moderate to severe cutaneous or abdominal symptoms of HAE. The primary efficacy endpoint was time to onset of symptom relief assessed by a Visual Analogue Scale (VAS) completed by patients; symptom score and global assessment score were secondary efficacy outcomes.
L2383: Patients with laryngeal symptoms were enrolled in an open-label arm of the study and their outcomes were to be reported descriptively. Comments communicated to the Applicant following review were to include adequate dose-ranging studies in the clinical development program, as well as validating the VAS instrument and justifying a time to onset of symptom relief that is clinically meaningful.
L2384: June 11, 2004
L2385: Request for SPA (SN 004); Protocol JE049 #2103 (SN 005): The Applicant amended the opening IND protocol and requested a SPA. The main changes were addition of an open-label extension phase, assessment of laryngeal symptoms using a 5-point symptom score, and new pharmacoeconomic and safety secondary endpoints. In a response dated July 29, 2004, the Division confirmed that the proposed endpoints and sample size were acceptable, with the caveat that onset of symptom relief should be clinically meaningful. The Division also advised that safety data should be recorded in detail from the extension phase of the study.
L2386: June 15, 2004
L2387: Fast Track Designation granted

L2392: • QTc data should be collected in the proposed PK study JE049 #1103 with additional time points at or near Cmax• Data obtained from the open-label, Phase 2 study JE049 #2101 did not fully support the 30 mg SC dose used in the Phase 3 pivotal trials• Pediatric use to be assessed in post-marketing phase
L2393: September 2005
